Who Is He?

If an earthquake had brought down the walls of the hospital, the shock could not have been greater than the sensation caused by the sudden death of the Chief Surgeon, Dr. Eugene Taylor. From the doorman and elevator operators to the orderlies, nurses and doctors, all were astounded, stunned, by the tragic news. "For twenty years, I have opened and shut the door for him on his arrival and departure from the hospital... always greeted me with a pleasant 'Good Morning!'... never failed to inquire after my health and that of my family... 

What a dear, lovable man he was!... May he rest in peace!" "Dr. Taylor dead? - Impossible! I brought him up by elevator to the floor of the surgery theatre only half an hour ago! 

My God! I can't believe he's dead." "He was getting ready for an operation. He was standing at the wash basin, scrubbing his hands, and suddenly - collapsed. I and the other orderlies helped carry him to his private office. A pity! A great pity! There aren't many like him." "The hospital won't be the same without him. Working beside him in the operating room and in the wards, was highly gratifying, not only for me, but for all the nurses. 

Whenever he was operating, one could sense the deep concern he felt for the patient. The patient was all important to him. What a surgeon! What a wonderful man!"

In the lounge of the medical staff, everyone crowded around Dr. Chester and Dr. Newman, and bombarded them

with questions. Dr. Chester had been the personal physician of the suddenly-demised surgeon. Dr. Newman had been one of his earliest assistants in the surgery department, and a likely candidate for the newly vacated post of Chief Surgeon. "Taylor had a number of attacks of angina pectoris during the past few days," Dr. Chester explained. "I examined him and prescribed several weeks of strict bed rest. I didn't mince words. I told him clearly and definitely that disregard of my orders meant endangering his life. He agreed, but he stayed at home no more than one day. He came back early this morning and went right to work. He 'died with his boots on."" "His sudden death is wrapped in mystery," remarked one of the doctors. "They say that before he expired, he asked that Shanan, the interne, be sent to him. He mentioned no one; not his wife, nor his children, nor his intimate colleagues at the hospital; only Shanan. It appears that he breathed his last, whispering some secret into the ear of this particular interne. What do you think about that, Dr. Chester?" "I can answer that," exclaimed Dr. Newman. "I know this fellow Shanan very well. He has a mystic streak in him and when it suits his purpose, he is capable of creating melodramatic situations. I wouldn't take all these rumours seriously. Shanan is a Russian immigrant, and in spite of the fact that he has been in this country for some time and is a graduate of Columbia, and has been an interne for the past three years, he is still in the grip of his old-country ideas. He's a queer fellow who is eager to attract attention by his insistence on remaining what he had been in some out-of-the-way little town in the Ukraine." 

Dr. Chester was thoughtful for a moment, then said: “I'm afraid it is not as simple as you think, Dr. Newman. Last night Mrs. Taylor telephoned me and told me that her husband had decided to return to the hospital, regardless of my strict orders, and despite his looking weak and ill. 

She asked me to come over in the hope that I might, after all, succeed in persuading him to change his mind. 66 'Chester,' he said to me, 'I must return to the hospital 10

tomorrow, just for tomorrow, and after that I shall carry out your instructions to the letter, for as long as you wish.' "Very interesting indeed, Eugene,' I retorted. 'And what, may I ask, is so important about tomorrow that you are prepared to risk your life for it?"" "He was silent for a moment, then replied: 'You must believe me Chester, it is important. To me, at any rate, it is very important - important enough to take the chance I am taking.'" "Only later, when Mrs. Taylor accompanied me to the door, did I learn from her why Eugene was so determined to return to the operating room. It seems that in the past three years he had become very much attached to Shanan, the interne. He saw himself reflected in Shanan, and taught him his methods in surgery. "However, it seems that on the very first day of Taylor's absence from the hospital, something happened to Shanan while he was performing a serious operation. This had had so depressing an effect upon him that he had come running to Taylor's house and told him that he had neither the stamina nor the ability of a surgeon and that he would never again take a scalpel in his hand. 66 

'Shanan is now very much like a swimmer who has nearly drowned,' Taylor said to his wife and he dreads the water as a terrifying enemy. The only way to overcome this dread is to force the nearly drowned swimmer to plunge immediately into the water again - and swim. That's what 

I must now do with Shanan. There's no other way..."

Dr. Chester fell silent and looked fixedly at Dr. Newman. "What happened after that?" asked several, all at once. 

Chester continued: "This morning, Taylor, as usual, came to the operating theatre, and called upon Shanan to assist him. They both started getting ready for the operation, and while scrubbing up, Taylor collapsed." "And Shanan?" they asked again eagerly. "The microphone summoned me urgently to Taylor's office. I knew that the calamity had occurred. When I entered, Eugene was dead. I found Shanan on his knees, with 11

his head on the breast of the deceased, murmuring words which I could not catch."

Silence reigned in the room. The doctors looked at one another in wonderment, and though no one uttered it, each one asked himself: "Who is he, this Shanan?” 12

Get Thee Out 

The Ukrainian village of Struga is not to be found on any map. There is hardly anything so historically significant about it as to merit its notation on the vast terrestrial globe. For little David Shanan, however, Struga was the center of the universe. To his childish thinking, there was hardly an event that could not have occurred in Struga. 

Those wonderful stories related in the Pentateuch which so fascinated the boy and were the source of his spiritual sustenance, must have taken place, he felt certain, in his own realm of Struga. His father's sprawling orchard, with its meandering paths and many trees, some gnarled, but all laden with apples, pears, peaches, plums and cherries, was indubitably the Garden of Eden itself. The Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil must be none other than that massive apple tree on the north side, whose boughs blotted out half the firmament and whose roots thrust up from the earth like gigantic sinews. The peasants all judged it to be a thousand years old. It was here that God must have invoked His curse upon the serpent and punished Adam and Eve by expelling them from the garden. 

David knew that after the great flood had been sent upon the earth as punishment for the wickedness of mankind,

Noah's ark had been stranded upon the mountain of Ararat - and little David's big black eyes would search for that spot among the crests of the Carpathian mountain range that seemed to stretch so far, ever so far beyond Struga, to where the sky had blended with the earth. Pharoah's daughter 13

had found Moses floating in an ark of bulrushes along the banks of the Nile- little David raced down the sloping banks past which the wide Dniester River coursed majestically, and came upon the precise place where the Egyptian princess must have found the infant Moses. Moses had grown up to deliver Israel from Egyptian oppression, leading the Israelites through the wilderness and through the lands of diverse peoples. Harassed, famished, in desperate straits, the 

Israelites had wandered through the desert for forty years before they were ushered into the Promised Land. David's father informed him that some time after, the Jews had transgressed grievously against the Lord, whereupon He had banished them from their own land and dispersed them throughout the world. Since then, David was told, the Jews had been living in exile for well-nigh two thousand years, hoping and praying that a new Messiah would appear to lead them back to the land of their fathers. David recalled once saying to his father: "Struga is the best country in the world. Let the Messiah bring all Jews to Struga!" Then his father had explained: "Struga is only a small part of the lands of Exile. It was 

God's wish that my wandering ancestors should find refuge in Struga. Our family has been rooted here for generations, but still this is not the land of our Fathers. Eretz Israel is that land! Every Jew yearns to be redeemed from the Exile and to return to the Holy Land.” 

Thinking of this, David lingered now on the river bank, steeped in sadness. He was overcome by a fervent love for his birthplace for its fragrant meadows; the fertile fields of wheat, rye and maize; the thatched huts of the peasants; the shepherds and their flocks; the magnificent Carpathian 

Mountains and forests; the ever-exhilarating Dniester River, teeming with white-bellied pike and carp disporting in its crystal waters. No, David was not too anxious to be liberated - to wander through foreign climes and among alien people. 

He did not relish the idea of misery and want. He would rather cling to Struga forever.

Instinctively, and symbolically, the lad dug his fingers into the moist earth of the shore, and then held up fistfulls of 14

loam, as though he were holding a treasure and was reluctant to part with it...

The unexpected chimes of the church bells roused David from his reveries. It was not a monotonous sharp tocsin, summoning the villagers to rescue their neighbors from fire, nor was it the tolling of funeral bells. No, all the bells, large and small, were mirthfully ringing as they did for Sunday services. Yet, it was neither Sunday nor any other 

Christian feast day. These chimes were ringing in honor of a momentous event in the history of the Russian Empire: the nation was celebrating the three-hundredth anniversary of the Romanov dynasty.

As though he had the premonition that he was destined to be the last sovereign of that dynasty, Czar Nicholas II had promulgated a decree that the event was to be celebrated with greater pomp and fanfare than any previous jubilee. 

Preparations for this solemn festivity had been going on for two years, throughout his far-flung dominion with its manymillioned conglomeration of peoples and races. All national affairs, whether economic, political, or cultural, were submerged and dwarfed by this impending celebration. Mother 

Russia had been flooded with all kinds of photographs of the royal family; school children were rehearsing new odes in honor of the Emperor; all religious institutions were to assemble their respective worshipers to pray for and glorify the Czar of All the Russias, and all his kin; army officers and other government officials, from St. Petersburg to the remotest hamlet in the bleak wastes of Siberia, were ordered to spruce up for the celebration, to stage parades, folk plays, dances and festivities, in keeping with the occasion. 

Now the church bells of Struga were obediently reminding the humble villagers of their duty. These peasants bore as little love for their present sovereign as for his predecessor. 

Czardom, to them, meant but two plagues, two thorns in the flesh: exhorbitant taxes that they knew went into the Czar's coffers, and the conscription of their twenty-one year old sons for a five-year stretch in the army, far from home. In the course of their military service, these sons became Russified and indoctrinated with peculiar ideas, so that after- 15

wards one could barely recognize their Ukrainian mother tongue when they spoke it. There was a strange air about them, and their attachment to Struga was noticeably weakened. Hence, it was more out of fear than anything else, that the Struga men of the soil attended church services that day. It seemed to them a shame and a sin to forego a day of plowing and sowing now, when the spring sun was showering its brilliance upon the awakening land. Who knew if the weather would be as good tomorrow? The potential loss of a good day's work, and their lack of enthusiasm for the Romanov monarchy, put the industrious peasants into a mood of indifference and lassitude. 

This lackadaisical mood of the villagers exasperated Vassilii Kravlenko, the chief of rural police. The son of a local peasant, he had been radically changed by his military service and its Russianizing influence. He was tall, well-proportioned, handsome, and dashing — with blue eyes, a crop of tawny hair, a sculptured nose, and thin lips that revealed dazzling white teeth. During his fifth year in the army he had been stationed with the St. Petersburg garrison, and this assignment had proved to be a turning point in his life.

The sergeant of Vassilii's detachment at that time had been a tall, broad-beamed, swarthy fellow, named Stepan 

Smirnov, with piercing black eyes and a mustache waxed to needle points. The son of a domestic servant to a wealthy landowner, he had been born and raised on the Crimean Peninsula. He hobnobbed with the aristocracy, and, at every opportunity, would mention this to the enlisted men under him. Had it not been for his semi-illiteracy, he would certainly have been wearing the uniform of an officer. 

As he proceeded to inspect the newly arrived detachment, Sergeant Smirnov halted in front of Vassilii and regarded him mockingly. Until Vassilii's arrival, Smirnov considered himself the best-looking man in the garrison. The young ladies strolling on the boulevards and in the city park used to single him out to flirt with, and even the wives of the officers were not entirely indifferent to his looks. Now Private 16

Vassilii Kravlenko had appeared on the scene as a rival, to threaten his position. "What's your name?" the sergeant asked gruffly. "Vassilii Ivanovich Kravlenko, sir!" "Where do you come from?" the sergeant continued. "From Struga - on the Dniester, sir." "Then you are a peasant — a mouzhik.” "That is right, sir." "What did you do at home would not give up. 

pasture swine?” The sergeant

Vassilii winced, but his soldierly discipline prevailed, and still standing at attention staring straight ahead, he blurted out: "No, not at all, sir!" And, after a momentary pause, unaware of what he was saying, he added: “Our swine pasture themselves!" 

The other soldiers burst into a roar of laughter. "Silence, you scoundrels!" the irate sergeant thundered.

Then he resumed his tirade againt Vassilii. "You son-ofa-bitch! You pig's snout! Where do you think you are among your Ukrainian hayseeds? I'll teach you to be a comedian! I'll settle your hash, you Ukranian scum, in a way that will make you forget even your bitch of a mother!"

This vilification of his mother made something snap in Vassilii's mind; all his senses were suddenly lashed into fury, and in an ecstacy of rage, the infuriated soldier struck his tormentor with all his might. In the next instant Vassilii realized that this offense called for a minimum prison sentence of ten years. He visualized himself being thrown into bleak, solitary confinement, cut off from his kin and friends in the prime of life, and he was filled with wretchedness and despair.

Count Mishkin, a general and a landowner, used to spend his summer vacations in his villa in the Carpathian Mountains. During the rest of the year, he resided in his St. 

Petersburg mansion, since he had to keep in close touch with the inner circle at court.

Like most Russian noblemen, Count Mishkin had been a 17

military man nearly all his life and had never completely severed his army connections. After active service in the 

Russo-Japanese War, the Count had been appointed to the post of commandant of the St. Petersburg garrison. When the serious charge against Vassilii Kravlenko came to his attention, he was angry, but also curious, since the matter seemed so incredible. The accused private happened to be a peasant from Struga - a native of the region where the Count had his own estates, forests, and flour and sugar refineries. What a coincidence - he could hardly believe it! 

Although he was usually stern and severe about military discipline, this affair suddenly struck Count Mishkin as smacking of slapstick comedy, and he began to chuckle. It ought to prove the funniest yarn of the year for the camarilla at the Imperial Court. He was irked by the idea of the culprit hailing from Struga - his own bailiwick. To

Το sentence a young peasant from Struga to some penal settlement in the God-forsaken Siberian tundra, was practically tantamount to depriving the Count, himself, of some personal property, something exclusively and intimately his own. Like as not, if Vassilii had committed his crime in the heart of Struga, Count Mishkin would have insisted on observing the strict letter of the law. However, here in the capital of Russia, so far from home, it was another matter. And so he decided to exculpate the soldier. 

The commandant rang a bell, and when an adjutant appeared, he rapped out: "Bring in the prisoner, Vassilii 

Kravlenko. At once!" "At your service, Your Excellency!" the adjutant replied, saluted, and left hurriedly.

Before long, the prisoner was led into the chamber, where the commandant was sitting at a Louis Quinze mahogany desk, with plush drapes covering the huge windows. 

Vassilii saw the commandant 

the man in whose hands his fate now rested as through a mist. His heart pounded and his knees wobbled, as he advanced slowly, flanked by two gendarmes. Within three paces of the desk, the trio halted, freezing to attention.

In a leisurely way, the commandant gestured to the two 18

guards to leave. Vassilii had by now regained his senses to some extent, and was amazed to recognize in the judgeadvocate his exalted neighbor, Count Mishkin of Struga. 

Could he, Vassilii, be in a trance? Was it a prank of the

Evil One? The confused private proceeded to make the sign of the cross. “Why are you crossing yourself, you stupid oaf?" the general taunted him. “You're not in a church now, and I'm not a priest!" “Oh, yes, indeed, it's you - Your Excellency," Vassilii mumbled. "How dared you raise your fist against a man of higher rank?" the general went on.

In his state of excitement, Vassilii failed to grasp the question and, as though bewitched, said: "Yes, it is really 

Your Excellency himself! This is like Struga... No matter what the punishment, it will be easier to take now." "Stop that silly talk and answer when you're spoken to," his interrogator commanded. "How dared you slap a sergeant? That is mutiny - and the Czar decrees the gallows for mutineers!" "I'm guilty, Your Excellency. I never intended to hit him. 

He had insulted my mother - and I struck him once, hardly realizing what I was doing." "You Struga peasants are a hotheaded lot, and ready to mutiny at the drop of a hat," the commandant went on. "Only last summer the Struga chief of police had to ask the authorities for a company of Cossacks, because your fellow peasants refused to send their sons to the recruiting station before the crops were harvested. You folk are not overly anxious to serve our Emperor, and even when you do, you rebel and mutiny. You could get ten years in Siberia for what you did." "I am guilty, Your Excellency; but the Struga peasants are not rebels. For that I would vouch with my life.” 

The commandant fell silent for a moment, then stood up and looked out of the window at the compound ringed with soldiers' barracks and watched an officer putting his detachment through their paces. When this officer caught sight 19

of the commandant, he barked: "Company, attention! Eyes left! Forward march!" Whereupon the detachment goosestepped in perfect unison. The general returned the officer's salute and turning to Vassilii, remarked: "Come here and take a look at these marching men. They are the lifeblood of our great homeland — and the Czar is its heart. Always bear in mind that without these two forces 

Mother Russia would be doomed." "Right, Your Excellency! The Czar and the army -" Vassilii parroted. "Yet my Struga peasants are rebellious against His Majesty, and reluctant to send their sons into the army." The commandant spoke in a tone of paternal regret. Vassilii sensed this to be the propitious moment to appeal for leniency: "Your Excellency, please save me from Siberia! I promise to serve you, like a dog, for the rest of my life! When I get back home I will set an example on how to serve and revere the Czar. Take pity on a fellow countryman, and I will be your slave to my dying day!" 

That same day Count Mishkin issued the following orders: 

Sergeant Stepan Smirnov, of the Second Company of the St. Petersburg Garrison, is in my judgment unfit for service in the capital. I recommend his immediate transfer to another district. 

Private Vassilii Kravlenko is to be forthwith released, and to be transferred to the Military Police at headquarters, under my command. 

Sergeant Smirnov sat at the St. Petersburg railroad station, waiting for the train that was to take him to his new post in Zankova - a God-forsaken little town in the 

Odessa district. What a comedown! And all because of a

Ukrainian hayseed - and the accursed Count of Struga! 

Ruffled and chagrined, the disgruntled sergeant sat nursing his grievance and hurt pride, and hatched a scheme for squaring accounts with both of them some day. 

Vassilii viewed his sudden liberation from what had ap- 20

peared to be his end in some Siberian dungeon, as nothing short of a miracle - as an act of God. From that day on, Count Mishkin came to mean more to him than a mere landowner and lord of the district north of the Dniester: 

Count Mishkin now assumed the character of a saviour, the star of his fate. Attributing to the Count the powers of 

Providence, Vassilii had begun to serve and worship him as though he were a divine being. He made it his purpose in life to look after his benefactor and to find grace in his eyes.

Vassilii plunged into his new duties as a military policeman with the zeal of a crusader. His chief duty had been to persecute the "undesirable elements" in the capital. At first he had been baffled by this phrase, could hardly pronounce it, and had been haunted by fear. Vassilii's superior officer in his new assignment was a gendarme named Serebryak - a red-haired, pot-bellied, pockmarked, pug-nosed Muscovite. Sensing the newcomer's bewilderment, Serebryak enlightened him with a cynical grin: 64 'Undesirable elements' is just meaningless official talk, which you can ignore. It simply means Jews - Jews who have no permission to live in St. Petersburg, because they are supposed to stay in the so-called Pale of Settlement. 

The Czar frowns upon them. A Jew must have a police permit to visit the metropolis and must not overstay the specified limit. But Jews are cunning and crooked as the devil, and like rats, they find hundreds of loopholes. It's our task to ferret them out and put them in the hoosegow." "Why do they come here then?" Vassilii wondered. "The devil knows why, but here they are," Serebryak commented. "They flock to the government officials and generals for all sorts of favors. They've got money, those anti-Christs, and by means of bribes, secure licenses for their shady deals. Well, its our business to find them and take them into custody."

Vassilii lost no time in acquiring the knack of ferreting out the "undesirables." He had been quite astonished to find that many of them, even when they possessed the required permits, still cowered before the police in instinctive 21

fear. His mentor, lecturing him on how to exploit these timorous creatures for supplementary income and a little fun, said: "Never mind if a Jew's papers are in order! Accuse him of forgery, of being a spy, a leader of the revolutionaries, of being against the Czar - and haul him off to the police station! On the way you'll have plenty of fun with him. 

Trembling so that even his beard shakes, the Jew will plead with you, call you 'Your Excellency' and even 'Your Highness,' and shove rouble after rouble into your hand." 

Under Serebryak's tutelage, during the last year of his military service, Vasilii had found his real vocation in life. 

The "palm-oil" acquired from the "undesirables," and the fun derived from tormenting his victims, had seemed to him the most attractive features of a police career. He would no longer be a man of the soil! After being mustered out, he would join the civilian police force with the blessing of Count Mishkin. The latter approved wholeheartedly of 

Vassilii's choice. The Count had long been apprehensive about the underground rumblings in Struga and especially about the peasants' indifference to Imperial decrees. It had occurred to him to set a thief to catch the thieves to entrust the law enforcement in Struga to one of their own kind. Vassilii's decision dovetailed with his own plans, and in bidding goodbye to the discharged soldier, the Count observed: 

“Kravlenko, I fully approve of your decision to serve our Czar. You're a splendid fellow, nor have you forgotten the fact that I saved you from Siberia and steered you into a fine career. You'll be given an assignment in the very heart of Struga. Let your fellow mouzhiks see what the 

Czar has made of you! I will recommend you for the post of chief of rural police there - but don't you disappoint me! The devil take you, but you're damned lucky! Try to be a loyal servant, and I'll take care of you. Keep an eagle eye on those peasants, and see to it that there isn't a squeak of discontent or rebellion!"

Vassilii Kravlenko's new post as chief of rural police in 22

Struga was by no means an easy one. He was not in his element as he had been in the capital of St. Petersburg, where he had served his apprenticeship under the gendarme Serebryak. The gold stripes and the epaulettes of his uniform, his dangling sword, his patent-leather high boots with the jingling spurs - all these merely aroused the curiosity of the local peasants, who had known him before he had been recruited into the army. They remembered him as the best-looking lad in the village, as well as the most knowing when it came to plowing, sowing and harvesting. They were not overawed by his uniform, and their approach to him was one of familiarity. Occasionally, when Vassilii would let himself go and drop the official Russian tongue for the regional dialect, the crowd would roar with laughter. 

Try as he would, Vassilii could not exert the authority of a Czarist law-enforement officer in Struga. He inspired no fear in his townsmen, and he was never addressed as 'Sir 

Officer.' They would not even call him by his full name of Vassilii, but addressed him as 'Vaska' - the old diminutive of familiarity. Just try to impose the law upon folks who called you 'Vaska!'

In the mansion of Count Mishkin, the Struga landowner, everyone was aware of the exciting story about Vassilii and Sergeant Smirnov - Vassilii's arrest, his narrow escape from exile to Siberia, and his promotion to the rank of Chief of 

Rural Police, thanks to the gracious intervention of the 

Count. The housemaids, liveried flunkeys, and coachmen, always accosted Vassilii with a significant smirk that bordered on arrogance. Both the members of the household and the innumerable guests at the mansion regarded Vassilii as a sort of odd, personal creation of the Count's. Now and then the ladies begged for the "peasant-police-officer" to be summoned to the manor house, so that they might catch a glimpse of the queer specimen. Even their admiration of his manly beauty and their forthright comments on his romantic possibilities served merely to vex instead of to gratify Vassilii, since their attitude had an undertone of derision rather than respect for his rank. Neither could he derive much fun at the expense of the timorous Jews -some- 23

thing he had been so fond of in St. Petersburg. Avrum

Shanan, a Struga Jew, had a way of circumventing him. 

There were not many Jews living in Struga, about half a dozen families in all, and they were regarded as a necessary evil by the other villagers. With the exception of Avrum 

Shanan, these Jews kept little shops where the men of the soil could exchange the produce of their farms and orchards for such indispensable commodities as salt, naphtha, sugar, leather, cotton fabrics and shag tobacco. They occupied small thatched huts and earned a modest living by trading with the peasants.

Avrum Shanan was an exception. A man of means, he carried on extensive transactions with the local squire as well as with the wealthier peasants of the vicinity. He would buy up the entire harvest, and then run the grain through his flour mill- the only modern bolting mill in the entire district. He also used to buy the complete yield of the fertile orchards, drying the fruit in special dehydrators and then packing it into crates and barrels. In addition to all this, he would contract for the entire output of the Count's sugar refinery. Because of Avrum's widespread enterprises, scores of barges, loaded with sacks of flour, crates of sugar, and casks of fruits, would glide along the Dniester to the ports on the Black Sea, where these products would be loaded onto freighters destined for far-away lands. Although Avrum 

Shanan and his wife and three children occupied a mansion surrounded by a sprawling orchard, they lived, otherwise, on as modest a scale as the rest of the Jews in Struga. 

Aside from the commercial dealings between the local peasants and the Jews in Struga, there was hardly any communication between the two groups. Though these Jews had lived in that village for generations, their Jewishness had not suffered in the least by contact with the world outside; inwardly, as well as outwardly, they clung to their traditional, orthodox way of life. They observed the Sabbath and other holy days, as well as the customs and precepts of the Mosaic law incumbent upon all Jews, without deviating therefrom. They even manifested the same innate apprehensiveness as all other Jews, regardless of whether they 24

resided in hamlets or cities.

Needless to say, the Jews in Struga had known Vassilii since his childhood, and also knew Ivan, his father, who at the least opportunity would start drinking and then proceed to pummel his wife. They also knew this drunkard's wife,

Paraska, and were familiar with all her misfortunes. She had had to work as a domestic for the local Jews, in order to provide the barest sustenance for her brood of six children. They could remember how the tall, husky Vaska had despised his father and had even threatened him with an axe when the latter, in one of his drunken moods, had taken the horsewhip to his wife. Had it not been for the neighbors, who had come running over in the nick of time, Vaska would at that moment have killed his father.

When, upon his discharge from military service, Vassilii had assumed the duty of the chief of rural police in Struga, the local Jews had decided to forget all about his childhood and their familiarity with him, and to defer to him, as befitted his new rank. They had pinned their hopes upon 

Kravlenko as the police chief, assuming that they would be able to get along with him, that they would no longer have to tremble before him. However, before long they met with bitter disappointment. 

It was a mellow Spring night; the earth lay soft, tender, and wistful - all sorts of verdure, stalks, buds, and flowers burgeoned cheerfully from its midst. The trees with their snowy blossoms disseminated an intoxicating fragrance that stirred and titillated one's longings. The shrill chirping of crickets, the croaking of the frogs, and the twinkling of the stars, had lulled the world into enchanted reveries in the mysterious serenity of the night. Here and there one could catch the yearning song, passionate and imploring, of some swain serenading his dearly beloved.

Avrum Shanan had been sitting in his study over a folio of Holy Writ. His head, covered by a silk skullcap, was propped up by two fingers of his left hand, while his right hand, from time to time, stroked his long black beard, bringing its point to his lips - a mannerism he assumed 25

whenever he came to a particularly knotty point in his Holy Writ or had to cope with some serious business. 

A woman's wail suddenly rent the quiet of the night. 

Avrum put his head out of the open window, trying to determine where the cry came from - apparently it had come from the orchard of Samborik, his old neighbor. "Yakov!" Avrum shouted to the night watchman, whose booth was situated at the main gate. "See what's happened at Samborik's and come right back. can be of any help." 

Let me know if we 

Avrum's alarmed command, echoing in the dead of night, aroused his entire family. His wife, Hannah, and his children Dvorah, Rachel and David, came running into the room in their nightshirts. Despite having borne six children, of whom only three had survived, Hannah, though pale as a ghost and frightened out of her wits, seemed, at this moment, much younger than her forty years, and pitifully helpless. "Take it easy, Hannah," Avrum reassured his distressed wife, his voice gentle and tender as always. "Yakov will be back soon to tell us what's happened at Samborik's. That's where the noise seems to be coming from. Get the children back to bed - David is barefooted, and is likely to catch cold." 

Yakov was not long in coming back. "Sir, Marushka 

Samborik's daughter - she's vanished! The whole village is aroused - they're determined to find her, no matter what! 

The Samboriks are in a panic - they're beside themselves. 

It's no trifling matter! She's only sixteen years old, a beauty, an only daughter - and so gifted! Vaska, the chief of police, is leading the crowd. He swears by all that's holy that he'll find Marushka - but not alive. He's sure that the Jews have stabbed her to death, so's to drain her blood to mix it with their matzos. As their Passover draws near, the Jews must have Christian blood, says Vaska -" 

"And what do you think Yakov - do Jews use Christian blood?" Avrum asked, trying to find out the view of a simple peasant. "Well, sir, I'm sure you would never harm a gentile. I would stake my life on that! I've known you long enough. 26

I've served as a watchman in this house for forty years. I even worked for your late father, may he rest in peace. But as for other Jews, the Lord knows what they're like; I couldn't vouch for them. Vaska says there's a Jew named Mendel 

Bailiss being kept in irons in a Kiev jail, because they found a dead Christian boy named Youshchinsky in his cellar, with thirteen wounds in his body. It was the Jews who bled that 

Christian boy - to use the blood for their Passover." 

Like a hen shielding her brood from impending menace, under her outspread wings, Hannah instinctively hugged her three children to her shivering body: "Avrum, I'm scared!" she blurted out. "I'm frightened to death. A handful of Jews in the midst of a mob of angry peasants is like a lonely sheep that has strayed into a pack of wolves. Avrum, I fear the worst!" Hannah's bloodless lips quivered.

Avrum was standing at the open window in deep thought, his dark eyes staring unseeingly into the outer darkness, his lips holding tight on a strand of his beard. "Yakov," he said at last to the watchman, "tell Alexey that I want him to harness the two white stallions to the phaeton, and pull up at the gate, beyond the orchard." 

Then, turning to his wife, he remarked: "Hannah, get dressed and dress the youngsters, then make yourselves comfortable in the orchard, under the big apple tree, and wait." “Avrum, I won't leave without you," his wife pleaded tearfully, "You must come with us." "No, my dear, that will never do," Avrum retorted. "The old blood-ritual lie is being revived in Struga; it can, God forbid, lead to disaster. My flight would only be interpreted by the peasants as a confession of guilt. Besides, I can't leave the other Jews to the mercy of the mob. I must stick to my post, so that with God's help, I may dispel the gathering clouds."

But Yakov had hardly managed to deliver his master's message to Alexey, when an unruly mob of peasants, carrying flaming torches, the voices of women even more clamorous than those of the men, poured through the main gate into Avrum's spacious courtyard. Avrum came out on the veranda 27

in a calm and leisurely fashion, apparently unruffled. At the sight of him, the hostile horde subsided for a moment. 

Only the intermittent loud sobs of Samborik's wife's suppressed weeping broke the charged and ominously sudden silence. Avrum stood aghast at what he saw. Among the crowd confronting him were the five Jewish shopkeepers of Struga - half-dressed, handcuffed, disheveled, their heads cast down despairingly.

Incited and led by the chief of police, the mob, before surging towards Avrum's home, swooped down upon the establishments of the five minor Jewish traders, ransacking and shouting wildly: "Where did you hide Marushka's body, Confess! Make a clean breast of everything and give the 

Christian girl back to us!"

Failing in his first attempt, Vassilii had intensified his diabolical instigations. "You'll not fnd Marushka's body at the homes of the poorer Jews! Let's go to their rich man - there are plenty of hiding places there, and that's where you'll find the victim of their ritual murder! Don't be fooled because these little merchants are putting on such innocent faces - at heart they're as cunning as foxes, that's what! They all had a share in draining Marushka's blood. Their god-damned Passover can't be celebrated without Christian blood! Handcuff these murderers of Christians and take them to the rich man!" 

The woebegone appearance of the captives, their shaking beards, their tottering walk, and the stark terror in their eyes were like balm to Vassilii's heart. They revived the long dormant, feral joy he had previously felt in ensnaring and manhandling innocent Jews in St. Petersburg. Leading the enraged mob and striding alongside the handcuffed victims, Vassilii was once more in his element and glorified in this new exploit.

The ominous silence on the part of the invading mob, as Avrum emerged on the veranda, had been a spontaneous mark of deference to him as if he were their undisputed master. This nettled Vassilii and he hastened to add fuel to the flame. "Where's Marushka?" he cried out. 28

Avrum descended the few steps leading from the veranda, and as he came up to Vassilii, he held out his wrists. "Officer," he said calmly, "handcuff me, just as you did the other five Struga citizens, whom you suspect of the same crime as you do me. If that's the law, the law must be obeyed."

Vassilii was well aware that he was no match for the other, either in intelligence or in a verbal argument. He recalled the instruction of his mentor, the gendarme Serebryak: "Never mind if the Jew's papers are in order! Accuse him of this and that -." Stamping his right foot, he bellowed: "Silence! All of you Jews are like that Mendel Bailiss in Kiev! I'll put all of you in chains behind bars! I'll settle scores with you for using Christian blood for your damned Passover!" “Officer, you don't scare me or those handcuffed Jews, in the least. Your accusations are nonsense. Charging Jews with the offense of killing Christian children for some ritual is the most dastardly crime against Christianity itself. Upright and high-minded Christians are well aware that it is merely a venom which irresponsible, wicked, and soulless enemies exploit, in order to poison the minds of the plain people, instigating hatred and pogroms against the Jews. I have nothing but pity for you, officer. As soon as the truth about 

Marushka's disappearance will become known, you'll be ashamed to wear that uniform, and you won't be able to look your fellow men in the eye!”

Many peasants lowered their eyes and cleared their throats uneasily. Avrum walked over to Samborik, and placing a comforting hand on his shoulder, remarked with profound compassion: "My dear neighbor, Marushka was practically raised in my house. We loved her as though she were a member of the family. The Lord knows how distressed we are at your tragic loss. Marushka's disappearance has broken the hearts of all of us."

Samborik embraced Avrum and began to cry bitterly. 

David, who had been observing the scene from his father's study, where he was clinging to his mother, suddenly broke 29

away from her embrace, dashed out to Samborik, and tugged at his shirtsleeve: "I know where you will find Marushka--" he began.

Everybody's attention was suddenly riveted upon the youngster. For a moment, Avrum felt stunned. The police officer, his eyes flashing with triumph, shoved Avrum aside and seized the lad's hand. "Good boy, David!" he exulted. "Lead us to the spot where your father buried Marushka."

David jerked his wrist free from the officer's painful grasp. "Marushka is on the bank of the Dniester," the boy declared. "She hid under the rock near the big well. We sat there together all afternoon. She sang for me - and she cried. She told me she had a little angel in her belly who wanted her to fly with him to heaven, because in Struga, the angel could not get out of her belly. She was never coming back from heaven. 'You go home, Davidka,' she told me, when the sun went down. 'Don't tell our secret to anyone. Only tomorrow, when they'll start looking for me, and my mother will be crying hard, tell them that Marushka went up to heaven, together with her little angel.'' 99 The mob, abandoning the handcuffed Jews, as well as the police chief and his talk of the blood ritual, stampeded toward the Dniester, where a huge grotesque boulder overshadowed a deep well, dug at the dawn of time. Through the clear water, the light of their torches revealed the body of Marushka - at the bottom of the well. 

Avrum's prediction proved true. With the recovery of

Marushka's body from the well, Vassilii's prestige sank in the eyes of the villagers. They felt ashamed and crestfallen before the Jewish shopkeepers. 

"Please forgive us - it wasn't our fault," they pleaded. "It's that Vaska, with his St. Petersburg gab, who led us astray. You can't make a silk purse out of a sow's ear and Vaska will always be Vaska. He'll never amount to anything."

The Jews disregarded the grievous incident. Thereafter, however, they, too, lost every vestige of fear before the police chief, whose adventure had turned out so ignominiously 30

and so shamefully degrading for him.

The Count had been quite perturbed over this episode. 

Before long, the villagers had discovered that the real culprit, the one who had seduced the sixteen-year old Marushka, had been the Count's own nephew, Vladimir, a lieutenant who had come to Struga to spend a month's furlough with his uncle. The peasants in general, and Samborik in particular, did not dare to protest publicly against the outrage committed by a kinsman of the squire, even though it had brought a whole family into disgrace and had caused the death of an only daughter. The Count, however, felt that the police officer, whom he had been instrumental in appointing as guardian over Struga, should have been intelligent and observant enough to foresee the outcome of the relations between a nobleman and a peasant girl. "You stupid lout!" the Count raged at Vassilii. "How did you expect young Count Vladimir to act toward that girl? Marry her and bring her to the Imperial Ball in St. 

Petersburg? You should have known that he would make her belly swell and then desert her!" "I'm at fault, Your Excellency," Vassilii cringed, "but one can't interfere with the young Count in such affairs. 

I dare say he's a hot-blooded fellow, sure enough - 

"That much I know myself, you blockhead!" the Count kept on with his tongue-lashing. "Its not the young Count you should have gone after; you ought to have warned Samborik, and advised him: 'Keep an eye on your young bitch, because she'll drop a bastard on your doorstep!' Then the thing would have been their headache, and not ours. If it became a public scandal later on, it would have been their own fault, since we had warned them. Understand? Instead of that, you have set the whole countryside against us!" "Yes, indeed, Your Excellency! But there was no time for warnings," Vassilii countered. "What do you mean, there was no time?" "If I may take the liberty of informing you, the young Count was horseback riding along the river bank, when he caught sight of Marushka washing her hair in the river.

He saw her half-naked; he dismounted, and in the next 31

moment, he started hugging and kissing her. She was thoroughly scared, managed to break away, and ran for cover under the rock. But he followed her, and after a few stifled outcries, I guess she submitted in silence." 

The Count's upper lip twitched nervously, betraying his inner relish. He barely restrained his enthusiasm over the dash and daring of his nephew, and in the deep recesses of his sinful soul, he envied him not a little. "But you were well aware that her disappearance was linked to that incident. Why then, did you stir up such a commotion against the Jews, and against Shanan, at that? 

He's mighty shrewd, and can dispose of a dozen fellows like you with no trouble at all!" "I'm at fault, Your Excellency," Vassilii still struggled, "but I figured it was better to throw several Jews behind bars than to set the entire village against its squire. How was I to know that the rich Jew and his little bastard would upset my plan? It never entered my head, that that measly little Jewish worm might know the whole secret!" 

At this, the Count cleared his throat, and remained silent for a moment a sign that he concurred in Vassilii's plan, and that he deplored its failure. Then he turned to Vassilii again: "I presume you are aware that throughout the Russian

Empire, they are preparing to celebrate the three-hundredth jubilee of the Romanov dynasty, which will take place in exactly one year. It will be a genuine celebration, with parades, feasts, masquerade balls, folk songs and dances, pageants, and music. The momentous event will be celebrated in every nook and cranny of our great Empire. And you had better see to it that the festivities are carried out in keeping with the occasion. I hold you personally responsible for the success of the celebration in Struga." 

It was little wonder then, that the indifferent, apathetic attitude of the Struga peasants so disconcerted the chief of police when the solemn day came. "Just see those sullen faces!" he muttered. "They look as if they were attending high mass, instead of celebrating a festival in honor of the Czar." 32

Following the invocation and prayers for the glorification of the Czar of All the Russias and all his kin, the worshippers shuffled out of the church to participate in the planned festivities. As had been previously arranged, the people marched in a ceremonial procession along the main avenue, which ran through the length of the village. The priest and the deacon, attired in their silver-embroidered vestments, headed the procession, waving small, smoking censers. 

They were followed by the village lads, carrying huge cruxifixes and icons. The lads, in turn, were followed by an interminable, snail-like, compact mass of coerced, apathetic humanity, shuffling along. The procession reminded one of a funeral cortege, rather than a festive parade. 

The church bells were continually pealing, filling the air with a charivari of vibrating and deafening sounds, which rebounded in strange echoes from the encircling Carpathian Mountains. Vassilii, in his resplendent uniform, was conspicuous in the procession. The marchers were approaching the public square, where, in accordance with the Count's wishes and expectations, they were supposed to manifest their joy and enthusiasm for the fatherland on this great occasion, through dance and song. "Look at them dragging their feet - like corpses!" Vassilii mused, sardonically. "They'll humiliate and ruin me; they'll bring a hornet's nest upon my head!" His anxiety brought cold beads of perspiration to his forehead. 

It was his father, the drunkard, Ivan, who came to his rescue. Ivan and some fellow topers, their arms thrown across one another's shoulders, were advancing in a jolly, cavorting group; they were in the first stage of tipsiness that is, none of them was exactly sober, but then none of them was dead drunk, either.

At any other time, Vassilii would have found it most embarrassing to be seen in public with his sot of a father. 

However, at this crucial moment, he considered his father's appearance a godsend; the only animated and jolly faces in the entire throng were those of his father and his father's cronies. "Father, it's good to see that you, at least, are merry. Look 33

at those sour, long faces you might think their wives had pegged out!" "Vaska, my son, well-water chills the blood, but a noggin of vodka makes the embers flare up - devil with it!" “Yes, but am I to get spirits for a whole village?" Vassilii argued. 

"You just look the other way, Vaska. Make yourself scarce for an hour or so- the devil with it! and we'll lay our hooks on some vodka to drink the health of the Czar may the Lord grant him long life!" "Father, what are you jabbering about? Speak plainly!" “A single stone smack in the middle of the window of the Government Liquor Store, and you can help yourself to all the spirits you wish, the devil with it! This is a holiday, the manager isn't around, and the chief of police happens to have his hands full elsewhere . . . Our peasants will be guzzling, in the meantime, and their blood will get good and hot, the devil with it!"

Vassilii pretended to object to his father's novel suggestion, even though he inwardly approved of it. "Don't be foolish, father," he remonstrated, half-heartedly. "Smashing windows on government property is a violation of the law. 

I can't permit it unless, of course, it's done behind my back -" 

Ivan caught on and smiled knowingly. Vassilii slackened his pace, fell behind the procession, and then, disappeared. 

He made his way along the orchards, heading for the bank of the Dniester. The day was sunny; the sky serene, without one cloudlet. The trees in full bloom, seemed like varicolored parasols; the grass was tall and fresh and soft. Bees buzzed, assiduously burrowing into blossoms, and birds on the wing, or in their treetop nests, chirped incessantly.

Gazing upon this scene, Vassilii's woes and cares subsided a bit. There was not a soul in sight, and he felt quite safe. 

He might as well sit down under a tree for a little while, as his father had hinted, and reappear on the scene only when the throng would be festive and in high spirits - "Singing and dancing, Your Excellency!"

David Shanan knew every bend and turn of the Dniester 34

like a book, and he also knew the topography of every orchard. He could find his way blindfolded through all the meandering lanes and footpaths, through hedges and fences, through dense shrubbery, and fields of ripe corn. 

The festive chimes aroused David's deep childish curiosity, and he sprinted toward the broad highway along which the procession was passing. Among the many, varied crucifixes and icons, what impressed the lad most of all, was a huge head of Jesus - with a pointed, fair beard, and half-closed eyes, from which tears trickled down over the pale, gaunt cheeks. From the moment that David saw this, he paid little heed to the marching peasants; his eyes were focused only upon the sorrowful face on the icon. When it had passed out of sight, he felt some sort of inexplicable longing. Lost in thought, David retraced his steps along the familiar pathways toward the bank of his beloved Dniester, the agonized face on that icon floating before his eyes. 

In a remote and deserted corner of Samborik's sprawling orchard, David suddenly stopped, petrified with fear. He found himself face to face with the chief of rural police. "Hey, there!" the latter bellowed. "What sort of devil's gotten into you, that a body can't keep anything secret from you?"

David stood still, his vocal cords paralyzed with fear. "No one is to know that I was here, David! If you dare breathe a word to anyone, I'll shoot you and your father down like dogs!"

At this point Samborik came out of the bushes. He was completely changed; gone was his usual sombre air. This ordinarily tranquil, respectable, and prospering man of the soil, now looked like Ivan the tosspot. Brandishing a halfempty bottle, he approached the officer and the youngster with wobbly steps. "Come here, Davidka," he began. "Why are you shivering like that? Did you happen to see the Evil One, or what?" 

Then, hugging the lad, he went on in a drunken voice: "Marushka loved you so dearly- like a brother. 'Where are you, Marushka?' 'I'm with Davidka, down by the Dniester.' 'Marushka, who did you pick those cherries for?' — 'For 

35

Davidka!' - 'Marushka, whose shirt are you embroidering there - Davidka's?" Samborik, bowed down and with his eyes shut, began to mumble, swaying like a sapling lashed by the wind: "She's gone up to heaven with her little angel. . . Marushka, my darling daughter, my little soul - my little Marushka —” 

He fell silent for a moment, then tossed his head, as though he were anxious to pluck the memory of Marushka out of his mind. Raising his bottle aloft, he turned to the officer: "Vaska, your father may be a drunkard, but he's a brave soul! 'Fellows,' I heard him yelling, 'is this a day of mourning, or a festival- the devil with it. Let's smash the windows of the Government liquor store! Those bottles stand there like little darlings, pleading 'Take us, dear men, and drink to your heart's content!' And no sooner did the crowd swoop down upon that place, than things began to happen. 'Samborik,' he says to me, 'what are you - an old woman or something? Here's a bottle for you - drink to the Czar's health, the devil with it! I've swilled half a bottle- and now I feel like doing a jig!'"

Vassilii snatched the bottle out of Samborik's hand, and turning his back, let the hard liquor gurgle down his gullet. 

With every gulp, he felt fresh courage coursing through his blood. The vodka seemed to drown all his disappointments and troubles. After emptying the bottle, he felt as frisky as a colt. He did not even care about Samborik having discovered his hideaway or his besotted father having been the ringleader in the raid on the liquor store. "Samborik, to the common public square with you!"

Vassilii commanded. "I'll see to it that all of Struga is assembled there. Why, His Majesty, the Czar himself, said to 

His Excellency, the Count: 'Mishkin, it is my wish that 

Struga should celebrate the day of my jubilee with song and dance. And what's more, you must reward the Chief of

Police, Vassilii Kravlenko, for the gallant way he carried out my command!" " "Well, Vaska, it's really the vodka in you talking now,"

Samborik jeered at him. "The Czar is mighty little con- 36

cerned with the affairs at Struga, and as to his talk with His 

Excellency - that, dear man, if you'll pardon me, is something you'll never know. True, you're a country police chief, but just the same, Vaska - once a peasant, always a peasant!" "Quiet, Samborik!" the intoxicated Vassilii thundered, “I'll have you know, that I'm not Vaska to the likes of you. You should address me as Officer, you son-of-a-bitch! The damned

Jews hereabouts are the only ones who address me as befits my rank -" 

Vaska lunged at David, but the boy nimbly broke away, and the officer fell flat on his face. Samborik, though unsteady himself, helped him to his feet. "Vaska, you may be a police officer, but you'd better let

Davidka alone. I'm telling you this as a friend I don't 

want you ever to touch a hair on his head. He may be a Jew, but I hold him as one of my own. You're not likely to understand it, but I'm warning you to leave the boy alone—” "You can go to the devil with your Jewish bastard don't you threaten me! You don't frighten me one bit, and the accursed Jews will never be privileged characters with me. In St. Petersburg, we chased them like rats, but in 

Struga they want to play at being squires."

When Vassilii reached the public square, he hardly recognized his fellow villagers. Empty bottles were strewn about. 

Knots of peasants, from dotards to striplings, their arms interwined, were weaving in and out, singing and yelling. 

Two fiddlers and a cymbalist were playing the thumping gopak, the only dance tune Struga had ever known. Young men in rainbow-hued shirts, worn over ballooning white linen trousers, tucked into the tops of freshly greased, high boots, and village belles, wearing wrap-around skirts, blouses embroidered with multi-colored designs, and strings of heavy beads, danced in pairs, whirling faster and faster to the dizzying tempo. Frequently, the male partners would squat and then leap up into the air, emitting long shrieks — a primordial mating call. 

The women romped and cavorted around their partners their masters and leaders in the gopak. As they danced frantically, their round, succulent breasts bobbed freely in 

37

their roomy blouses. The pleats of their skirts swirled, baring flashes of white, girlish limbs, which kindled the passions of their young partners and revived sinful notions in the minds of the middle-aged and elderly men looking on. Now and then some lad, no longer able to restrain himself, would draw his dancing partner closer to him, and disappear with her into the thick growths of the nearby orchards. 

Ivan Kravlenko had become the hero of the day and was showered with praises on all sides. “Ivan, dear man why, you're nothing short of an angel! You let fly one stone and the crowd was in for the treat of their lives!" Yakov, Avrum Shanan's watchman, who had also managed to drink his share, sang his praises at the top of his voice. “What else can you get us with a well-thrown stone?" "Presents, that's what!" Ivan shot right back at him, his eyes bloodshot, and his arms flailing. "I can get presents for you and for your wives and your daughters, the devil with it!" "Ivan's got presents for us!" voices in the throng shouted, and they all drew closer to the reeling Ivan. "Follow me, brothers!" Ivan sputtered. "To the Jews!" 

As the mob echoed the yell of "To the Jews!" Yakov suddenly came to his senses, and headed for Shanan's house, as fast as his legs would carry him. "Master, Ivan Kravlenko is leading a mob against the

Jews!" he blurted out to Avrum and his family, who were seated at their dining table. 

The spoon dropped out of Hannah's hand, smashing the plate in front of her. She sat stunned, staring at her husband. "Yakov, you've been drinking," Avrum spoke with restraint. "You're drunk and talking nonsense. How could 

Ivan lead a mob? Where is his son, the chief of police?" "Father, don't ask where the chief of police is," David pleaded in a barely audible, beseeching voice. “No one must know where he's hiding, or he'll shoot both of us down like dogs."

Avrum was disconcerted by what the boy said. But just as he was about to speak, Israel Lifshitz, one of Struga's 

Jewish shopkeepers, rushed in, his face streaked with blood. "Reb Avrum" he gasped, "they've started a pogrom! Let's 38

-- see what we can do to help! The whole village is drunk – 

Ivan, the police chief's father, is leading the mob. They are plundering the stores and beating up the Jews - look at me! And things may get even worse. Let's go, Reb Avrum the peasants have respect for you -" 

"Alexy!" Avrum called out to his coachman, who happened to be eating in the kitchen with his wife, the cook. "Get on a horse and ride as fast as you can to the church, and ring the bells for a fire! Yakov, you run to the mob, and when you hear the fire alarm, yell for everybody to go to the public square. Shout with all your might: "There's a fire at the square! There's a fire at the square!' Find Samborik, and tell him I ordered this, and he'll help you."

The startling tocsin, the usual summons, brought the drunken villagers to a sudden halt. Fear overcame the looters. 

Each asked himself where the fire was. In his own house? 

His own granary? His own stable? There was a babble of questioning voices all around. The half-drunk sobered quickly; the dead-drunk stood by helplessly, mumbling incoherently. All their recent hilarity had vanished. "To the pubic square! There's a fire at the square! Run to the square, good people!" Yakov's yells rent the air, as he dashed from group to group.

Some hugged articles they had grabbed from the Jewish shops; others flung these away, as if they were unclean and loathsome. Everyone sensed that something unusual was happening, All those who had taken part in the pogrom, as well as those who had been merely curious spectators - sped to the public square. 

Avrum Shanan stood up on a stone in the middle of the square, surrounded by youthful couples, who had broken off their dancing. Peasants, who had thrown away their loot, advanced sheepishly and slowly towards the circle around the rich Jew, while others tried to hide their booty and kept at a distance, using those in front of them as a screen.

Serious, calm, pale but resolute, Avrum, with his windblown, pointed, black beard, wearing his long black gabardine and Hasidic hat of black velvet, had the majestic appearance 39

of a Biblical shepherd-patriarch, towering above a herd of sheep and cattle. "Citizens of Struga" Avrum raised his voice. "Aren't you ashamed of your evil actions? Weren't you afraid to commit such a great sin against God? How could you let yourselves be deceived into becoming a band of robbers? Won't your consciences bother you for having plundered and beaten your Jewish neighbors, with whom you and your fathers and forefathers had always lived in peace? Mob rule will not be tolerated; the law will punish the guilty. Tomorrow, high officials of the state police will arrive, and they will sentence the guilty to long terms in jail. And as to the looting and damages - all of you will be held to account!" "Well said! It's the truth you're saying," sporadic voices arose in the crowd. "It's not our fault - Ivan dragged us into this! Tell us what we are to do now, and we'll listen and then follow your advice." "Don't you dare!" The voice of the chief of police split the air like the roar of a wild beast. He elbowed his way arrogantly through the throng toward the center, where Avrum was standing. His eyes blazed with fury, and his foaming mouth was twisted in a sardonic grimace. "How dare you assume authority?" he rasped out, gnashing his teeth. "I am the law in Struga! We're making merry for our Little Father, the Czar. Going on a spree once in three hundred years is no crime!" "Well said, Vaska! Good lad, Vaska!" scattered voices greeted him.

Avrum immediately sensed the rift in the assemblage: one faction, quite remorseful, was inclined to put things to rights; but another group, gloating over the commotion, was only too anxious to fan the flames. It was a critical situation. If 

Avrum could only fathom which faction was in the majority! 

He hoped that the majority would like to see justice done, would hold out for peace and good will. Relying on that possibility and ignoring the flustered officer, he raised his voice again: "Fellow citizens, kind people - break up and return to your 40

homes! For God's sake, don't defile your hands with stolen goods and blood! Go on home! Come back to me tomorrow, and I'll help you to pay for the damage for which you will be held responsible!" "Don't you dare!" the officer yelled, brandishing his revolver threateningly. "Whoever dares to leave this spot will be shot down like a dog! I'll not permit a Jew to disrupt the celebration in behalf of our Czar! You fiddlers - why are you standing there like dummies! Start the music, you sons-of-bitches! You should celebrate this occasion with singing and dancing! That is the express wish of His Majesty, the Czar that is the command from His Excellency to me! What is this - a meeting against the Czar? The Jews are rebelling against the Czar! On with the dance - I'll make all Struga dance!" 

The musicians exchanged glances of dismay, put their fiddles slowly under their beards and took to scraping mechanically, producing discordant, lifeless notes. The young couples, though still holding hands, could not seem to rekindle the fervor of the interrupted gopak.

Staggering, and like a madman, Vassilii made the rounds of the various groups, waving his revolver and growling: "On with the dance! All the people of Struga must dance! 

I'll show you how to rebel against the Czar – dance, you sons-of-bitches!" 

His brazenness had a paralyzing effect upon the throng. 

As he wavered in and out of the crowd, Vassilii stumbled against Avrum. Pointing his gun at him, he commanded: "Dance! Let's see you dance, Jew! You claim to be a man of 

Struga? Then join in when Struga goes on a spree!"

They all held their breath. The musicians fell silent. All eyes and ears were intent upon the two men in the center. 

Avrum suddenly sensed a warm, familiar body hugging his knees; he lowered his right hand and stroked David's head. David made common cause with his father by burying his face in the palm of that hand, giving it a prolonged, devout, childish kiss. This contact with his son charged his father's being with extraordinary confidence and valor. "Officer, you can't force people to dance when their hearts 41

aren't in it," Avrum spoke up in an unruffled voice. "You made the Struga citizens drunk so they could drown their sorrows in vodka. Well, officer, you failed in your mission - the villagers are in no mood for dancing. Do you expect now to banish my sadness with that gun? You have failed again, officer. I'm in no mood for dancing -"

The Count's admonition that Shanan was extremely shrewd and could dispose of a dozen fellows like Vassilii flitted through the police chief's mind. This keen-witted Jew could make a fool of him again - and that must never happen. 

He must not be shorn of his glory again, come what may! 

Seizing David and holding him aloft as one might hold a puppy by the scruff of its neck, Vassilii commanded: "Dance, you accursed devil! Dance, you Jew, or I'll shoot down this bastard of yours before your very eyes!"

David did not resist. Dangling in the air, he felt a choking sensation as the collar of his shirt squeezed his throat. Yet, he was not alarmed; the pride and courage of his father seemed to have been imparted to him as well. His father's incisive words. "Officer, you've failed in your mission 

I'm in no mood for dancing," still rang in his ears. With a gun pointed at his breast, his father was still challenging the officer fearlessly. The little fellow was extremely proud of his courage; he cherished and idolized him. Though he himself was in agony at the moment, in keeping with his childish conception of the world, he was experiencing a feeling of bliss and gratitude for the privilege of suffering for his father.

Leaping like a tiger from a remote corner of the public square, Samborik struck Vassilii's arm with his powerful fist. 

The blow was like that of a dull axe. Wincing with pain, the officer released the youngster, who dropped at his father's feet. Samborik's unexpected, daring deed electrified the throng. "You swine!" Samborik growled furiously at the stunned officer. "I want you to leave Davidka alone! You can kill me, but, by God, as long as I'm alive, you shall not harm Davidka!"

Vassilii's face flushed with anger. His wild-eyed stare was 42

suggestive of a blinding projector on a pitch-dark night.

His nostrils and pursed lips quivered. He resembled a bundle of dynamite surrounded by flaming torches. An explosion seemed imminent.

Without uttering another word and with a determination that harbored malice, destruction and revenge, Vassilii pointed his gun at David. In his bulging eyes now devoid of any humaneness, Avrum could discern cold-blooded murder. . . . In the next instant, he seized the officer's hand which clutched the weapon and deflecting it away from its intended victim, he screamed desperately: "Officer, I'll dance for you! I'll dance for the crowd! 

Musicians, let's have a gopak!" 

A sinister smile of triumph suffused Vassilii's face. "I've brought that mighty, shrewd Shanan to his knees at last!" he reflected. "Let's see whether His Excellency will still think that that Jew can lick ten like me!" Replacing his gun in its holster, he motioned to the assembly to make room for the spectacle.

The native, rustic musicians struck up the gopak phlegmatically, and Avrum launched into his cringing dance. . 

His slender figure held erect, his arms folded on his chest, his half-closed eyes turned heavenward, he leaped about, his swift-moving feet barely touching the ground. The primitive gopak melody sounded to him like an angel chant of

Kedusha (the solemn proclamation of God's holiness). 

A sense of unalloyed happiness suffused his entire being, and he seemed to soar in a realm of religious exaltation. 

His dance picked up momentum becoming ever more rhapsodic and soul-stirring, until it became an invocation to the Almighty — a paean of gratitude for the Lord's graciousness in endowing him with the indomitable courage thus to sacrifice his pride and vanity, in order to redeem his only son. At that moment, Avrum was riding the crest of a unique world - a divine realm, in which there was a full outpouring of the heart and one felt oneself close to the kingdom of heaven. His feet continued to move about rhythmically as he stretched both arms to the sky, and his lips murmured a prayer to God: 

43

"Father in heaven, Creator of the universe, hearken to my prayer with mercy and good will. . . . Lord of all lords, accept my praise of Thy Holy Name for the great kindness Thou hast vouchsafed me, in that

Thou guidest me in my moment of need and peril, as Thou guided Father Abraham, and would not let him sacrifice his only son. Barukh Atta Adonai,

Elochenu melek ha-olam, hagomel lechayavim tovot shegemalani kol tov - 'Blessed are Thou, O Lord, our God, King of the universe, who vouchsafest benefits unto the undeserving, who hast also vouchsafed all good unto me.''

The religious ardor of Avrum's dedicated dance, accompanied by his prayer, electrified the spectators and instilled the fear of God in them. Instead of a source of diversion, they now discerned a divine being in the dancing 

Jew and they quailed in awe. Here and there, humblespirited Christian peasants, some of them kneeling, made the sign of the cross. In addition to the strains of the gopak and the rhythmic thud of Avrum's nimble feet, the wail of a youngster echoed. It was the voice of David, prostrated at his father's feet, clutching his head with his hands and lamenting: "Papa did not withstand the temptation! The pride and glory of his courage has gone forever! He won't be able to look a person in the eye now. People will poke fun at him. 

My father the best father in the world has been ridiculed by the entire community of Struga." The innocent, childish heart was full of anguish. 

Why that sudden intense hush? Why had that irate officer fallen silent? Why had the derisive taunts and snickers ceased? Puzzled, the lad slowly raised his eyes and was astonished at what he beheld. His father's face appeared pale and haggard, and his beard, more pointed than usual; tears were trickling down from his half-closed eyes, along his gaunt cheeks. "The procession! The agonized face on that icon has come to life!" flitted across David's mind. That aggrieved icon face, which had seemed so remote and alien to him, now 44

became intimately known and dear - as personified by his father. . . . His father had triumphed once again! He had demonstrated to the rustics that given faith and kindliness, an inanimate icon could be restored to life, with a heart and soul!

David rose to his feet. As though in a dream, he clutched firmly at his father's gabardine, and father and son trudged off from this spot, where a new sacrifice had been made. 

The crowd of onlookers shuffled off, one by one. The plunder, abandoned on the public square, was mute testimony to regret and remorse. ... Vassilii, defeated and shorn of his glory, proceeded to his father's hut, there to drown the vexation and ignominy of his defeat, in drunkenness. 

That night, David lay tossing and turning on his bed, unable to sleep though his eyes were heavy with weariness. 

A persistent, inner agitation, connected with the turbulent events of the day, kept him tense and wakeful. 

Avrum was likewise overwrought and unable to doze off. 

As was his custom, he tried to assuage his inner turmoil by concentrating on a tractate of the Gemarra. But his mind and heart were still throbbing with the preceding, shattering experience. Poring over Holy Writ, which in the past had served to dispel disturbing thoughts, did not soothe him now. His intellect could not comprehend the ancient words of wisdom. His ears did not grasp the words that he uttered while studying, though he spoke much louder than usual, as if aiming to stifle his inner voice. His sad intoning reechoed throughout the huge dwelling. 

Hannah, too, lay awake, staring wistfully into the dark.

She understood her husband well and knew that his sorrowful chanting of the Talmud, at so late an hour, was an expression of his spiritual turmoil. Tears suffused her eyes, and her heart ached for him. She felt drawn to console and comfort him. A profound compassion coursed through her being; her heart was palpitating, as she hearkened to the sad intoning of her husband. The door to her room slowly opened, and David, clad in his long nightshirt, tiptoed towards her bed. “David, my little boy, why aren't you sleeping at this late 45

hour?" Hannah asked, somewhat alarmed. Then she tucked him under her covers, and, in a transport of maternal affection, snuggled close to him, caressed him, and kissed his forehead and his blissfully closed eyes. "My child - pride and joy of my life," she mumbled, her motherhood yearning towards him. "Mother," David remarked, as he nestled eagerly against her warm body; "Struga is the best and the prettiest village in the world. And you are the best and prettiest mother in the world. I shall always be with you, and I shall always remain in Struga!" "How about your father, David? Your father loves you so very much," Hannah interposed. "My father is the best father in the world," David commented. "I love him very much. When I grow up, I'll be like father. I, too, will study the Talmud like father does, and I, too, will chant, just as he does. It is so good to listen to him - you feel like crying then It is so good - as it was when Marushka used to sit with me on the riverbank, tickle me under my ear and sing a song about another Marushka, who had died because no one loved her. . . Mother, how does one die?"

David's strange question perturbed Hannah, sending shivers down her spine. She associated it in her mind with her last visit to Dr. Torchinsky, in the city of Polipovka. During that examination, the physician had probed a growth in her abdomen that had been a source of anxiety to her for some time. The doctor's face had turned grave and worried; the wrinkles on his forehead had deepened. Removing his pincenez and pointing them towards her, he had said: "My dear, I'm a bit worried about your condition. You may have to be operated upon. But, first of all, it's important to build you up and put some color in your face. 

You are a bit anaemic, and this medicine will enrich your blood."

With reference to this conversation, she had informed her husband only about her anaemia. She had not mentioned a word to him regarding the suspicious growth, and the opera- 46

tion that indicated. She had a fearful premonition that should she undergo surgery she would not survive. . . . Now 

David's query about death dampened her spirits, and she strove to master her feelings. "Mother, how does one die?" David persisted. "My child, you are too young to think and talk about death," Hannah answered. She tried to allay his fears. "You are just starting to live. You have to grow up, study and become a big, strong, clever and good man." "Mother, why did Marushka die?" David was not to be deterred. 

After a moment"s hesitation, Hannah, as though groping in the dark, answered: "Because that was God's will." "Why does God wish people to die?" 

"No one can account for God's will, my son," Hannah enlightened him. "He is the Creator and Supreme Being of the world, and He does with it as He sees fit. He can make the world sunny and warm or cloudy and cold; He makes one wealthy, another, poor; He blesses one with good children, who are studious; and another with bad ones, who are unwilling to learn. .." She was at a loss for a moment, uncertain as to whether she ought to utter what was on the tip of her tongue. Then she continued in a quivering voice: “— and He determines who is to stay well, and who, sick; who is to survive, and who is to die. . . 66 

"Mother, was Marushka sick?" "Yes my child, Marushka was sick - alas, poor Marushka was very sick! And it was God's will that the ailing Marushka should leave this earth. He summoned the Angel of Death and commanded him: "Descend to earth, locate Marushka and bring her soul to heaven'. 99 "Mother, must all sick people die?” “No, my child; if it be God's wish, a sick person recovers. 

He calls Angel Raphael and commands him: 'Descend to earth, find this sick person, post yourself at the head of his bed, and make him well! See to it that the doctors prescribe the right kind of medicine. Should the Angel of Death, 47

through some mistake, attempt to take his soul, do not permit it; chase him away - and do not leave the sick person until he has fully recovered.' . . ." "Mother, does the Angel Raphael also take care of Dr. Torchinsky?" "Yes, my dear. Every doctor is a messenger from God; and he is accompanied by the Angel Raphael, who communicates kind thoughts to him and shows him how to make the sick well again." "When I grow up, I'll be like Dr. Torchinsky. I'll make sick people well, too - but not in Polipovka! I'll remain in Struga. Mother, can the Angel Raphael also come to Struga?" "Yes, my son; God and His angels are everywhere throughout the great, wide world." 

Avrum's chanting ceased. Muffled footsteps were heard.

Avrum quietly walked to David's room, and when he did not find the lad there, a smile brightened his haggard face, as he correctly guessed his son's whereabouts. He entered the bedroom and chided the youngster, half-seriously, halfjokingly: "Is that nice, David? A lad of seven, who is already studying the Pentateuch and its Commentaries - still sleeping with his mother?" 

Hannah hugged her son and pleaded: "Avrum, please leave the child alone. He is worn out. The night is practically over, and he hasn't slept a wink.”

Avrum sat down on the edge of Hannah's bed and pressing the point of his beard between his lips, declared: "Hannah, it won't do to keep David in the village.
