The responsibility of Jewish parents in the upbringing of a son is a serious one. David must acquire a thorough knowledge of the Torah, and facilities for that are wholly lacking in Struga. After the Sabbath, God willing, I will take him to Polipovka and enroll him in Reb Itzikel's Yeshiva (Seminary).”

Hannah sensed a throbbing pain in the region of the growth. Something tore at her heart and biting her lower lip, she felt herself on the verge of collapse. David began to cry: "I don't want to go to Polipovka! I want to stay in Struga!" 48

Avrum seated his son on his lap, wiped his tears away and embracing him tenderly, asked: “David, do you know who the first Jew was?” "Yes, Papa - Father Abraham.” “And do you know how he came to be the Father of the Jews?" "No, Papa that I don't know." 

“Well, let me explain it to you. Abraham was living in a village called Haran. He, as well as his parents, lived in a beautiful house, alongside orchards, meadows and a river as big and as splendid as the Dniester. But God's ways were unknown in Haran, and no one paid any heed to His Word there. And so, God said to Abraham: 'Get thee out of thy country, and from thy kindred, and from thy father's house, unto the land that I will show thee.'” "Abraham departed from his father's house, followed in God's way, and hearkened to God's Word. . . . That is how he became the Father of the Jews. And we have inherited from him a great love for God's Holy Torah. . . . Where the learning of Torah is concerned, we are always prepared to leave our birth place and the home of our parents." 

David was sleeping soundly on his father's lap, long before Avrum had finished his story. Avrum carried the boy back to his own room and tucking him into bed, kissed him on his forehead. The Divine Presence seemed to be shining on the sleeping youngster's face.

In the courtyard of Avrum Shanan's mansion, near the huge gates, stood the big phaeton harnessed with two stallions that champed at the bit and kicked impatiently at the flagstone paving. Yakov, the watchman, and Alexey, the coachman, carried out the bulging valises and fastened them to the springs of the vehicle. 

Dvorah, who was only two years older than David, but appeared much more mature, grieved over his impending departure. Her eyes were red-rimmed from intermittent crying spells. It was hard to tell, however, whether her weeping was spontaneous or was brought on by the drooping spirits of her mother, who kept alternately wiping away her tears 49

and sighing. As for the four-year old Rachel - she could not grasp why her mother and sister were so downcast. Even 

Alexey's wife, the cook, looked sullen and tear-stained. 

Little Rachel found the occasion rather stimulating and mirthful. She ran back and forth from the veranda to the courtyard, watching the two manservants putter about with the luggage, and each time a horse's hoof would strike a cobblestone, she would dash into the house.

Hannah's health had declined considerably during the past few days. Her pallor had become more pronounced, and her eyes, with dark rings under them, now receded deeply into their sockets. "Hannah, dear," her husband had soothed her time and again; "it won't do to keep David cooped up in the village. 

I, too, shall miss him a great deal, but we must not permit our sentiments to undermine the upbringing of the child in accordance with the Torah and good deeds." “Avrum," she answered, "I am quite aware that David must go away for the sake of his education, and believe me, I am doing all I can to restrain myself. But the Lord knows my heart cries in me, and it is so hard to part with him.”

Dressed in new clothes, as befits a lad headed for a Yeshiva, David now darted down the riverbank. 

"Where are you rushing to, David?" his father called out. "The horses are already harnessed — we'll set out soon. We've got quite a distance to cover, and the earlier we start out, the better." "I-I wish to say goodbye to the Dniester. . . ." David answered haltingly. "I'll be right back."

On reaching the Dniester, David solemnly contemplated its placid, mirror-like surface. Slowly, he picked up a handful of small pebbles, and tossed them, one by one, into the stream. Each pebble, as it struck the surface, created a ring that widened and widened rhythmically all the way to shore - and then disappeared. To David, it seemed as though the 

Dniester was waving a friendly and nostalgic goodbye, through those playful ripples. He sauntered over to the huge rock and sat down on the edge of the well, where Marushka used to sing her fascinating songs to him. He gazed for a long 50

while into the crystal-clear water, and much to his surprise, although he had tossed no pebbles - here too, were those rippling rings. His tears dropped, one by one, into the well ...

David returned home by way of Samborik's orchard. On the way he stumbled upon a big rose and plucked it, along with its stalk. Samborik was standing near his granary and as he caught sight of the youngster, he greeted him cheerfully: "Davidka, my dove! What do I hear that you are deserting us? Well, perhaps that's the proper thing for you. Your father is a clever and upright man. You try to follow in his footsteps, and we'll all be proud of you. Well, let's shake hands, and I'll wish you goodbye."

Samborik stretched out his hand, but David, instead of accepting it, embraced the peasant, crying fitfully. Samborik caressed his head, mumbling: "Goodbye, Davidka - may God always be with you. Don't forget us, my dove."

Wiping his tear-stained face, David handed him the rose and blurted out in a choked voice: "This this is for Marushka. 99 The phaeton, drawn by its two fiery steeds, galloped along the broad highway. David was leaving his beloved 

Struga and his home. The familiar orchards and meadows gradually receded from sight. Sitting beside him, his father clasped his hand tightly. The smoothly rocking springs of the phaeton slowly lulled the youngster to sleep - and as he was drifting off, he seemed to hear the echo of the 

Biblical words: "Get thee out of thy country, and from thy kindred, and from thy father's house, unto the land that 

I will show thee. 99 51

The Lord 

Is My Shepherd Dr. Torchinsky sat, exhausted, at his small desk in the city hospital in Polipovka, his huge head propped on the palms of his bony hands, his eyelids heavy with slumber.

For sixteen hours, without any let-up, the sixty-two-year-old physician, fatigued and beset with cares, had been on his feet in the operating room, performing surgery upon one patient after another, summoning up from he knew not where, additional energy and endurance. It was only when he had at last sat down, that his body and mind had registered their categorical protests, clamoring for rest, for a long respite.

The air was filled with the incessant, heart-rending moaning and groaning of the hundreds of casualties with which the hospital was crowded. During the last two months, as the lines of battle had drawn closer to the Carpathian Mountains, Polipovka had become an important and bustling medical center of World War I, with all its attendant horrors. The evening of August 11, 1914, flitted across the surgeon's mind - only two years ago! What nightmares the world had lived through in that relatively brief period and it was still engaged in a life-and-death struggle. 

On that notable evening in 1914, Count Mishkin, the landed proprietor of Struga, had given his annual masquerade ball. His palace, situated in the very heart of the 

Carpathians, was ablaze with lights. Its tall, white marble 52

pillars, multi-colored fountains, palisades, and alleys of pine trees presented a scene of grandeur that fascinated the eye and mind. A thousand guests, comprising the elite of the 

Russian aristocracy, had assembled from all over the Empire, and a forty-piece orchestra from St. Petersburg had been imported especially for the occasion. The alternately soothing and stimulating phrases of Strauss waltzes wafted through the open windows of the mansion and were swallowed by the primordial forests that clothed the rounded slopes of the mountain range. The carefree couples, gliding over the grandiose ballroom - secure in the protective anonymity afforded by their masquerade disguises - provoked each other's curiosity about their respective identities, and tantalized their partners' emotions through their bodily proximity and whispers of unrestrained passion.

Butlers, in gold-braided livery, were busily making the rounds, carrying silver trays laden with crystal glasses of bubbling champagne. Chefs, wearing snow-white aprons and high hats, presided over the long tables, crowded with an infinite variety of European and Oriental hors d'oeuvres. 

The Count, in the full uniform of a general, was playing the genial host - greeting some of his guests with courtly charm, and others, with good-natured familiarity. "Ah, Torchinsky, how have you been?" he beamed. "Delighted to see you. What, not masked? Well, of course, that 

French beard of yours could hardly be disguised! . . . Tell me,

Torchinsky, what's the name of that German who cures syphilis with those mysterious numbers - six hundred or nine hundred? the devil knows which." 

"Your Excellency, I am delighted to know that you are interested in diseases of sin," the physician replied. "That is a sign of youth. The name of the German is Ehrlich, and the mysterious numbers, are six hundred and six, and nine hundred and fourteen." "Torchinsky, though I am no scientist, I dare say it is not scientific to designate a remedy with a numeral," the 

Count ventured. "May I inform you, Your Excellency, that the numerals are not the official technical terms of Ehrlich's anti-luetic 53

injections," the doctor replied. "This is how it came about:

In the course of his experiments, Ehrlich learned that the deadly poison, arsenic, possesses the distinctive ability to combat the microbes of syphilis, known as Spirochaeta pallida.

However his particular problem was the determination of the proper proportion of arsenic, for otherwise, the drug might prove lethal not only to the germs of syphilis, but to the patient as well. Consequently, he cast about for a formula that would blend arsenic with other chemical substances, so that only the syphilis germs would be affected. 

For many years, he exprimented with dogged perseverance, on numerous formulas. In 1910 his six-hundred-and-sixth compound proved to be a formula which effectively attacked the specific scourge of syphilis. Ehrlich named his epoch-making discovery, Salvarsan, although he and his coworkers still referred to it as six-hundred-six. Salvarsan, however, did not prove altogether effective, because it had some undesirable after-effects so Ehrlich resumed his research. In 1912, he finally worked out his nine-hundred-andfourteenth compound (eventually known as Neosalvarsan), and that proved to be the combination of arsenic with other drugs, which had the maximum effect on the germs of syphilis and created a minimum of damage to the healthy tissues of the patient. Ehrlich and his collaborators still refer to Neosalvarsan as nine-hundred-and-fourteen." 

"That's really amazing, Torchinsky!" the Count remarked fervently. "I must mention this at Court. The Czar relishes all news of progress in our civilization." "Your Excellency, you undoubtedly mean 'syphilization,' the doctor quipped, “since the more civilized the world becomes, the more 'syphilized' it gets." "Magnificent!" the Count burst into laughter. ""Civilization' and 'syphilization!' Witty, really! Bravo, Torchinsky – I will even overlook your French beard for this." 

The Count's laughter was interrupted by a footman: "Your Excellency," the latter reported, bowing and scraping, "a special courier from Polipovka is anxious to see you immediately. He claims the matter is urgent." 54

Knitting his beetling eyebrows and showing unrestrained irritation, the Count strode out of the chamber. He returned in a few minutes, decidedly over-wrought and scowling grimly at a telegram he was clutching. At a signal from him, the orchestra abruptly ceased playing. The guests stood perplexed, for it was only eleven o'clock - fully an hour before the ceremonial discarding of the masks was to take place. 

All eyes were riveted upon the Count, as he announced in a quavering voice: "Ladies and gentlemen, Germany and Austria have just declared war on Serbia. Because of an entente cordiale, our country is obligated to come to the defense of Serbia. Our Emperor has ordered a general mobilization of our military forces. I am duty-bound to offer my services to our Czar and Fatherland, and I am certain that such is the case with most of you. Ladies and gentlemen, the ball is over!" 

Incredible! A sudden earthquake and the collapse of the

Carpathian Mountains could not have caused greater consternation. Gone were decorum and etiquette; it occurred to very few to make formal farewells.

Utter confusion reigned as the guests, oblivious of the traditional good manners and courtesies of class and rank, jostled and nearly trampled one another. They hastened to their carriages,, as if the full force of the impending hostilities were concentrated exclusively on this spot of the vast Empire. The holiday finery and jewelry, in which the guests strutted and swaggered such a short while ago, now seemed particularly worthless. The lackeys, chefs, coachmen and domestics felt a relative equality with their masters; in this common catastrophe, they were all affected by the mass psychosis of fear.

The sudden departure of the guests left the palace enveloped in a strange air of desolation. Under the dazzling lights of the huge crystal chandeliers and candelabra, the goblets of flat champagne, the tables laden with untouched delicacies, the deserted orchestra platform, and the overturned fauteuils, all looked as forlorn and abandoned as items in the hold of a sinking, deserted ship. Strewn on the 55

parquet floor, the hastily discarded masks grimaced grotesquely.

A light shiver ran through Dr. Torchinsky's body, but his head resting on his hands remained motionless, and his eyes were still shut. He remembered how on that evening, two years ago, he had returned to Polipovka with the Count, each sunk in his own thoughts. "Torchinsky, we're making common cause with the Serbs." 

The Count had been the first to break the silence. "France is on our side, and England is an ally of France. This, brother, adds up to a world conflict." "Your Excellency, do you know why you were so fascinated by Ehrlich's six-hundred-six and nine-hundred-fourteen?" the doctor had asked. "Because you were carried away by the bulldog tenacity, the patience and endurance of that German biochemist. Ehrlich is no exception among them. Their achievements in the realm of science are even surpassed by their technical skill. Who knows what formidable war strategy they have prepared?" "Torchinsky, it isn't technique that turns the tide of battle, but soldiers," the Count had retorted. "We've got so many soldiers, that we shall bury the enemy under a shower of our caps!" "Bury them under a shower of our caps?" the war-weary 

Dr. Torchinsky was muttering to himself now, two years later. "Yes, under a shower of our caps - but in each cap, a Russian head There was a light tap at the door, and a nurse tiptoed into his study: "Sorry to disturb you, Doctor. You probably dozed offand no wonder, you must be exhausted. It is not humanly possible to carry on at such a terrific pace. But I had to see you. The soldier in Ward II, from whose right lung you removed a bullet this morning, is now running a high temperature - almost forty-one (centigrade). You requested that we report immediately any sharp change in this case. Forgive my awakening you, but —” "No need to apologize, nurse," the doctor replied, trying to put her at ease. "You did not really awaken me; as a matter 56

of fact, I was not sleeping- just trying to get a little rest. 

Let's go and see the patient."

Four out of the five wards in the Polipovka city hospital had been requisitioned for war casualties, thus reducing the facilities for the civilians by 80%. The military wards were crowded with narrow, iron cots. Casualties also lay on straw mattresses which had been placed on the cement floors, wherever space permitted. Under such circumstances, it was well-nigh impossible to observe even the most elementary sanitary precautions. In order to mask the stench of sweat, filth and festering wounds, carbolic acid and iodine were used profusely and added their pungent odors to the offensive hospital air.

One of the cots in Ward II was occupied by Sergeant Smirnov. His right lung had been pierced by a German bullet, and he had been brought to Polipovka with a train load of casualties. On the following day, Dr. Torchinsky had operated and removed the bullet. Now he lay breathing heavily the pallor of his bearded face emphasized by the hectic flush on his gaunt cheekbones.

Dr. Torchinsky's concern rose as he took the patient's pulse. The full, strong and regular beat meant that there had been no loss of blood. The high temperature, he reflected, was probably due either to the process of absorption, or to some new infection. That confounded bullet should have been removed long ago! During the ten-day span in which it had lodged in the patient's lung, it had damaged the surrounding tissue, thus creating multitudes of dead cells. The latter were noxious, and the absorption of their poisons by the body frequently resulted in high fever. The combination of dead cells and an open wound created a veritable paradise for microbes the second great enemy of Russia's armed forces. Microbes consummated what the 

Germans initiated. Ten days! Good Lord - the front was only thirty miles away - why ten days? 

Smirnov, his burning eyes popping out of their sockets, suddenly sat up, scarcely able to hold his head erect, because of the acute pains in his chest. Gritting his teeth, he muttered incoherently: "Traitors, parasites! Shoot them down 57

like dogs! No transportation, no artillery, no firearms . . . 

And soldiers - Russian soldiers - dying. all for nothing.. 99 "Nurse, he is delirious," Dr. Torchinsky pronounced authoritatively. "We must reduce his fever. Double the dose of acetyl salicylicum and watch him closely. If he keeps on tossing like this, tie him hand and foot, or he is liable to reopen his wounds and suffer a hemorrhage, and that would be fatal." "Who the devil is delirious?" Smirnov spoke quite lucidly and distinctly. "No one's going to tie me up, or I'll knock his teeth in! I know what's wrong with me. . . Doctor, I heard everything, and I understand everything!"

The doctor tried the patient's pulse again and said reassuringly: “Good, very good - you're a brave fellow, Smirnov. Men like you can conquer even infections and microbes. 

You'll get well - you'll get well and go home. Where are you from? Whom have you got at home a wife, a sweetheart?" 

"I'm from the Crimea, and I have neither wife nor sweetheart. There was no time for anything. I finished my military service term two days before the war broke out, but instead of being sent home, I was shipped to the front." "Well, you'll be able to go home now," the doctor comforted him. "You're entitled to a furlough." "I don't want to go home; I want to return to the front," the sergeant answered. "You're a hero, Smirnov," the doctor commented, "and 

I'll report your words to the military authorities. You'll be awarded a decoration." 

"I don't want decorations! I just want to get back to the front, because I want to be with my brother-soldiers — I want to stick around with them until the right moment comes, when we'll be able to square accounts with those who are to blame for our defeats!" "How can you tell who is to blame?" "We know, right enough! They're all to blame, from the very crown to those sons-of-bitches, the nobly-born generals!" the soldier exploded. 58

"Relax, fellow!" the doctor advised him. "Such talk in wartime can get you into trouble. What are you a revolutionary?" 

"Yes!" Smirnov was defiant. "I and millions like me.

The entire front is, as a matter of fact. You want to know why it took ten full days to cover thirty miles? Because there are no railroads! The Germans, who are paupers compared to us, have ten times as many railroad facilities as we Russians, with our endless natural resources. Do you know how many batteries of artillery we were supposed to have? Eight hundred! And do you know how many our army actually got? Sixty-yes, sixty! And that against three hundred and eighty German batteries! Instead of a hundred and thirty-five thousand machine guns, the mighty Russian army had only fifteen thousand! And instead of eighteen million rifles, the armies of our Empire had only five million!" "Where did you get all these statistics?" the doctor asked curiously. "We know what's what!" Smirnov insisted. "The front is swarming with our people, plain folk, peasants whose heads are the first to roll - millions of them - people who have been searching for the truth for years and years and who are learning the truth at last. It won't be long now, and we'll settle all accounts!" 

He fell silent. Beads of sweat stood out on his forehead; the crimson flush had vanished from the pale, bearded face, that was now distorted with wrath. 

Dr. Torchinsky removed his pince-nez with one hand and running the other over his tired eyes, he sighed wearily: "Poor Russia, your troubles are just beginning. May the 

Lord have mercy on you. Nurse, let's go to the civilian ward. I want to have another look at the woman I'm operating on tomorrow. Her condition is poor, and her prospects for recovery, even poorer. Yet we must resort to surgery unfortunately, we have no alternative." 

"I'm ready, doctor," the nurse answered. "You undoubtedly mean that Shanan woman from Struga."

The mention of Struga aroused Smirnov's interest. He 59

regarded the doctor and the nurse curiously and made an effort to say something. He was so weakened, however, that he soon gave up and closed his eyes again, mumbing incoherently. The doctor leaned over to catch the muttered words. What he heard astonished him no less than the preceding revolutionary tirade: "Struga . . . Count of Struga . . and his slave Kravlenko . . . I've got a long-overdue account to settle with them -"

In the Yeshivah of Reb Itzikel, the Polipovka Rabbi, the third-grade students, seated on hard benches ranged about long tables, were studying aloud, and in unison. The melodious reading of the Talmud floated out through the partlyopened, high windows, and mingled with the prosaic din of the world outside the hubbub of many human voices, the tramping of countless hurrying feet, the clatter and din of iron-rimmed wheels rolling along the cobbled streets, and the screeching and rattling of speeding trolleys. 

The chanting of the pupils poring over the Talmudic tractates had as little influence on the bustling activity outof-doors as the latter had upon the diligent study of the students. The chorus was compounded of the rhythmical cadence of the younger children's high-pitched voices, the crowing, strangely non-resonant, changing voices of the somewhat older pupils, and the steady, distinct voices of young men - punctuated by the melancholy, hoarse voice of Reb Zechariah, the teacher. The sea of Jewish learning is profound and deep, and it is difficult to keep afloat therein. 

From early morning until late at night, the pupils racked their brains struggling to comprehend the intricate disquisitions of the Mishnah and Talmud, of Rashi, and the later commentators, the Tosaphists.

Like the skilled conductor of an orchestra who can perceive the faintest false note and dissonance, even if it emanates from the most remote instrument, the ever-alert

Reb Zechariah missed the lyrical and plaintive voice of one of his pupils, David from Struga. The failure of a pupil to participate in the communal study of the group was considered a desecration of the Lord, and such laxity was, 60

naturally, not to be tolerated. If the offender was an average pupil, Reb Zechariah would unceremoniously reprimand him and insist on having him recite the entire lesson aloud before the class. This was considered a harsh punishment, and the pupils did their utmost to escape it. 

David, however, was no ordinary, mediocre student. During his three years at the Yeshivah, he had come to be known as an iluy, or prodigy. His ardent desire for learning, his extraordinary ability and precociousness, his ingenuity in comprehending the most abstruse passages in a Talmudic tractate, were admired by all. Moreover, he was endowed with a prodigious memory. The Talmudic tractates and its commentaries which he studied engraved themselves on his mind so clearly, that he could repeat them from memory, without missing a single diacritical mark. 

It did not take long for Reb Itzikel, himself, to become aware of the gifted youngster the son of his beloved adherent, Reb Avrum of Struga. The sage took a liking to this pupil, not merely because of his diligence and ability, but also because of his innate refinement, his mystic air, and wistful eyes, that reflected a mellow sadness and longing. 

The Rabbi's two children, Yosef and Miriam, also became very much attached to David. Yosef, though three years his senior, treated David as an equal and continually sought him out, especially during the leisure hours which the pupils enjoyed on the Sabbath and other Holy days. Yosef was particularly fascinated by David's stories and vivid descriptions of his native village of Struga, the Carpathian Mountain, the Dniester, and the meadows and woodland surrounding it. As he listened, Yosef would give free rein to his imagination and share David's exciting experiences. When the narrative touched on Marushka, he would listen, enraptured. A sweet warmth would permeate his being; his heart would beat rapidly and arouse within him a deep desire for a girl like Marushka.

On the night that David told Yosef about Marushka and the "little angel in her belly," and how her body had been found at the bottom of the well, neither youngster was able to fall asleep. They divulged to one another the hidden 61

secret that each knew the meaning of a "little angel" in her belly, and two still-childish bodies flamed until dawn with that magic fire, which is the driving power to mankind's perpetuation. . .

Miriam was one year older than David, but regarded him as her senior - especially after a certain Sabbath afternoon: 

David was the only one of the student body at the Polipovka Yeshivah, who made no secret of his love for the Rabbi's fenced-in orchard. From the moment he stepped into it, his dark, dream-haunted eyes would shine with a deep happiness; his pensiveness would vanish, and he would be transformed into an unruly and mischievous lad. He would sprint between the trees like a deer, challenging Yosef and Miriam to a race, or he would dash over to a tree and embrace it, as though welcoming a long-lost kinsman. "Why are you so fond of the trees?" Miriam asked him on that Sabbath afternoon. "Because they remind me of Struga, my native village, and my home. Because they are my best friends," David replied with unrestrained fervor, his arms still hugging the tree. 

Miriam lowered her head, her thick blonde braids swaying like ripe ears of corn. Some loose strands fell over her forehead and screened her big blue eyes. With hands clasped behind her, she stood still as a statue, but breathing hard, her firm young breasts with their buds of ripening womanhood rising and falling. "And I thought that Yosef and I were your best friends,” she remarked with restrained resentment, after a prolonged silence.

David stepped over to her, pushed her hair gently back, and taking hold of both of her hands, gazed into her eyes. "Your eyes are as serene and as deep as the Dniester,” he said. "Your throat is snow white. My mother, too, has a white throat a soft, warm, white throat." He leaned forward gently and pressed his lips against hers. A strange blissfulness and a new joy suddenly awakened within him. 

After that Sabbath, the Rabbi's orchard took on new meaning for David. He would look for any excuse, no matter how lame, to visit the orchard as often as possible - but 62

no longer just to run, jump, and embrace the trees. 

There was a spot in a remote corner of the orchard, which was known as the "Newlyweds' Home." It was a sort of cave hemmed in by two ancient chestnut trees that had toppled over in a storm. Their boughs and twigs were intertwined in such a way as to form a hollow large enough to shield several persons from view. This cave was linked with the following legend: Several decades ago, when Rabbi Yosefel, the great-grandfather of Reb Itzikel, was still alive, a plague which had taken a great toll of lives had swept over Polipovka. 

Rabbi Yosefel urged that a collection be made for some poor, orphaned, spinster's dowry, and that because of this meritorious deed, the plague would be halted. Accordingly, the marriage of this orphan was performed in the Rabbi's orchard, near the two chestnut trees. Suddenly the sky became overcast with black clouds, and a violent storm sprang up. 

Lightning struck the two chestnut trees, toppling them to the ground, and burying the bride and groom underneath them The plague was over. Reb Yosefel ordered that the toppled trees were not to be removed, but were to remain as a lasting symbol of the two holy souls, because of whose sacrifice a community of Jews had been spared from annihilation. The name of this spot remained forever known as the "Newlyweds' Home." The "Newlyweds' Home" came to be David's and Miriam's trysting place. The first time that they approached the spot, Miriam halted at the entrance and said in dismay: “David, this is the Newlyweds' Home. No one ever enters it! It is said, that each midnight the two come out and go through their wedding dance, in their shrouds."

Taking firm hold of Miriam's hand, David led her into the gloomy, musty cave. Thick gray cobwebs dangled from the intertwined boughs, which had become covered with layers of old, yellow-green moss. Here and there, however, a streak of daylight penetrated the murkiness. "Aren't you at all afraid, David?" Miriam asked in a soft, wondering voice. "No, I'm not afraid," David reassured her. "There's a much bigger cave in Struga, under a huge boulder on the bank 63

of the Dniester, and I was never afraid to go into that. We'll come whenever we can, and we can tidy up this 'Newlyweds' 

Home' a bit. We'll sweep out the cobwebs, and I'll break off this bough so that we can see the sky. It will be like looking through the branches covering a booth during the Feast of Tabernacles. We will sit right on this stump, and no one will know where we are." "Is it all right?” Miriam asked in a tremulous voice. She felt her young breasts becoming firmer and a delectable tingle course through them, and as she drew closer to David, his nearness intensified the tingling and throbbing. David embraced her, burying his mouth in her white, soft throat.

The warmth of her body brought a joyous glow to his being and rewarded him with a contentment in his interminable longing for his mother's endearments, and for Marushka's love songs. 

His frequent meetings with Miriam at the "Newlyweds' Home" did not distract David from his studies. If anything, his voice was heard more resonantly and fervently than ever above the voices of the other pupils — and he seemed more eager than ever, when it came to Queries and Responses. 

Reb Zechariah would comment, time and again: "David of

Struga, you are a freak; you have the body of a child and the head of a graybeard.”

It was natural then, for the teacher to be puzzled on this day by the absence of David's familiar voice from the general chorus. Peering over his spectacles, he caught sight of David in an unusual pose: the young scholar sat bent silently over his Talmudic folio, with his head propped on his hands, staring listlessly ahead, oblivious to his surroundings. Reb 

Zechariah's gaze at the mooning David and his sudden suspension had the same effect on the class, as a conductor dropping his baton would have on an orchestra. All fell silent, fixing their attention on their classmate.

The sudden intense hush in the classroom roused David from his reverie. He took in the scene at a glance, stood up slowly, and to everyone's surprise, without uttering a word and walking somewhat unsteadily, he left the room. "Yosef, you probably know what is wrong with David," 64

Reb Zechariah turned to the Rabbi's son. "You are his close friend; you've been like a brother to him ever since he has been living with your family. Can you possibly account for his unusual behavior today?" "I certainly can, Rabbi," Yosef replied. “David's mother is very ill; she has been brought from Struga to the hospital here and is scheduled to be operated upon today. I have heard 

Reb Avrum of Struga say that Doctor Torchinsky is very much concerned about David's mother, because she has cancer of the stomach." "It's a pity, a great pity," Reb Zechariah sighed. “Well, let's get on with our lesson - and, through His goodness may the Everlasting One take pity upon her, and restore her to good health."

As David was making his way toward the gate of the 

Yeshivah courtyard, he heard Eliezer, the Rabbi's sexton, calling to him: "David, where are you going? Come along to the Rabbi; he asked me to fetch you. He wants to see you right away."

At the Rabbi's house, David found that his father had already arrived. His first instinct was to rush to embrace 

Avrum, but he succeeded in restraining himself from so childish an outburst and greeted his father calmly and controlled as befitted a Seminary student. "David," his father said in a quavering voice, "you'll come along with me to the hospital in a few minutes. Your mother wishes to see you before the operation. But before we leave, ask the holy sage, Reb Itzikel, to wish her a speedy recovery. A child's plea carries far more weight than that of an adult —” In his predicament, David stood with bowed head. He had never imagined that he would some day appear before Reb Itzikel and ask his blessing. Reb Itzikel had always been regarded as a righteous man, born of the righteous - a holy man of divine power, whose blessings were considered heaven-sent.

Reb Itzikel was not quite forty when his beard turned almost entirely white, his high forehead became wrinkled and his back bent. His deep blue eyes alone resisted this 65

premature aging and continued to hold their youthful gleam. 

Only a few very close friends and followers of the Rabbi were aware of the reason for his premature aging. Though some of them occasionally pondered over the matter, none dared to voice his opinion or so much as hint at it. Not even the opponents of the Polipovka rabbinical dynasty, nor the Hasidic followers of other opposing spiritual leaders, whether from nearby or remote provinces, would discuss this most vulnerable point in Reb Itzikel's life. As to his own numerous followers, their reticence was a matter of course. They kept the secret well concealed, apprehensive lest misfortune and the divine curse befall them.

It so happened, that upon the demise of Reb Itzikel's father, Reb Moisheleh, the Polipovka Rabbinical Court was in danger of disintegrating. It split into two factions, one of which maintained that since Itzikel, the only surviving son of the deceased, was eighteen years of age and unmarried, he was neither mature nor competent enough to head the Polipovka Rabbinical Court. They felt that the latter ought to merge with the Minkover Court, which was under the sway of Rabbi Yechielekel, a brother of the late Reb Moisheleh and Itzikel's uncle. As it would all remain in the same family, the merger of the two Rabbinical Courts was bound to benefit both the dynasty and its followers, this group urged.

The second faction, however, felt that a merger with Minkov was tantamount to urging the eclipse of Polipovka and would lead to the collapse of the traditions of Polipovka Hasidism that had prevailed for generations. At long last a compromise was reached: Itzikel, the only surviving son of the deceased Rabbi of Polipovka, and Rivkaleh, the nineteen-year-old daughter of the Rabbi of Minkov, were joined in wedlock, and the young groom assumed the rabbinical duties of his late father.

The new Rabbi's wife was blonde, blue-eyed and endowed with queenly beauty and grace. She led the life of an aristocrat, with servants, carriages, imported clothes and a luxurious boudoir and read all the classical works as well as the latest novels. Reb Itzikel cherished her and loved her to distraction. 66

For seven years, their marriage was childless. This was a source of grave concern to the young couple and a great disappointment to the Rabbi's followers. One can, therefore, imagine their overwhelming joy when at the end of that time, the Rabbi's wife gave birth to a son and, two years later, to a girl. 

Childbearing seemed to accentuate the beauty of the Rabbi's wife. The succulence with which motherhood endows a woman, manifested itself especially in the shapely, symmetrical limbs and fully-blossomed contours of her body. 

Reb Itzikel's married life was a most happy one; he seemed to tread on enchanted ground. At times, however, he was haunted by a nameless fear - by a premonition of some evil that would arise to dispel this bliss. During his fervent prayers and study of Holy Writ, he overflowed with gratitude to the Lord for all the blessings bestowed upon him. 

But then God willed that his wife should become barren again. An unrest, a strange thirst and hunger, harassed her inner being. Swinging his pince-nez on their ribbon, Dr. 

Torchinsky had cautioned her: "It's a shame, my dear, a great shame! You are like a tree in sap- the more fruit it bears, the more its boughs blossom, and the less fruit, the quicker its boughs become gnarled. You must conceive, my dear. . .

Finally, at the suggestion of his wealthy and worldly followers, Reb Itzikel sent his wife to a famous sanatorium abroad, not far from Vienna. It was rumored that gynecologists at this institution were performing miracles. An atmosphere of despondency settled over the Rabbi's household during his wife's absence. Not only was Reb Itzikel pining away for her, but the two children, Yosef and Miriam, wandered through the silent spacious rooms like desolate orphans. And 

Hannah Faiga, the wife of Shimmon, the Elder, stood confused and distraught at the helm of the gloom-ridden household.

Rivkaleh was originally supposed to spend four weeks at the sanatorium, but her sojourn lasted much longer. Reb 

Itzikel at first rejoiced when he learned that the specialists had extended his wife's stay at the sanatorium, interpreting 67

this as hopeful reassurance. What baffled him, however, was the fact that her letters became less and less frequent, with each one shorter and cooler than the preceding one. And then, almost on the heels of the last, and coldest letter, he received a notification from the Polipovka Bank: “The check for the amount of two hundred roubles made out to Rivka Sneurson has been returned from Vienna with the explanation that the addressee has gone." 

That same day, Reb Itzikel set out for Vienna. On reaching the private Jewish convalescent home where his wife had been staying, he was handed a sealed envelope containing a brief note: "Fate has united me here with another man whom I love. By the time you read these words, I shall be on a liner bound for America, where a new life awaits me a life that I could not have with you. Forgive me, and kiss the children for me. Rivka."

On returning home, Reb Itzikel remained in seclusion for three days and three nights. He would neither see anyone, nor eat throughout that period. Shimmon, the Elder, kept vigil for hours at a stretch, perched on a tabouret on the other side of the Rabbi's door, tapping lightly with one finger from time to time, and pleading in a whisper: “Holy 

Rabbi, have pity. Let me bring you a glass of hot tea, you may faint, God forbid.”

During the Rabbi's self-imposed isolation, Shimmon kept watch like a loyal dog. As long as he could hear his spiritual leader's footsteps, he remained fairly calm, but the moment that silence fell within the study, he grew alarmed and resumed his light tapping and pleading.

On Friday, the fourth day, the study door opened, and Shimmon was stunned by the sight that met his eyes. He could hardly recognize the Rabbi, who had become, in those few days, wizened and stoop-shouldered, and whose hair had turned white. "Shimmon, the Holy Sabbath is drawing close," the Rabbi told him. "See to it that everything is carried out accordingly. 

Please bring the children here." "Holy Rabbi," the other mumbled, aghast, "You've aged so in the last few days! Your grief must be boundless. But 68

praised be the Lord, that I see you alive!"

Yosef and Miriam advanced toward their father haltingly, their heads bowed to receive his blessing; Shimmon and his wife stood in the doorway, wiping their eyes and not daring to enter. The grief-stricken Rabbi barely touched the heads of his children, as he croaked hoarsely: "Shimmon, I want your hand in a promise — that the name of their mother shall be obliterated from their memory and shall never be mentioned again in my lifetime —”

Shimmon regarded his promise as a sacred oath and carried out the Rabbi's wish without the least deviation. And little by little, the two youngsters came to dread the very thought of their mother, and in due time, her name and face faded from their hearts and minds. . .

Although David had been living under the Rabbi's roof during his years at the Yeshivah and was looked upon as one of the family, he was still in awe of Reb Itzikel. 

It was Reb Itzikel's wont, at the conclusion of the Sabbath, after the Havdalah service, to examine Yosef and David on their studies of the past week. He would reiterate his conviction that in the sayings of the sages could be found the founts of eternal wisdom, and the answers to questions not merely of the past, but of the present and future as well and not for the Jews alone, but for all mankind. Reb Itzikel was a student of world literature and was well versed in Russian, German and French. When he referred to the works of the modern thinkers, he was able to prove that essentially their theories were nothing new, since they were all to be found in the wisdom of the Torah. Young David hearkened to his mentor's words with fervor and veneration. 

He looked forward eagerly to these sessions with the Rabbi, at the conclusion of the Sabbath. The Rabbi's study, its walls lined to the ceiling with books, became associated, in the boy's mind, not with the dispensation of blessings, but with the Torah and the erudition of past, present and future with the Torah between man and his neighbor, with the Torah as a way of life. 

David was well aware that hundreds of the Rabbi's Hasidic 69

followers, not merely from Polipovka, but from other communities as well, came flocking to him with their personal cares and problems, pleading for guidance, consolation and blessing. These suppliants were first screened by Shimmon, who, seated at a small desk near the Rabbi's study, acted as his receptionist. The applicants would pour out their griefladen hearts to the Elder, either weeping softly, or speaking in subdued tones, so as not to be overheard by the others awaiting their turns. The discreet and experienced Shimmon would extract the vital information from their torrents of words and reduce it to a concise memo which the suppliant would present to the Rabbi upon entering his study. The spiritual leader received his followers individually; after glancing through the pertinent note, he would, with great compassion, confer his blessing and consolation. "Do not despair. The Lord does not like to see His creatures steeped in sadness. Hence he offers us the opportunity of studying the Torah, of observing His divine precepts and performing good deeds, so that we may derive courage from them and rejoice. Go home in good health, have faith and confidence, and the Almighty will help you."

David knew that these simple words harbored a great, latent, living force. The Hasidim came to the Rabbi sad and downhearted, but departed in good cheer and filled with hope. David knew also that his force did not emanate solely through these words, for time and again he had repeated them to himself, yet, had never felt himself uplifted. Their dynamic influence stemmed from the fact that they were uttered by the Hasidic leader who drew his inspiration from a wellspring of humaneness, inspired by a vision of God. 

"Rabbi, it is written in the Torah that Adam was created in the image of God," David recalled having said to Reb Itzikel in this same study. "Yet Maimonides says, in the third article of his Creed, that the Creator, blessed be His 

Name, has neither body nor any image or form whatsoever." "Yes, David, you are right, and your question is a keen one," the Rabbi replied. “But the phrase 'image of God' does not imply that it is a mortal's body which is in God's image, but rather his soul. The meaning is that every human pos 70

sesses the divine spark, and when one achieves merit through study of Holy Writ, through the obedience of divine commands, and the performance of good deeds, then that divine spark in us which is the image of God, comes to the fore. 

Do you grasp the meaning now, David?" "Yes, Rabbi, I do," the boy said. And at once two people who had achieved this merit had sprung up in his mind- 

Reb Itzikel, and his father.

Suddenly David's heart began to pound. He was stunned, because Samborik, too, appeared in his mind as one also possessing this divine spark. “Rabbi, can a gentile, too, achieve the merit of the image of God?" David asked with misgivings.

Reb Itzikel pondered the question and fixed his keen blue eyes on David. "The Torah, my son, says that God created Adam in His own image. Adam, in Hebrew, means 'man,' and not 'Jew.'

The image of God, therefore, applies to any human being. 

The non-Jew, like the Jew, through his good deeds, can also attain the image of God. . .” "What are you thinking of, David?" his father asked. "Why don't you ask the Rabbi for a blessing for your mother's recovery?"

Tears suffused the lad's eyes, and he could not utter a word. Reb Itzikel interceded, asserting: "Don't despair, my child. The Lord does not like to see

His creatures grieving. To this end did He present us with the opportunity of studying the Torah, of observing His divine precepts and performing good deeds, so that we may derive courage and joy in an hour of grief and pain. My child, go to your mother's sick bed, have faith and confidence, and the Almighty will help you —”

A feeling of relief and comfort coursed through David's being. The Rabbi's simple and familiar words gave him courage and inspired him with hope.

Hannah lay on a screened-off bed in the only ward for civilians in the Polipovka Hospital, waiting to be wheeled into the operating room. The ravages of her disease had left 71

her unrecognizable: her body had shriveled, her bones protruded under her wrinkled, sallow skin; her nose had become pointed, her eyes had sunk into their sockets and her lips were ashen. Excruciating pains shot through her monstrously bloated abdomen; cold sweat beaded her forehead. 

As a nurse approached to give her an injection of morphine, she pleaded in a barely audible voice: 

"Please, nurse not now. I am expecting my son, and I'd rather not have him find me in a dazed condition. Just freshen my face a little with a damp towel. Thank you, nurse!"

As David approached his mother, the exhilaration and hopefulness with which the Rabbi's blessing had inspired him, vanished. No, this woman could not be his mother! 

His mother had a soft, lily-white throat, full red lips and glowing dark eyes! Who was this strange, ailing woman, all skin and bones, the very sight of whom scared one so? "David, my little boy," Hannah murmured tearfully, stretching out her gaunt hands. "How my heart has longed for you! You've grown so big - you are a real man now!"

His mother's words awakened in him all the lonesomeness he had felt for her. He flung himself into the thin, pleading arms and gave way to uncontrolled weeping. 

"Don't cry, my son; wipe the tears," his mother urged him. "It's not right for a student in a Yeshivah, and a prodigy at that, to cry on his mother's shoulder like a child." She stroked David's head tenderly, a gentle smile of joy and calm hovering on her lips and went on, ever more faintly and haltingly: "David, my son they will take me away from here soon, and I don't know whether I'll ever come back alive but wherever I'll be, I'll pray to God that you may grow up to be a good man There's so much wickedness. in the world - and so little loving-kindness. David, my little boy I love you so! I missed you so much. And now that you are with me, I don't miss a thing - even my pain has subsided. I missed you so... And when the grass in Struga turned soft and green, and the Dniester flowed peacefully and unruffled, I saw you there always, and my longing for you increased. I was afraid to be taken to the operating-table, 

72

as though I was in the shadow of death. But holding you close to me, I feel good, and have no more fear. You are my courage and my consolation 99 Raising his head slowly, David gazed at his mother's bloodless but serene face and said tearfully: "Mother, dear mother, I'll pray to God to make you well, to send the Angel Raphael to guard you until you are well again. I'll pray and recite 

Psalms for you: "The Lord is my Shepherd, I shall not want. He maketh me to lie down in green pastures; He leadeth me beside the still waters. He restoreth my soul; he guideth me in the paths of righteousness for His name's sake. Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil, for Thou art with me; Thy rod and Thy staff, they comfort me."

Two nurses placed Hannah on a wheeled stretcher and took her out of the ward. David remained sitting on the empty bed and felt a heavy, depressing loneliness in his heart. "Mother, mother," he murmured. "Your sweet last words - how much they are like my Psalms! . . . As God is my witness, I swear to abide by your wishes as long as I live!"

Reb Itzikel and Avrum Shanan of Struga, his best friend and most faithful follower, had been secluded in the Rabbi's private study for over three hours, conferring on a most important matter. The two had engaged in lengthy conferences before, but none had ever lasted this long. The 

Rabbi's intimates, as well as his other followers, tried to guess the nature of this mysterious conference and advanced all sorts of suppositions.

Avrum Shanan had been a widower for three years, one of the Hasidim pointed out. "He hasn't turned fifty yet, so they are probably trying to make a match for him, and he wants to consult the Rabbi before venturing into it." "Avrum Shanan came to Polipovka only to take his son, David, back to Struga," another announced. "But there's 73

-- more to it than you might think; the story is that the Rabbi is anxious to marry off his beautiful and gifted daughter,

Miriam, to David — a prodigy, and the son of such a wealthy man as Avrum Shanan that would be quite a match. The elite, the Torah and wealth - a sensational combination!" "That's not the case at all," a third interposed. "They are just haggling, that's all. Avrum Shanan is probably reluctant to pay for his son's six years of tuition, while the 

Rabbi isn't letting such a plutocrat get away without paying; he's most likely demanding a new dormitory for the Yeshivah, where the out-of-town students could get shelter and food under the Rabbi's supervision."

All those gathered in the Rabbi's anteroom were consumed by curiosity, as the time dragged on. Some cast sidelong glances at Shimmon, supposing him to be in the Rabbi's confidence. But the Elder did not utter a word, and by his silence, seemed to stress his allegiance and devotion to his spiritual leader; he appeared to take it for granted that the conference behind locked doors was being held for highly valid reason.

As a matter of fact, Shimmon had no inkling of what the Rabbi and his guest were discussing. He felt that the parley must be a most important one, for the Rabbi had summoned him on the previous evening and told him: "Shimmon, Avrum 

Shanan from Struga is due here tomorrow. Show him in as soon as he arrives, don't receive anyone else, and see to it that we are not disturbed until our consultation is over." 

Shimmon was carrying out his instructions. 

Avrum Shanan's present visit had been arranged at Reb Itzikel's request for the express purpose of bringing David home. The letter Avrum had received from the spiritual leader had been strikingly laconic, almost like a telegram, and contained a note of urgency about it: 

"To my dear friend, Avrum Shanan – may his light shine forth! Your son, David, must leave Polipovka and return to Struga. The reason for this decision of mine cannot be elaborated upon in a letter. We will discuss this matter personally." 74

Having read the letter, Avrum Shanan had ordered Alexei to harness the large phaeton and had immediately set out. 

Though outwardly serene, he was inwardly overwrought and apprehensive. 

What could have happened? he pondered. Illness? Not likely - the Rabbi was aware that there was no doctor in Struga and would not urge David's return home. Bad conduct? That was unthinkable in David's case. One had to be patient, he reflected; the journey would take two full days, and too much introspection could drive one insane. One had to have faith and keep calm.

It was the time of year when spring blends into summer - when the fields are bright with ripening grain; when the sun sends down its warm, soothing rays; and when the air is filled with refreshing and goodly odors. The horses raced along the wide, unpaved highway, leaving thick clouds of dust in their wake. Avrum leaned back, making himself comfortable. The even rocking of the carriage springs made his body sway lightly. He closed his eyes.

The political situation and the dismal, alarming conditions of the day filled his thoughts. The ignominious defeats of the Russian troops and the exposed corruption among the high-ranking commanders served more and more to disillusion and blight the hopes of the soldiers at the front and the hungry and frustrated laboring classes at home. Strange stories, filtering down to the people through devious channels, added fuel to the fire of unrest. These stories dealt, for the most part, with the fantastic adventures of a certain perfidious monk named Rasputin. He was said to have cast a spell over the Czarina and her household by his hypnotic powers and had come to be a sort of diabolical force behind the Russian throne. The revolutionary elements, quick to forge the iron while it was hot, used these tales to incite the masses both on the war front and in the factories and mills 

urging them to revolt against the corrupt and putrefying autocracy. Then, the unexpected happened - on March 12, 1917: 

The general strike of all workers in both civilian and military establishments, lasting for several days, had cul- 75

minated in a colossal mass demonstration on March 12 in St. Petersburg, the capital, whose Germanized name of 

Petersburg had been changed to the Russified "Petrograd," at the outbreak of World War I. The commanding officers of the army and navy had been enjoined to send a punitive expedition against the recalcitrant demonstrators, but, to the amazement of all, the soldiers and sailors ordered out against the insurgent masses, had made common cause with them and had turned their guns against their own commanding officers. The three hundred and four year old 

Romanov dynasty had collapsed. . . .

Army discipline deteriorated; respect and authority vanished overnight. Soldiers tore the epaulets from their officers' uniforms. Chaos and anarchy reigned. The disgruntled infantrymen, retaining their firearms, deserted the front in countless thousands, some heading for home, others going wherever their feet led them. Numerous bands of armed deserters took to raiding and terrorizing the land, especially small towns and settlements. The newly-formed Provisional 

Government, under the leadership of Alexander Kerensky, made every effort to reorganize the demoralized army, in order to continue the war along with its allies, but all these efforts proved futile; the Russian people were weary of war.

Avrum sighed and resumed his desultory thoughts. True enough, the Russian people were sick and tired of wars, yet Providence had apparently willed that the suffering should continue a while longer just the same. Though the fighting between the Russians and Germans had ceased, peace had not ensued. The revolution had brought civil warfare in its wake, involving Bolsheviks, Ukrainians and Czarists, and this had been raging for over two years now. The Bolsheviks aspired to a dictatorship of the proletariat; the Ukrainians yearned for political independence and autonomy; and the 

Czarists were determined to restore the monarchy. The carnage was widespread; the country, devastated; the people, bewildered. It was hard to tell whose rallying cry was genuine and sincere - whose banner to follow, whom to believe, 76

and whom to be wary of. Pretenders, heading bands of armed highwaymen and adventurers, sprang up like mushrooms after a rainfall and waged internecine warfare against one another. It was the persecuted Jew, however, who drained the cup of misery to its dregs. As usual, the Jew became the scapegoat. The Jews were to blame, no matter what happened. Thus, the country had suffered defeat, because the 

Jews were supposed to have betrayed it, to have revealed important secrets. The revolution had come about, because 

Jews were absorbed in their thick Talmudic folios, "studying how to create havoc throughout the world." The Jews were at the mercy of every wind that blew, and entire communities were being ravaged and massacred. Every Jew went in fear of his life. A familiar, tragic history was repeating itself. Firmly established families were being uprooted, and the staff of the wanderer was once more the Jew's prop and support. Thousands of Jews relinquished their hard-earned, wretched belongings, fleeing along out-of-the-way paths, under cover of darkness. They entrusted their lives to Ukrainian guides, handing over their last few coins of silver and gold, to be smuggled across the frontier, across the Dniester to Bessarabia, to Rumania. The townships and villages across the river swarmed with homeless refugees, crowded into barracks like gypsies. Prominent householders and merchants were beggared and turned vagabond overnight. There were rumors that Jewish benevolent organizations in America had sent emissaries to collect the Jewish refugees from the Ukraine, to resettle them in the far-off New World . . . Avrum was beset with fears; who could tell how Struga would fare? Chills ran down his spine at this thought. Poor David - he was so fond of Struga. He sent up a silent plea: "Lord of the 

Universe, why? Why are we not left in peace?" A critical situation, indeed. "There is no justice, and there is no judge." God alone knew how all this would end. "The sun is going down - dusk will be setting in soon," the coachman turned around to address his master. "It would be better to put up at the White Inn. I doubt if it is wise to go any further tonight. We're getting close to the Verbivka 77

Forest, and it's most dangerous to be there at night. These are difficult times! There are evil men around and the forest is full of them." 

The White Inn was situated on the way to the Verbivka Forest. Its name, known throughout the district, originated from the fact that the area surrounding the inn, was, through some phenomenon of nature, chalk white. This white area was conspicuous by its contrast to the rest of the fertile black soil, so characteristic of Ukrainian fields. The white oasis in the midst of the endless black loam could be seen for miles around.

As the phaeton rolled through the gateway of the White Inn, both Avrum and Alexei were struck by the unusual quiet. They heard neither the neighing of horses nor the babble and hubbub of coachmen and travelers; absent also were the rattle and clatter of china and cutlery. But above all, Shmul Verbivker, the proprietor of the White Inn, was nowhere in sight. Shmul considered Avrum Shanan's every visit to his inn an honor. He would always wait on his esteemed guest personally, ushering him into the house with a hearty welcome and much to-do. "Baila!" he would shout. "Mr. Avrum Shanan is putting up for the night - get our bedroom ready for him!" This meant that the innkeeper and his wife woud have to spend that night in a cubbyhole in the attic.

Now, however, an unfriendly atmosphere pervaded the place. Instead of the hospitable Shmul, a pug-nosed, paunchy, red-haired, pock-marked soldier emerged from the inn. A rifle was slung over his shoulder; his holster bulged with a revolver, and a sword dangled at his right side. "Turn around! There's no more room!" the belligerent soldier yelled, with a typical Moscow accent. "Get going or I'll shoot!"

The sight of the heavily-armed, pugnacious fighter appalled Alexei so, that he wheeled the coach around, without waiting for his master's customary order. "Serebryak!" a voice echoed from within. "Who is it? Who are you talking to?"

Avrum flinched, and beads of perspiration suddenly stood 78

out on his forehead;

he had recognized the voice as that of 

Vassilii Kravlenko. 

At the outbreak of World War I, Vassilii Kravlenko, the chief of rural police at Struga, had been drafted and immediately sent to the front. Thereafter, he seemed to have vanished; no one knew his whereabouts. His father, the drunken Ivan, had taken it for granted that his Vaska had made the supreme sacrifice on the battlefield in behalf of the Czar and Mother Russia, and this served as his excuse for drinking bottoms-up with all and sundry, for the repose of the hero's soul.

Vassilii had spent six months at the front without getting so much as a scratch. For the most part, he had been in the advance lines with the bullets whistling about him, but he didn't even attempt to take cover when the enemy sent over a barrage. He came through all the skirmishes unscathed and gradually began to believe that he was impervious to 

German bullets. He lost all fear of facing fire.

One damp, cold day in 1915, Vassilii's detachment was pulled out of the trenches and given a ten-day furlough in the Galician town of Sugatin, some ten miles behind the front. It was there that Vassilii felt the full impact of weeks of accumulated fatigue and lack of sleep. As he pulled off his high boots, his rotting socks of thick wool fell off his feet in pieces, spreading a dreadful stench. Vassilii slumped on a straw pallet and fell into a sleep from which no power on earth could have aroused him. 

He slept for twenty-four hours, and on awakening, felt a parched dryness in his mouth and throat. He stepped out of the barracks and walked bare-footed through the snow, the biting cold stirring him out of his drowsiness. Ambling over to the well in the middle of the yard, he pulled up a bucket and drank out of it greedily — streams of cold water flowing from the corners of his mouth, along his beard, throat, and through his collar, down over his chest and body. He drained half the bucket and dumped the rest of the water over his head, inhaling deeply and shaking himself like a bear. Then he re-entered the barracks, shed his wet clothes for the 

79

first time in several weeks - and rubbed his hairy body down with a coarse towel. From his knapsack he got laundered underwear, socks, trousers, and a shirt, and as he dressed, a satisfied feeling of warmth and well-being came over him. 

He paused for a moment to bask in his sense of good fortune - his prowess, and the conscious wonder of still being among the living. His famished body was clamorously making its wants known. Vassilii felt as though he could devour the whole world.

He knew that liquor was prohibited in the war zone and was not willing to procure it on the sly. There were numerous teahouses in Sugatin, and they were all crowded with soldiers. 

The air in these places was invariably saturated with the reek of perspiration and tobacco smoke and filled with the nostalgic songs of the men, sung to the throbbing accompaniment of accordions. Vassilii dropped into one of these places, ordered a big pot of tea, a loaf of white bread, and a pound of sausage, and devoured his food as ravenously as a wolf. 

Suddenly he felt a pair of fiery eyes boring through him and looking up, he saw a young girl - little more than a child - gazing at him and smiling coquettishly. Roundfaced and attractive, she wore a red blouse and had a white scarf with green flowers thrown over her shoulders. 

Vassilii's blood pounded in his head; she reminded him so much of Samborik's Marushka, whom the Count's nephew had so flagrantly taken under the huge boulder. . . . There had been but one outcry from her, and then she had fallen silent like a dove. He stepped over to the girl and took hold of her hand. She offered no objection and her dark eyes enticed him. "What's your name, beautiful?" "Katerina - Katerina Budenko," the girl replied, squeezing Vassilii's hand meaningfully. Her body moved abruptly, and her firm young breasts swayed and aroused in Vassilii an insatiable desire to possess her naked body. Tingling with excitement, he breathed: 

"Tell the truth, beautiful — did you ever have a sweetheart?"

Katerina giggled a little, then pulled Vassilii by his hand 80

and said softly and longingly: 

"Come with me; we live right above – just one room and a kitchen. There's nobody home now; father is at the front, and mother is out working - she never gets home till late at night. Come on!" she urged him, her eyelashes fluttering coquettishly. 

Vasilii's heart was pounding heavily; his blood churned in his vessels, and the succulence of his glands filled him with the stiffness of steel. He was overwhelmed by an animallike mating instinct, and would have killed, if need be, to attain his end. Aroused and tantalized, he followed the girl. 

A dim narrow hallway . . . rickety stairs of rotted wood . . a door creaking with rusty latch and hinges. .. a lowceilinged garrett ... and a bed. Vassilii lifted the girl with his herculean arms, and, throwing her on the bed, tore at her blouse and skirt. "Don't you go tearing my clothes, soldier," Katerina pleaded. 

I'll undress myself." 

In the next moment, his eyes fell on her nude, white body - her freshly matured and upstanding breasts, her long legs, her round thighs - and his blood seethed. Seizing her in an iron grip, and growling in triumph, he joined with her in a wild orgasm which flowed abundantly from the depth of his bone marrow, to the tiniest nerve fibres of his skin. . .

Calmed and soothed at last, Vassilii lay in bed, hugging the girl's nude body. "So young and so tasty!" "How old are you, Katerina?" "Fifteen," she answered, squirming a little and giggling. “All you men are so powerful and wild, but you almost smothered me!" "All men?" he raged. "What are you - the Sugatin bitch for all the dogs of the army to use? Look out, beautiful! As long as I'm around, don't you dare look at another soldier! 

Remember that, or I'll strangle you, sure enough!"

With a catlike purr the naked Katerina snuggled even closer to her companion, and stroked his head for a moment, then turned it to her and glued her lips to his. But Vassilii's sated body did not respond. Katerina's purring no longer held any lure for him, and his eyes betrayed a certain in- 81

-- difference. The girl sensed this, and instinctively drew her white scarf over herself. She lay quietly and exhausted, aware of a burning pain in her belly feeling the thick, viscid mucus, like the pus of a festering wound, slowly flowing along the inside of her thighs.

A surprise attack by the (German) Wehrmacht along the entire Galician front created a crisis which necessitated the recall of all the furloughed soldiers, abruptly reducing their scheduled rest period from ten to six days. This emergency order concerning leaves arrived at night, and a general alarm was sounded. The soldiers, sleepy and disgruntled, swore and cursed as only soldiers can, at the thought of returning to the front.

Vassilii was unable to rise. He lay on his straw pallet, wearing his overcoat under the blanket, tossing and turning in a hectic fever, that made his teeth chatter. His abdomen was greatly swollen, because of the abnormal distension of his bladder; urination meant excruciating pain, and he was barely able to void a few bloody scalding drops. His organ was so red and inflamed, that any contact with his underwear caused him unbearable distress, and a gummy, green pus trickled from his swollen urethra.

The military surgeon who examined him nodded his head compassionately, and asserted: "You've got the clap, brother - and not just the ordinary kind! You've got a present that you'll remember for the rest of your life. The inflammation is so acute that it has blocked your urinary canal. The infection has spread like wild fire, so that the sores extend to the genitals. We'll have to send you to the nearest hospital to have the sores lanced and their pus drained. It'll be a long, drawn-out affair unusually long-" 

"Excellency!" Vassilii wailed, still feverish, "if I've got the disease that makes a man's body rot away, I'd rather shoot myself —” "No, you won't rot," the doctor reassured him, "but you're likely to be left with a permanent defect that will require the catheterization of your urinary canal from time to time, 82

otherwise you won't be able to pass water. However, brother, I doubt if you'll ever again be a ladies' man "A little bitch like that!" Vassilii gnashed his teeth. "And yet she could infect the whole army! I'll kill her." "You'll remain here until tomorrow," the medical officer commanded. "A transport of casualties is due to leave for 

Kamenko within two days, and I'm assigning you to it. The medical report and all the necessary documents will be prepared at once. You sure got yourself a dose, brother — a mighty nasty clap. On the other hand, you won't see the front for a long time — not for a very long time!"

Vassilii had always taken pride in his strong, well-shaped body. He had always felt an aversion for any weakness and physical defects and had never felt the slightest compassion for the many beggars and cripples who made a display of their deformities and malformations. Whenever he came across a deformed person he felt like spitting in disgust, as though he had been touched by something repulsive and obscene - and there had been an especially strong repugnance to venereal diseases. He was well aware that gonorrhea and syphilis were the filthiest of ailments and could cause the body of the victim to reek and waste away. He had also understood that this sort of infection usually came from prostitutes and had consequently never had any desire for them. They disgusted him, and he always avoided them.

Because of his virile body and good looks, Vassilii had never had to resort to strumpets. He had had no difficulty in attracting the prettiest women - even those in high society. 

For the most part, he had carried on with young married women, whose husbands were either shriveled and bald aristocrats, or elderly potbellied bankers - able to offer their wives money and social status, but unable to gratify their starved womanhood. For these women, the days passed in palaces and mansions, symbolized their wealth and security; the evenings spent at masquerade balls, operas and casinos, meant diversion and social position. Their nights in bed, however, signified for them loneliness, longing, and sexstarvation. And so Vassilii had fared rather well; one or another of these lonely, wistful, and romantic souls was 83

always bound to come his way, and his youthful, robust virility served to satisfy her. 

Now Vassilii tossed on his straw pallet in the barracks, worrying and cursing about how the "plaguiest" of all diseases was gradually ravaging his limbs, wasting and mutilating the joy and pride of his life - his once stalwart physique. He bared his teeth as a wounded tiger bares his fangs and with every nerve and sinew, longed to quench his pain and downfall with the sweetness of revenge.

He called to the caretaker of the barracks, handed him two ten-rouble notes and said: "Now listen, you old devil. I am sick and must have a bottle of hard liquor. I don't know how much you Sugatin scoundrels charge for your rotgut. Anyway, you've got twenty roubles there; get me a bottle and keep the change. Get moving, and remember that I'm not paying for water, but for the real stuff!"

When the old caretaker brought back the bottle and saw what happened to it, all he could do was to take off his shabby forage cap and make the sign of the cross; only the devil could do such a thing! Once Vassilii got the neck of the bottle into his mouth, he started sucking down its contents, as a hungry calf sucks milk from a cow's teat. He did not stop to draw breath until the last drop of vodka was gone, and then he flung the empty bottle away, yelling to the dumbstruck old fellow: "Come here, you old scarecrow, and help me put on my boots!" "Lord have mercy!" the old man pleaded. “Where do you want to go, dear man? You're sick and your belly is full of drink; you'd better stretch out right here and take a good nap.” 

"To the devil with you - get out of my way!" Vassilii brushed him aside, advanced a few steps and stopped. 

He felt a sharp pain in his groin, like that of a raw, open wound, and in order to be able to walk, he had to spread his legs like one who has been in the saddle for days. He felt such contempt for himself that he spat in disgust. The old man tagged along after him at some distance. 

Vassilii reached the narrow, dim hallway, made his way 84

up the rickety staircase, and with one blow of his fist, smashed in the door of the attic-room. His sudden appearance, and his ferocious and thunderous face, stunned Katerina, who stood as if petrified. "You mangy bitch!" Vassilii raged. "The German bullets spared me, but you - you god-damned bitch, you have sentenced me to death! Look, you've turned me into a leper!" 

Before Katerina could say a word, his steel-like fingers tightened in a vise about her throat. Her dark eyes opened wide, and for a fleeting moment, before a final darkness engulfed her, they took in his turbid, death-like stare. The strangler, oblivious to the cracking of the small bones in her neck, maintained his grip, strengthened by his frenzied hatred.

When the old man from the barracks and the people, who came running in response to his cries, succeeded in releasing the girl from Vassilii's grip, she fell prostrate - and dead at his feet.

The chairman of the court-martial, a youthful-looking colonel, turned to the accused: "Vassilii Kravlenko, the Military Tribunal of the Kamenko district has taken into consideration all the facts and circumstances pertaining to your case and after careful deliberation, has found you guilty of murder in the first degree. 

The law allows you the privilege of having a last word before sentence is pronounced. Have you anything to say?” "Your Excellency," Vassilii implored, "Please don't sentence me to Siberia! Send me rather to the front - to the very firing-line, without any relief or furlough for the duration of the war. I'd rather be killed than serve in Siberia!" "I'm sorry, Kravlenko, but I cannot comply with your wish. I want you to know that you have committed the greatest crime against society - murder! And you have to be punished for that. Serving at the front and dying on the field of battle in behalf of the Czar and Fatherland is not punishment - it is rather an honor and privilege! Vassilii 

Kravlenko, it was the unanimous decision of this Military Tribunal that for the murder of the girl, Katerina Budenko, you were to receive a life sentence at hard labor in Siberia. 85

However, having taken into consideration your fine record in fighting at the front, your sentence has been commuted to only twenty years." 

Everything turned black before Vassilii's eyes; he swayed, and almost fainted. The incident with the Sergeant Smirnov, whom he had struck, passed vividly through his mind. Had it not been for the Count of Struga, who had acted as his guardian angel at the time, he would then have been banished and sent off to a chain gang. His eyes swept the room, as though searching for the Count, but he saw instead the two military guards who were nudging him to get started on his long journey to the bleak wastes of Siberia. 

It took four long months for Vassilii to get to the prison in Irkutsk. He had no conception of how vast Russia was, as he crossed it in various locked freight cars. The thousands of miles over steppes and fields and through forests, towns and villages were all alike to him. But the nearer he drew to the Siberian region, the stranger did the inhabitants and their speech appear. He had not realized that so many 

Mongols were a part of the Russian population. Due to the urgent movement of troops, the freight trains carrying prisoners were frequently side-tracked for weeks at a time. Along with the other prisoners Vassilii had to endure hunger, cold, filth, and lice - these were literally swarming over the scratched and bruised bodies of the manacled passengers. 

However, Vassilii was by now indifferent to hunger, to cold, and to filth. Nothing seemed to him as overwhelming and appalling as his venereal disease, and the fact of his exile to Siberia. He yielded to an insidious apathy, neither complaining nor protesting about anything and came to attach little importance to life; there was little in prospect for him, not only for the distant future, but for the very next day. A single thought kept pecking away at his dulled mind: “I am a miserable prisoner, a rotting leper!"

Pure chance jolted Vassilii out of this lethargic and despairing frame of mind. On reaching the Irkutsk prison, the new prisoners were immediately divided into two groups the so-called politicals and the criminals. The prison warden, 86

a stocky Tartar with small brown eyes and high cheekbones, marched by the two rows, scrutinizing the inmates shrewdly and addressing them with his guttural, Mongol accent: "You're in Siberia now! You're here not because of your good deeds, but for your crimes. I'm warning you that I don't play any favorites! A political prisoner who has rebelled against the Czar is no better than the thief who has killed while committing a robbery. I'm warning you forget you ever had a home! No one is ever freed from here, and no one ever escapes. We are surrounded by thousands of miles of tundra, swamps, snowdrifts and virgin forests. If you'll work hard and obey the discipline, you'll get along.
