But if you choose to be lazy and mutinous, I'll not only make your life miserable, but will shorten it for you. Hey, Serebryak, take over!"

On hearing the name of Serebryak, Vassilii was startled from his lethargy. A spark of hope twinkled in the gloom surrounding him: his erstwhile friend and adviser of his gendarme days in St. Petersburg appeared before him. Though in prisoner's garb, the latter was clutching a cat-o-nine-tails. "Follow me!" he bellowed. "To the baths with you! Got to get you deloused, the devil with you!"

Vassilii plodded along his column, still lost in wonder. 

Serebryak spotted him on entering the steam-baths and blurted out: "Vaska, how did you ever get here? I can hardly believe my own eyes! Whom did you hit now? The Colonel, by any chance? And your guardian angel, the Count of Struga didn't he want to save you this time?" "Let me be, Serebryak!” Vassilii pleaded. “I'm in no mood for joking. I've strangled a bitch and got twenty years for it. But you - how did you ever get here?" "Not now, Vaska," the other shook his head. "I've got to delouse this gang of prisoners first; then we'll do some chinning. The warden is my buddy, and he picked me as his assistant. You know I'm very handy with the knout! I'll ask him to assign you to my cell. The devil with it, we'll have a swell time, just as in the old days in St. Petersburg - hey, Vaska?" 87

"Well, Serebryak, let's hear your story," Vassilii asked his newly found, old friend when they were alone at last. 

Serebryak appeared grim; the pockmarks around his pug nose were more pronounced than ever, and his thick lips were twisted in a wry grimace of bitterness and malice as he began: "Do you recall the 'undesirable elements?' One day as I happened to be walking out of headquarters, I saw a tall Jew with a black beard and the usual sidecurls and wearing a long silken gabardine, coming toward me. 'Hold on!' I yelled. 'Where are you heading? Let's see your documents!' He stopped in his tracks, and his devil's eyes went right through me - I actually got the chills. Then he reached into his breastpocket, and took out a leather wallet bulging with hundredrouble notes, and as free and easy as you please, without the least fear, showed me a document permitting him to stay in the capital. 'That's forged!' I yelled at him. 'You're a revolutionary! You're prowling around headquarters in order to spy - come along to the police station, you lousy Jew!' He took the document out of my hand, put it into his breast-pocket still without a trace of fear, and without saying a word, but his eyes seemed to burn me like glowing coals. What sort of a Jew could this be? I'd never yet come across one who didn't try to slip me something while I was hauling him off to the police station. Maybe he was no Jew at all. I thought — maybe he was the devil himself, disguised as a Jew! It was the first time in my life, and the first time since I'd been rounding up 'undesirable elements' in the capital, that a Jew had ever scared the daylights out of me! Actually, it wasn't the 

Jew himself, but his eyes and his steady silence that got me. 'Serebryak,' I said to myself, 'what's happened to you? Have you gone soft, or are you turning into an old woman?' And raising my knout, I commanded him, 'March to the police station, or I'll flay the mangy hide off you!' 

"Just then a carriage-and-four pulled up in front of us, and a general hopped out. I let the knout drop and drew up at attention. But the general, without paying any attention to me, said to the Jew: 'Well, did you enjoy your walk, Mister Gershenzon? You've got an excellent carriage — and those horses are like eagles! It was a pleasure, I assure you! 

88

This is the headquarters, Mister Gershenzon, and we are just in time for our interview with the Count. Shall we go in?' 

And to me he called out: 'Hey, you! Hop in and show the coachman where to wait until Mister Gershenzon comes out!' "At your service, Your Excellency!' I answered and hopped into the carriage. 'Who's that Jew?' I asked the coachman as we started off. "Why, that's Solomon Gershenzon, the "merchant prince" in our district,' the man told me. 'He supplies provisions for the entire Russian Army; he knows the Czar, himself!' 

Then, looking cautiously about him, the coachman whispered: "There's talk of a war, and without provisions, brother, you just can't fight. So we were invited to St. Petersburg, him and me, for a talk with Count Mishkin - they even sent the General after us! We got here only a couple of hours ago and put up at the Grand Hotel. My boss is a strange fellow; he comes out of the hotel and says to me: 'Pavel, it's a beautiful day I feel like taking a walk. You wait here until the General tidies up after his trip. As soon as he comes out, tell him that I am walking over to headquarters and bring him there in the carriage.' Yes, my boss is kind of strange; he's usually quiet, but when he opens his mouth everyone else pipes down, and the biggest squires and generals just listen, nodding their heads and telling him: 'That's so, Mr. Gershenzon; right you are, Mr. Gershenzon!'"' 

“Well, Vaska, I had a feeling that some sort of misfortune was in store for me, and I really got the shivers! I was right, for before I knew it, an officer with two gendarmes dashed out of headquarters after us. When they drew abreast of the coach, the officer rapped out: 'Hey, you - soldier, hand over your revolver and sword; you're under arrest! Gendarmes, take him to the police station, and lock him up until further orders!' "I, Serebryak, the best dog-catcher of the Jewhounds, was being taken into custody by two gendarmes - and on whose account? None other than a lousy Jew's! I was boiling mad; 

I thought I'd blow up at any moment. Anyhow, they threw me into the brig, and after the first couple of minutes I could feel that I was not alone. The cell was quite dark at 89

first, but in a few minutes, when my eyes got used to the darkness, I caught sight of him —”

At this point Serebryak fell silent. His lips were compressed, his nostrils quivered, and he breathed heavily, as he clutched the cat-o'-nine-tails with both hands. "What happened? Why the sudden silence? Who did you see?" Vassilii prompted.

Shaking as if in a fever, and grinding his teeth, Serebryak stared with bloodshot eyes into a corner of the cell, reliving that sight. The vials of his wrath spilled over again as he resumed: "He was squatting in a corner, swaying and mumbling to himself. He, too, had a black beard and ritual sidelocks. 

They all have black beards and sidelocks that shake like devils!"

Serebryak sprang to his feet. His face was flushed crimson and foam had formed at the corners of his mouth. He started to crack his cat-o'-nine-tails like a raving lunatic. "Accursed Jew!" he sputtered. "You mangy bastard! Come on, speak up! Take your devil's eyes off me or I'll skin you alive!" "Stop that, Serebryak!" Vassilii tried to restrain him. "Have you gone out of your mind? Who are you whipping? 

At whom are you yelling?"

Gasping for air and drenched in perspiration, Serebryak desisted at last. His face was grotesquely contorted, and his eyes glazed like a drunkard's. "The gendarmes at the police station came running into the cell, and just as you now, Vaska, they barely managed to pacify me," he went on monstrously, without the least remorse. "That Jew the devil knows who he was - lay there cut to shreds, and dead. I got life in Siberia. 

• . • 

The outbreak of the revolution and the overthrow of the Czar rocked the nation like an earthquake. The foundation of the empire caved in overnight, and a new blazing fire was ignited in the hearts of a hundred-and-eighty-five million 

Russians a fire of freedom, equality, and brotherhood. . . .

In the Irkutsk prison, the news of the revolution caused a - - 90

minor revolt of its own: the political prisoners seized power without too much trouble; the wardens and the guards were disarmed; the knouts of the trustees and the iron bars of the cells were broken; and the red flag was hoisted over the observation tower.

Sasha Belov, a political prisoner, slim and of slight build, with a shock of blonde hair, and a pince-nez bestriding his hooknose, was exuberantly lifted to their shoulders by two fellow-prisoners. 

"Comrades," he began his harangue, spreading his arms like an eagle, "the chains of centuries-old tyranny have been broken at last! The Czarist regime, along with all its myrmidons, has been overthrown and put to flight. The blood spilled by the fighters for freedom has been duly rewarded we are free! All of us now belong to one great brotherhood. 

The revolution has granted full amnesty to all the condemned in the prisons of Czarist persecution. We are free, Comrades! The Revolution now stands in need of those who have suffered the most from the Czarist despots. It is our duty, Comrades, to rally round the red flag, to join the ranks of the revolutionary cadres. It is our moral duty, now, to see to it that the Czar and his Imperial flunkeys shall never regain their former autocratic power. Long live freedom, equality, and fraternity!" "Serebryak," Vassilii remarked, "I'm heart and soul in favor of this freedom to return from this cursed exile and to go without fear wherever I wish, but I don't like the way the

Revolution is disposing of the Czar and the landowners. I must get to Petrograd, by hook or by crook. I'll pump the bastards full of lead if they try to lay a hand on the Count of Struga!" "Vaska, it doesn't sound believable — this is all a dream!" the other chimed in. "Free again! I'm going with you, Vaska. 

Give me your hand, the devil with you! I'm in this with you, come what may!"

If transportation had been poor before the Revolution, it had now deteriorated to such an extent, as to be virtually chaotic. Tens of thousands of soldiers, without any leadership or discipline, as well as thousands of amnestied prisoners 91

released from the country's penal institutions, lay sprawled out on the bare ground around the numerous railroad stations, waiting for days and sometimes weeks, in the hope of boarding some passing train or even of clinging to the outside of one of its cars. Each car was literally packed to the ceiling with the passengers having to stand up for days on end. The roofs and outside steps of the cars were black with human cargo, like piles of manure swarming with flies on a hot summer day.

Vassilii and Serebryak were traveling in just such a car, indifferent to the attendant discomfort, as long as they kept moving away from Siberia and closer to their goal, which was 

Petrograd. One night, Vasilii started moaning and cursing: "I'm in bad shape again, Serebryak - I can't pass my water! 

God-damn all the bitches! I can't stand it any longer, Serebryak! Do me a favor and send a bullet through my head!

I'll be troubled with this all my stinking life. The damned thing ought to be cut off!" "Cut out that fool talk, Vaska!" Serebryak answered brusquely. "Just be a little patient. As soon as we get to the next station, we'll get off and look around for an infirmary or a country doctor, and you'll have your water drained off. 

This isn't your first time, Vaska, nor will it be the last you know that. Just try to bear it, brother!"

At two in the morning the train ground to a stop at last. 

Vassilii and Serebryak got off and learned that the local station was that of the town of Bikov. They began walking through the quiet, deserted streets. Now and then they caught the restless barking of a dog, or snatches of drunken singing somewhere far off. After every few steps, Vassilii halted and in agonizing sweat, managed to void a few painful drops. "Serebryak, I'm bursting - I can't stand it any longer!" he pleaded, half-crying at one point. "Shoot me, you son-ofa-bitch!"

There's a church over there, Vaska," Serebryak encouraged him. "The house alongside it must be the priest's. We won't budge from there," he promised, "until we get you some help."

Serebryak had to pound on the door for quite a while 92

before an old priest, wearing a long robe and soft slippers, and carrying a lamp, let them in. "What do you want, lads?" their host inquired. "We can offer you only black bread and a few apples. Other soldiers got here ahead of you and have eaten up everything. It's a pity. They're hungry - they're all hungry.” "It's not food we're after, Little Father," Serebryak spoke up. "My friend needs help - he's very sick. His bladder is blocked, and he can't urinate. His canal's got to be stretched and his water drained. He's bursting, Little Father - please help him!"

Vassilii prostrated himself before the priest, screaming. "Help me, Little Father - I can't stand it any longer! 

Help me or shoot me down!" 

The parish priest became excited and looking about him, bolted the door, set the lamp on the floor, and whispered: "I'll be right back. There are several officers of high rank hiding below, in the cellar. The Provisional Government had them arrested for resisting the Revolution, but they got away. They came to God's house, seeking sanctuary - God's house offers shelter to all the persecuted. I heard one of them addressed as 'doctor'; he must be a military surgeon. I'll bring him up."

The old priest went down the dark stairs and returned shortly, accompanied by a middle-aged man with a black goatee, who was dressed in an officer's uniform (without any epaulettes, however). The latter carried a small, leather case. "Well, brother, stretch out - flat on your back," he told

Vassilii, “and unbutton your trousers. Lie still — it will take only a few minutes and you'll get relief.”

From his case he extracted a thin rubber tube and several metal bougies of graduated sizes, bent at one end, and observed: "With these instruments, we can open and dilate your canal. We'll start with the thinnest and then follow up with the thicker ones, and when your canal gets distended enough to admit this rubber tube, the 'catheter,' then we'll draw off all the stopped-up water. I know it will hurt like all hell, my friend; however, you'll have to be game to the last. Kindly close the cellar-door, Father, otherwise the 93

commotion up here is likely to disturb His Highness, the Count of Struga. I've administered a sedative to him, and he has just dozed off."

The mention of the Count's name acted like magic on Vassilii. He suddenly forgot the excruciating pain and jumped as if he had been bitten by a snake. With eyes blazing, he seized the doctor's hands and shouted: "The Count of Struga, did you say? Where is he?" "Do you know the Count?" the amazed surgeon asked. "Who are you? And who's your friend? Are you the plainclothes police of the Provisional Government, by any chance?” "I'm Vassilii Kravlenko - one-time Chief of Police of Struga, and the Count's most loyal dog. Where is he?" Vassilii in his excitement shook the doctor's hand. "He's in the cellar. We are all on his staff," the doctor explained.

Vassilii slumped to the floor again, spread his legs, and called out jovially: "Get to work, doctor! Now you can even shove a telegraph pole in there — you won't hear a peep out of me! Serebryak, we've found him to hell with Petrograd! Bikov is my capital!"

The doctor shoved the metal sounds in succession into Vassilii's urethra, dilated it, and drew off a full pot of urine. 

He then gave him six tablets of urotropine, remarking: "Take a tablet in a glass of water every four hours. It will reduce the inflammation and lessen your pain in urinating. You'll have to dilate your canal as long as you live. Don't neglect it too long; attend to it every three months regularly, and you'll avoid getting your bladder blocked up. Here are several bougies for you and the tube. Keep them dry and sanitary- and guard them like a treasure! 

No matter where you are, as long as you have these instruments, even a hospital orderly will be able to help you. Now, Kravlenko, tell me how you got here."

Vassilii relaxed slightly, as he arranged his clothing. "Serebryak and I were doing life-sentences in Siberia: I, for strangling the little bitch who gave me the clap, and he for doing in a Jew. The revolution set us free and we've been 94

tramping for months, making our way to Petrograd – we were, in fact, seeking to be near His Highness." 

Vassilii went down to the cellar. The Count, tired and pale, was seated there on a crate of church-candles and was wrapped in a general's fur-lined overcoat, with his hands thrust into the sleeves as if in a muff. Prostrating himself before the Count, Vassilii proceeded to kiss the latter's hidden hands. Then he said: "My radiant master! I thank the Lord for having found you. We will never leave you again. Wherever you'll go, we'll go too!" 

"Kravlenko, you at least have always been loyal," the 

Count said warmly. "Times have changed - yes, how they have changed! When the revolution broke out, General

Kornilov was Commander-in-Chief of all our armies. He was most anxious to restore order and discipline, but the Provisional Government ordered his arrest for that - and arrested me and my staff along with him. We had a narrow escape, but thank heaven we are here. Now we have to make our way to Rostov-on-the-Don. General Denikin is organizing a new army of volunteers there a force that will fight for the honor and the restoration of the crown, which will be placed again on the head of the dethroned Czar. It is rumored that England and France are willing to support Denikin and his volunteers, in order to give Russia a breathing-spell. All of us must get to Rostov-on-the-Don as quickly as we can, for Russia and the Czar!”

The group in the cellar swore allegiance to the Czar and to the Count, and before long, succeeded in joining the armies of General Denikin. 

For eighteen months on end, Denikin's White Army had gone from victory to victory, in the civil war against the Red 

Army of the Bolsheviks — under the leadership of Leon Trotsky. The insignificant force of nine thousand volunteers, with which General Denikin had launched his campaign, had grown into a formidable army of a hundred and fifty thousand; he had captured such important centers as Kharkov, Poltava, Odessa, Kiev and Oriol. The greater part of 

Southern Russia was under his domination. But just when his goal of crushing the Bolsheviks and restoring the old 95

regime seemed about to be realized, the Red Army redoubled its resistance on an extensive front around the city of Kupyansk, beating down the repeated onslaughts of Denikin's men and bringing about a lull in the fighting. 

Count Mishkin was in command of the right wing at Kupyansk. During the last six weeks war of attrition, he had attacked four times, only to be thrown back each time. The left wing had fared no better. The situation became critical, and the local commanders sensed, just as Denikin had, that the decisive battle would be fought in the immediate vicinity of Kupyansk. Several days later, the Bolshevik General, Budionny, and his cavalry, succeeded in breaking through the front at Kupyansk, where they had been skirmishing up to that time. The Whites were routed and fell back in disorder. 

Count Mishkin, deeply concerned, was bending over a map of southern Russia, running his index finger up and down over it, sighing from time to time, and nodding his head ruefully. "Come here, Kravlenko!" he called out, and Kravlenko came running. "I wish to talk to you about a very serious matter. Look at the map. Our men are now in retreat from 

Kupyansk - there it is on the map. From Kupyansk I draw a line to this point, which is Odessa. The estuary of the 

Dniester is in Odessa. Do you see the blue line? That's the 

Dniester. Whoever follows that blue line from the south to the north will arrive at approximately this point and will find himself in Struga - "Are we heading for home, Your Highness?" Vassilii asked. "Don't be an idiot!" the Count retorted. "How can you go home, when the territory is occupied partly by the 

Bolsheviks and partly by the Ukrainians?” "I beg your pardon, Your Excellency," Vassilii apologized, "but I don't understand why you point the road home to me, when one can't get there —” "Kravlenko, the situation is an unfavorable one," the 

Count interrupted. "The Bolsheviks are no longer the shabby, barefooted tramps they were eighteen months ago. They have introduced an iron discipline; they have enough weapons; and their soldiers go into battle strengthened by a determina- 96

tion to win, as though fighting for something worthwhile. . . 

But with us it's the other way around. True, the epaulettes have been restored, but discipline is still lacking. Our soldiers haven't the will to fight; they enlist only to pillage and massacre. The moment they enter a town or village, they loot it and victimize the entire population. This is no army it's an enormous band of highwaymen!" "Your Excellency, soldiers must have some sport!" Vassilii protested. "War can become a boring affair, so the men have a little fun. Christians are not molested; and there's no need to pity the Jews, since they are to blame for all our troubles. 

They are the ones who brought on the revolution! Why, their Commander-in-Chief, Leon Trotsky, is a Jew!" "That's not the point, Kravlenko!" the Count argued. "I don't begrudge our men a little fun at the expense of the 

Jews - Russian soldiers have always gone in for that. What

I am concerned with is that our fighters can hardly be called an army. It's as though our men have no one to serve and no one for whom they would be willing to sacrifice their lives on the battlefield."

Vassilii "What do you mean, there's 'no one to serve?' complained. "There's the Czar, Your Excellency!" "Kravlenko, the Bolsheviks have shot the Czar and his entire family," the Count remarked unhappily. "The sad news was brought to us last night by a special courier. This has been kept secret, for such news could demoralize the army even more. Now listen carefully, Kravlenko. The divisions under my command are retreating in the direction of Odessa. There I have two alternatives: I can board a foreign ship headed for England or France, or I can follow this blue line of the Dniester back to Struga. I'd rather do that.

In the quiet of my estates, we'd be able to weather the storm until things settle down-" "You're right, Your Excellency - about the safety of your estates in Struga," Vassilii parroted him. "The mansion, the 

Carpathian Mountains, the Dniester, the pine woods, the peasants - and home! Oh, it'll be good to get back home!" "Kravlenko, the Rumanians have occupied Bessarabia," the Count continued, "and this means that the entire area 97

on the other side of the Dniester is in their possession. But to set out on our side of the Dniester is most dangerous we might be captured by the Bolsheviks. So we'll follow the 

Rumanian bank of the river. Once at Odessa, we can cross the Dniester, and if we make it worthwhile for the Rumanians, we can get safe-conduct from them as far as the Verbivka Forest. At that point, we can wait till the middle of the night and then row across to our own bank. We will be protected by the forests and close to home." "Right you are, Your Excellency!" Vassilii nodded approvingly. "Close to home - very close to home." "You and Serebryak will organize a unit of several dozen loyal, brave fellows," the Count went on. "You'll make your headquarters in the Verbivka Forest, and you will communicate every night with me, at my hideout in the mansion. 

Everything must be carried out cautiously and secretly." "I am appointing you hetman," the Count added. "It will be your duty to guard Struga and the mansion against any and all attacks. You and your men will constitute the secret armed force around Struga." "Your Excellency, what can I offer the fellows who join my contingent?" Vassilii asked. "Each will get a fine saddled horse, firearms, ammunition and three hundred roubles a month," the Count answered. "And how about a little fun, Your Excellency? When the chance turns up a party of Jewish travelers, say — will my men be allowed to levy a little contribution?" 

The Count fell silent, but his upper lip was twitching, a sign of tacit consent.

Business at the White Inn suddenly slackened. Shmul 

Verbivker, the innkeeper, and his wife, Baila, were greatly worried. The Verbivka Forest teemed with bands of brigands who waylaid travelers continuously, so that only a few ventured in that direction. Every day brought further news of sporadic plundering and killing in the area. In the face of the increasing tension and fear, the Jewish couple were at a loss as what to do next. Though they were afraid to stay, 98

they hesitated to pick up the wanderer's staff and leave. 

Their decision was made for them the day an armed rider drove up to the Inn and without dismounting, cried out: "Hey! who's the proprietor here?" 

Shmul came out, and trembling with fear, answered: “I am. . . Anything I can do for you?"

Even the horseman was surprised at Shmul's appearance at the Inn. For the past few weeks, the armed band "operating" in the Verbivka Forest, under the leadership of Hetman Kravlenko, had been terrorizing the travelers and the neighboring farms and villages. A pall of fear hung over the entire area, so that people steered clear of it. The Jews, especially, were petrified, since the new hetman considered the raids on them not only a necessary diversion for his brave lads, but an almost obligatory carrying-out of the usual order of the day. Jews rarely ventured into the vicinity of the woods; they fled the farms and villages, joining the overcrowded Jewish communities in the cities. This, they knew, was not much of a safeguard in itself; however, they believed that being among one's own people in troubled times gave one a moral lift and some encouragement. Whatever was in store for the community would also be his lot, each Jew felt. Consequently, Shmul's clinging to the White Inn astonished the cavalryman. He regarded this Jew as intrepid. "What's your name?" he asked. "Shmul Verbivker," the innkeeper answered. "I'm the owner of the White Inn. Anything I can do for you?" "You are a brave Jew, and I don't want to harm you,” the rider commented. "But I'll give you a piece of advice: get out of here as fast as you can. The hetman is due here any minute. I was sent merely to reconnoiter. I know that he and his adjutant, Serebryak, won't spare you. So get going, Jew!"

Not long afterward, Vassilii and Serebryak dismounted and entered the Inn. There they found everything in order tables and benches, bottles and glasses, even the kegs of cider and wine. Vassilii kicked in the door of Shmul's bedroom - there, too, everything seemed in order: the beds were properly arranged; the clothes-closet, in order; the chest, 99

neatly closed on stacks of laundered underwear and tablecloths; there were gleaming white spangled curtains at the two small windows. "Hey, Fedka!" Kravlenko called to his scout, "Where is that anti-Christ, Shmul?" "I don't know, Hetman, I found the Inn as you see it, without a living soul in it." "It's a shame, Serebryak - a great shame," Kravlenko mourned. “I was about to hand him over to you. You could have given him the quietus with your cat-o'-nine-tails — and no one would have sentenced you to Siberia for that!" 

"Vaska, where are we? How safe are we here?” Serebryak asked nervously. "No need to be afraid, Serebryak. I know every inch of this territory. This is the White Inn - it belongs to that little Jew, Shmul. It's quite likely that he and his old woman have got away and won't dare to come back. From now on we are masters here! We'll spend our days in the forests; the Inn will be bolted shut. But at night we'll make this our headquarters.” "Vaska, when are you taking me to Struga? You've been promising me to take me there for the last six weeks, but every night, you go alone. What in the hell kind of friendship is that?" "It's not my fault, Serebryak; those were His Excellency's orders." "To hell with your Count! You and I made a pact: wherever you went, I was to follow." "Serebryak, the power is now in the hands of the Ukrainians; they have driven the Bolsheviks out of the entire Dniester region," Vassilii explained. "One of their commanders is on friendly terms with the Count, and the two are negotiating for our contingent to merge with the Ukrainian Army. If this should take place, all of us will march into 

Struga, and won't have to be in hiding anymore." "Vaska, why did you keep quiet about all this until now? 

Let's shake hands, you son-of-a-bitch. Wenches of Struga, we're coming! Vaska, save ten girls for me, the devil with you!" Serebryak was vastly more cheerful now. 100

"Shut your trap, Serebryak," Vassilii scowled. "Girls don't mean anything to me any longer — and you know it, you bastard!"

They heard the sound of wagon wheels and opened the door in time to see a great carriage roll into the yard of the Inn. Serebryak, not anxious for strange company, dashed out and roared at the coachman who wheeled the coach around and raced to the edge of the thicket. "Whom were you talking to?" Vassilii asked, when Serebryak entered. 

"The devil knows some sort of squire; I hardly saw his face. I just yelled at the coachman, and he scuttled off like a scared rabbit."

Somewhat reassured, Vassilii narrowed his eyes as though gazing into the future. “When I bring my fellows to Struga," he said to Serebryak, "I'll let you take care of the girls. As for me, brother - there's a special sort of treat waiting for me: Shanan and his bastard!" "Avrum, my dear friend! Praised be the Lord for bringing you here in good health!" Reb Itzikel welcomed his beloved follower, as Avrum Shanan was ushered into the Rabbi's private study on his arrival from Struga. "Rabbi, I have been in great danger throughout my journey," Avrum informed him. “The roads are no longer safe to travel, and there are bands of cut-throats lurking in the woods. But what worried me most was the thought that something had happened to David. Your urgent note for me to take the boy back has upset me. What has happened, Rabbi?" "Nothing too terrible has happened to David so far, but some danger is impending," Reb Itzikel answered, “and I wish to get him out of its way. I am very fond of him, and I want to save him before it is too late." "Pardon me, Rabbi, but I don't understand you. What has happened, Rabbi?" Avrum asked anxiously.

Reb Itzikel nodded his head sadly and intoned a passage from the Song of Songs: 

101

They made me keeper of the vineyards; but mine own vineyard have I not kept. "God knows how devoted I have been to Him and with what love I have planted the seeds of the Holy Torah in the hearts of our people, especially of our children. I have always asked the Lord to grant me an alert mind and a stout heart, in order to exert the proper influence on our people in general - yet I have failed in this mission, with my own son. 99 "Rabbi, be merciful!" Avrum pleaded. "Please open my eyes, so that I may see the light. What has happened?" "I did not know," the Rabbi said sadly, "nor did I even suspect that the fire of the Torah was merely smoldering, instead of burning as a bright flame, in my own son. It was only after the upheavals and the revolution had shaken the foundation of the world, that I realized that he had been drawn into the new whirlpool, and that the light of Torah was gradually dimming within him. Yes, my dear friend, Yosef has become a revolutionist, and he exerts great influence over David. Rescue your child, Avrum! Take him back to Struga, where you may keep an eye on him, and guard him from this peril. Devote some part of each day with him to the study of Holy Writ and teach him traditional Judaism. Shield him until the turmoil of the nations subsides, and the gathering clouds dissipate, and men once more come to recognize the eternal truth of God's ways."

Avrum sat stunned. The last thing he would have expected as likely to happen to David was that he should be a revolutionist. "I beg your pardon, Rabbi," he said brokenly, "but why do you assume that Yosef has a greater influence on David than you have to say nothing of the Yeshivah, the Torah, his respect for me and for his traditional Jewish upbringing - all that has influenced him since his infancy? As far as 

Yosef is concerned, it is incredible that such a boy should suddenly renounce his heritage and the basic tenets of Judaism, in favor of revolutionary slogans! It could be that he is 102

only demonstrating a youth's momentary enthusiasm for something new."

Reb Itzikel rose, opened a side door leading to another room, and called out, “David! Come and greet your father."

As David entered, Avrum was elated at the sight of his son, whose resemblance to his late mother was now more striking than ever. The lad had grown up; he seemed older, stronger and more serious; his dark eyes had become more penetrating, and his upper lip and cheeks showed a dark silken down. Father and son felt drawn to embrace each other, but masculine pride deterred them. They shook hands, as befits mature men, and their eyes alone acknowledged their mutual love. 

"David, it is the wish of the Rabbi may he stay wellthat you return to Struga and remain at home until, with God's help, things have settled down," Avrum told his son. 

David looked down silently, unable to gauge his bearings. 

His once-beloved village of Struga, for which he felt such nostalgia and to which he had for years yearned to return, was now, when his dreams were about to be realized, leaving him sad and indifferent. Why? Was it because the true worth of his cherished birthplace had vanished with the death of his mother? Or was it that the six years since his departure had made a gap that could not be bridged? Or was his apathy due to the deep roots he had planted in Polipovka – which now made it just as difficult to break away from its moorings, as previously, from Struga? Could this reluctance to leave perhaps be laid to his affection for Miriam, to her caresses and tenderness? He turned all these questions over in his mind, but try as he would, he could not truly account for his indifference to returning home. In addition, he felt a strange premonition - which he could not clarify. He was most anxious for his father to remain with him in Polipovka, rather than for them both to return to Struga. "Rabbi," David turned to Reb Itzikel, "every day we recite the passage, 'The whole earth is full of His glory,' so why must I wait in Struga for better times? Wherein is the merit of Polipovka smaller than that of Struga? After all, 103

God - praised be His Name! - is the same throughout the world; can anyone escape Him?”

Avrum's eyes filled with tears of pride and joy, and he was most eager to hear the Rabbi's response. “David, my son, you are right," the Rabbi replied, “and it is a delight to hear such words. True, one cannot escape 

God. But sometimes one has to run not from, but toward 

God. There was a time-only six years ago - when your father took you away from home and brought you here, so that you could devote yourself to the study of the Torah and of the ways of God. Now, my son, I am urging you to go back, so you can be removed and isolated from the great danger that hangs over us a danger that may profane our 

Torah and subvert the ways of God. . ." 

The side door flew open, and Yosef, highly agitated, dashed into the Rabbi's study and blurted out: "Father, a peasant has just brought in the dead bodies of Shmul Verbivker and his wife! Both were found murdered along the road to the Verbivka Forest. They must have gotten away from the White Inn, looking for safety, and fallen into the hands of the cut-throats, who are carrying on in their bloody work unhindered and unpunished — and they'll keep on, until they are stopped!" "And how my son, do you propose to stop these forest brigands?" "Certainly not by reciting Psalms, Father!" Yosef replied, "nor by warming the benches of the Yeshivah - but by armed resistance!" "My son, more power is to be found in Psalms and in warming the benches in the Yeshivah, than in arms," the 

Rabbi answered gently. "With arms, one competes in murder; whereas with Psalms and Torah, one brings soulfulness, amity and the light of truth. Had it not been for the Psalms and the Yeshivah benches, we would have perished long ago. 

With arms alone, we could never have survived!" "These are not thoughts of enlightenment, but of darkness, Father," his son argued. "To permit one's self to be slaughtered like a sheep and to call it 'sanctification of the 

Name' is not sanctity, but cowardice!" 104

Reb Itzikel gripped the edge of his desk to steady himself. 

He picked up a newspaper clipping and turned to the young firebrand. "Yosef, my son, this item reports that three hundred revolutionaries, refusing to surrender to the thousands of Ukrainians who surrounded them, permitted themselves to be slaughtered like sheep, to the very last one, as they shouted, 'Long live the Revolution!' Do you know what comment your leaders Lenin and Trotsky made concerning them? I will read it for you. "The three hundred cherished our ideal far more than their own lives and have sacrificed themselves for the revolution. That sacrifice, we shall never forget!' "Now, my son, I wish to ask you in what way your three hundred victims, who sacrificed themselves for the revolution, are lesser cowards than the millions of Jewish victims, who have sacrificed themselves through the millenia, for the lofty ideal of Torah - the greatest vision of all, whether present, past or future? Are the Jewish victims cowards, because we call their acts 'sanctification of the Name,' and are the other victims heroes, because you choose to label their act as 'the revolution?'' 

99 "Not at all, Father the difference is not in words, but in deeds!" his son retorted hotly. "You wish the mourner's prayer and the 'Praised be the true Judge!' phrase to be said at the funeral of the murdered Shmul Verbivker and his wife. But instead of indulging in tears and lamentations and seeking consolation in blessings at the graves of the murdered, I prefer taking an oath to avenge their innocent bloodand not a hundred years later, or in the hereafter, but right here and now! The revolution carries the banner of freedom, for all people who have been persecuted and outraged by the Czarist tyrants throughout the centuries a freedom that is the same for all, whether Jews, Gentiles or Tartars!” 

"Yosef, my son, you need hardly be reminded of the cardinal tenets of Jewishness. Jewishness is Torah! The essence of Torah is the essence of our life! It goes without saying, that in essence we are affluent, even though we live in penury, because the Torah nourishes us spiritually to such a degree, that our physical needs pale into insignificance. Do 105

you realize, my son, that thanks to the Torah, we are the only nation in the world that has no illiterates? Because the poorest of our poor considers it his primary duty to send his child to the cheder (Primary School) to study the Torah. The ideals of freedom and fraternity are not new to us. We have been teaching them to the world for thousands of years. 

The first commandment of the Torah starts with the ideal of freedom: 'I am the Lord your God, Who brought you out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage.' The ideal of brotherhood is one of the fundamental pillars of the 

Torah: "Thou shalt not take vengeance nor bear any grudge against the children of thy people, but thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself.' Yes, my son, our destiny is closely linked to the Torah. He who digresses from it is like a storm-tossed leaf disunited from its stem."

The discussion between the Rabbi and his son was suddenly interrupted. David's voice resounded unexpectedly: “Rabbi, I am no longer in doubt - I'm going back to 

Struga with my father!" His firm decision, and the resolute tone in which he declared it, deeply impressed the Rabbi and Avrum. They both felt that David had, with these words, decided the outcome of the idealogical conflict between Reb Itzikel and his son. The Rabbi had won. . . . 

David returned to his room and started packing. Yosef came in and watched him quietly for a while: “David, we are at the parting of the ways," he said at last. "We are drifting apart. I am emerging from a dark cave into the broad daylight, while you are seeking the darkness of the cave, like a bat that dreads the light." 

"Struga is my home, the home of my parents and ancestors,' 

David countered. "The bright light in our home is the light of Torah and that has never gone out. My father's chanting, as he studied the Gemarra, was always to be heard in our home, and now I will join him in that study, in the peace and quiet of our village." "Peace and quiet!" Yosef taunted him. "Your father's humiliating dance, in order to save your life from the drunken mob in Struga - was that 'peace and quiet?' When Marushka committed suicide, but that same mob accused your father 106

-- of murdering her, and wanted to massacre all the Jews of Struga on a trumped-up blood-ritual charge — was that 'peace and quiet?' Or is it perhaps to be found in the slaying of 

Shmul Verbivker and his wife?"

David made no reply but continued packing. Yosef, calmer now, walked over and embraced his friend. "Forgive me, David," he said, "it's my restlessness that makes me talk like this. The future alone will tell which one of us has chosen the right road. . A happy journey, my friend."

David and Miriam sat quietly for a long time in their trysting place, holding hands. Miriam turned and gazed lovingly into his eyes, then placed her head on his knees. 

He stroked her thick, long blonde hair and caressed her white throat, which led so magically to contact with her full, throbbing breasts. "Miriam, darling - my beautiful, good Miriam,” David whispered. "I'll be on my way to Struga long before sunrise.

I'm so sad and downhearted because you're remaining here - and I can't go on without you! If I could only have you with me, always! I remember six years ago, when I was leaving my mother in Struga. It felt so lonely then too, and I spent that night in her bed, and she hugged and kissed me. 

Miriam was suddenly seized with an overwhelming feeling of jealousy towards David's dead mother. Her blood pounded in stormy waves, and she was overcome by a wild desire to reclaim David from that other woman forever. She stretched out on the damp earth, pulling David towards her with both hands. She was trembling and burning with an overmastering gush of youthful, female succulence. Pressing his head against her bared breasts, she murmured as though in delirium: "David, I'm yours - yours forever, David. Oh, how I'll miss you! I'll be longing and waiting for you until you come back to me!”

David was in a transport of delight. For the first time, his awakened manhood was subjected to the supreme temptation and the law of nature triumphed Miriam ex- 

perienced a fleeting, sharp pain, only to be rewarded a mo- 107

ment after with waves of sweet intoxication and blissful oblivion. . .

As the carriage drew near the outskirts of Struga, David's tension relaxed a little. The Carpathian Mountains towered against the horizon. Here and there, between meadows and thickets, one caught sight of the glittering waters of the meandering Dniester River. David broke the silence that had prevailed until then, and exclaimed jovially: "Father, I can already sense the nearness of home. What hidden power is there in this place that makes it so invigorating and refreshing?" "My son, you were born and raised in Struga. Just as one feels a longing for one's childhood days, so one longs for the spot where those days were spent." "Father, it isn't just because I was born there - Struga is such a wonderful place! I remember having once told mother, 'Struga is the best and prettiest spot in the whole world, and in the whole world, you are the best and prettiest mother! I shall always be with you, and I shall always remain in Struga." "Yes, my son, people go dreaming, planning, aspiring. as the saying goes, 'man proposes, but God disposes.' Your mother was so young when she died. . . And you — you are already a grown-up, and dreamy-eyed; it was all I could do to persuade you to come back with me. The restlessness of the world has made inroads even into Struga, and its old calm and security have changed a lot. As a matter of precaution, I've sent your sisters to your aunt in Slobodka." "You sent Dvorah and Rachel away?" David voiced his extreme surprise. "As a matter of precaution? In what way is Struga less secure for my sisters than for the two of us?” "For girls, the danger is far greater than it is for men. 

You're no longer a child and must understand that girls can be violated by the bandits."

His own behavior with Miriam in the Newlyweds' Home during their farewell flashed across David's mind. Had that, too, been violation? If that were so, he was probably no better than any of the so-called irresponsible, lawless elements. He 108

was suddenly consumed by a longing for Miriam. A vague sense of guilt and regret coursed through his being. When the general turmoil would subside, he promised himself, he would return to Polipovka and fall at her feet; he would beg her forgiveness - and never again part from her. . . "Why are you silent again, my son?" his father asked. "What thoughts made you so sad?" "Father, how old were you when you married mother?" 

David asked.

So this was the reason for his son's pensiveness and moodiness. Yes, Avrum reflected, how foolish parents can be! They fail to see when their offspring cease to be children and become part of the stream of life. 

"I was eighteen," the father replied, "and your mother may she rest in peace was two years younger than I. However, we were betrothed long before that; I was only ten and your mother eight, when her parents and mine made the match. Our marriage took place when I had turned eighteen the traditional age for that 'step.'' 

David listened attentively. He was most anxious to ask his father whether he and Reb Itzikel had ever planned a similar betrothal for their children, but lacked the courage to do so. Through his breathless expectancy, however, David's wish communicated itself to his father, and the latter said, somewhat haltingly: "With God's help, when things quieten down, I will arrange with Reb Itzikel for your betrothal to Miriam." 

David's face turned crimson. His heart pounded, and he lowered his head, overwhelmed by a tremendous joy and a feeling of inner surcease. His father, too, was visibly moved; he cleared his throat, tears glistening in his eyes. The bond of mutual understanding between father and son became stronger than ever.

The horses were running at a round pace that made the carriage sway rhythmically. The sun shed its lavish light and warmth, and the serene blue sky was not marred by a single cloud. Peasants were weeding the fields, bending over the waving, green stalks of grain. Those, who caught sight of the carriage, bowed in deference to their eminent townsman. 109

"Where are you off to?" Avrum questioned his son goodnaturedly. "We've just arrived after such a long journey - why don't you rest up a bit in your room? Aren't you anxious to look the house over after so many years?" 

"I've already had a quick look – everything is as it was. 

I'll take a walk to the Dniester, Father, and come right back." "Be careful, my son," his father cautioned. "Since the

Rumanians captured Bessarabia, they have become masters on the other side of the Dniester. You can't go too close to the river - their patrols are always on the look-out and threaten to shoot those whom they suspect of wanting to cross over to their side. Struga is now on the frontier of a foreign power." "Father, Samborik's brother, Andrey, lives just across the river, but his fields are on this side, and he used to come over every day — in a small boat during the summer and on foot across the ice in the winter to work with Samborik. 

How does he manage things nowadays? Doesn't he come over any more?" 

"Andrey is an exception," Avrum explained. "He has made friends with the Rumanian patrols, and they are conveniently blind. He crosses over every Monday at sunrise, works here through the week and slips back at dusk on Saturday. People in Struga have become used to this arrangement, and no one makes any fuss about it." 

David trudged down the valley to his favorite spot on the riverbank. He sauntered through the orchards, all of whose paths and fences had been familiar to him since childhood. 

These had not changed in the least during the years he had been away. But he was overtaken by a chill melancholy; he missed the riotous singing and chatter of the peasant women, as they washed and pounded their course linen in the serene river; he missed, too, the hubbub around the heavily loaded barges and the sight of the patient peasants fishing with their sprawling nets. Today, on the other side of the river, a 

Rumanian sentry, armed with rifle and bayonet, was pacing back and forth. At every step his bayonet, reflecting the glare of the sun on the water, glittered brightly — as though flashing across a stern warning. 

110

David entered the cave under the huge boulder, sat down at the edge of the well and riveted his eyes upon the surface of its translucent water. Various episodes of his childhood years in Struga flitted across his mind. As though in a trance, he saw a strange sight reflected at the bottom of the well - Marushka's body lying there motionless, while his father performed a rapturous, Hasidic dance around it. . . 

The touch of a hand on his shoulder roused him from his reverie. Startled, he turned his head and saw Samborik. The peasant's shock of once fair hair, was now nearly white, and two deep furrows flanked his nose - but his eyes had changed little; they still revealed the same goodness, devotion, and honesty. David sprang up and embraced him. "Davidka, my dove!" Samborik greeted him. "How you've grown! Step back a little - let me have a good look at you. 

A real man! Yes, yes - it's my Davidka! I haven't seen you in all of six years. I heard your father had brought you home, so I dropped everything and ran to see my Davidka. 'Where is my dove?' I asked your father. 'He's gone down to his beloved river' he told me with a smile. "The river is merely an excuse' I said to myself. I could have bet I would find you sitting at the well under the boulder. You haven't forgotten 

Marushka, eh? No, my dove - you and I will never forget Marushka.. 99 Samborik cleared his throat loudly, and dabbing at his eyes with his work-weary hands, he apologized: "I've become an old fool! The least thing, and I turn on the tears like an old woman! There, it would be better if you told me what you learned in the big city. You must be a rabbi, by now - eh?" "No, Samborik, I'm not a rabbi, and I don't want to be one." "Well, of course, you don't have to be a rabbi," Samborik hastened to say. “You'll be a businessman — an honest, godly man and a great businessman like your father — eh?” 

"No, Samborik, I don't wish to be a businessman either.”

I want to be a doctor; I want to heal sick people, to bring them relief in their misery." "A doctor, did you say, my dove?" Samborik was surprised. "And what does your good father have to say to that?” 111

"My father doesn't know it yet; only you, Samborik, and my mother-" David answered diffidently and quietly. "Your mother?" Samborik wondered. "Your mind must be wandering, my dove. Your mother's been dead these past three years - may she rest in heaven!" "When I grow up, I'm going to heal the sick," David insisted. "That's what I told my mother on the night I left her to go to Polipovka. And ever since then, whenever 

I think of my future, I can visualize myself only as a doctor."

They were suddenly conscious of the sound of soldiers singing in the distance, against the muffled tramping of many horses. Both knew that such a combination meant there was a cavalry detachment near. Samborik became uneasy and restrained David from venturing out of the cave for a look, by remarking: “Davidka, my dove, it must be Vaska Kravlenko. He is expected to march into Struga with his band. The damned scoundrels have been carrying on in the woods for some time; now they've been made a part of the Ukrainian Army. 

I'm sure you remember Vaska's father, Ivan. Well, when he's drunk, he brags about his son having become a general, no less, and says that this same 'general' is accusing the Jews of bringing about the revolution and killing the Czar, and that he intends to square accounts with them for all of this." 

Shivers ran down David's spine. "Samborik," he barely managed to say, "I want to go home, right now. I want to be with my father." “No, Davidka, let me find out what's going on outside first. 

You stay here, and don't budge until I get back. You've grown up - you aren't a little boy any more. Vaska had better not lay eyes on you - not at this crucial moment.”

Before long, Avrum Shanan's house, courtyard, and orchard were surrounded by the horsemen of Vassilii's detachment. 

They were ordered to let no one in or out of the cordon. "This is a nest of Bolshevik spies, and we must destroy them like bedbugs!" Vassilii's orders were relayed to the men. 

When Vassilii himself entered the courtyard, accompanied by Serebryak, Avrum appeared on the veranda, followed by 

Yakov, the frightened watchman, Alexey, the coachman, and 112

the latter's wife, the cook. 

"Back into your holes, you Jew dogs!" Vassilii shouted, pointing his cat-o'-nine-tails at the servants. "It's not you 

I'm after, but him that Trotsky spy! Serebryak, this is 

Mister Abramka of Struga! Do you know what the Count once told me? Why, that this Jew is so shrewd, he could dispose of ten fellows like me. Where is your bastard?" he bellowed at Avrum. "I hear he's grown so big, that he's spying with his damned father! Where is the Bolshevik front located?

When did you dispatch the news of the Ukrainian forces and their positions to your brother Trotsky? You think we don't know that you handed over a crateful of gold to your communist commissars? Where is your bastard? Produce him this very minute I want him right at your feet! And what's more, you'll dance again - not for the Struga crowd this time, but just for me and Serebryak. You won't spellbind us the way you did the mob, and we won't cross ourselves for you, either!" 

Avrum was stunned by this tirade but remained outwardly calm. One thought flashed through his paralyzed mind – David! David must be saved. His reluctance to remain at home must have been the will of Providence; Samborik was with him and was bound to save him. . . .

Growing impatient, Serebryak snapped his knout with all his might, and it whistled through the air and wound itself around Avrum's neck and head. A trickle of blood appeared on his pale face, congealing on his graying beard. 

Avrum seemed impervious to pain. He straightened out his slim figure, and raising his half-closed eyes heavenward, he muttered the last words of the Confession of Faith: "Thou Who art the Father of the fatherless and Judge of the widows, protect my beloved kindred with whose soul my own is knit. Into Thy hand I commend my spirit; Thou hast redeemed me, O Lord, God of truth. Amen, and Amen!”

His eyes fierce, Serebryak shook so violently with rage at sight of the immobile Jew before him, that he almost toppled from the saddle. Feeling much as he had on that night in the 113

Irkutsk prison, when he had unburdened his mind to Vassilii and had ended his tale wildly flailing his cat-o'-nine-tails at his hallucination in the cell, Serebryak continued to belabor Avrum, who still did not move. Avrum was bleeding profusely, and his strength was waning, but he refused to budge. 

As he was nearing death, he cried out, with a superhuman effort, the ancient Hebrew prayer: "Hear, O Israel, the Lord our God, the Lord is One!" "Vaska," Serebryak screamed, horrified at his victim's reluctance to die, and at the strange phrases on his lips, "Who is this Jew? Look at him - just like Christ on the cross! Let him go let's clear out of here, Vaska!" 

"What you, too?" Vaska was beside himself. "First he bewitched the crowd with his dancing - and now you! Run away from him? Don't you dare! Don't you move an inch, you son-of-a-bitch!" Swiftly he grabbed his rifle, took aim at 

Avrum's chest and fired. . . An echo, as of a mild thunder, reverberated in the nearby mountains. As the puff of smoke dissipated, Avrum swayed and toppled down the steps of the veranda. The blood that trickled from his breast was thirstily absorbed by the Struga soil. . .

Vassilii was not completely placated by Avrum's violent death. He felt, intuitively, that as long as David was alive, the son must avenge his father's death, sooner or later. Doing away with David, therefore, now became Vassilii's primary goal in life. "Serebryak, take charge of the brigade!" Vassilii rapped out a command. "Patrol the road leading into and out of 

Struga. See to it that the Shanan bastard doesn't get away from here! Look sharp and you'll recognize him. He's got dark devilish eyes like his father. If you happen to bump into him, remember: a bullet into his head - quick-without stopping to think or pity him! If he doesn't slip out of the village, I'll find him myself! None of you know the hideouts in Struga as I do- you're strangers here!" And having issued his orders, Vassilii proceeded on horseback to the riverbank. 114

The blast of the gun had convinced Samborik that tragedy had at last occurred. He could no longer do anything for 

Avrum, he reflected, but David must be saved, come what may.

He scurried through the short-cuts in the orchard and returned to the cave, out of breath. Then, grabbing David's hand, he dragged him along to an old dilapidated barge and whispered: “Davidka, jump in there and cover yourself with the old nets. Don't stir, and don't answer anyone's call! For God's sake, stay put until nightfall, when I'll come to see you." “Samborik, why are you so excited?" David asked. "I want to go home - why don't you let me go home? Father will be worried; I want to be with him. . .' 99 “Davidka, my dove, there's no time for talk now," Samborik cut him short. "Do as I tell you. A great misfortune has happened. . . Vaska is after you; he aims to harm you; there's murder in his heart. Hurry up, my dove, and hide yourself, and be patient. I'll be back as soon as it gets dark.” 

David crept into the hold of the battered barge that was half sunk in the sand and muck of the shore. The complete darkness, the stench of mildew, the thick dangling cobwebs, the squealing of hungry rats, the beating of the surf, and the creaking of the rickety old boards made him feel as though he had been hurled alive into a grave. The sudden burst of events had had a shattering effect on him. Now Samborik's footsteps became even fainter and soon were no longer audible. 

A deathly silence settled over the dark, putrid barge. 

Little by little, David began groping in his mind through the fog and maze induced by his sense of shock over what had happened. His keen intelligence defiantly demanded an explanation: Why is Vassilii bent on harming us? Is it because he is a Christian and we are Jews? In that case, why is Samborik, a devout Christian, friendly to us? Does Vassilii hate us because of father's wealth? Then why does he also hate all the other Jews of Struga, who are poor? Why has my wonderful Struga, with its glorious Dniester and Carpathians, meadows and woods, become confined to this dark, 115

reeking grave? Why must I elude Vassilii? The Rabbi, Reb

Itzikel, says that we can withstand our enemies by prayer and by studying the Torah. . .

David's line of thought was suddenly interrupted by the noise of a galloping horse. The rider dismounted not far from the barge, and David could hear him approach. At the entrance to the dismal vessel, he called out: "Whoever is in there, had better come forward or I'll shoot!" David held his breath. "I'll count three and then fire. One. . . two. three. 99 

The blast of a gun echoed in the gloom, followed by a flash. The barge was filled with smoke that tickled and choked David's throat. His efforts to stifle the cough inside him seemed to tighten the sinews in his head to the point of bursting.

From the other side of the Dniester, David could hear commands in a foreign tongue ring out and then the quick salvo of a machine-gun. The horseman grumbled angrily: "Those damned Rumanian devils! I fire one bullet, and there they go with machine-gun volleys!" Then he hopped into his saddle and galloped away.

David pushed away the rotting, ill-smelling fish-nets under which he had lain hidden, and disregarding Samborik's advice, stuck his head out, inhaling the refreshing, fragrant air of the summer dusk. He resumed his interrupted thoughts. How could one subdue Vassilii with “prayer and the study of the 

Torah?" How fast Vassilii had taken to his heels, when the

Rumanian had responded with a barrage to his single rifleshot! David recalled Yosef's words to Reb Itzikel, "Not with

Psalms, father, nor with warming the benches in the Yeshivah, but with arms in our hands. . .' 

Now the sun was setting beyond the hills; a cool breeze rippled the placid surface of the river. Night was settling in a leisurely way over the dreamy village. The lowing of a cow and the barking of a dog echoed in the distance. 

Exhausted by their toil, the men of the soil, both young and old, were yielding to the delights of rest and slumber. The stars twinkled merrily, and the monotonous croaking of the frogs began along the shoreline. 116

At midnight, Samborik, barefooted and crawling on all fours to avoid detection, made his way to the barge and called in a barely audible voice: "Davidka, Davidka! Come here, my dove! I've brought you some bread, butter, and milk; you've hardly had any food all day." 

In answer to the summons, David jumped from the barge to the shore with alacrity and fell to drinking and eating ravenously. Samborik watched him silently for a few moments and then began gently: "Davidka, you are no longer a child.

Brace yourself, my dove; a misfortune has happened - a great misfortune. . . Vaska. your Father . . . he's killed your Father, Davidka. . ."

David's heart seemed to fail, and his knees buckled under him. He slumped to the ground and buried his head on Samborik's knees. He remained motionless for a few moments, and then snapping out of his semi-stupor, leaped up, and without a word, dashed off in the direction of his home. Samborik scrambled up and ran after him, pleading: “Davidka, Vaska has surrounded the village with his men, and he is out to destroy you, too! Save yourself, my dove! I can swear that your father's last wish was to save you. . .

Paying little heed to Samborik's entreaties, David sprinted along, beyond fear. He was assailed by a gnawing grief and remorse for his failure to be at his father's side during the latter's final ordeal. In the house, he found only the cook, who sat on the threshold of his father's study, her hands over her face crying as bitterly as if she had suffered the loss of her own kin. 

On seeing David, she embraced him and wailed through her tears: "My young master, your good-hearted father is no more! 

Those hoodlums have killed him! Such a holy soul gone from this world! . . .'

David stood there stupefied; his tear-ducts seemed to have dried up. He felt removed from everything about him. Freeing himself woodenly from the woman's embrace, he asked grimly: "Where is he. . . Where is father?" 117

"Yakov and Alexey have carried his body over to the big apple tree - it was his favorite spot in the orchard," the cook replied. "They are digging a grave for him this very minute." 

When David approached the "thousand-year-old” apple tree,

Avrum's grave had already been filled. Yakov and Alexey were kneeling alongside it, mumbling a prayer and crossing themselves. David stood in silence for a long while, his eyes riveted upon the fresh grave, and then Samborik, who had followed him, touched his hand tenderly and said: “Davidka, my dove, I had hoped to have your father buried in town among Jews but Vaska won't permit it, and the village is surrounded by his men . . . You're a learned boy, Davidka almost a rabbi - you say a prayer on his holy grave. . ."

The thud of boots and the jingle of spurs was heard nearby. 

“Davidka, this must be one of them," Samborik whispered in a quavering voice. "Perhaps Vaska himself. . . Hurry, my dove, and hide in the bushes, near the fence! We'll stay here, and nothing will be suspected." 

But David was not to be deterred. His eyes were still fixed on the grave.

A fierce-looking soldier with drawn gun emerged from among the trees. "Who's there?" he asked gruffly. "Is the 

Jew buried? Well, that will do; no need to hang around here. Get going!"

The soldier advanced a few steps, and peered into David's face. David remained motionless, completely oblivious of him. 

The soldier removed his Cossack headgear slowly, bowed, and made the sign of the cross. Then he said to David: “Are you the son? The hetman, Kravlenko, is looking high and low for you. 'Fedka,' he says to me, 'go and have a look; if you find him at his father's grave · a bullet in his head, and finis.' Make yourself scarce, my friend! Disappear from 

Struga. I, Fedka, have not seen you. . ." He put on his hat, turned, and vanished into the nocturnal gloom of the trees. 

"Lord have mercy!" Samborik exclaimed. "A miraclea great miracle has taken place! Your father's soul has shielded you and softened a brigand's heart toward you. Davidka, 118

escape! My brother Andrey will lead you across the river.

In the Rumanian territory you won't face any danger. Later, when things settle down, you'll come back to claim the estate that belongs to you."

Shaking off his stupor at last, David said, in a voice charged with pain and grief: "Not with prayer, Father, nor with the study of Torah, but with weapons in our hands. . .' "What are you saying, Davidka?" Samborik asked. "What do you mean, my dove?" "Father knows what I'm saying, Samborik," David replied. "Father understands. No, Samborik, I don't intend to hide - neither here, nor on the Rumanian side. I wish to get back to Polipovka; I should not have run away from there. 

I made an awful mistake I took the wrong turn. I must get back to Polipovka. Yes, Samborik, I want and must take the new road. 

The creaking of the wheels of a peasant cart echoed in the night. On top of the sacks filled with potatoes, wheat, and rye, sat the peasant, holding the reins of the sluggish horses as they trotted leisurely along the highway leading out of the village. Sitting back to back with the driver was a young peasant lad, his hands hidden in the warm, thick, woolly hides of two trussed sheep. "Halt!" a stern voice commanded. "Where are you heading, peasant - and what are you carrying there?" "I'm heading for Polipovka, dear soldier," the driver explained. “I've got some grain and a couple of sheep which 

I hope to sell in the city market-place. One gets a little higher price there. It's easier to get a bit of kerosene, salt, and a piece of leather there too, they say." "And what about you, young fellow?" 

"Pardon me, dear soldier," the driver interposed. "That's my boy the poor lad was born a deaf-mute. It's a pity- 

God's creature - he can see and feel, but he can't say a word. He's wretched when he stays alone, so I take him along to keep an eye on the sheep. 99 The soldier stepped over to the cart, stroked the fur of 119

the sheep a few times, and reaching the boy's hands, squeezed them lightly, and remarked to the peasant: "Go ahead! God be with you, and may you prosper." "Thank you, dear soldier - thank you, and may God bless you!" "Hey, Fedka, whom have you stopped there?" another guard called out. 

“No one — an old peasant is creeping along with his nags to work in the field."

Day was breaking. The crowing of cocks echoed in the distance. A bright pink gradually suffused the sky. The stars paled and vanished. A round, fire-red platter loomed on the east horizon, heralding the arrival of a new, sunny summer day. The peasant detoured to the right, and through a side road, drove into a small, dense thicket. “Davidka, my dove, here we part," the peasant said. “I must get back, for as long as I'm at home, Vaska will assume that you are in Struga. I'm sure that the soldier Fedka may the Lord bless him! - recognized us both; but he won't talk. I'm unharnessing the younger nag for you. Take along the two sheep - they'll make you seem more of a peasant, and may protect you against any robber attacks in the Verbivka Forests. In this sack you'll find oats for the horse, and bread and hard-boiled eggs for yourself." 

David broke down. He embraced Samborik, dug his head into the peasant's chest, and sobbed loudly and bitterly. "Cry to your heart's content, my child - it'll do you good."

Samborik comforted him. "It will bring you relief, and your soul, filled with hatred now, will be cleansed. Go back to the big city, my child. Learn the wisdom of the world, and be a good, upright man, as your father was." "No, Samborik, it's not with goodness that a person avenges innocently shed blood, but with blood! Yes, blood for blood!" "Davidka, the world keeps going because there are some decent men in it. If you start shedding blood, you'll be like 

Vaska. . . . The world of Vaska will vanish, but the world of those who fear God will live forever."

David looked at Samborik with bewilderment. He discerned in the peasant the image of a saint; and in his simple 120

words, he detected the convincing truths of Reb Itzikel's Torah. "Davidka, my dove, God created me a simple peasant, and my prayers are simple. Who knows if I'll ever see you again?

To part from you now is as difficult as it was for me to part from Marushka. .

Samborik knelt and turning his head heavenward, prayed fervently: "God in heaven, Lord of the universe, protect Davidka from evil and lead him in the ways of loving-kindness and goodness. . . 

When David, riding horseback, reached the highway, he turned his head and caught a last glimpse of the crests of the Carpathian Mountains. He recalled again his first departure from Struga six years ago. Oh, the tears he had shed then! Those, however, had been tears of love and longing. 

But today... today he was escaping from Struga — orphaned, mortified, and persecuted. . . From the summit of the mountains his gaze wandered down to the spot where he had left Samborik. The peasant was still kneeling in his ardent prayer.

David sensed an urgent need for the solace that he, too, had often found in prayer. Now, leaving Struga and his father's grave behind him, he expressed his boundless grief through the same Psalms he had said, when he took leave of his mother forever. 

The Lord is my Shepherd; I shall not want.

Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil, for Thou art with me. 121

Pursuing Shadows 

Ilya Grigorovich Maximov, the director of the Polipovka State Academy, sat in his office, engrossed in thoughts about the current trying conditions, and mulling over the pleasant memories of the past. As for the future there was not a single bright ray in his mind. The day ahead, and all the days to come, were steeped in a dense, impenetrable darkness. 

On the desk in front of him lay the most recent decree delivered to him by a special courier. With head propped on two outspread fingers of his right hand, Maximov contemplated the typewritten lines again and again. Yet the more he pored over the message, the more incredible the new situation appeared to him, and the harder to fathom: 

April 15, 1922

To Comrade Ilya Grigorovich Maximov, Director of the Polipovka State Academy Comrade: 

By order of the Commissariat of People's Culture and Education of the Polipovka district, you are to be removed from your post; the supervision of the

State Academy is being delegated to the Commissar of Political Instruction, Comrade Yosef Shneierson. 

Your functions will terminate with the graduation exercises of the year 1922, according to the following program and order: 

1. The graduation exercises will take place on May 1, 1922. 122

2. It is obligatory that all students - male and female - as well as the entire faculty, participate in the 

May First parade. Everyone is requested to wear a red emblem on the right side of his chest. 

3. Following the parade, all students will assemble in the large auditorium of the Peoples' House for the graduation exercises. 

4. The following Government representatives, aside from yourself, will be seated on the rostrum: the 

Military Commissar, Comrade Stephan Smirnov;

Commissar for Political Instruction, Comrade Yosef Shneierson; and Commissar of Peoples' Culture and 

Education, Comrade Mikhail Gruzinov. 

5. The three above-mentioned Commissars, and one of the graduates, whom you will designate, will deliver addresses, following the distribution of the diplomas. 

6. You are emphatically ordered to see to it that the address of the valedictorian conforms to the spirit of the Revolution, and is in keeping with the campaign against all those forces of darkness that aim to suppress the Revolution. 

7. You will be held personally responsible for scrupulously carrying out this edict. (signed) Mikhail Gruzinov

Commissar of Peoples' Culture and Education 

During the three decades of his tenure as Director, Maximov had ruled this educational institution with an iron hand. 

Like the Czar, who had peremptorily reigned over the sprawling Russian Empire and his millions of subjects and functionaries, Maximov had domineered over his miniature empire, the State Academy, and all its students and teachers. 

Tall and broad-shouldered, with a leonine head accentuated by a goatee, Maximov's very appearance in the corridors of the Academy would throw all those present into fear. 

Maximov regarded discipline as a cardinal principle. He believed firmly and inexorably in its connection with pedagogy. According to his unequivocal conviction, education was impossible and futile without discipline. "Discipline," 123

he was wont to say, "holds the key to the molding of the character of our intelligentsia!"

Decorum was of no less importance to Maximov. The uniforms of the students and teachers had to be spotless, and their buttons had to sparkle. Students were forbidden to patronize teahouses, saloons, or other such public establishments, so as to preserve their reputation for respectability. They were not supposed to sit in the park, or to roam the streets in the evening.

On learning of a breach of discipline and decorum on the part of any student, the Director would invariably summon the offender to his office and reprimand him severely: "Young man, wearing the uniform of the Polipovka State Academy is an honor and a privilege that you don't deserve. Your behavior has been scandalous. I warn you that any further violation of the prescribed discipline will not be condoned, but will mean instant and unceremonious dismissal from school!"

The two Academy buildings were the finest in Polipovka. 

They looked like identical twins - one for the boys, the other for the girls. The two structures were separated by a double row of tall pine trees, which created the impression of keeping constant vigil, like austere sentinels, so that the segregation between the two groups of students would be complete.

The one-story rectangular edifices were surrounded by huge, ornate windows and had towering white columns at the entrances. Long corridors bisected each of the two buildings with geometric precision into symmetrical halves. The classrooms, the Director's office, the laboratory, the library, and even the washrooms - all opened into the corridor, each through a single door. A guard, posted at one end of the corridor, could see all the movements of students in their respective classrooms. Thus, the Director was able to survey the goings-on; nothing escaped him. Everyone dreaded him; he was the terror of the Polipovka State Academy, which he had ruled for all these years, without deviating one iota from his principle of regimented discipline. 

Then the Revolution burst upon the scene, and in the 124

twinkling of an eye, shattered the foundation and scuttled the pillars on which the Czarist empire and arbitrary power had rested for so long. Unfortunately, this same debacle also toppled Maximov's personal empire and authority. Conditions had been fairly tolerable in the interim following the first years of the Revolution, when the governmental set-up and the dominance of various political parties fluctuated frequently. In the confusion, the status of the educational system was left more or less undisturbed. But when the 

Bolsheviks seized power, everything was changed. 

... "Dictatorship of the proletariat!" Maximov muttered to himself, heaving a sigh. "What chaos! The education of our youth, our culture in the hands of untutored, ignorant, and cultureless elements! One would think that they couldn't do without graduation exercises! It's a mere excuse for their 

Bolshevist propaganda. The commissars will wax eloquent, and even the address of the valedictorian must be imbued with their doctrine. ‘. . . In the spirit of the Revolution in the spirit of the fight against all the forces of darkness that are anxious to suppress the Revolution. . .' Which graduate am I to appoint as the valedictorian?"

He was especially irritated by the last paragraph of the decree: "You will be held personally responsible for scrupulously carrying out the edict." A chill ran through him. 

Those commissars! They were only looking for an excuse to denounce him as a saboteur and counter-revolutionary - and eventually give him the quietus. . . Whom could he entrust with the delivery of a revolutionary speech that would meet with the approval of the commissars? 

The name of David Shanan suddenly occurred to him. 

That Shanan was an odd fellow a brilliant mind too bad that he was a Jew. . . A confirmed Bolshevik, yet without a trace of arrogance. Always cordial and correct, like a genuine aristocrat... A strange - very strange - fellow! He discusses 

Russian classicists such as Tolstoy, Dostoyevsky, and Gorky, and even the Bolshevist apostles, Marx and Engels - and sprinkles those discussions with quotations from the Bible and the Talmud. Strange, indeed, that Jew, Shanan. Why, his very admission to the Academy had been unusual. 125

Maximov recalled his first meeting with David Shanan, when the lad dropped in about a year ago. "What can I do for you, young man?" Maximov had asked his visitor. "I wish to complete the prescribed course of study of the State Academy," the applicant had replied. 66 

"To complete?" " Maximov asked. “Your face is not familiar to me I doubt whether you've ever begun here. Young man, don't you think that it is a bit odd to begin with "completing?" " "I have studied the entire curriculum of the first seven grades privately - mostly by myself," David had informed the Director.
