"I'm ready to take the examinations and become eligible for admission to the eighth (final) class. I am anxious to complete the course of the State Academy as soon as possible." "How old are you?" "Fifteen." "What have you been doing until now? Why haven't your parents tried to enroll you in the Academy before?" "I studied at the Rabbinical Academy." "Why did you make a change from the Rabbinate to this Academy?" "I don't want to be a rabbi - I wish to study medicine." "What is your name?" "David Shanan." "Are you the son of the Struga Shanan? As I recall it, your father just recently passed away." "My father did not pass away - he was murdered!" 

"I am sorry to hear that. He was a very fine man - I heard much about him. By the way, what are your political views, young man?" "I'm on the side of those who are liberating the world from slavery, who extend equal rights to all men to live freely, and who take up arms against the hooligans and their provocateurs."

Notwithstanding his youth, David had articulated these words with enough conviction and solemnity to make his interrogator fidgety. To cut short the interview, the Director 126

took out an application form and said: “Shanan, fill out this application blank and be here for your examination within two weeks. It is quite likely that your private, hasty preparation will prove to be inadequate for a satisfactory coverage of the studies of seven grades, and the outcome of your examination will determine which class you fit into. You are quite young, and it won't hurt you to complete the Academy course a few years from now." 

David had passed his examination with high honors to become the youngest student of the eighth class. In place of the Yeshivah, with its hard benches, and its students intoning abstruse passages and commentaries of the Talmud in fervid unison, David found himself in the totally different environment of the Polipovka State Academy. Here, at his own desk, he could sit and listen all ears and eyes - to his instructors, as they drew diagrams and chalked up formulas for physics, chemistry, astronomy and mathematics, on the blackboard. 

David's aim of completing the Academy course in a hurry became secondary, for his desire to acquire as much knowledge as he could now dwarfed every other goal for him. Just as his keen mind had previously absorbed the wisdom of the ancient Hebrew sages, it now eagerly quaffed secular knowledge from the new founts. He was especially fascinated by the subjects of zoology and classical literature. In fact, when Ivan Ivanovich, the teacher of zoology, had finished his first lecture on the "Processes of Nutrition in Animals,"

David had been so impressed, that he remained sitting at his desk as though spellbound. "Shanan," the instructor turned to the young student, "it could be that my lecture holds little interest for you, and that you are engrossed in your own, more important thoughts. 

However, you will not pass your examination in zoology, unless you 'digest' the 'nutrition." "

The rest of the class burst out laughing. David stood up and somewhat confused, said: "I beg your pardon, Ivan Ivanovich. I did not mean to convey the impression of a lack of interest in your lecture. 127

On the contrary, I was so fascinated by it, that I was still lost in thought when it ended." "Really?" the instructor waxed sarcastic. "I have a tiny suspicion that your allegation of being fascinated is merely a diplomatic alibi to wiggle out of a predicament. I assure you that your classmates and I would very much like to share your great enthusiasm. Would you be kind enough to tell us what you have learned here about the process of nutrition?” "Nutrition is the essence of bodily existence," David replied promptly and distinctly. "The world around us creates the sources of nutrition in the form of eating, drinking, and breathing. It is only after the food and drink are digested that they prove useful. Digestion is carried out by the stomach and its glands. Upon being digested, the consumed food becomes ready for distribution throughout a person's body. 

That is the function of the blood circulation. The blood absorbs the digested substances, steering them to all the microscopic cells of the body. Wherever blood circulates, food is bound to make its way. The beating of the heart sets in motion the flow of the blood, and the fresh air that we inhale into our lungs adds oxygen to the blood, thanks to which the digested food is consumed and energy is generated for the functions of the body. But before food can be consumed and become an organic part of the body, it must first be assimilated. Fire inevitably leaves a residue of ashes that must be gotten rid of. In like fashion, as a result of food consumption, the body also forms its wastes which must be eliminated in the form of stool, urine, sweat and exhalation of carbon dioxide. Digestion, circulation, assimilation, and elimination are the processes of nutrition." 

The teacher was struck with wonder: such an intelligible, yet simple explanation! Such natural lucidity was possible only when the subject one was discussing was particularly dear to one, like an organic part of one's self. . . "That is splendid, Shanan! Pardon my being suspicious. 

Thank you. You may sit down."

An intense hush filled the classroom. Most eyes were downcast, a symbol of the other students' discomfort and shame at their previous scornful laughter. 128

Still standing, David inquired hesitantly: "Ivan Ivanovich, may I ask a question?" "By all means - go ahead." "Do the processes of body nutrition apply also to the nutrition of the spirit?" "Shanan, that is a philosophical question," the instructor answered; "I must admit that I am not qualified to give a conclusive answer on it. What prompts you to ask that? 

You undoubtedly have a good reason..." "My father had an extensive library," David explained. "Entire walls in our home had been converted into bookshelves that were stacked with thousands of volumes. I once asked my father whether an individual could ever manage to read through and absorb such a wealth of literature, and yet retain in his memory all that he had read and studied. 

As I was listening to your lecture on body nutrition, I suddenly remembered my father's answer at that time, and it started me thinking of what he had told me. It went something like this: ""The world contains a wealth of books and knowledge, just as the fields and orchards abound in grain and fruit. 

The latter nurture and sustain one's body, while books knowledge - nurture and sustain one's soul. The body will shrivel and wither for lack of sustenance; the same holds true for one's soul. The body is nurtured via the stomach; the soul - through one's mind. From there it travels to one's heart, where one fathoms it, and extracts its quintessence. 

Thereupon it becomes a true part of one! It enriches one's soul by giving one spiritual sustenance and values by which to live. . . Only the essential remains; all else vanishes from one's mind through the capacity for forgetfulness. The teacher contemplated David in silence. An air of solemnity pervaded the room. Smiling sheepishly, David concluded in an apologetic tone: "Pardon me, Ivan Ivanovich, it's just a weakness of mine always to see the new sprout from the old. . .' 

At a signal from Maximov, Anton, the guard, appeared at the door and inquired: 129

"You rang for me, Your Excellency?" "Yes, Anton, but I've asked you time and again not to address me as 'Excellency.' Such a salutation is prohibited now the Government frowns upon it. You must address me as 'Comrade Director.' 999 "What's this 'Comrade' business to me? You'll pardon me,

Your Excellency, but I'm too old to dance to a new tune. 

I've served Your Excellency for a quarter of a century and these young upstarts that have usurped the power now and are reducing Mother Russia to ashes, won't change me." "You had better be careful, Anton," Maximov cautioned. "Lately, people have paid with their lives for lesser crimes than addressing their superiors with aristocratic titles. The world has become barbaric, my friend; one has to be very careful. . . The morning lectures will soon be over. I want you to go to the eighth class and tell the student David 

Shanan to come here." "At your service, Your Excellency!"

Ten minutes later, David tapped on the door of the 

Director's office, and at his behest, entered. "Be seated, Shanan. How are you getting along during these last days of your study at the Academy?" "Well - and not so well. Well, because I am drawing closer to my goal. Not so well, because I feel rather sad about leaving the Academy so soon. It's opened up a window for me into a new world of knowledge which was alien to me before." "Thank you, Shanan. My old heart delights in such warm words." "Ilya Grigorovich," David remarked, looking down, "I am also unhappy at the thought that when I leave the Academy, I will no longer have the pleasure of listening to your lectures on Russian literature. You and Ivan Ivanovich are two teachers whom I'll cherish all my life. When Ivan Ivanovich lectures on zoology, his students get a brand-new conception of animals. 

The secrets of their instinctively clean and natural life come to light; it seems that if man were to live that way, he would turn out to be a nicer and better individual. 

When you lecture on literature, we can see the poets as the representatives of soulfulness and beauty and the novel- 130

ists as the emissaries of righteousness, inner redemption, and 

God in one's heart."

Maximov was visibly stirred. Swelling with emotion and blinking slightly at the involuntary tears that filled his eyes, he regarded David for a moment, then said: “Thanks, Shanan – many thanks. 

. . You have spent but one year inside these walls, and yet you are saddened at the thought of parting with the Academy. After all, this institution has been only a transitory period in your life. Your future is just beginning. Great, thrilling moments are awaiting you the university, the study of medicine with all its fascination and wonders, the glorious profession of healing men's bodies. And still you are disturbed at the prospect of leaving here! Now, just imagine how depressed I must feel!

After being the head of this Academy for three decades, I, too, must part from it to face a future of drab, lonely wretchedness." "Are you leaving the Academy? Why?" "Yes, Shanan - according to the latest decree of the Commissariat."

The Director handed him the document. "Incredible!" David gasped after reading it. “Ilya Grigorovich - Yosef Shneierson has been my friend since my childhood; we've practically grown up together! Of course, he's a good and loyal revolutionary - but to replace you? Why, that's absurd! He's never set foot in an Academy! He is a fine political commissar, because he's a staunch believer in the Revolution, just as he used to believe in God, before. When they listen to him, everyone who's persecuted and exploited gets hope and comfort from his words and wants to join the ranks of the fighting working class. But what has that kind of political persuasiveness got to do with directing people's education? What relation is there between the sciences of zoology, physics, chemistry, geometry and algebra, and the revolutionary slogans and revolutionary struggle?" "Shanan, you've never denied your Bolshevik leanings, so you're better qualified than I am to find an answer to these questions." Maximov answered wearily. "Truthfully speaking, I'm confused and depressed about it all... Shanan, 131

I have asked you to come here because I've decided to appoint you valedictorian at the graduation exercises. You've read the Government order, so you know that my life depends on what kind of speech you make. I'll be held personally responsible for the message and spirit of that valedictory. . .' 

Tumultuous, peremptory voices suddenly sounded outside; then Anton whined: "That's unheard of! No one ever enters the Director's office without first being announced!" "Out of the way you Czarist cur!" a voice hit back. "Respect for the Soviet authorities! That bourgeois lackey ought to be shot!"

The door flew open and Mikhail Gruzinov, Commissar of Peoples' Culture and Education, and Yosef Shneierson, Commissar for Political Instruction, entered, excited and angry. 

Both were in military attire; they wore blue shirts, wide breeches, lacquered boots with high tight-fitting tops, leather jackets, and khaki Budionny pointed headgear adorned with a red star. Heavy Browning revolvers dangled from their wide leather belts. Gruzinov, a tall, slim young man in his early twenties, with thin features, wearing a pince-nez over his sharp, greenish eyes, advanced unceremoniously toward the desk and shouted at Maximov: "Comrade, what sort of an autocrat regime are you carrying on here? What sort of Czarist practices and manners? 

Tell me please, is your State Academy aware of the existence of the Soviet Government, or do you still chant 'God save the Czar?' This is a counter-revolutionary nest! It's got to be uprooted without further ado!" 

Maximov stood pale and shivering. Through bloodless lips he mumbled: "I beg your pardon, Comrades. Anton the guard is not vindictive; he is a simple, naive fellow. He cannot change easily." "Comrade, the Revolution has no time to be a nursemaid to pampered children. We will mercilessly crush everything standing in our way - and on the ruins of the old rot, we'll erect the new life of the dictatorship of the proletariat!” "Can I be of service to you, Comrades? To what honor may I attribute your visit?" Maximov asked with servility. 132

"I am Mikhail Gruzinov, and this is Shneierson, the Political Instruction Commissar. There will be a minor change in the last decree, Comrade. Your function as Director here will cease immediately! You are ordered to hand over, at once, the keys of your office, without touching a single document even if it's of a personal character. You will continue your lectures on Russian literature for the time being, but you will not utilize the prepared material unless it is first approved by Comrade Shneierson, who is to be the new school director, as of this moment."

Maximov's eyes opened wide, and stared as though through a grey mist. Woodenly, he took a bunch of keys out of his pocket and dropped them on the table. The dull thud they made as they fell pierced the deathly silence. Then, without uttering a word, and still staring blankly, he shuffled mechanically, puppet-like, out of the office. The crack of a gun-shot caused the high windows to vibrate. Sprawled on the other side of the door lay the body of the Director, a freshly bleeding wound in his right temple. His right hand was clenched about a revolver from which rose thin threads of smoke and on his dead lips was a smile of redemption.

An air of festivity pervaded Bolshoy Boulevard, the central and main thoroughfare of Polipovka. All the stores were closed and factories were at a standstill; the stalls and booths in the marketplace were as deserted as they were on a Sunday or Holy Day. Red flags fluttered from all windows and balconies. Small knots of people gathered on the sidewalks, and a multitude of men and women of all ages, paced up and down. Everyone wore some red emblem either on his breast, on his coat lapel, or on his arm. Even the teamsters adorned their horses and vehicles with red bands, and thin, red ribbons were strung along the tops of trolley cars. 

It was a serene, warm May First. The sun shone in the cloudless sky. A gentle breeze caressed the faces of the congregants and carried to their nostrils the fragrance of the blossoming flowers and acacia trees, which dotted the numerous parks and orchards in and around Polipovka.

The crowds became aware of the music of a military band, 133

punctuated by the rhythmic, monotonous beating of drums. "The parade! The parade!" echoed everywhere. The throng, calm and placid until now, swayed, jostled and elbowed everyone jockeyed for a vantage point from which to get a good view of the columns marching in the May Day parade. 

Stepping briskly in the vanguard, in time to the music were the various detachments of the Red Army – heavy and light artillery, machine guns, cavalry and infantry units. In the front, two soldiers were carrying aloft a huge red placard as wide as the boulevard, adorned with the slogans, "Long 

Live the Red Army!" and "We Will Mercilessly Destroy the 

Enemies of the Soviets Within and Without!" 

The army units were followed by the Young Communist League, (Komsomol) and by students from the Polipovka State Academy. They carried banners stating, "Down With the Czarist and Bourgeois Educators!" After them camewith diminished enthusiasm and considerable fatigue - factory and mill workers, and peasants of all ages, who had been brought from the outlying hamlets and villages for this special occasion. This phalanx, too, carried conspicuous red placards bearing the inscriptions, "Workers of all Lands Unite!" "The 

Land Belongs to the Peasant!" and "Down With the Exploiters and the Gentry!" 

The military detachments, comprising the first columns of the multitude of marchers, approached the huge, sprawling compound around the People's House of Polipovka. Heading the cavalry unit was the War Commissar of the Polipovka district, Stephan Smirnov, astride a spirited chestnut stallion. 

He sat proud and upright in the saddle — like a sculpture of horseman and steed cast as one. His strong, erect pose accentuated the quality of manhood evident in his tall, imposing figure; his dark, piercing eyes, and severe, regular features attracted the young girls who gazed at him ardently with unconcealed desire, and married women cast longing eyes at him surreptitiously. 

Stephan Smirnov was fully aware of his powerful attraction to the opposite sex, and gloated over it - this ability to conquer women's hearts and to kindle in them a blaze of 134

passion had come easily to him. However, there was always a special yearning and lust for the women who seemed beyond his reach. In these he found a challenge that instilled in him a primitive savagery, and no earthly peril or risk would restrain him from attempting conquest and complete possession of them. The more difficult the challenge, the more desirous its object became. He was obsessed by a restless passion which gave him no relief until his male potency was consumed. 

Riding at the head of his cavalry troop in this May First demonstration, he paid no heed, however, to the hundreds of women whose eyes were riveted upon him. He felt indifferent toward them, for the hot blood now raced in his heart and limbs for one woman only. The woman who had seemed the most unapproachable in the long history of his loveadventures now belonged to him. He had never before experienced such a flame and fire of passion. He could still hardly believe that it was real. The ravishing blonde seventeen-year-old Miriam, sister of the Political Commissar, and bride-to-be of the student, David Shanan was now his! 

Miriam, Miriam - the name rang in his mind and his dark sparkling eyes scanned the sea of faces in quest of his beloved. 

Stephan Smirnov had been assigned to Polipovka some three months ago. He was expected to put an end, once and for all, to the counter-revolutionary bands still hiding out in the adjacent forest and hills. Sergei Shenilov himself, the 

Commander of the Red Cavalry, had summoned him to his headquarters in Gomel and had said to him: "Comrade Smirnov, your services to the Revolution thus far are noteworthy. You have distinguished yourself, especially in the punitive expedition that is being carried on against the enemy of the Soviets. The successful extermination of our enemies in the area between Gomel and Kiev is due in great measure to your indefatigable efforts, and we are most grateful to you. But we are getting unfavorable news from the sector north of the Dniester. Countless bourgeois nests 135

are thriving there. Some of the gentry are still carrying on as in the Czarist days. Wealthy peasants (Kulaks) ferry their produce across the Dniester to the Rumanian markets; speculators are multiplying and armed bands of the White Guard run riot in the forests. That district must be cleaned out! The Soviet Government must be established firmly, come what may! You are being ordered to Polipovka to take charge of that region."

Mention of the word Polipovka touched Smirnov. Were it not against the tenets of communism, he would now believe in Providence. For the past ten years, the fires of revenge had been smoldering within him, for it was ten years since the Count of Struga and his favorite, Kravlenko, had humiliated him and had almost destroyed his military career. Fortunately, the Revolution had broken out and that had turned the tables: the erstwhile oppressed were now on top, wreaking their malice on the oppressors. 

P-o-l-i-p-o-v-k-a! It was a magic word in his ears. Polipovka! 

It was there that old Doctor Torchinsky had saved his life by removing the German bullet from his lungs; it was there that he had learned that Struga was a part of the Polipovka district - and the estates of Count Mishkin were situated in Struga.... "What thoughts are you engrossed in, Comrade?" the

Commander demanded, tugging at his long, black mustache, "Have you any reason for rejecting this assignment?" 

"No, no, Comrade Commander on the contrary, I am very happy. . . I am at your service. As a matter of fact, I've been in Polipovka once before — during the Czarist regime. I was hospitalized there. When do I leave, Comrade Commander?” "Tomorrow!" Shenilov answered peremptorily, raising his hand in a snappy salute, which indicated that the conference was at an end and that Smirnov's commission to Polipovka was now - law!

Upon his arrival in Polipovka, Stephan Smirnov had assumed command of the District Commissariat of War. He proceeded at once to call an emergency meeting of all the commissars and leaders of the local Communist Party. The 136

Political Instruction Commissar, Yosef Shneierson, was the first to address the meeting. "Comrades!" he exclaimed grandiloquently, "at this primary meeting with our new War Commissar, Comrade Smirnov, it is important first that we ourselves understand the fundamental principles in our hearts and minds, indelibly and forever. We approach the people, the common workers and peasants, and we call upon them to enlist in the ranks of Bolshevism. To most of them, the term 'Bolshevism' is an empty sound because we ourselves are not sufficiently rooted in our ideology — our rallying-call, our influence, is neither impressive nor convincing enough. Comrades, we ourselves must first be indoctrinated with the theories of Bolshevism! 

We must study the history of the Bolshevik Party thoroughly; and even more, we must become versed in all the duties imposed upon us by the Party! "The struggle against Czarist oppressors was initiated by Plekhanov when he organized 'The Group For Workers' Emancipation.' That organization, in turn, gave rise to various splinter groups, varying in their programs, but all aiming at securing freedom for the people. The Social Democratic 

Party turned out to be the most militant among these and, therefore, was persecuted mercilessly by the Imperial authorities. Prison, gallows, exile to bleak Siberia or to alien lands that was the lot of most leaders of that movement. "At the Congress of the Social Democratic Party which convened in London in 1903, the majority (Bolshinstvo) voted for the principles that were proposed by our leader, Nikolai 

Lenin. That majority (Bolshinstvo), split from the others and assumed the name of 'Bolshevik' - in contrast to the minority (Menshenstvo), that was guided by Axelrod, and called itself 'Mensheviki.' 

"Our leader, Nikolai Lenin, maintained that it is incumbent upon a party member to abide unconditionally and with heart and soul by the decisions of the Central Committee; that furthermore, he must be prepared like a soldier on the firing-line to carry out every mission. "What are the principles of our glorious party, and what is the role of a Comrade of our party?" Yosef went on with 137

fervor and erudition, to expound on the subject according to Karl Marx's economic philosophy, and pointed to the responsibilities of a Communist as set forth by Lenin. 

Smirnov sat impatiently smoking one cigarette after another, alternately crossing his legs in his restlessness. He was both irked and bored by the Political Commissar spouting so many statistics and theoretical interpretations, with such countless names and dates, and such a flood of high-sounding words. The speaker's elongated, typically Semitic nose, disheveled hair, singsong Jewish accent and gesticulations were annoying. Strange thoughts flitted through Smirnov's mind.

Under the Czar's rule, he had remained a sergeant,' barred from promotion because of a lack of education. "Brother, if you haven't attended school, you'll not reach beyond a sergeant's stripes!" Now comes the Revolution, and I, Sergeant

Stephan Smirnov, am Commissar of War! Under the Czar, this would be equal to being a Governor-General like Count Mishkin himself. Here I've achieved all that without formal education. . . But now there is a new blight of the intelligentsia for you - Political Commissars with their glib tongues. The devil knows what he's prattling about there!

He's out to save the workers and peasants! The Revolution has played into his hands, as well. Under the Czar, he couldn't spend a night in the capital; but today Trotsky is the Czar, and this little Jewish Commissar is chanting the laws of the new government with the same intonation as if he were reciting Psalms. Smirnov was so engrossed in his own trend of thought that he failed to notice that Yosef was concluding his address with the customary slogans, "Long Live the Communist 

Party!" "Long Live our Leaders, Lenin and Trotsky!" "Long 

Live the Dictatorship of the Proletariat!" 

The Chairman announced: "Comrades, our next speaker will be War Commissar, Comrade Stephan Smirnov." "Comrades," Smirnov began, "I have been commissioned to take charge of the War Commissariat of the Polipovka district. The entire area is infested with Czarist scoundrels; there are numerous bourgeois nests around here! The gentry is still lording it as if nothing had happened. Peasants are 138

shipping their produce across the Dniester to the Rumanians. 

Speculators are springing up like mushrooms. It's my duty to carry out a purge, the likes of which you have never seen. 

It's not with fancy speeches that we'll establish the Bolshevik rule, but with this!" - Smirnov tapped the holster of his revolver. "You can fool around with long-winded sermons at public meetings all you want, but I'll deal with the enemies of the Soviet in my own way! A Military Tribunal will be instituted at once. I demand that the Secret Extraordinary 

Commission be under my jurisdiction from now on. I insist on mass arrests of all the bourgeoisie and the Kulaks. The 

Military Tribunal will judge and mercilessly sentence to death all present and potential enemies of the Revolution!" "Comrade Chairman," Yosef flared up, "I wish to protest against Comrade Smirnov's disparaging remark about the importance of political education. Comrade Lenin himself, the greatest expounder of Communism, delivers four-and-fivehour addresses on the doctrine of our Party." "Lenin also says," Smirnov flashed back angrily, "not in four or five hours, but in a few brief words, "The bourgeosie is a malady that cannot be cured - it must be destroyed!' 

Let me assure you, Comrade, that my mission meets with 

Lenin's approval more than yours! The devil with it!"

Smirnov blurted out, banging his fist on the table, so that the inkwells rattled. "Shenilov sent me here to clean up this section and it shall be done! No games of culture with me! 

I had enough of it during the Czarist regime! I'm after action right here and now the devil with it!" 

Smirnov's second duty upon his arrival in Polipovka was of a purely personal character, and had no relation to his functions as a Commissar. From the first moment he had learned of his new assignment, he had pictured the Polipovka Hospital in his mind, where he had hovered between life and death, and where old Doctor Torchinsky had once ministered to him with such fatherly devotion. He could even remember the dialogue that had taken place when he had been discharged from the infirmary. “Smirnov, you're a lucky fellow," the doctor had said, placing his hand on the latter's shoulder. 139

"You've had a narrow escape; that was a very serious operation. Too bad that you're refusing to go back home. The

Crimean climate is good for convalescence." "No, doctor, there's nothing for me to look forward to at home. I'm yearning to return to the front lines. Crucial days lie ahead - days of great happenings that will change the history of Russia."

Doctor Torchinsky had meditated for a moment, then he heaved a sigh and remarked: "My friend, no matter what you do, may God be with you - and with Russia. Who knows, perhaps those great happenings of yours will bring you back to our region. If so, drop in on us and let us have another look at you." 

As Smirnov now rode on horseback into the spacious courtyard of the hospital, he scanned the face of the building trying to locate that window from which he had gazed during the weeks when he lay there bedridden and despondent, yearning for health and life. Yes, there it was the third window from the right. And there was the cherry tree - and the bench, hewn of stone old, familiar sights. 

It was as though he had been there only yesterday, inhaling that wonderful, invigorating air into his damaged lungs. 

He dismounted and advanced in a leisurely manner toward the cherry tree. He did not notice that someone was sitting huddled on the bench, in the deep hollow of the stone. At the sound of his footsteps the figure jumped to her feet, startled, and found herself face to face with the 

War Commissar.

Smirnov gaped. The sight took his breath away. He was confronted by a youthful girl of exquisite beauty. In her long cape of black velvet, she appeared stately and majestic. On her thick blonde hair she wore a blue silk scarf which was wound around her white throat, and fell softly in two wide trails along her straight shoulders. Her small nose, sensuous red lips, like juicy raspberries, and eyes as deep and blue as the skies on a sunny spring day, had the perfection of a work of art.

Regaining his equilibrium after what seemed like a considerable lapse, Smirnov clicked the heels of his fine leather 140

boots, saluted, and rapped out: "May I have the honor of introducing myself - Stephan Smirnov, War Commissar." "You frightened me," the girl replied, smiling coquettishly. "I didn't hear your footsteps and I thought you'd fallen from heaven. You are the new War Commissar! The way I heard it - you really started a 'war' at yesterday's session. "I doubt very much if I would have failed to notice such a bright star as you there." Smirnov told her. "I'm certain that you didn't attend yesterday's urgent and secret meeting - yet you seem to know what went on." "Comrade, I'm an international spy, and I'm using my feminine wiles to extract government secrets from you," the girl said mockingly. "Permit me to prostrate myself at your feet and to offer all the top secrets of the Government for a single loving look from you," Smirnov answered. At this, they both burst into laughter. "Comrade Commissar, I'm deeply moved by your eloquence," the girl declared. "You're a genuine knight straight out of the romantic novels! I had visualized you as being quite different, too!" "What am I hearing?" Smirnov nearly shouted. "I'm beside myself with joy! Have you really been thinking of me? 

It's music, sweet music to my ears and a delight to my heart.” "Comrade Commissar, you're just wonderful!" the girl laughed, tossing her head. The scarf slipped, and the wind played with her thick blonde hair. She raised her left hand, trying to restrain the curls which tumbled about her radiant face, and extended her right hand dramatically to Smirnov: "Give me your hand, Comrade! Allow me to be the first to congratulate you on the knighthood and chivalry that you are introducing into Bolshevism! You are a refreshing change for Polipovka, Comrade, and in the name of our youth - thank you!"

Smirnov took hold of the girl's hand, gazed intently into her eyes, and lifted her thin, long white fingers to his lips.

The girl trembled slightly and gradually withdrew her hand. 

Then, inclining her head slightly she said with a smile: "Pardon me Comrade, but I must be on my way. My father 141

is ill; I've just fetched some medicine for him from Dr. 

Torchinsky. The chloroform odor in the hospital almost put me to sleep, and when I came out into the air again I felt so pleasantly refreshed that I sat down on this bench. I never dreamed of such a stimulating meeting. I must be on my way now. Good-bye Comrade." "Please don't go yet," Smirnov pleaded. "Who are you, lovely one? You've bewitched me and I'll follow you like a slave! What's your name, bright star? Please don't go yet." "I must go now. Perhaps another time." "Tomorrow?" "Tomorrow four in the afternoon, at the same spot," the girl whispered reassuringly, and departed hastily.

Smirnov stood spellbound, watching the gently swaying silhouette of the mysterious girl disappear from sight. A profound desire to be loved by this beauty enveloped him. A stubborn inner ambition welled up within him — no matter who she might be, no matter how hard to attain - she would be his.. 

The touch of a hand on his shoulder stirred him from his reveries. He heard the familiar voice of Doctor Torchinsky: "Smirnov, my dear fellow! Is it a dream or a reality? I caught sight of a handsome horseman and I rubbed my eyes in disbelief. 'I could swear,' I said to myself 'that it's 

Smirnov! I must go down this minute and verify! Let me look at you. How are things with you? You look a picture of health and my guess would be that you're as sound as a bell. Judging by your uniform, you must be a big shot. Come into my office. I'll order a glass of tea and we'll chat a bit.” 

Smirnov clasped the doctor's long, wrinkled hand warmly, but kept his eyes fixed on the spot where the black cape had passed out of sight, then asked: "Doctor, who is she?" "She?" the doctor queried, amazed. "In whom are you interested, my friend?" "In a ravishing beauty, in a black velvet cape. She was here a moment ago - picking up a medicine for her father." "Young blood has its strange and sudden reactions" the 142

doctor commented. "One glimpse - and already infatuated! 

She's not for you, my friend. Her name is Miriam Shneierson.

She's the daughter of a well-known Rabbi who has a great following, not only in Polipovka but in other cities as well a godly man and highly educated. The Revolution, however, has disrupted his kingdom, too. He's taken it very much to heart and is critically ill now. His days are numbered. "She 

99 

a Jewess? Incredible! She doesn't look it not her features, not her behavior, and not her talk." "Incidentally, she's also the sister of the Political Instruction Commissar, Yosef Shneierson," the doctor continued. "His father felt that his son's embracing Bolshevism was a great calamity - worse than if he'd been converted to Christianity. In fact, it's really because the son turned revolutionary that his father got heart failure and collapsed." "The Commissar's sister," Smirnov muttered under his breath. "So that's where she learned about yesterday's meeting - and about me." "And if that weren't enough she's also engaged to that young Shanan fellow," the doctor rambled on. "He's a brilliant student, now graduating, and is anxious to study medicine. He's very bright - was supposed to study for the 

Rabbinate but after the Ukrainians killed his father, he joined the Communist Party in order to avenge his death.” "Miriam Shneierson, Miriam Shneierson," Smirnov, still bemused, repeated the name. Subduing a smile, he reflected: 

The fact that she was the daughter of a grand Rabbi, the sister of the Political Commissar and the bride-to-be of a young communist, was for the veteran conqueror of women's hearts more than offset by her mysterious but encouraging promise to meet him tomorrow. If anything, the complications of the situation kindled his infatuation for this girl even more and whetted his desire for her. He now looked closely at the doctor's face, and remarked joyfully: "I'm happy indeed to see you again, doctor! I thought of you often and never forgot my promise to call on you the first chance I had to visit this region." 143

"Thank you, my friend 

- many thanks. I'm most happy to see you. Now tell me, Stephan - what brought you back to our parts?" "Surgery!" "Surgery? You're talking in riddles, my friend.” "Doctor, what do you do when a sore grows and threatens to destroy the entire body? You operate! You take a knife - and you cut out the ugly pest. That's exactly what I do with the body of our society. I cut out and remove the diseased, the wicked, the foul, in order that the Soviet regime may survive!" "What bitterness there is in your words! Surgery, my friend, is not as simple as you assume. One has to determine first and foremost, the site and scope of the malady. Otherwise, one is likely to remove a healthy limb and instead of life, bring about death. Yes, my friend, one must be wellversed and alert. In medicine this is known as diagnosis. One also has to constantly bear in mind the danger of surgery because of its high rate of mortality." "The Revolution cannot be deterred by mortality," Smirnov retorted. "The Revolution demands victims, and many an innocent person topples along with the guilty. To make sure of a thorough job in removing a diseased organ, you've also got to probe with the knife in the surrounding healthy tissue! The purge that we are carrying out must be thorough and absolute! The counter-revolution must be destroyed without leaving the least trace; small individual nests may revive the bourgeoisie and Czarism!"

Doctor Torchinsky looked downcast, and his shoulders seemed to stoop under the burden of bygone years and past generations. Then he asked in a subdued, hoarse tone: "Smirnov, do you really think that by recklessly spilling the innocent blood of one's own people, a permanent government with a right to exist can be established? I doubt it .. There is still a God in the world - He will not permit it!" "There's no God in the world!" Smirnov thundered. "In the course of thousands of years, the bourgeois world confused and obscured the minds of the common people with a scarecrow which they called 'God.' Churches were erected, 144

thousands of pot-bellied priests were pampered, and all thrived on the sweat and toil of the worker and peasant!"

A Red cavalryman suddenly rode into the hospital courtyard, advanced toward Smirnov and declared: "Sorry to disturb you Comrade Commissar, but a telegram from the headquarters in Gomel has just arrived and I thought that there should be no delay on such an urgent message." 

Smirnov opened the telegram and read: "To the War Commissar, Stephan Smirnov, Poli povka: The Central Committee of the Extraordinary 

Commission in Gomel has approved the establishment of a Military Tribunal in your region, with full authority to impose and carry out capital punishment, through shooting, upon those defying the 

Soviet government." 

Smirnov smiled wryly, secure in his unlimited power over his humble, oppressed subjects. All the stumbling-blocks had been removed from his plan! He could now launch into a purge that the Polipovka district would never forget. He quietly mounted his steed, and the latter, feeling its master's weight, pranced about nervously. Sitting in the saddle, Smirnov appeared like a giant in comparison with the elderly stoop-shouldered doctor standing at the side. "Good-bye doctor!" Smirnov said, and wheeling, he saluted and departed. Swaying in the saddle to the rhythm of the galloping horse, Smirnov patted the telegram in his breastpocket. The mere touch of that scrap of paper filled him with triumph, vanity and power. He flung back his shoulders and held his head high. His heart beat in rhythm to the horse's trot and gave him the feeling that he trampled undeterred, upon man and God.

Dr. Torchinsky stood in the courtyard, broken-hearted and forsaken. He felt a sharp stab in his chest. He put his hand on his old, aching heart that now more than ever thirsted for compassion and yearned for the worship of God and elevation of man.

The next day, Miriam did not appear at the promised 145

meeting place. Smirnov waited for her for a whole hour without taking his eyes off his wrist watch. Each movement of the minute hand on his watch jarred his nerves as he impatiently strode back and forth. In his anger and resentment he reflected: "My instinct for women doesn't fool me! She was anxious to come! I could tell by her handgrip, her quavering voice and the twinkle in her eyes. . . 

To the devil!" he blurted out finally, stamping his foot, and throwing himself into his saddle with such force that the startled horse rose on its haunches. "One doesn't play cat and mouse with Smirnov!" he exclaimed, and raking the animal with his spurs he galloped off in the direction of the Rabbi's Court.

There he dismounted, tethered his horse, and entered the courtyard. He looked about him. The scene was deserted and quiet. Most of the buildings were of simple structure, “like neglected barracks," he mused. A strange sorrow seemed to emanate from their grimy, peeling walls and their high, dust-covered windows. He stood at a loss where to turn next. 

Across the way from a half-opened door, issued quaint and biazarre human voices. He advanced slowly, with curiosity, toward this only sign of life, and holding his breath wormed his way through the half-opened door to find himself in the Rabbi's synagogue. In the dim twilight, several scores of worshippers, their prayer-shawls pulled over their heads, were sorrowfully chanting Psalms in subdued voices. Grotesquely long and wide shadows accompanied the swaying of the worshippers; the plaintive intonations and mystical atmosphere exerted a strange influence upon Smirnov. He was for a moment completely oblivious of his mission and greatly absorbed in this sight which suggested to him some medieval ritual. Then suddenly he caught sight of her and his heart began to pound.

She was sitting among the women, in one of the long, narrow galleries ranged along the interior walls. The open frames of these galleries were overhung with wrinkled curtains, yellowed with age. Bent over prayer books, the women, all of whom were wearing headkerchiefs, joined in the plaintive outpouring of prayer. Miriam's head, covered with a black 146

silk scarf, gleamed through an opening in the curtain like sudden lightning in the dark of night. Electrified, Smirnov riveted his eyes upon her. 

What's going on here? Smirnov wondered. How does she fit into this picture? What a sharp contrast between these mysterious surroundings — and her! As though charged with a hidden hypnotic force, his keen, penetrating eyes reached her through devious ways and mesmerized her. She turned in his direction unobtrusively, and acknowledged his unwavering fiery gaze with a light smile. Smirnov discerned in that smile a confirmation of his earlier conviction that she had really intended to meet him. "Something unforeseen must have happened. . . She'll come - she'll be mine. . ." "Comrade, can I be of any help to you? You seem lost here," a young pale lad wearing a student's uniform said to him. The unexpected voice roused Smirnov from his reveries. "Thank you Comrade," he replied affably. "Can you, perhaps, tell me what's going on here?" "The Rabbi is critically ill," the student told him, “and his followers whose spiritual leader he is, are praying for his recovery." "Nonsense!" Smirnov snorted. "That's for the Middle Ages! 

Why don't they call a good doctor instead?" "There comes a time, Comrade, when the best physicians are helpless," the student remonstrated. "The patient has already received all that medicine can offer but without any improvement. He is sinking fast. His kin and adherents are anxious to save him, and are at a loss as to what to do next. 

In such distress a person yearns to open his heart, not to mortals, but to the invisible, the superhuman — to God. 

Yes, Comrade, to implore the Lord from one's inner depths one finds a measure of relief and solace therein." "That's bourgeois sentimentality! Do you really think that these Jews will restore their leader's health with their tears and prayers?" 

"That's what they believe - and hope. "Nonsense! Belief and hope are bourgeois narcotics to obscure the minds of the masses!" "On the contrary, Comrade - faith and hope are human 147

attributes, without which no campaign could be won and no ideal could be realized. Without faith and hope, the revolutionaries lingering in the Siberian dungeons could never have lived to witness the realization of the incredible - the triumph of the proletariat in the Russian Empire!" "Pardon me, Comrade, but how can you compare the belief of a revolutionary in justice and freedom with the faith of a bourgeois in the miracles of a God?" "Faith and hope are individual emotions, just like love. 

Who can appraise or limit a person's genuine love?” "... Speaking of love, Comrade - perhaps you know the 

Rabbi's beautiful daughter?" 

"Yes indeed, Comrade - she's my fiancee." "What an idiot I am! Of course you must be Shanan!

Old Doctor Torchinsky told me about you. You are too tolerant of religious poppycock for a party member. We'll have to denounce you to the Political Commissar, and let him enlighten you on the principles and duties of a Communist!"

Smirnov said the last in a humorous tone and placed a friendly hand on David's shoulder. But David was oblivious of him, for he had become totally absorbed in the concluding, stirring words of a prayer uttered by the leader of the congregation: "May it be Thy will, O Lord, my God, God of my fathers, speedily to send from heaven a perfect cure, both spiritual and physical, unto my sickness. For 

Thou, O Almighty King, art a faithful and merciful physician. Blessed art Thou, O Lord, Who art the

Healer Of All Flesh." 

David silently turned to usher Smirnov out of the House of Worship, and at the exit they came upon Yosef. "Comrade Shneierson," Smirnov greeted Yosef amiably, "I'm sorry to hear about your father's illness. I see you're consistent in your political convictions: you haven't participated in the synagogue services. It's not easy to isolate oneself from one's own kind, with whom one has been raised since childhood." 148

"The Revolution, Comrade Smirnov, does not recognize kin in the biological sense of the word. Only those who side with us and join our fighting ranks are to be considered as 'one of us.'" "That's interesting, very interesting, Comrade Shneierson! 

You'd better keep an eye on our Comrade Shanan. His leanings toward religious sentiments hardly conform to the Party ideals. As Political Commissar you ought to become acquainted with Comrade Shanan's interpretation of hope, faith and love." 

""Hope?' Yosef seethed. "Hope is the sentiment of a weakling! True revolutionaries cannot rely on hope! One has to fight and win! As to faith - naturally, we've got faith, but not in the mystic powers of religion! We believe in justice, and in the aims of the Revolution and its leaders!” "What about love, Comrade Shneierson? How do you interpret love?" "Love is a free expression of physiological needs and emotions between two individuals of opposite sex. The bourgeoisie made love into a by-product of religion, and twisted it into a tangled skein of social and ethical laws which have to be uprooted — together with the rottenness of the old regime."

David stood by silently, his eyes downcast. When he could no longer stand the pain of Yosef's words, he said: 

“I can well understand and justify the destruction of an old regime that allowed pillage and murder to go unpunished. 

I, too, have joined the ranks of the Revolution in order to exterminate that outrage of wild beasts masquerading as men. 

To destroy and trample upon the noblest elements of the human spirit, however to exterminate the quintessence of the human soul - hope and faith – and to befoul and degrade the purest human emotion, love that I am not prepared to do, even as the price of revenge for the murder of my father! Excuse me Comrades, but my Rabbi is dying, and I wish to stand by him in his last moments." He turned abruptly and stalked off in the direction of Reb Itzikel's home. 

Miriam appeared in the doorway of the synagague. The grey, drab surroundings suddenly brightened. Her pale, 149

delicate face, contrasted by her soft, black silk headkerchief, now appeared more mature than her age. Dressed in a white blouse buttoned up to her throat, and in a long, black velvet coat, her beauty was more accentuated and her appearance more attractive than ever. 

Smirnov stood spellbound, his eyes blazing with lust. With the tip of his tongue he moistened his lips, which had become dry with the rising turmoil of passion within him. He faintly resembled a hungry tiger licking his chops, as he instinctively discerns the presence of his long-awaited prey. "How exquisitely beautiful she is," he murmured at last. "Comrade Smirnov, meet my sister Miriam," Yosef said. 

Saluting smartly, Smirnov answered: "A pleasure. . . I'm grieved by your father's critical condition. I wish I could help in some way." "Thank you, Comrade," Miriam replied. "I'm touched by your kind consideration. But unfortunately, nothing can be done for him now. Doctor Torchinsky says that his condition is hopeless. You'll forgive us, Comrade Smirnov my brother and I must be with our father now." "Of course. May I escort you part of the way? After all,

I'm a stranger here, and your acquaintance soothes my loneliness." "Comrade Smirnov, you're too romantic for a War Commissar of the Red Army. You had better be careful - my brother is likely to rebuke you for this at the next meeting of the Party... However, I find you most refreshing!"

Smirnov hardly grasped Miriam's words. As though bewitched, he could not tear his eyes from hers.

She sensed his passion in his deep, penetrating look, and a slow blush suffused the paleness of her face. "Good-bye Comrade," she said in a quavering voice, and turned toward the door of her father's home. Smirnov's eyes followed her, and the sight of her slender feet and rhythmically swaying hips aroused a storm in his agitated body. 

An air of despondency hovered over Reb Itzikel's home. 

The hubbub and tumult attendant on the visits of the Hasidic followers who used to engulf the Rabbinical Court, 150

occupying every available inch of space, was no more. No longer could one hear the fervent, deep sighs and see the gesticulations of an assemblage of adherents, praying. The carefree hilarity, the melodious singsong, the ecstatic dances of the Hasidim, were also woefully missing; nor did one hear the wondrous tales about their spiritual leader and his father and ancestors. Gone was the familiar sight of hundreds of Hasidim and their wives and children flocking to the Rabbi to implore his intercession with the Almighty, pleading for health, livelihood, or a propitious marriage for a daughter. One no longer heard the lament and the wail of woe which customarily preceded the Rabbi's blessing, and the comforted sigh of solace that followed it. 

The Rabbinical Court was now forsaken and deserted. Here and there, individual kinsmen and followers, bowed with sorrow, emerged like shadows of stray souls. Only occasionally, was the silence of the prevailing grief and despondency pierced by a heartrending sob or moan.

First the Revolution, then the prolonged civil war and last, the establishment of the Communist regime - these were the factors that had contributed to the downfall of the Rabbinical dynasty of Polipovka. Wealthy Hasidic fol lowers of leading cities who were well acquainted with the political situation of the country had pleaded with the Rabbi to flee to go abroad where he would be welcomed by Jewish communities; to find refuge in lands where traditional Judaism was still thriving and where his leadership would attract new adherents. "That will not do," Reb Itzikel had answered them all, categorically. "I can discern a heavenly sign in the dissolution of my Court. I must have deviated from the righteous path somewhere, in my serving of the Lord. Perhaps I was not deemed worthy of raising my own flesh and blood in the light of the Torah. I could not implant in my own son the abiding seeds of faith. . . Having failed the Almighty in my mission with my son, how can I even think that I have succeeded with my thousands of followers. No, dear friends, I will cling to the sinking ship as long as it will keep afloat; and when the time comes, I will go down with it. • · 151

Reb Itzikel had held his beloved pupil, David, in great esteem. He was especially elated when during the Rabbi's critical dispute with his son, Yosef, David had decided to return to his father's home, where he would be away from evil influences and from the contagion of the Revolution and its atheistic, Bolshevist ideology. On the day of David's departure to Struga, Reb Itzikel overflowed with gratitude to the Lord of the Universe for His grace in giving David such a genuine love of the Torah a love which he had failed to kindle in his own child. In David's decision, Reb Itzikel was able to see the merit and justification of his own further existence; he was animated both spiritually and physically; he felt exhilarated. Nor did he make a secret of this, and shared his inner joy with his relatives and friends. Several times that day he expressed his pleasure at the nice weather, although actually the sky was overcast, and it was raining heavily. He patted Miriam's cheek - a display of affection he rarely indulged in and remarked gleefully: "Daughter, you're not a child any more -'may you be spared the evil eye!' I hardly noticed until now how grown up you are. God willing, I'll get a bridegroom for you the likes of whom is hard to find!"

In the midst of his usual daily prayers in the synagogue, he intoned the passage: "Many are the thoughts in a man's heart; but the counsel of the Lord, that shall stand," in a way which inspired the rest of the worshippers to increase the fervency of their chanting.

The Rabbi's cheerfulness and high spirits disappeared on the following day. His closest friends expressed concern about the strange change in his mood, but refrained from discussing it. Reb Itzikel alone knew the cause of his depression, and did not reveal it to anyone. At the root of it was a dream a strange dream! The memory of it robbed him of the desire to pore over Holy Writ, and even of the power of reasoning. A dream is generally relegated to oblivion upon waking but not so this one. It remained clear in all its details and he could not dismiss it from his mind. . 

He had dreamed that he was a shepherd tending his flock of sheep in the shade, when dense black clouds sud- 152

denly glided across the sky, and in the accompanying blast of thunder he could distinguish a voice calling: "Isaac,

Isaac - take two of your best sheep, and proceed to the 

Struga hills! There you will sacrifice the two sheep for me!"

He walked and walked and his feet sank in clay. He was at the point of exhaustion; his shirt stuck to his sweating body, but he kept plodding on and on. At long last, the mountains were within sight; he could plainly see their towering summits reaching into the clouds - and again he heard the voice: "Isaac, Isaac - it's not the sheep I crave as a sacrifice, but your beloved pupil, David. . .” He was seized with a terrible trembling, and as he looked about him, he saw David tied to a tree with a stout rope. David was calm, but large tears, like pearls, rolled down over his face, and his expression seemed to call to him with a heartrending, silent plea...

Reb Itzikel awoke drenched in perspiration, his heart pounding. He could not doze off again. He felt a distressing premonition which he was unable to shake off. 

A few days later, when Reb Itzikel had succeeded in mastering his feelings and was engrossed in a Talmudic tractate, a tumult in the courtyard reached his ears. He looked out of the window and saw a peasant lad on horseback. He could not understand how this boy could cause such a turmoil. He was about to return to his study when the door flew open and Shimmon, the Elder, burst in exclaiming: “Rabbi, Rabbi, a misfortune - a great misfortune has happened! Reb Avrum Shanan of Struga has been murdered!"

Reb Itzikel felt a stabbing pain in his chest which seemed to constrict him in an iron vise, to the point of suffocation. 

The blood vessels in his throat tightened to the point of bursting. The muscles running from the nape of his neck along both shoulders and arms twisted and stiffened in excruciating pain. His body was bathed in sweat and his limbs felt chilly and clammy.

Disguised in peasant garb, David entered carrying the two trussed sheep on his shoulders. The excited throng that had gathered, quieted down and stepped aside making way for him to pass. He advanced toward the Rabbi without uttering 153

a word, and stood before him. Reb Itzikel stared, frightened, at David. In his state of shock he was aware of the big tears, like pearls, that trickled from David's eyes and rolled down over his face. His expression wore the same silent, heartrending plea, which it had when he saw him in his dream. . . . 

Reb Itzikel's face was deathly pale; his nose seemed more pointed, and his blue, bloodless lips quivered pitifully. The cold, clammy perspiration had benumbed his body. His glazed eyes turned upward, and seated in his chair, he lapsed into unconsciousness.

Reb Itzikel had regained consciousness, but was bedridden for the rest of his life. Doctor Torchinsky ordered absolute rest and quiet for the patient, whose heart was so impaired and weakened, that he could no longer stand up. "The least excitement or agitation could prove fatal in his case," the physician informed the Rabbi's family and friends after he had been summoned to attend to the stricken sage. "His heart is badly damaged, and we have no medication for such a condition. The only hope for his recovery is in his remaining completely calm. First of all — no visitors! 

Neither relatives nor strangers. Is that clear? Neither relatives nor strangers!" 

Shimmon, the Elder, bent his clumsy body over the physician's hands, and kissing them, blurted out tearfully: "Doctor, I'll take good care of the Rabbi — I'll permit no visitors at all! Doctor, I'll follow all your instructions. 

Please save my Rabbi!"

As usual, Shimmon faithfully lived up to his word. He nursed Reb Itzikel day and night, not leaving him for a moment. The Rabbi hovered between life and death for several months. Because of his weakened heart, his lungs would become flooded with a foamy, dirty-brown phlegm, and suffocating, he would struggle with all his might to draw breath into his overloaded chest, panting, snorting and wheezing. His lips were blue; his eyes bulged as he desperately sought air; cold sweat covered his pale face. "When this happens, you won't have much time to run for 154

me,” Dr. Torchinsky told Shimmon during the first of such attacks. “If you wish to save your Rabbi you must know what to do! Every moment is valuable. The loss of a single minute may prove fatal! When one of these spasms of suffocation occurs again, compress his right or left arm tightly with whatever you have at hand – some string, a sash, or a towel. A blue vein will bulge out there. Pick up a knife that's as sharp as a razor, run it through the flame of a candle several times, and then make a long cut with its point in the bulging vein. As the blood spurts, place a glass underneath the flow until it fills up, then loosen the tie. At the same time, keep a cylinder of oxygen over his mouth and nose. And remember - always keep a reserve of several cylinders of oxygen in his room because without oxygen he'll suffocate."

Shimmon slashed away at the bulging veins of the Rabbi's gaunt arms, and with the cylinders of oxygen, sustained his phlegm-soaked, barely functioning lungs. The attacks of flooded lungs diminished little by little, and a slight improvement was marked in the patient's condition. Doctor 

Torchinsky compounded medicines in his own laboratory in the hospital to strengthen the patient. These mixtures spread a pungent medicinal odor throughout the courtyard. 

The Rabbi swallowed the bitter liquids like a submissive child, eyeing Shimmon with deep, quiet gratitude. "That Shimmon is a peculiar fellow," Doctor Torchinsky once said to Miriam, when she called at the hospital for one of her father's medicines. "He's as devoted and loyal to his master as an obedient dog. He has the body of a bear, and the soul of a child. A peculiar — a very peculiar fellow. You ought to be thankful to him, and to him alone, for your father's survival." 

"Doctor Torchinsky," Miriam said, "I beg of you - please tell me the truth. How is my father getting along? Does he have a chance to recover?" "I'm sorry to tell you, my dear, that the situation is rather critical. Your father is in grave danger and his prospects are not much to speak of. His heart has resigned. 155

Truthfully speaking, to me it's a wonder - even a miracle - that he is still among the living. I can't understand by what mysterious power he survives." "The situation then appears hopeless, Doctor," Miriam said flatly. "How long do you think my father can last?” 

Doctor Torchinsky was amazed at the equanimity and self-control displayed by this young Jewish daughter — not at all in keeping with the usual Jewish temperament, he reflected. "It's hard to say, my dear," he answered. "It could happen any minute - today, tomorrow, in a week, a month, two, or maybe a year. It's hard to predict. He is critically ill- and anything is possible. . . ." "Doctor Torchinsky, do you happen to know young Shanan of Struga?" "Young Shanan of Struga? Shanan of Struga? No, I doubt if I've ever seen him. His mother died in this hospital several years ago and I think that his father was recently murdered by some Ukrainians. Why do you ask?" 

"Young Shanan - David is his name has been raised in our home since he was seven years old. He studied under the supervision of my father who became very much attached to him; he loved the boy as much, if not more, than if he were his own child. During the past few days, my father has been asking to see him. But Shimmon won't permit ithe adheres strictly to your order of 'No visitors - neither relatives nor strangers!' Doctor, I believe that seeing David would brace my father a great deal. If you think that such a visit wouldn't prove dangerous in any way, I'd be very grateful to you for fulfilling his wish." "Yes, of course - this could be arranged," the Doctor agreed. "But tell me, my dear, what does young Shanan mean to you?" 

Blushing slightly, Miriam answered calmly: "I have a feeling that my father wishes very much that David and I should become engaged. He always hoped that his daughter would become betrothed to the son of his most beloved friend and most devoted follower, Avrum Shanan of Struga." "That's your father's wish. Is that also your wish, my dear?" 156

-- "I don't know. We've practically grown up together. I think I'm fond of him — but not in the way a woman loves a man; more the way a mother loves her child. He's terribly attached to the memory of his mother; it often seemed to me that he lived and breathed in her shadow and that irked me. I used to be jealous of his dead mother, and did everything in my power to wean him away from her. 

At last, I succeeded in taking his mother's place. 

"You mean to say that he's juvenile spiritually and physically that he lacks the physical and spiritual strength of an adult?" 

"It isn't that; he's quite strong physically. He spent his childhood in the Carpathian Mountains. He's tall, handsome, and well-built, too. Spiritually, he's a giant. His ability in studying is unique. He prepared himself in a few short months, and entered the eighth class of the State Academy. 

He intends to study medicine. He has a strong will and character, he fights for his convictions, and expresses them freely.

He joined the Bolshevist party recently because he wants to avenge the murder of his father. No, physically and spiritually he is quite strong and yet somehow, still a child. . .' 

Doctor Torchinsky eyed Miriam sympathetically, then removed his pince-nez, nodded his gray head, and with halfclosed eyes, remarked: 

"Wife and mother, man and child – these are old conflicts, my dear, very old conflicts. The strongest man is often the most frail child; and the most ardent, passionate love for a woman is often love for a mother!"

Miriam was not wrong in her guess. Reb Itzikel was highly pleased with David's first visit, and tried his utmost to stay calm and unruffled. Doctor Torchinsky had admonished Shimmon that the visit to the patient must not exceed five minutes and must be kept free of emotional disturbance. Reb Itzikel knew that Shimmon would carry out the 

Doctor's orders faithfully, and would not deviate from the prescribed five-minute limitation even by a second. He was also aware that he had no alternative but to submit to the strict regimen governing his convalescence, and he was, therefore, anxious to pass on to David as quickly as possible the 157

thought that held his attention to the exclusion of all else. "David, my son," the ailing Rabbi began in a low, weak voice, "your father, of blessed memory, and I never discussed it openly, but in our hearts we had hoped that in due time you and Miriam would become engaged. It was God's will that your father depart from this life in such a tragic way; it was likewise God's will that I become a hopeless invalid as weak as a new-born babe. Come here, my son give me your hand, and let it be in good luck... When my health improves a bit, the Lord willing, we will hold a formal betrothal as is customary among Jews." 

David extended his hand to the Rabbi, fully conscious of the sublime joy that coursed through his being. He was about to speak when, Shimmon motioned to him, saying: "David, you'll forgive me, but the Doctor ordered strictly not more than five minutes." He escorted the visitor to the door, and there, whispered in his ear: "Good luck, David, and may this news bring the Rabbi a speedy recovery."

His five-minute visit with Reb Itzikel had awakened in David feelings which he had never before experienced. "I am a bridegroom and Miriam is my bride!" he kept musing, and could scarcely believe that it was true. Many times, in his daydreams he had imagined himself engaged, and eventually married to Miriam, but it had always appeared to be a far-away dream. The sudden realization of this dream filled David with a consummate feeling of masculine maturity. 

David was seized by an overwhelming desire for Miriam. 

He craved the glowing sweetness of her body, her ardent, tender embraces, the unforgettable pleasure, and the soothing, deep gratification that only she could afford him. 

David had been bound to her since that day of his departure for Struga, when Miriam had first submitted to him in the heat of her passion, and since his return, he could not be without her for long. After a day or two at most following a tryst with Miriam, his youthful blood would begin to seethe within him. He would pace about restless and fidgety, would find it difficult to eat, drink, sleep, study, and even to think rationally. Various details of his last intimate meet- 158

ing with her would flit through his mind, and he would conjure up erotic pictures of their last meeting. . . Feeling irresistibly drawn to her, he would find her, and they would join together again in the "Newlywed's Home," in the dense orchard bordering the Rabbi's court.

There, kneeling before her, he would bury his head in her lap and his trembling hands would rove over her hips, her slender body, while he murmured: "Miriam, I love you so much - I cannot be without you, I cannot go on without you! Don't leave me alone – I want you forever near me. . .'

He spoke in a barely audible voice, the passion within him setting his limbs aquiver.

She would hold his head and draw it close to her face, suffused with a secret and triumphant smile, sealing his halfopen, wistful mouth with her own burning lips. Then he would become as weak and submissive as a child. . . She would loosen her clothing, press his head against her round, firm breasts, caress him with all the endearments inspired by her motherly instincts, and encourage his hands to wander wildly over her naked body. He would burrow into her with all the sinews of his ebullient body, in the most vigorous passionate love. . . "Mother, mother, dear mother!" he would murmur in his intoxication, and in the ecstasy of mutual, interminable bliss and happiness, she would moan in response: "Little David, my baby! My boy!" 

Miriam too, had taken it for granted that David would be her fiance and husband. But she was reluctant to think or talk about this, because it made her sad she felt a vague apprehension about David becoming her husband. True, she was in love with him, and was enraptured over her intimate relations with him; she adored his longing dreaminess and his tenderness; she relished his childish delight, when she held him to her body helplessly entranced. Yet she had a strange disquieting feeling that love for a man was different from her love for David.

David sought for Miriam, and as they made their way into the orchard, he hugged her close exclaiming: "Miriam, Miriam, we're engaged! Our dream is realized at last!" He 159

showered her with passionate kisses, his lips wandering over her forehead, eyes, the tip of her nose, her mouth, then lingering at her throat. "David dear, you're choking me," she gasped, as she tried to free herself of his grip. "You're mine - you're my fiancee, my wife! I'm the happiest man in the world. I feel as though I've suddenly become the ruler of the entire world!"

He grasped both of her hands and spread them wide, while his loving gaze hungrily roved over her from head to foot. Then he embraced her again. “Miriam dear, I want you. I want you with all my might. I've never felt like this before! I no longer feel like a child at his mother's breast, but like a man, manfully possessing his wife." 

Miriam lay there, dispirited. David's sudden roughness and flaunting of manly prowess had depressed her. She now knew that just as David's usual, tender, childlike love was completely gratifying to her, his raw, blatant aggressiveness as a mature male, was pitifully inadequate and disappointingly empty for her. . .

Reb Itzikel's condition did not improve. He became weaker, day by day, until he was incapable of the slightest physical effort. "Air - I have no air," he would complain. "Shimmon, please put another pillow under my head. The higher my head is, the easier it is for me to breathe."

It was difficult for him to eat or drink because he lacked the energy to chew and to swallow food. He would doze off, exhausted, immediately after eating the few tablespoonsful of food which Shimmon fed him, as one feeds an infant. 

"Rabbi, try," Shimmon would plead with him; Please take another spoonful – you hardly have any strength left." "Shimmon, my dear friend Shimmon, what a kind heart the Lord has given you! I cannot eat it seems that my body doesn't require any more food. . . I would eat, if only for your sake, but I can't I can't I can't, my friend." 

With a bowl of broth in one hand and a spoon in the other, Shimmon would stand bowed over the patient. Reb 

Itzikel would doze off again and his labored breathing, ac- 

160

companied by a steady, weak moan, would punctuate the prevailing quiet; the spoon trembling in Shimmon's big hand would touch the rim of the bowl, faintly sounding a staccato, bell-like ring; the tears glistening in Shimmon's red-rimmed eyes would reflect the sun's rays which streamed in through the high window, and illuminated the pale face of the dying Rabbi.

Throughout the long siege of Reb Itzikel's illness, Shimmon never lost faith that his beloved Rabbi would get well, thanks first to the Lord, and then to Doctor Torchinsky. He carried out all the Doctor's orders as though they were the Lord's precepts, and felt confident that the Doctor's remedies would speed the Rabbi's recovery. Doctor Torchinsky often said to Miriam: "That Shimmon is a remarkable individual.

He possesses great faith. It may surprise you to know, my dear, that he has even influenced me. Though I'm aware that hardly anything can be done for your father, nevertheless, I keep preparing these bottles, with a secret feeling that maybe they will mysteriously achieve the impossible after all. 

For the first time since the Rabbi's sickness, Shimmon sensed today that he had lost the battle. As long as Reb 

Itzikel had behaved like the average ailing mortal who is anxious to get well and is pleading for help, Shimmon had been full of hope. Today, however, something that Shimmon had long dreaded had taken place. When Shimmon, as usual, had offered a spoonful of medicine to the Rabbi, the latter had pushed it gently aside, saying: "No there's no need for it any more." "Rabbi, this is a fresh bottle of medicine," Shimmon pleaded. "Miriam brought if from Doctor Torchinsky only yesterday." "My dear friend, the care that I am in need of cannot be found in Doctor Torchinsky's bottle, because He❞— the Rabbi pointed upward — “did not put it in there." 

The Rabbi appeared to be completely transformed: gone was the pitiful wretchedness of a critically ill person; gone was the anguish in his eyes, which once again showed the clear vision with which they had previously been able to 161

discern the deepest secrets of those at whom they looked. 

Shimmon felt anew the full impact of the Rabbi's holiness, and shivering slightly, mumbled: "Rabbi, I'm frightened - I don't understand your words..." "Our great Prophet Jeremiah says, 'Heal me, O Lord, and 

I shall be healed,'" Reb Itzikel answered. "First, one has to merit the Lord's healing. . . Yes, Shimmon, my friend, His healing is the essence, the very essence. 

"Holy Rabbi," Shimmon muttered, as he retreated in awe from the patient's bed. The vials of medicine resting on the nearby tabouret seemed to lose their magic, and in Shimmon's eyes, appeared to be profaners of the Divine Presence with which the Rabbi was now endowed. Shoving them aside, Shimmon took the Book of Psalms, and in a doleful voice proceeded to intone: "Hide not Thy face from me in the day of my distress; incline Thine ear unto me; in the day when 

I call, answer me speedily."

A smile of peace and calm crept over the Rabbi's face. He breathed more easily now and his moaning ceased. Shimmon hastened to the synagogue, and exclaimed to the handful of worshippers who appeared for the morning prayers: "Fellow-Jews, the Rabbi is gravely ill! We have to implore the Lord for mercy! Let us all recite Psalms!"

The news of the Rabbi's critical state spread like wildfire and before long the synagogue was jammed with men and women. Through their sad intoning of the Psalms, they pleaded for their Rabbi, and opened their embittered hearts steeped in gloom over the present ominous days. 

It was on that solemn day of prayer for the Rabbi's survival that the new War Commissar of Polipovka, Stephan Smirnov, appeared in the House of Worship, determined to find Miriam.

David opened the door slowly, and tiptoed into the Rabbi's chamber. Reb Itzikel's pale, haggard face blended with his white beard. Smiling wanly, he said faintly, yet distinctly: “David, my son - it's good to see you. Come here, sit near me. I want to chat with you a bit - it's been a long time since we talked. There is no official betrothal yet such is 

162

probably God's will. doesn't consult us. He keeps his own accounts and 99 "Rabbi, don't try to talk. It's too much of an effort for you," David pleaded. "You have to relax - Doctor Torchinsky has ordered complete rest for you." · • · "Doctor Torchinsky is a fine man a kindly man and a good Doctor - because he has God in his heart. A good Doctor must have faith. He must always bear in mind that he is merely the Lord's messenger, that the "Healer Of 

All Flesh" is the One who always keeps vigil for him." "Rabbi, in this day and age medicine is a science which explains the specific facts of illness and health,” David said. "The time when the sick were cured by means of witchcraft and miracles is gone. The skill and knowledge for treating sickness depends nowadays on medical science." "Even though medicine is a science," the Rabbi replied, "the true physician must be more than a scientist. He must also possess soulfulness. Science, my child, is an important advance of the human mind and of human achievements in the material, practical realm, but it has nothing to do with the human soul. Let's take the heart as an example. Science can tell us about the heart's structure, about its chambers and vessels, and the manner in which they function in health and in illness. Scientifically speaking, the heart is quite likely a remarkable machine; and when a machine breaks down, naturally, the 'mechanic' must know how to repair it. That all seems very simple, but it really isn't. Because the human heart is more than a mechanical apparatus - it's also the source of human tribulation and joy, of love and hatred, of good and evil; it's the place of soulfulness. Often, very often, my child, one's heart suffers not because its mechanism is impaired, but because the soul is distressed and no one can enter these inner recesses of the heart scientifically. 

To do that, one has to be worthy of becoming the true emissary of the "Healer Of All Flesh..." Reb Itzikel fell silent, exhausted, and closed his eyes. His breathing was short and rapid, and on both sides of his neck the swollen blue vessels pulsated laboriously, in unison with the irregular beating of his heart. 163

David sat on the edge of the bed, eyeing the Rabbi and silently admiring him. How confident and clear are his ways - ways of righteousness and godliness! How good it is to follow in his footsteps! David stood up to leave the room, but Reb Itzikel opened his eyes and remarked, smiling: "Don't go yet, David. I'm not sleeping - I merely closed my eyes." "Rabbi, you're tired. Perhaps I should come back later?" "No, don't go yet; I haven't finished my conversation with you. How are you progressing in your studies, David? Do you devote a little time to the study of Holy Writ?" 

David lowered his head and said nothing. He did not wish to grieve the ailing man further, by letting him know that he had not opened a Talmudic tractate since he had enrolled in the State Academy. "Thank you - many thanks." "Thanks for what?" · · "For feeling the way you do - guilty and uncomfortable. 

The roots of truth are deeply embedded in you.
