I thank you, Lord, for this great grace; and let it also be a ray of solace and light in the gloomy, forsaken grave of my devoted friend Avrum of Struga. . . "Rabbi, forgive me, but I don't understand. . . You are undoubtedly aware that since the murder of my father, I have followed Yosef's ways - I've enlisted in the Revolutionary movement. I've become a Communist.” "David, my son, when our Father, Jacob, sensed that his hour had come, he summoned his sons and said to them: 'Gather yourselves together, that I may tell you that which shall befall you in the end of days.' Jacob was well aware of his children's merits and shortcomings, their good and bad traits. Judging them by their ambitions, in the days of their beginning, he could forsee the outcome, in their days to be." "Rabbi," David asked breathlessly, "do you believe that my ambitions at the dawn of my days justify what I am to become ultimately?" "Yes, my child. Your father planted in you a sense of tradition and self-esteem; your mother developed love and 164

goodness in you; the Torah sustained you with faith and wisdom; and you were endowed by God with the best soil in which to receive these things, so that they would be incorporated in you forever. . . Yes, my child. . . you could be worthy. becoming the true emissary of 'Healer Of All Flesh. 999 • • The Rabbi barely managed to utter these words, and with a last deep gasp for breath, expired. David lay his rad on the Rabbi's motionless chest, and crying bitterly, intrmured, "Rabbi, Rabbi, how can I be worthy? How?"

Two months had passed since Stephan Smirnov had assumed the duties of War Commissar in the Polipovka district. 

In the course of this relatively short period, by means of a series of purges, he had plunged the entire region into a state of panic and terror. The mere mention of "Smirnov" was sufficient to make one's blood run cold. "To destroy the counter-revolution at its roots!" came to be his favorite slogan. He proceeded to hound and to persecute ruthlessly all the elements that "Throw dark shadows across the bright light of the Revolution." "We'll break the backbone of that giant monster, the bourgeoisie!" Smirnov raved, banging his fist on the table, while addressing a meeting of local party leaders. "We'll destroy the sinister forces of slavery! We'll curb and stifle clericalism from poisoning the minds of the people! We'll crush and stamp out the entire merchant class, who stuff their fat bellies and their bulging pockets through their nefarious speculations! We'll shoot the manufacturers and gentry down like dogs! A living manufacturer is a leech, ready to suck up the workers' blood! A living country esquire is a cancer that is always ready to devour the poor peasant. The firing squad for them - without a fuss!" 

The terror raged on. No one was certain that he would not be dragged out of bed at midnight and "in the name of the Soviet government arrested!" No one was certain that he would not be accused of counter-revolutionary acts and sabotage - that he would not be indicted as "an enemy of the people" before the Military Tribunal. 165

Smirnov had no need of special proof in order to purge someone. A suspicion was enough, especially if the past of the accused had the least relation to clericalism, business activities, or property ownership.

The sessions of the Military Tribunal were open to the public, and were held in the auditorium of the Polipovka Peoples' House. Smirnov acted as chairman of the Court, and the fate of the accused was usually determined and sealed even before the trial began. The entire procedure of reading the bill of indictment aloud, and hearing the testimony of witnesses, was merely a formality. The verdict, almost invariably prepared in advance, and always the same, was concise: "The prisoner has been found guilty of treason against the Soviet Government by the Polipovka Military Tribunal, and is being sentenced, as an enemy of the people, to the highest degree of punishment - to be shot! The sentence is to be carried out at once. No appeals!"

The Polipovka Prison was situated in the town's northern suburbs. A prison, like all prisons, surrounded by a high stone wall containing a huge, massive gate fashioned of dry old timber and reinforced with iron bars and bolts - it had, at one time, been a popular landmark of the city. One would frequently hear such expressions as, "Come, I'll accompany you as far as the prison"; or, "How far is it? As far as from the market place to the prison"; or "It was so noisy, that their voices could be heard in the prison.”

The inhabitants of Polipovka had also loved to stroll near the prison. A big, sprawling meadow, replete with multicolored wild plants and bushes, extended from the prison all the way to the Polipovka forest. For generations it had been a favorite romantic meeting-place for many a lovelorn couple, in the cool shadows of its tall, massive, century-old oak trees, until Smirnov had chosen the edge of the forest bordering upon the open space of the prison, as the place at which his Tribunal carried out its executions. Then the beloved meadow came to be the symbol of a tragic via dolorosa past which marched those doomed to death by the 166

Tribunal of the Red Terror. The romantic air of the forest vanished; people referred to it now as "the Slaughterhouse," and to Smirnov, as the "butcher."

According to the regulations of the "Center," the Tribunal had to consist of five members: three judges, a prosecutor, and an attorney for the defense. Smirnov abided by the regulations - he and two adjutants of the War Commissariat comprised the chief three judges, while Yosef Shneierson of the Political Commissariat and Mikhail Gruzinov of the Commissariat of Culture and Education alternated as prosecutor and defender.

When Smirnov was first authorized by the center to establish a military tribunal in this district, he had convened the commissars of the local Soviet, and declared: "Comrades, the Tribunal is a military one; the judges will therefore be representatives of the active Red Army. They will consist of me, as War Commissar, and of two other comrades of my commissariat. According to the regulations of the center, a certain formality is required in the procedure of the Tribunal. We've got to have a prosecutor and a defense attorney." "Comrade Smirnov," Yosef voluntereed, "I'd be delighted to fill the post of prosecutor. I think it would be in keeping with my duties in the field of political education." "Comrade Gruzinov," Smirnov then turned to the Commissar of Public Instruction, "you are appointed defense attorney. A mere formality, you understand — you won't overwork yourself in defending. 

"Comrade War Commissar," Gruzinov objected, “can you tell me how a Communist, and a Commissar of People's 

Education, can possibly undertake the defense of a counterrevolutionary? Why that's absurd! A civilian attorney should be drafted for that task- they're skilled in that sort of thing. 

They can defend guilty ones - they've done it all their lives!" "We can't permit that, Comrade," Smirnov retorted. "We must not allow an outsider to take charge of the defense; it's too risky. The trials are open to the whole public, and civilian attorneys with their inbred principles of justice are 167

likely to evoke the people's sympathies — a factor that's got to be avoided. We're not engaged here in some contest of opposing attorneys - this is a purge, Comrades!" "I can't see where political education differs from people's education," Gruzinov argued more calmly. "If the Political

Commissar feels that the prosecution is in line with the principles of his department, I don't see why it doesn't conform to the principles of my commissariat, just as much. 

Why must I be the defender?" "I agree with Comrade Gruzinov," Yosef interposed. "Both commissariats have to work hand in hand. I propose that 

Comrade Gruzinov and I alternate in the duties of prosecuting and defending at the trials of the Tribunal. That will prove, to a certain extent, the solidarity and co-operation existing between the two Commissariats." 

Later, on their way home Gruzinov said to Yosef: "Comrade Shneierson, I want to thank you for the quick solution you gave to the critical situation that arose at today's session. I don't see why our co-operation shouldn't be extended even more." "I'm ready to co-operate, Comrade Gruzinov," Yosef assured him. "Comrade Shneierson," the other continued, "it's high time to introduce the reforms of our Party into the local State Academy. The old director, Maximov, has to be removed. 

He's too deeply rooted in Czarism - it's too late to remedy that. We've got to install a Party member as director of that institution. Comrade Shneierson, you're the right candidate for that post." "Thank you, Comrade Gruzinov. It's a great responsibility and I hope I won't disappoint you." "Comrade, I would appreciate your keeping this decision of mine secret until it becomes officially promulgated," 

Gruzinov added. "Certainly, Comrade, certainly. Secrecy is one of the cardinal principles in Party discipline."

Count Mishkin could not become reconciled to the idea that the government of the huge Russian Empire would 168

remain in the hands of the Bolsheviks. "No, Kravlenko, it cannot happen and will not happen!" he exclaimed, after Vassilii had brought him the latest communication of the "champion and liberator" of the Ukraine, Ataman (Hetman) Petlura. The edict declared that the Ukrainian Army must disband temporarily, because it could no longer withstand the pressure of the Bolsheviks. This communique concluded with the following rallying-cry: "Atamans, fighters and Ukrainian brothers! Don't give up the fight! Don't surrender to the commissars! Don't give up your arms! Disperse and go into hiding in the steppes, forests, and hills of our Ukraine! From your hideouts, swoop down and attack the enemy wherever possible! Have patience and faith! The time of complete liberation will come, and the heroes will emerge from their secret underground into the open spaces of a liberated Ukraine!" "Your Excellency," Vassilii asked impatiently, "what's to become of our platoon in Struga? Close to two hundred men - a small army in itself! It's no longer possible to remain in the village."

The Count did not even hear the question, and pacing nervously up and down in his study, he raved: "Impossible! I don't believe it, Kravlenko! It's merely a temporary victory on their part. A band of ragamuffins under the leadership of Trotsky that near-sighted little Jew, with the goatee, couldn't possibly conquer and lord it over such a mighty Empire as Russia! What does he think he is concocting - a pudding for the Sabbath? The hundred and eightyfive million Russians won't permit it." "That's right, Your Excellency! But in the meantime, what are we to do with our squadron?” "Yes yes, the squadron. . . One can't hide two hundred soldiers, especially cavalrymen, under one's apron. Get your men together and tell them about the decree." "Pardon me, Your Excellency. After all, I'm their hetman and they'll expect me to lead them, as I did before we joined the Ukrainian Army." "No, Kravlenko, we're in a different situation now. You, along with two of your most trustworthy fellow-soldiers, will 169

-- remain with me. We'll hold out in this palace as long as possible. In the event of an emergency we'll cross the Dniester to Rumania. Definite plans will have to be worked out plans that can be carried out promptly and without complications, if an emergency arises. Yes, three of you will stay with me; as for the rest thank them in my name and let them go with God's blessing." 

"At your service, Your Excellency! Your orders will be carried out! Permit me to say that Serebryak will be one of those staying with me." "Very well. After all, he's been one of us since the Petersburg days. A brave fellow indeed, and I believe, trustworthy. 

What about your second choice? Whom do you have in mind in addition to Serebryak?” "Fedor Zakuto, Your Excellency. We call him Fedka. We've always used him as a reconnaissance man. Quiet as a cat, he can sniff the enemy ten miles away.

During the first few weeks under the Bolshevist regime, the Count lived in a state of constant tension, expecting a Red Army detachment to pop up and take the palace by assault at any moment. But nothing of the sort happened. The calm of Struga, the solemn hush of the surrounding Carpathian Mountains, the tranquil flow of the Dniester, remained undisturbed. On a few occasions, several Bolshevik horsemen rode through the village and then disappeared. In general, however, one got the impression that the new rulers cared very little about Struga and what went on there. 

Affairs in Struga glided along in their usual pattern. Peasants, as always, tilled the soil; shepherds, as always, drove their flocks to pasture; church bells, as always, summoned the people to prayer and to funerals; and the villagers, as always, gazed with awe and humility on the Count's palace, which was surrounded by mountains, and whose towering, white marble pillars glistened through the slits in the encircling forest of pines.

Little by little, Count Mishkin was able to compose himself. The centuries during which his palace had existed undisturbed, and his undisputed heritage of wealth and 170

power lulled the Count again into a sense of security. He began to think that the fear and alarm with which he had been haunted during the past few weeks had perhaps been unfounded. He was reminded of the old soothsaying, "Threatening clouds with but a mere drizzle," and felt even more at ease.

However, his loneliness in the mansion and his isolation from people of his own social class began to irk him. He longed for social intercourse with those people with whom he had something in common with whom he could exchange views about the current grim situation, and reminisce about the glorious past.

The name of Doctor Torchinsky suddenly occurred to him. "I ought to invite him for a little vacation," the Count decided; "it would be pleasant to spend a couple of days with him here." "Kravlenko," Count Mishkin called out, "I wish to send a personal letter to a close friend in Polipovka — to invite him to be my guest for a few days." "At your service, Your Excellency! When do you want me to set out? Whom shall I ask for in Polipovka?” 

"Not you, Kravlenko - you're too well known in that area.

Send the other two; they are strangers there and will not attract any attention from friends or relatives. Let them pick out a plain coach, and harness only two horses the less of a display they make, the better and safer. When they arrive in Polipovka, let them steer clear of the main streets and center of the city. Tell them to proceed straight to the hospital, and hand over my letter directly to Doctor Torchinsky to no one else!" 

"At your service, Your Excellency! Whatever you wish it will be carried out."

The Count took a sheet of paper with his coat-of-arms embossed on it, and proceeded to write: "Dear Torchinsky: 

You'll undoubtedly be surprised to hear from me. 

I have dispatched two messengers to you, and I invite you to be my guest in my home, in Struga.

Let us spend a few days together and revive an old 171

friendship which I need now, more than ever before. So far, the changes of 'the outside' have not disrupted the calm of Struga. Please come, dear Doctor!

My body is not ill, but my spirit is struggling with longing and loneliness.

Your Count Mishkin" 

The death of Reb Itzikel had a strange and profound effect upon David. He was puzzled - why should the emotions he now felt be so similar to those he had experienced at the death of his mother - not his father, but his mother. Why?

Why had the Rabbi's demise opened the long-dormant wounds that had been inflicted on his spirit by his mother's death? 

David kept wondering. 

nor had

Gradually he came to a plausible conclusion. He had not witnessed the act of his father dying, as he had with the Rabbi - his father's was a violent death. He had never truly taken leave of him as he had of the Rabbi he ever heard any significant last words from him. . .

It was different, however, with his mother. He had witnessed her dying, had taken leave of her, and had heard her final words to him.

David knew that the initial step toward his medical career was to enter a university. His graduation from the State 

Academy, therefore, became the first stepping-stone on a long, arduous journey of preparation for his career. He had applied himself wholeheartedly to his studies; he had never missed a lecture, was always on time, was never remiss in his homework, and was the most conscientious and diligent student in the graduation class.

Both the faculty and his classmates regarded him as a sort of phenomenon. Among the graduates, he was the only 

Bolshevik and official Communist Party member, but his conduct and attitude toward his studies were so commendable, that it evoked admiration and surprise simultaneously. 

When the Bolsheviks seized power, discipline at the State Academy ceased completely. The revolutionary slogans of freedom, and of "destroying the old regime with its representatives" intoxicated the minds of the young students to 172

such a degree, that they became brusque, brazen and unruly. 

The faculty, being of “the old regime,” had to tolerate and indulge their disobedience. They had no other alternative. . . 

What baffled them most was the fact that the Bolshevik, Shanan, proved to be the most exemplary and ideal of all the students a blessing and delight for every teacher, anywhere, at any time.

David was so engrossed in his studies that even his meetings with Miriam became less frequent. Whenever he felt especially drawn to her, he would concentrate on his studies even more, and endeavor to withstand temptation by recalling Reb Itzikel's last words. . . He would often close his eyes, and struggling with his emotions, re-engrave in his mind the road to his goal: "To study medicine, I've got to enter the University! Graduation from the Academy will open those doors for me! This is my aim, first and foremost - nothing else matters!" 

David noticed that Miriam had changed considerably since Reb Itzikel's death. She became more pensive, more serious, and even somewhat indifferent about their meetings. Just as she had previously used any lame excuse for a tryst with him, to encourage and stimulate their mutually passionate relationship, she now resorted to all sorts of excuses to avoid any such rendezvous and to restrain him from those intimacies which had hitherto filled their lives with such happiness, in terms of both the present and of the future they could look forward to. 

At first, David attributed Miriam's transformation to her continued mourning over the tragic loss of her father, and to her realization of the importance of his graduation from the Academy. She did not wish to distract him from his studies, he thought, and felt grateful to her.

Then suddenly, something happened that threw him into utter confusion. He lay awake all night, racking his brain, unable to find a logical explanation to what had happened. 

He arose tired and depressed after his sleepless night. His head felt heavy, yet devoid of the slightest thought. 

He sat in his classroom, absent-minded, oblivious to every- 173

thing. When the instructor of Russian Literature concluded his analysis of Pushkin's The Prophet, he followed his usual custom of calling upon his students to answer certain questions, and turned to David. "Shanan, how would you interpret the poet's words, 'I sensed the throbbing of the heavens?" "

David did not reply; he did not even stand up, but remained completely absorbed in his own thoughts. This was such inconceivable behavior for the usually attentive and polite David, that Maximov stared at him and asked: "Shanan, what's the matter with you? Are you ill?"

The student seated alongside David nudged him, and the latter snapped out of his reveries, and said: 

"I beg your pardon, Ilya Grigorovich. I didn't hear your question nor your lecture, for that matter. I don't feel up to par. Will you permit me to be absent this morning?" "Yes, of course. Not only this morning - you can stay away for the rest of the day. I hope you'll feel better tomorrow."

David went outside. It was the beginning of April, and 

Spring had emerged rather early. The sky was crystal-blue, and the sun showered its glow and warmth on the earth. The air was filled with that wonderful fragrance of early Spring that animates the world with a strong urge for awakening and life. . . David paid no heed to nature's display. Details of the episode kept rushing through his mind over and over again:

It had happened at three on the previous afternoon. The 

Director had come into their classroom and announced that the astronomy teacher had been taken ill, and there would be no lecture. The students had, accordingly, dispersed an hour-and-a-half earlier than usual. 

On his way home that afternoon, David had succumbed to the Spring weather and decided to spend his unexpected leisure with Miriam. Not finding her in her room, he had gone out into the courtyard, where he saw her standing near the small gate of the fence. She wore a dark blue suit; her jacket, tight-fitting at the waist, sharply outlined her 174

tantalizing, firm breasts, and the slim skirt hugged her hips and flowed smoothly along her slender legs. The large red silk bow around the broad collar of her snow-white blouse, underlined even more her berry-red lips. From under a small blue hat that sat at a jaunty angle, her thick blonde curls cascaded along her straight back. How beautiful, how wonderful she was! "It's you, David! You're supposed to be at the Academy. 

What are you doing here?" she asked, obviously caught offguard by his sudden appearance. “Miriam dear - you're just gorgeous! The world in its 

Spring grandeur pales beside your beauty!” “David, darling-Spring has affected you," Miriam said dryly. "But tell me, what made you come here in the middle of the day?" 

"A miracle, my sweet a veritable miracle! The stars and fates in heaven have taken pity on me. They couldn't bear to see my burning longing for you any more! Those stars refused to submit to being studied by our astronomy teacher today, and I was suddenly rewarded with an eternity of freedom-an-hour-and-a-half, no less." 

He drew nearer to her to take hold of her hands and was on the point of whispering, "Dear one, let's go to our Garden of Eden. 99 Her reaction, however, stunned him. She turned swiftly aside avoiding the touch of his hands. He noticed a slight look of disgust on her face.

He felt a pang in his heart. His inborn pride and sensitiveness were painfully wounded, and he stood flabbergasted and insulted. "Miram, what's happened? I suddenly feel that my presence is obnoxious to you...' 

"David, don't talk nonsense. I'm simply not in the mood," she replied, patting his cheek. "Not today, dear - I don't feel up to it. It's a little tougher this month as a future doctor, you ought to understand. I'll go to my room and lie down for a while. You go back to your books, dear. There are only a few weeks left. I've heard that the graduation 175

exercises will take place during the May First celebration and I want you to excel, to be the first in your class." Then, planting a kiss on his forehead she turned to go.

As his eyes followed her, he was sorry about the resentment he had just felt towards her. After all, she was not her usual self right now. . . . even animals become tired and supersensitive during their menses, he knew. 

He felt comforted by this explanation, and breathed the fragrant Spring air freely again. His thoughts wandered back to his wonderful childhood in beloved Struga, and he yearned to be near the moist soil and blooming trees. He made his way into the orchard and wandered among the trees, caressing and hugging them, just as in the days when as a seven-yearold youngster he had first come to Polipovka to study Holy Writ.

Suddenly, he caught sight of Miriam. She came out of the house, paused for a moment, looked about a few times and then walked off at a fast pace. 

He was dumbfounded. He followed her, unable to account for his action at the moment. Only one thing mattered to him now: not to lose sight of her among the pedestrians, and come what may, to find out where she was hurrying so purposefully.

She turned into the narrow side street leading to the City Hospital. She must be sick, he reflected with alarm and remorse, and she was keeping it secret from him so as not to worry him. She was undoubtedly heading for Doctor 

Torchinsky's office. David wished to overtake her, to drop at her feet and beg her forgiveness. 

Too late! she had entered the hospital courtyard. But what was this? She avoided the entrance to Doctor Torchinsky's office, and turned aside to the wooded area of the hospital. She went further, advancing toward the stone bench... Someone was sitting there. . . Who could that be? 

What was going on? Why had Miriam been in such a hurry to come here? 

Hearing her footsteps, the figure jumped up from the bench, spread wide his arms and enveloped her in their embrace. 176

David was stunned by what he saw: Miriam and the War 

Commissar, Stephan Smirnov! With consternation he watched her lips cling thirstily to his. Then the two of them, still embracing, slowly slumped to the bench. . .

Bewildered and dejected, David roamed the streets all morning. A tangled skein of questions, for which he could find no rational answers, kept preying on his weary mind. 

He felt that he could not go on like this- he must get hold of himself. He must restore his peace of mind and the consistency of his convictions. 

"Why? Why are the foundations of my world crumbling as though they were built on sand? My beloved Struga - the place of the dreams of my childhood - shattered and crumbled like sand! The old traditional home of my parents, my mother's warmth of peace and security, my father's spiritual and material riches — everything shattered and crumbled like sand. . . The Rabbi's bright ways of Torah, humaneness and godliness shattered, and crumbled like sand. . . And now

Miriam - the queen in the kingdom of my dreams - shattered and crumbled like sand. . . Why? Where is my weakness to create? Wherein lies my failure to build? There's no one I can turn to; I'm suddenly so alone - I've remained so alone. 

In his absent-mindedness, David bumped into a squad of Red Army soldiers, their rifles drawn, with two prisoners in their custody. "Where do you think you're going? Out of the way!" a soldier barked, pushing him aside so roughly that he was thrown back and fell on the sidewalk. 

This sudden incident served to rouse David from his torpor. 

He jumped up, dusted off his trousers, and mechanically tagged along after the convoy. 

Like other onlookers, David, too, craned his neck and looked about him. Of the two prisoners in peasant garb, one was middle-aged, pot-bellied, with a red pock-marked face and flat nose; the other, with blonde hair and cheerful, lightgray eyes, was taller and appeared to be considerably younger. 

This younger prisoner seemed familiar but David couldn't 177

recognize him at the moment. He ransacked his tormented brain for a possible clue. Curiously enough, all those personal problems that had plagued him a moment ago had suddenly vanished! All that mattered to him now was to recall this prisoner's identity. 

Yes! David slapped his forehead. Of course, that's him!

The soldier who had stood beside his father's grave in Struga! 

The same soldier who had allowed Samborik to drive out of Struga, although he knew the lad in peasant garb hugging the sheep on the wagon was none other than me. . . The other soldiers had called him Fedka. It's him, all right! Twice at his mercy, and on both times he saved me from sure death - from Vassilii's murderous pursuit!

David hastened to the War Commissariat. A Red Guard halted him at the entrance: "Where to, Comrade? Let's see your document! You can't get in here without a permit!" 

"I'm a party member - my name is Shanan. I must see 

Comrade Smirnov at once- it's urgent!" "A Party member, did you say? That's a different story. 

The first door on your right."

David knocked on the door, and Smirnov's voice shouted "Enter!" 

Seeing David upset and excited, Smirnov at once assumed that this was the visit of a jealous and jilted fiance. “He must have found out about Miriam and me," Smirnov reflected, and resolved to take a firm attitude toward Jewish provincial hysteria. "Ah, it's you, Comrade Shanan come in, come in," Smirnov was unusually cordial. "You're rarely seen at Party meetings nowadays. I've heard that you're very busy with your graduation. Please sit down. Can I be of any service to you?" "Comrade Commissar, I wish to get your permission to visit a prisoner!"

Smirnov was taken aback by David's odd and unexpected request. At the same time, he felt happy inwardly for he would hardly have relished a jealousy scene. "Is it possible that he doesn't know it yet - or is he pretending not to know?" Smirnov mused. "He's anxious to visit a prisoner - 178

of all things! Well, the devil with him! Let's get rid of him!" "A permit to see a prisoner?" Smirnov reiterated, with an air of amazement. "Who is that prisoner? What's his name? 

What are your relations with him?” "I don't know, Comrade Commissar," David answered. "I only know that in his own crowd he's known as Fedka." "Comrade Shanan, coming from a Party member and from an outstanding graduate from the State Academy, your words are, pardon my saying so, stupid. Why are you so anxious to visit a prisoner whom you've never met, when all you know about him is that they call him Fedka? Tell me does that make any sense?" 

"Comrade Commissar, he saved my life! I accidentally recognized him. On two occasions, he could have handed me over to the executioner - and he didn't do it. . . When he saved me, I too, was a stranger to him; he didn't even know my name... Yet he actually saved me." "In other words, you're anxious to reciprocate: he once saved your life, and you now wish to repay him. That's mighty nice! I promise you that this fact will be taken into consideration during his trial. As a matter of fact, I would not object to you appearing as a witness for the defense. 

But for you, a Party member to visit him in prison - this is neither proper nor desirable." "I must see him, Comrade Smirnov. It is far more important than you imagine!" 

"Please cut out the mystery talk plainly. What's your connection with the prisoner?” "He's the only one who could lead me to the murderer of my father. . .' 99 "Comrade Shanan, I wish to call your attention to the fact that it's the Extraordinary Commission and the Military Tribunal that take care of cut-throats, counter-revolutionaries, saboteurs and the enemies of the people. It's your duty to pass on any pertinent information to the authorities. You mustn't initiate any steps of your own - it's against the law of the Soviet government. Incidentally, who is the murderer of your father?" "Kravlenko — Vassilii Kravlenko of Struga!" 

179

Smirnov suddenly changed. His face paled. His eyes glittered, and his lips quivered as he muttered under his breath: "Struga... the Count of Struga and his henchman, Kravlenko... The long-overdue account is about to be settled - at long last. . ." 

"Pardon me, Comrade, but I don't quite understand you.' "It's nothing. I was just thinking out loud, it's a weakness of mine. When I'm considering something serious, I often talk to myself. Comrade Shanan, the murderer of your father, along with the Count of Struga, are recorded in our files as dangerous enemies of the people. Both must be brought before the Military Tribunal! The circumstances, comrade, are different now and I feel justified in changing my attitude. You'll be permitted to visit the prisoner Fedka, because I believe you could be of help to the Party and the government. You may promise Fedka freedom in exchange for revealing the hiding place of the Count and of Kravlenko!" 

Fedka recognized David at once, and with eyes as merry as ever, gazed at his visitor. About David's offer of freedom, the prisoner spoke calmly, with a carefree smile: 

"You're kidding, brother. To help if possible — that I'll do; but to betray - never!" "Your life is in danger: you'll be tried on the charge of counter-revolution. But I can set you free - I've got the promise of the War Commissar.” "Thank you, brother. The price is not for me. They can't shoot me more than once, the devil with it! To help - yes; to squeal and betray - not Fedka!"

David put both his hands on Fedka's shoulders, gripped them tenderly, wheeled around and departed with a sense of shame.

The information which David could not obtain from Fedka, Smirnov obtained from Serebryak, without any effort. "You're a Muscovite, not a Ukrainian - so why are you wearing Ukrainian clothes?" Smirnov addressed him brusquely. "Why did you deceive the Red Guard when you tried to get into the city? Your alibi was that you were going to 180

the hospital to pick up an invalid, but they found a secret document from the Count of Struga in your possession. 

You're his spy! He's in charge of a counter-revolutionary organization that aims to destroy the power of the Soviets - and you are his spy! Therefore you'll be sentenced to death in less than twenty-four hours. . . . However, your life can be spared if you wish..." 

"Spare my life? How?" "Through serving the Soviet power!" "What am I supposed to do, your Excellency?" "There's no such a thing as 'Your Excellency!' All are equal! Everyone, big or little, is addressed as 'comrade!'" "What do you want me to do. . . Comrade?" 

"Hand him over that's what! You don't have to lay down your life for him! He wouldn't even let you touch a small portion of his nobleman's hide. Hand him over and then join our ranks."

Serebryak told all, and Smirnov used it. He learned every detail of the Count's daily routine in his palace at Struga. 

He found out about the Count's strategic plans, including the secret underground passageway leading to the Dniester through which the Count intended to escape. Rowboats, he learned, were moored at the bank so that one could easily use them to sneak across the river to the Rumanian side. 

Do Rumanians permit such boats to row across the 

Dniester to and fro?" "They pretend not to see — it's a common affair in Struga. 

From the bank one can shout across the river. The Samborik brothers row across practically every day." "The Samborik brothers? Who are they?" "Peasants of Struga - living on opposite sides of the Dniester." "A net of espionage and sabotage right under my nose!" 

Smirnov raged. "Smuggling and illegal emigration are going on without any restraint. I'll show them how to conduct themselves on the frontier - I'll shoot them down like dogs!"

From the moment that Serebryak and Fedka had left for Polipovka with a view to bringing Doctor Torchinsky back 181

for a visit to the Struga mansion, Count Mishkin became nervous, tense, and very impatient. He was well aware that a round trip to Polipovka and back would take at least three days, and also surmised that the doctor would require an additional day or two to arrange for the administration of his hospital duties during his absence. The Count could not anticipate the arrival of his visitor until four or five days hence, but nevertheless, he could not restrain himself from glancing at the clock every so often, and from jumping up at the least noise of horses' hoofs. He lost his appetite and could not sit still for long. During the day, he would pace the verandas and alleys of the palace nervously; and at night, he would toss and turn in the throes of insomnia. 

On the fourth day, when the sun retreated beyond the peaks of the Carpathian Mountains and a chill twilight settled on the mansion, the Count's tenseness turned into fear. A grim premonition constricted his chest and he experienced a choking sensation. "Kravlenko" he called out, "I have a feeling that something's wrong. If those two fellows have been trapped by the Reds, then we're in grave danger. "Go down to the shore and get a boat ready. If the two don't return by midnight, we'll cross to the other side. Go through the cave and make sure that all the doors open easily." "At your service, Your Excellency!" Vassilii answered submissively, and trudged off toward the "old wing" of the palace.

It was nicknamed "old wing," because it harbored all the antiques and heirlooms of the Mishkin dynasty: portraits of ancestors of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, bronze statues, rare old weapons, cuirasses, shields - and the family tombs. The alcoves, hewn out of the massive granite walls, contained the graves of the Count's ancestors, as far back as great-grandfathers of the preceding centuries.

In the center of this crypt, on a floor made of square carved rocks, was a heavy iron door. When it was opened, the rusty hinges would make harsh, grating sounds like the protesting voices of the dead, resentful of this intrusion upon 182

their eternal peace.

As Vassilii raised the massive door, he gazed down into a dark, yawning chasm, like the jaw of some pre-historic monster. He descended five stone steps and found himself in the underground cave.

According to the tradition of the "Mishkin dynasty," this cave was hallowed by the family as much as the sepulchre containing the corpses. The palace library and the annals of the count's family contained no information bearing upon the cave. Weird legends about that cave were rampant in the adjacent village. Some peasants believed that the Counts used to snare their opponents in that bleak dungeon, leaving them there to die of starvation and fear.

Holding a lantern, Vassilii made his way along the long, narrow subterranean passage, which was actually hewn through a side of the Carpathian Mountains. At the other end of the passage were five stone steps leading up, identical to those at the entrance. The end of the passage also had a massive iron door which opened into a bright, spacious terrace protected with high, wide windows. From here, one could see the gigantic ridges of the Carpathians and the dense pine woods, which reflected in the placid waters of the Dniester, looked like a congregation of worshippers bowed in silent prayer.

The terrace, flanked by two symmetrical, semi-circular rows of stone steps, and reinforced with white-marble balustrades, led to the bank of a small Dniester inlet. This spot, with its clean, white sand and crystal-clear water, served as a private swimming place for the Count and his guests. A flagstone pathway meandered along the hilly slopes from the bay to the palace.

On sultry summer days, the Count and his gay entourage of guests used to descend here, some on horseback, others driven in phaetons, to cool off in the clear, refreshing waters. After the bathing, the guests would gather on the terrace where they would recline on comfortable chairs or divans, while they chatted, sipped coffee and liqueurs, and nibbled on exotic delicacies.

No one dared to look at the door in the center of the 183

terrace floor; everyone was aware that it led to a subterranean cave, about which neither the Count, nor any of his household, ever so much as breathed a word. Now and then someone might have glanced at it furtively, but for the most part, it was conveniently ignored.

Close to the inlet, under jutting, bizarre boulders, and in the shadow of tall pine trees, there was a row of furnished cabins in which the guests of the palace enjoyed all the comforts pertaining to their bathing. At the end of this row of cabins was the boathouse, which extended right onto the water. Here, numerous row boats of all sizes were moored. 

Two wide half-doors opened from the boathouse onto the Dniester, and its waters were ever ready to transport any floating object from the interior. 

Vassilii reached the five steps leading out of the cave, raised the iron door with his powerful arms, and emerged on the terrace. Compared with the inky darkness of the underground passage, the dimness of the twilight seemed like bright sunlight. He put out the lantern and hurried down to the boathouse. He remained dumbfounded by what he saw the boathouse was empty! There was not a single boat in sight! He stood flabbergasted for a few moments, a chill slowly running down his spine. 

Suddenly he heard the sound of hurrying footsteps, accompanied by the jangle of spurs and dangling weapons. A hobnailed boot thrust open the side door of the boathouse and a Red Guard, with drawn rifle, stood framed in the entrance. He barked: "In the name of the Soviet Government - you're under arrest!"

As Vassilii emerged from the boathouse, his eyes fell upon scores of Red Army men posted along the entire shoreline. "How did they all get here?" he wondered, staring blankly. "They couldn't have gotten here from the highway. I myself reconnoitered that area on horseback for a stretch of ten miles, and didn't spot a single Red Army man! Unless they followed our secret path along the bank. . . But that's hardly likely! We ourselves have only recently beat a path through 184

the Verbivka Forest. As a matter of fact, only the Count, Serebryak, and I were aware of this secret path to the palace - no outsider had an inkling of it." 

"Don't drag, Comrade! Get going to the Commissar!

He's waiting for you," the Red Army soldier prodded Vassilii, and approaching one of the cabins, he knocked on the door. 

Stephan Smirnov appeared in the doorway. Vassilii stared, then shook his head in disbelief, like one who, stunned, tries to get his bearings. The few moments it had taken him to traverse the underground passage now seemed to him to have been an eternity. "Sergeant. .. Stephan. . . Smirnov. . ." Vassilii muttered under his breath. Across his mind there flashed the scene of ten years ago, when he had struck the sergeant in St. Petersburg. Now that sergeant had become War Commissar. · • 

"I'm lost, he muttered to himself. "I'm lost, and so is the Count - everything is lost..."

Smirnov had never forgotten the pain and vexation he had suffered during that St. Petersburg incident. The mere thought of it would infuriate him, and set his blood boiling. Then he would take comfort in the hope and aim of his life, to find that Count and his Ukrainian henchman, and settle the score with them.

It had taken ten interminable years, but now at last, he had attained his goal! There he stood before him, that

Ukrainian hayseed, just as he had stood before him in St. Petersburg. 

"What's your name? Where are you from? What did you do at home - pasture swine?" Smirnov shrieked, incited by the hatred and wrath this man still aroused in him, reiterating the questions he had fired at him back in St. Petersburg. 

Vassilii stood flabbergasted without uttering a word. He neither heard nor understood the yells of the Commissar.

One predominant thought kept pounding in his mind: “Lost - everything is lost. Everything. . ." "Comrade Commissar," another Red militiaman addressed 

Smirnov, "I didn't find anyone in the underground passage.

Guards have been posted at both entrances. Mishkin is under arrest in the palace. Everything has been carried out suc- 185

cessfully! All of Serebryak's information turned out to be correct!"

Enraged, Vassilii leaped at the Red guard, seized him by the lapels of his uniform, and thundered: "Don't you dare say that! You're a liar - a god-damned liar! Not Serebryak - Serebryak wouldn't betray! You sonof-a-bitch! Serebryak didn't betray! I'll crush you like a louse!"

The blast of a gun cut short Vassilii's growling. His ferocious expression suddenly changed to one of stupefied astonishment. He lost his grip on the soldier's lapels, his head drooped on his chest, his knees buckled, and his body slumped to the moist, sandy bank of the Dniester. 

Nonchalantly replacing his revolver in its holster, Smirnov commanded: "Comrades, mount the horses and to the palace!" 

The Red Cavalry troop lined up in military formation behind their leader. Smirnov, sitting erect in the saddle, faced his men and commanded: "Company! Sound off! Forward, march!"

The soloist of the detachment began to sing the familiar and favorite army song, "The Little Apple" (Yablohtchko): "Apple, apple, little apple, Roling down the track, You will tumble in a ditch, And we shan't see you back."

The rest of the men joined in, and the chorus reverberated in unison, the horses trotting along, and shaking their heads in rhythm to the tune. The singing Red horsemen turned into the paved, sloping road linking the inlet with the palace, and wound their way uphill, gradually disappearing from sight. In the enveloping dusk, the sounds grew terrifyingly, as the surrounding Carpathians sonorously echoed and re-echoed the song.

The bank was deserted. A breeze from the river blew lightly over the lifeless body of Vassilii Kravlenko. 

For the first time since the outbreak of the Revolution, the "Palace of Struga" was again animated and illuminated 186

- a sign of jubilant celebration. For the first time in countless centuries, the "Palace of Struga" received the unwelcomed intruders, the rulers in the name of the Dictatorship of the Proletariat instead of the welcomed guests of the nobility and of Imperial circle. 

The liveried coachmen and the luxurious phaetons were no longer in sight at the columned entrance. They were replaced by a drove of saddled horses that were neighing, stamping their hoofs, shaking their heads, and greedily devouring the oats and barley in the sacks dangling from their muzzles, their nostrils blowing away the clinging chaff onto the lush grass at the foot of the nearby palisades. 

In the huge reception-room of the palace, there were no banquet tables set with silver, crystal, and Chinese porcelain crockery. There were no lackeys and servants in gold-andsilver embroidered livery in attendance. They were replaced by a swarm of weary, hungry Red Army men, sprawling on the parquet floors like picnickers in a park. Some sat or reclined on their dufflebags and overcoats; others ate, and others scratched their itching, sweaty bare feet. The hanging crystal chandeliers shed a glaring light, and the reflection of the men in the long wall mirrors appeared like an endless throng of strange wanderers.

There were no strains of Strauss waltzes emanating from the palace; there were no cavaliers bowing chivalrously before the ladies, inviting them to dance, awaiting their feminine responses. This was now replaced by the drawnout sounds of an accordion accompanying the soldiers' songs. 

A soldier expressed his love-lorn emotion in an ardent Cossack dance, his booted feet moving rapidly over the smooth, gleaming parquet floor, tapped to the hand-clapping rhythm and hullabaloo of the rest of the servicemen as they joined in the merrymaking.

The Count of Struga, ruler of the palace, was not now in his General's uniform; he was not the genial host, smiling a courteous welcome to his guests. The Count of Struga was now a captive in a secluded corner of his palace. He was still under the impact of the catastrophe. "This can't be a reality" he mused. "It's a nightmare. . ." Why he had just 187

dispatched Kravlenko through the underground passage to the inlet to prepare a rowboat. He had been alone. 

Time was dragging painfully. Kravlenko should have been back by now. Where could he be? Growing impatient, he had walked off to the Old Wing. The entrance to the cave had been open - he had heard footsteps. What was going on?

His ears were ringing - could that be possible? He could hear the steps of several men and, it seemed, the jangle of spurs. Voices were drawing near. Whom was Kravlenko leading here? He felt stunned. Armed soldiers had surged up the steps one after another, and surrounded him. "Count 

Mishkin?" one of them had asked. "In the name of the Soviet 

Government - you're under arrest for high treason. ." And now he sat locked in the crypt.. 

"My premonition did not deceive me," he told himself now. "I missed my chance. Deliverance was within reach and too late. . . I waited too long I put everything at stake and lost. No, they won't put their paws on me! I'll not become a toy for their red tribunals. Strange, fate must have willed it that out of the hundred-odd rooms in the palace, I should chance to be in this crypt. 

99 The Count slowly looked about him. A long iron rod protruded above the alcove which contained his father's tomb. "How did that rod get here?" he marveled. "I've passed through here so many times, and never saw it before." 

As though walking in his sleep, the Count approached the alcove, staring at the iron bar. He mounted the stone plateau in front of his father's grave, and stood between the two halves of the thick, black plush portiers, holding on to their twisted draw-cord. He threw one end of the cord across the iron rod, fashioned a noose, slipped his head through it, and closing his eyes, plunged downward.

On the other side of the door, a wistful sentinel hummed the haunting refrain of "The Little Apple": "Apple, apple, little apple, Rolling down the track, You will tumble in a ditch, And we shan't see you back." 188

The Count's body swung to and fro like a pendulum. Long shadows pursued it, as though escorting the last of the Mishkin dynasty, and seeking a place of eternal peace for him in the sepulchre of his ancestors. . . 189

The Legacy 

When Smirnov returned from his expedition to Struga, the Secretary of his Commissariat met him with an air of anxious concern and a feeling of relief that he was back. 

The Secretary's anxiety was due to the recent unexpected arrival in Polipovka of a representative of the Central Committee of the Party, who had lost no time in demanding an immediate meeting of all the commissars of the region. Such sudden moves by the Centre Committee always disconcerted the Secretary, especially now, when his chief, Smirnov, had left on a punitive expedition and no one knew when he would be back. He breathed more freely now that Smirnov had returned. "Comrade Commissar," he greeted him, "I'm so happy to see you again. Everyone has showed up - you were the only one missing. The Comrade from the Centre has just arrived. 

You'd better hurry. Comrade the session is about to start." "Comrades," the representative of the Centre was saying, as he concluded his two-hour long lecture about the history of the revolutionary struggle and the further progress of the Party; "according to the reports we have received thus far, the activity in your district has been slow, phlegmatic and lethargic. Our enemies eagerly look forward to such weaknesses on our part. They lie in ambush biding their time for the right moment to pounce on us again and suck the blood of workers and peasants. I've been delegated here from the 

Central Committee with this warning to all Commissars: 190

'Comrades, it is imperative that every assignment be carried out at once.' Here are some concrete examples: "Comrade Gruzinov, your Commissariat of Education has submitted a report to the effect that the Czarist director of the Polipovka State Academy will be dismissed, and his position taken over by a Party member. So far, this has not been done. Delay and procrastination in carrying out a resolution are signs of weakness! The Central Committee orders that this dismissal be carried out at once! "Comrade Shneierson, you reported plans for the conversion of your parent's house into a club for Communist youth. Why are these plans lagging behind? The Cenrtal 

Committee commands that this club be opened at once without any further delay! "Comrade Smirnov, you've reported that the Dniester shore along the new frontier with Rumania is swarming with obstructionists, counter-revolutionists, and speculators. The 

Central Committee requires immediate action to ferret out and destroy these harmful and dangerous elements!" "Comrade Chairman," Smirnov interrupted at this point, “I'm happy to announce that I have personally carried out an expedition near the Dniester, in Struga, during which both the Count of Struga and his chief conspirator, the hetman, bandit Vassilii Kravlenko, were liquidated. The arrest of certain other frontier speculators and smugglers can also be expected any day." "Very good, Comrade Smirnov, I'll report that to the Central Committee - to expect it any day. Just see to it that it's not any week or any month!"

Immediately after this meeting, Smirnov dispatched a special courier to Struga, to the Commander of the military detachment that he had left there. The courier carried a concise and emphatic order: "The Struga peasant, Samborik, is to be taken into custody immediately and turned over to the Military Tribunal, and a full account given about his activities along the Dniester."

Also, as soon as the Commissars' session had ended, Shimmon, the Elder, received the following written order: "You are hereby requested to evacuate within forty-eight hours all 191

inhabitants of the dwelling formerly occupied by the deceased religious leader, Itzik Shneierson. This building is being requisitioned for the establishment of a Government 

Educational Program." 

It was on this same day, too, after the Commissars' meeting, that Gruzinov and Shneierson had burst into the office of the State Academy Director, Ilya Grigorovich Maximov, and had promptly ousted him from his position. The Political Instruction Commissar, Yosef Shneierson, had taken the keys from the Director and old Maximov, unable to tolerate the humiliation, had put an end to his life. . . 

David stood over Maximov's lifeless body, amazed at the cold-blooded indifference with which he was contemplating the placidly smiling countenance of his dead teacher and mentor. "Strange!" he pondered. "Where are my feelings of compassion and pity? Was this old teacher a coward or a hero? No, certainly no coward! He beat them all! He flung his few remaining years at their feet and wouldn't crawl before them like a worm! He squared accounts with them quickly enough! Yes, that's the way it has to be - swim or sink! You can't tread water in a stormy sea. And that's what life is like today - a rough and stormy sea. . ." 

David suddenly sensed a strange awakening within himself. 

Life had assumed a new value for him. "To swim that's the most important thing!" he kept saying to himself. “He who'll swim with endurance, will survive. Yes - swim or die. . . 

And I shall swim!" "Hey, you there!" Gruzinov suddenly shouted to the old watchman Anton, who was kneeling before the corpse of his former master, Maximov, praying. "This is not a church! 

Stand up! Remove this body and get the place cleaned up - at once!"

Anton did not stir; he was oblivious to the Commissar's command. Stroking the director's frigid forehead, he kept repeating in a wailing voice: "Your Excellency, they have murdered you - those infidels! The Lord will not remain silent! He'll square accounts with them! God will not remain silent. . .!” 192

David roused himself with a start as someone grasped his shoulders. He turned around and found Yosef Shneierson standing behind him, a jovial smile on his face. A feeling of disgust came over him at Yosef's touch and he quickly freed himself from it. "What's the matter with you?" Yosef inquired, surprised. "Why do you take this suicide so much to heart? Let there be one Czarist dog less! I've got some good news for you that'll make you dance with joy! Smirnov announced officially today that Kravlenko, your father's murderer, has been executed, along with his protector, the Count of Struga."

The "good news" about Vassilii's death did not exhilarate David, and he did not feel like dancing with joy. On the contrary, he became sadder. How he had yearned for revenge!

And when it had finally happened, he saw the emptiness of it. A vision of his father's pale face, shadowed by disappointment, flitted before his eyes. He could hear again 

Samborik's words: "If you start shedding blood, you'll be like

Vaska... The world of Vaska will vanish, but the world of 

God in one's heart will live forever.. .." "What's wrong with you, David?" demanded Yosef. "I can't understand you! Did you hear what I just told you? 

Smirnov has shot Vassilii Kravlenko! Kravlenko has been liquidated!" "I heard you - yes, I heard you! What do you want me to do - pin a medal for heroism on Smirnov? Smirnov hasn't liquidated Kravlenko - he's merely taken over his role! When 

Kravlenko had the power and the weapons, he shot down whom he pleased. Now, the roles are reversed: Smirnov is the one that has the power and the weapons, so he shoots down whomever he pleases. He's just another Vaska in disguise!" "You're talking so bitterly because you're jealous of Smirnov. You hate him because he's in love with Miriam. But

Smirnov's infatuation for her is a private and personal thing which has no relation to the principles and ideals of the 

Communist movement. A sincere and conscientious Comrade of the Party has to be objective about things like that. He mustn't permit himself to become influenced by middle-class sentimentality." 193

-- 
-- David remained silent. Yosef's words rang inane, worthless, absurd to him. He was agonized by a thought: Smirnov, Gruzinov, and Yosef - the carriers of the new precepts of cruelty and bloodshed; his father, Reb Itzikel, and Samborik the adherents to the ancient precepts of kindness and humaneness! A mixture of remorse and profound longing swept over him. "Ilya Grigorovich," he murmured, bowing his head over the body of the Director. "I shall be the valedictorian at the graduation exercises on May First. You can depend on me. . . I'll not betray you..."

Then David wheeled around and headed for the door with firm steps. Gruzinov and Yosef exchanged meaningful glances.. The War Commissar, Stephan Smirnov, insisted that May First be celebrated in Polipovka with a stupendous demonstration of "Soviet Might" and "People's Solidarity." He thus hoped to convince the "higher-ups" that during his brief period of less than three months' military command of the Polipovka district, the attitude of the local populace toward the Bolshevik regime had progressed and blossomed considerably. 

The task of demonstrating "Soviet Power" was easy. Smirnov simply issued a stern command that all military detachments of the entire district — artillery, cavalry, and infantry were to participate in the parade armed and in full regalia. How to display the "People's Solidarity," however, proved to be a more difficult problem, because most of the townsfolk seemed to lack the spontaneous desire to celebrate 

May First with any enthusiasm. "They haven't been indoctrinated for that yet... They're still not adjusted to the new regime. The transition from 

Right to Left came too suddenly for them... They're still in a state of doubt and indecision. . Thus argued the Commissars and other Party functionaries. 

"Damn it!" Smirnov shouted, banging his powerful fist on the table. "What do they think this is — child's play? It's the 

Soviet Power! Whoever entertains doubts and begins wavering is a provocateur - the firing squad for him! Shoot down 194

a score of these wobblers, and proper support for the Soviet will spread like wildfire among the rest of them!"

Ever since preparations for the May First celebration had begun, Smirnov had become more and more irritable, overbearing, and pugnacious. Today, just three days before the great event, he was unendurable. No one could say a word to him without him flaring up. The entire personnel of the Commissariat was bewildered and nervous. Smirnov kept finding fault with everything they did, and the more criticisms he made, the greater was their confusion and the more frequent their mistakes. Smirnov would bellow, pound the table with his fist, and pace to and fro in his office like a caged tiger.

The only one aware of the true cause of Smirnov's irritability and inner turmoil, was Smirnov himself. The flurry and bustle attendant to the preparations for the May First celebration were merely his alibi. What was really exasperating and frustrating him, was Miriam's continued refusal to yield completely to him. He had been meeting her almost daily during the past weeks. She would throw herself into his arms, cuddle up to him, purr like a cat, press her hot lips to his, wind her arms around his neck, and tickle his mouth and teeth with the tip of her tongue, until he, tingling with desire, would crush her in his steel-like arms, and sink his teeth into her throat. She would feel his taut, passioncharged limbs against her, and she would gasp for breath in his iron grip. The pain of his biting teeth would be sharp yet so sweet... Just then, when it would seem that she was overwhelmed by him, she would slip out of his grasp, and murmur: · • "Stepka dear, not today. If you love me truly, leave me be now. Perhaps next time. . ." · • Recalling these encounters, Smirnov would feel hot anger rage within him, an anger he kept venting upon his subordinates in the Commissariat. During the day, he could accomplish nothing; and at night he could not sleep - the thought of Miriam did not leave him for an instant. "What a girl she is! The fires of the Orient are smoldering in her! I'm mad over her! What's keeping her from me? 195

Her fiance, that student? Absurd! She doesn't need a suckling - she needs a man! What I can give her, she could never get from her Jew-boy, the devil with it. . .! Could it be her Jewish tradition? Maybe the ghost of her father, the 

Rabbi, with his trembling beard and side-curls, comes before her each time she's ready to succumb. . . Damn it! I could have taken her by force a dozen times! Women are all alike they pretend not to want it, they kick and bite; but when it comes right down to it, they yield like chicks, and groan with joy! I'm being consumed by fire - I'm dying for her... Today - it must happen today, or I'll go out of my mind, the devil with it!" 

He glanced at his wristwatch, and although his meeting with Miriam was still two and a half hours away, he opened the door leading into the general office and shouted to his secretary: "Comrade Pavlov! My horse! Carry on with the schedule as arranged. Don't go looking for me I'll not get back to the office today. Whoever needs me is to see me tomorrow." 

Smirnov hopped into his saddle so energetically that his horse instinctively sensed his master's state of excitement. The horseman's agitation and turmoil seemed to seep into the animal, so that both blended into one taut and impatient being bounding forth in a swift gallop. 

The faster Smirnov's horse galloped from the city, the more violently the blood coursed through his veins, and the more unbearable became every minute of his anticipation. He suddenly reined in his horse, and swiftly headed toward 

Miriam's home. . It was now ten days since Miriam had broken off completely with David. It did not surprise her that they should sever their relationship; she had expected that to happen, ever since she had become intimate with Smirnov. The way it had actually occurred, however, had stunned her so, that as yet she had not recovered from it. 

She could remember all the details of that fateful day. 

She had learned from her brother, Yosef, who was now the 196

new Director of the State Academy, that David had completed his examinations with high honor. He had topped the list of graduates and had been appointed official representative and valedictorian of the graduating class during the May First celebration. "If he's completed all his exams now," she had asked her brother, "how come I haven't seen him yet?" "That, my dear sister, I don't know. I can only tell you that your fiance needs a little shaking up. He's behaving very strangely lately. All his actions are peculiar, and he's not very discreet about what he says. Comrades in the Party are all amazed at the way he's changed, and they're becoming suspicious of him." "It's probably just that he's been under too much of a strain studying for his exams," Miriam answered. "Let's hope that's all it is! You may not believe it, but the fact is that even when I told him about Smirnov killing his father's murderer, he not only didn't show any sign of being pleased about it, but I could swear he actually felt disappointed. He even called Smirnov a bandit." 

At the mention of Smirnov's name, Miriam blushed furiously, and, to hide her embarrassment, ran her fingers nervously through her blonde hair, tossing her head back several times. "You don't have to blush. I told him that his hatred for

Smirnov was just his middle-class jealousy. Revolutionaries and Party members have got to allow freedom in love.” "Whoever appointed you as my defender?" Miriam raged. "Who asked you to talk to David about things that concern only me- me, personally, and no one else?" "Don't be melodramatic! David didn't so much as bat an eye when I spoke about Smirnov's interest in you. I can assure you that the suicide of that Czarist dog, the Director,

Maximov, meant a lot more to him than my remarks about his being jealous of Smirnov. . . Incidentally, what do you expect to do-be the bride at two weddings?" "To the devil with you! That's my own affair, and I haven't come to you for advice!" 197

"What's the matter with you, Miriam? What are you mad at me for? We've been loyal friends to each other since we were kids. 

Miriam lowered her head, and after a momentary silence, suddenly asked: "Yosef, why didn't we have a mother when we were children? What ever happened to her?"

Yosef walked over to her, put his hand on her forehead, and said mockingly, “Oh, you're running a high temperature - you've got 106° at least. Or else, maybe you're just out of your mind! What's this silly question got to do with what we were talking about? Our mother must have died long ago, that's all." "Died? Without there ever being a memorial service observed for her? With no mourner's prayer ever being recited for the wife of the Grand Rabbi of Polipovka?” "Well, I'll admit I've wondered about that too sometimes.

But the mysterious silence there's always been about our mother is something I got used to long ago. I guess you could say Mother's fate has been the best-kept secret in the history of the Polipovka Rabbinical Court!" "Yosef, our Mother didn't die. It's quite likely that she's still alive." "What makes you think that?" Yosef asked, gazing at her in wonder. "While I was clearing out the stuff in the house for the evacuation, I found a white silk wedding gown in one of the trunks, with this note pinned on it."

Yosef took the note from her with trembling hands, and read: "Fate has brought me together with a man whom I love. When you read these words, I'll be far out to sea on my way to America, where a new life is awaiting me a life I could not have with you. 

Forgive me, and kiss the children for me." 

Rivka "Life is stranger than fiction," Yosef remarked, as he gave this note back to Miriam. "If we were to read something like that in a novel, we'd consider it improbable and incredible. ‘A figment of the author's imagination,' we'd say. 198

But why get sentimental over this? I don't see how it can have any importance or influence on my life or yours at the moment."

Miriam leaned back on the couch, and staring blankly ahead, answered with an air of indifference, "You're right - this can't have any importance or influence for us now." 

Yosef left, and Miriam remained alone again, wrapped in thought. She felt an odd mixture of indignation and compassion towards her mother. Indignation, because this was a woman who had left her children motherless. How often 

Miriam had envied those who had mothers they could speak about! How she had envied David his deep love for his mother! Yet, she felt compassion for her mother too - for this woman who had cut her heart in two, leaving one half with her children, and seeking new happiness in a distant, alien world with the other half. "Poor, unknown mother of mine!" she whispered to herself.

All at once, Miriam was seized with an intense longing for David. She missed the way he gently caressed her while she pressed him to her warm body, cuddled and kissed him, and listened to his sweet, passionate words, She put on a thin calico dress, tight around the waist, accentuating the lines of her figure, She looked at herself in the mirror, and satisfied, thought: "David will surely desire me."

Miriam had not found David at home. The housekeeper whose tenant he had been during the past year had welcomed her with her usual bustling amiability, and babbled: "My soul, you shine like the bright sun! You smell like a fresh fragrant rose. The young Comrade isn't here. Most likely he's at Dr. Torchinsky's again. Ever since he finished his exams, he spends whole days at the hospital. How he smells of carbolic now! He wants to become a doctor, the young Comrade. Well, may God help him!" "Malvina Petrovna," Miriam said, "it's five o'clock already.

Don't you think that it's about time for Comrade Shanan to be having his supper?" "Five o'clock? My God, I haven't even started cooking yet, either. He'll be home in an hour now, too - that's for sure!" “Very well, Malvina Petrovna. You can go into the kitchen 199

and start preparing supper, and I'll wait for him in his room."

The instant that Miriam entered David's room she instinctively sensed a change in the atmosphere. She was quite familiar with this room, and with her eyes closed, could have pointed correctly to every piece of furniture in it, and of the least noticeable object in the cupboard and in every drawer. Something essential was missing now, and for a moment, she couldn't say what. Then it suddenly dawned on her - her pictures! There had been a dozen photographs of her in different poses, at different ages, all prominently displayed in his room. There was not a single one visible now. 

She walked over to his desk, and opened the side drawer where he had always carefully stored away all the letters which she had written to him during the course of their long friendship. The drawer was empty now, except for a single envelope addressed in an unfamiliar handwriting. She opened it, and found a note inside: "Dear Shanan:

I slipped out of bed, got dressed quietly, and tiptoed out of the room. You were sleeping so soundly, that I didn't want to wake you. Thanks for the happiness you've given me. I'll see you tomorrow at the hospital. I love you. Forever yours, Anastasia" 

Miriam felt the blood rushing to her face. She was overcome by a sense of shame and vexation. Her pride was deeply hurt. It seemed to her as though David had spat on her, and flung her out. 

She heard footsteps outside, accompanied by what sounded like a lively, jovial conversation. Then the door opened and 

David appeared on the threshold, arm in arm with a young red-headed girl. She was of medium height, fresh-looking, and apple-ripe. Her hair was fashioned into a double-braided halo, wound firmly around the crown of her head, She had a freckled face, a high forehead, big dark grey eyes, a small nose, and wide lips that seemed always ready to smile, revealing two rows of even white teeth. One could glimpse a nurse's uniform under her light blue spring coat. The high collar of her blouse hugged her throat, and her red cross 200

apron revealed the contours of a well-developed young girl. 

David and the girl both fell silent when they caught sight of Miriam. Embarrassed, the redhead eyed David quizzically. "Anastasia," David murmured, "meet Miriam Shneierson, the sister of the new Director of the State Academy. Miriam, this is Anastasia Bogatina, the best nurse in the Polipovka Hospital."

Anastasia made an involuntary gesture to shake hands with 

Miriam, but then drew back. Miriam's air of cool indifference prohibited any effort at a warm approach. She did not even look at Anastasia, while a single thought kept running through her mind. "So that's who's taken my place with him! Impossible! he still loves me! I'll get him back – I must get him back!” 

Miriam did not take her penetrating gaze off David, as though she were anxious to divine whether in the inner recesses of his heart there were still those bonds of love with which he had hitherto been tied to her. Still ignoring the nurse and masking her emotions, she remarked quietly to David: "David, I wish to see you alone. I'm sure that Comrade 

Bogatina will excuse us." 

Blushing with confusion, Anastasia began to apologize: "Please, please don't let me interfere. Goodbye, Shanan. 

Pleased to have met you, Comrade Shneierson. Until tomorrow at the hospital Shanan, goodbye." 

After the girl left, a strained silence ensued. Miriam was the first to break it. "I see that you've carried out a thorough 'Miriam purge.'" "Purge?" David asked with a wry smile. “You even speak the way he does. You were the goddess of my fairest dreams, but you've disgraced and polluted yourself." "You speak of disgrace and pollution? By what right? Because of your orgies with Anastasia Bogatina? Smirnov hasn't shared my bed with me as yet!"

David grabbed both of Miriam's arms, pulling her toward him roughly, and as if all his pent-up hurt and anger had suddenly exploded, he shouted into her face: "Don't you dare mention Anastasia! She's an innocent 201

victim, a victim of the blaze you kindled in me, which consumes me day and night. She's a victim, because with her 

I'm only trying to put out the flame of a love which doesn't belong to her, and she knows it.. 

"David dear," Miriam whispered faintly, "I want you back.

I want you, because it's me you love. I can feel it, even in the way you're angry with me now." 

"Yes, I love you. I love you very much, but we no longer belong to each other. You're dead for me as dead as 

Marushka, as dead as my mother, as my father, as the Rabbi.

Each one of you gave value to my life, and each one of you has left me."

On her way home, Miriam felt as though she was returning from a funeral. A dismal loneliness enveloped her. Her empty heart bitterly mourned the premature death of her first love.

Smirnov dismounted. The jangle of spurs punctuated each thud of his footsteps as he mounted the stairs leading to Miriam's room. Without knocking, he pushed open the door, and with glittering eyes, distended nostrils, and panting, quivering lips, halted before an amazed Miriam. "Stepka," she exclaimed, "you scared me to death! You blew in like the wind. I didn't expect you 'til an hour from now. What's the matter with you? What's happened? What are you so excited about?"

Miriam was not dressed yet. She wore only a sleeveless silk kimono over her nightgown. Her light blonde hair cascaded over her bare shoulders like long strands of fine silk thread. All the mysterious details of her slender, girlish figure showed tantalizingly through her thin, transparent garb. On her bare feet, she wore loose red velvet slippers trimmed with white fluffy fur. 

Smirnov stood entranced, oblivious to Miriam's astonished questions. She appeared to him now like the first Biblical woman divulging to the first Biblical man the hidden secrets of nudity, and inciting him to the intoxicating sweetness of sin.

Smirnov could not control himself any longer. He lost every 202

vestige of reason and common sense. A primitive, overbearing animal-power was now his sole motivating force. Seizing 

Miriam with both hands, he lifted her like a child, and put her down on her bed. Then he pulled open her kimono, and fell upon her nude, young body, growling like a wild animal. . 

Miriam sensed that now the inevitable must happen – and she submitted without the slightest resistance. 

Smirnov bit into her throat, breasts, and hips with his sharp teeth, sucking in the endless delightfulness of her body, for which he had so yearned. Miriam, in her complete submissiveness, accepted Smirnov's brutal mastery. His manly vigor was satiating an intense desire that had long lain dormant in her hot-blooded body. . . 

Calmed and satisfied finally, Smirnov lay beside Miriam.

Propping himself on one elbow, he stroked her disheveled hair with his free hand. "At last you're mine - you Oriental wildfire!" he murmured. "You've surpassed all my expectations! From whom did you inherit your fire - your father, your mother, or maybe your race? I've always heard that Jews were hotblooded, and that's why they marry young, while they're still almost children." 

Miriam lay silent, her head resting on her hands, clasped behind her neck. Smirnov continued to caress her, gazing into her eyes, and then happily repeated: "I can hardly believe it - you're mine, all mine! Just let anyone try to take you away from me- my name isn't 

Stephan Smirnov if I won't put a bullet in his head! And you, my flaming wildcat, you bear that in mind too! That fiance of yours, that little snotnose, you can send him to the devil- he's not for you!" 

Miriam's big blue eyes darkened. Her eyelids slowly closed, and the long eyelashes cast little shadow branches on her cheeks. From the corners of her eyes, tears began to trickle tears that she could no longer restrain. 

"Are you crying?" asked Smirnov. "That's silly! What's there to cry about? To be the sweetheart of the War Commissar is pretty important let me tell you. There's been a Revolu- 203

tion, my dear, and the world belongs to the leaders of the 

Revolution. By my side, under my protection you won't miss a thing. May First will be celebrated in three days and we're going to have a grand demonstration of revolutionary might, and that demonstration is going to be led by the War Commissar, Stephan Smirnov, your lover! Are you satisfied — eh? 

Well, then, open your eyes and kiss me!" 

Miriam opened her eyes and planted a light kiss on his mouth. "No, no, the devil with it!" Stephan shouted. “Don't you ever kiss me like that again!" 

He glued his lips to hers with such force, that she gasped for air. She slowly spread out her arms, tightly wound them around his neck, and answered his kiss the way he wanted it... 

Around the Polipovka People's House, a vast uninhabited terrain sprawled almost endlessly – the area of the famous Polipovka market fairs. Peasants from near and distant farms and villages flocked here to sell their agricultural products and cattle. Horse-dealers from the remotest provinces and from abroad came here to sell and buy horses of all breeds - dray-horses, plow-horses, thoroughbreds for the gentry's phaetons, hunters, and racehorses. Here also, sheep, cattle, and pig dealers from all over the far-flung Empire, gathered to obtain meat on the hoof for their own use as well as for export.

During a market fair the area seethed like a gigantic cauldron, and the tumult could be heard for miles away.
