Horses neighed, cows lowed, pigs squealed, lambs bleated; acrobats and comedians performed tricks; gypsies engaged in fortune-telling; beggars, blind, crippled, and freaks squatted or crawled about pleading for alms in weird tones, whining or warbling sad folksongs accompanied by melancholy tunes of lyres and accordions; carousels turned, as their monotonous hurdy-gurdy music rent the air; marketwomen loudly called for customers to buy their wares, their cookies, wafers, cider, and soda water; drunkards sang, laughed, cursed and brawled; the feet of tens of thousands of humans 204

tread in their own dung as well as in the manure of horses and cattle.

Today, however, this area was hardly recognizable. Because of the May First celebration it had been cleaned up, strewn with white sand, and every pole and stray tree was embellished with fluttering red banners.

A high, rectangular platform, fashioned of stout boards and covered with red cloth, had been erected in the center of the compound. Two huge portraits of Lenin and Trotsky were mounted on the front wall of this structure. 

The long, marching columns of the May First parade were now advancing one after another, toward the cleaned bedecked area around the Polipovka People's House. Each detachment, each group, every section of the marchers with its respective placards of diverse revolutionary rallying cries and slogans, halted at a designated point. 

The Commissars Smirnov, Shneierson, and Gruzinov, appeared on the platform. The orchestra struck up the Internationale, whereupon all the military detachments froze to attention and an intense hush enveloped the vast throng of onlookers.

The Political Commissar and new Director of the State Academy, Yosef Shneierson, was the first to address the assemblage. After a lengthy review of the history of the 

Revolution and of the political, economic and social ideology of Bolshevism, Yosef dwelled on the absolute importance of attracting youth to Communism and to the Bolshevik regime. With oratorical grandiloquence he shouted: "Comrades! The rising generation must belong to us! The first steps have been carried out. The Czarist leaders in charge of the education of the young have been dismissed and liquidated! Education is now under the jurisdiction of the 

Soviet power! In my capacity as Director of the State Academy, I have the pleasure to call upon the valedictorian of this year's graduates of the Polipovka State Academy, the excellent student and the true-hearted and loyal Party Member 

Comrade Shanan!" 

David made his way to the platform. This was the first time that he had ever been called upon to address a great 205

throng. To his amazement he did not lose himself and did not feel the slightest fear before this sea of faces. Before his eyes there flitted the image of Director Maximov's face as he had lain dead, and in his ears echoed the words he had uttered then: "Ilya Grigorovich, you can depend on me -I'll not betray you. . ." "Comrades! with nostalgia in our hearts, we are taking leave of the State Academy, where we have spent the happiest days of our youth. We are leaving the walls of that temple within which we have warmed ourselves, grown and matured before the bright glow of culture and knowledge. None of us knows what the future has in store for him nor where his destiny will lead him. However, we, the graduates of the Polipovka State Academy, are valiant, proud and happy, because the rich background of culture and enlightenment which our educators have given us will always help us in the future, and be the reliable guiding North Star of our destinies. "With no less nostalgia do we take leave today of our teachers, who instructed and trained us with heart and soul, and who inculcated in us the concepts of humaneness and righteousness. 

Yosef turned pale; Smirnov's face flushed with anger; and 

Gruzinov could barely suppress a sardonically spiteful grin at this clumsy failure of the Political Commissar. 

"What's going on here?" Smirnov asked Yosef, out of the corner of his mouth. "Where does he think he is in a synagogue? 'Righteousness! Humaneness!' The devil with it!

Not a word about the Revolution! This is catastrophic! His damned mouth ought to be shut up before it's too late." "Comrades!" David continued with enthusiasm. "With bowed heads we mention today those of our teachers who have prematurely been. . ."

Smirnov jumped to his feet, gave a hasty signal, and the orchestra struck up the Internationale, like a bolt from the sky. The military units snapped to attention. A silence again fell over the great throng. David stood with mouth agape, never clearly uttering the words he had intended to say in memory of the dead Director. His voice was drowned out by the loud sounds of the trombones, the thumping of the 206

drums and the clashing of the cymbals. His unspoken words hovered above the assembled multitude like invisible ghosts conveying a mute message... 

Shortly after the May First parade, David was hailed before a special emergency session of the local Party group, presided over by the Political Commissar, Yosef Shneierson. "Comrade Shanan," the chairman said, "You are requested to give an account of your unforgivable behavior during today's celebration. Your speech was not only contrary to 

Party spirit, but actually counter-revolutionary, and even provocateurish. We consider your action a breach of the fundamental principles of Party ideology and discipline. For this, you may be subjected to arraignment before a Military 

Tribunal. What's your explanation?”

David remained calm. He was deeply convinced that what he had done had been the right thing to do, and he answered quietly: "There are things that cannot be influenced by political consideration and that cannot be restrained by Party discipline. For instance, one cannot, by means of Party discipline, change the radiance of the sun, the direction of the wind, or the cloudiness or serenity of the sky. I fail to see how the teaching of biology, chemistry, physics, astronomy and mathematics, could be influenced by one or another form of government regime. I am likewise quite certain that the teaching of world history and world literature cannot be excluded from an educational program, regardless of who the teachers are." "Comrade," Gruzinov interrupted him, "I wish to call your attention to the fact that we have a Commissariat of Peoples' Education that looks after the reorganization of the education programs in accordance with our ideology. I assure you that we know what we're doing!" "Pardon, Comrade Commissar, but do you really think that the dismissal of a distinguished and experienced Director and the appointment in his place of one who has never attended a State Academy, is proof of knowing what one is doing? That is not only ridiculous but destructive, right 207

at the budding stage of a revolutionary educational program." 

Gruzinov's nose twitched nervously, causing his pince-nez to bounce. David's logic annoyed him, and he replied indignantly: "The Revolution, Comrade, is young! We still lack experienced Directors but the Czarist educators have no place in our program. They must be liquidated!" "That's exactly what I can't understand! Why not utilize the skill and knowledge of the Czarist teachers? Why not follow the Army's example? The Army didn't shoot down the Czarist officers! Who do you think is training and commanding the victorious Red Army? Experienced Czarist officers! Because there are no Red Officers as yet!"

A fist came down on the table with a crash, causing everyone to wince. Smirnov, who had been sitting silent all this time, raging inwardly, turned to David with venom and contempt: "Don't you dare drag the Army into your counter-revolutionary dung, you son-of-a-bitch! What do you aim to do - set yourself up as a Rabbi in the Party? I ought to shoot you down like a dog! My hand wouldn't even tremble! You 

Struga scoundrel! Nothing good comes out of Struga! It's an accursed Czarist nest a nest of counter-revolutionaries!

It ought to be set afire at its four corners and wiped off the earth! Too bad that the Tribunal will only attend to that saboteur Samborik. His whole damned village ought to be condemned!" 

David was stunned. He turned deathly pale. His lips quivered, and in his eyes there crept a look of abject fear. 

The sudden change in David's appearance at the mention of Samborik's name was so astonishing that the effect of the urgent Party session was momentarily forgotten. Even Smirnov paused, and knitting his beetle brows, glowered at him with suspicion. David asked in a barely audible voice: 

"Samborik Samborik before the Tribunal? For sabotage? 

There must be some misunderstanding. .. It's a terrible mistake... It mustn't happen. . . He's innocent - he's innocent of any crime. 

"What sort of nonsense are you babbling?" Smirnov de- 208

manded, and turning to his colleagues, he exclaimed: "Comrades, if this is a trick of his to weaken our position 

for and to distract our attention from his case, he'll not succeed the devil with it! It's as I've said before: all stand up each other! Comrades! I propose that the Citizen Shanan be expelled from the Party, and that his only chance to escape arrest for treason be to volunteer as a private in the Red 

Army within twenty-four hours!"

David paid little attention to the Party's possible decision about him. Smirnov's proposal appeared insignificant in comparison with the death peril facing Samborik. He didn't even hear the chairman declare: "Comrades! Since there are no objections, Comrade Smirnov's proposition is unanimously accepted, and the Comrade Secretary is directed to dismiss the citizen Shanan from the Party at once!" 

Everyone started to leave. They avoided looking at David, not so much because of any discomfort, as because of cautiousness against incurring any suspicion of sympathizing with an excluded Party member and a possible traitor. 

David remained standing. He kept thinking desperately: “No, it mustn't happen! I must save him! But how? God in heaven, how?"

Yosef walked by him, and like the others, averted his eyes. 

David grabbed his hand and pleaded in a trembling voice: "Yosef, please help me! Samborik mustn't be condemned! 

He's innocent! I'll stake my life on it!" "You're out of your mind! Your own life is now at stake! 

Wake up, David! Don't tighten the noose around your neck with your own hands! Forget Samborik, and save yourself! 

Enlist in the Army at once- and stay alive!" Yosef snatched away his hand and departed, leaving David without the least ray of hope for saving Samborik's life... 

David went out, and not knowing what to do, hurried toward the prison. Suddenly he halted and said to himself: "Where am I hurrying to? It's senseless, it's absurd! The prison gates are guarded and no one can enter without a special permit from the War Commissar. The War Commissar 

Smirnov! He's all-powerful now. Samborik's life depends on a single word, a single wink from him. He's the only one who 209

could save Samborik! But how? How does one crack that granite wall around his heart? How beg for compassion from so morbid a mind, that it is never satiated with enough innocent human blood?"

Strange, it didn't even occur to David to plead forgiveness or clemency in his own behalf. He did not hesitate to challenge all the commissars, including Smirnov, notwithstanding all their threats to persecute him and even hail him before a Military Tribunal. His determination not to bow before injustice prevailed over his will for survival. For Samborik, however, he was prepared to do anything to plead, beg, cry, implore. Nothing could humiliate him, as long as he could save Samborik's life. 

He suddenly saw a spark of hope - Miriam! She knew who

Samborik was - she knew it was Samborik who had risked his own life to save him. She knew how kind-hearted and humane Samborik was. She used to listen, spellbound, to his many tales about Samobrik's simple integrity and justice. 

When Miriam opened the door and caught sight of David, her blood swirled in her like a tidal wave. A happy warmth and hope seeped through her. She interpreted his crushed appearance as an expression of his deep longing for her and as a reflection of the torture he had been through while depriving himself of her ardent, passionate love. She stretched out her hands to him, and with tears of joy, said: "How deeply you've suffered, my beloved! How terribly you've tortured yourself, my darling! Come to me, my David, come. 

David did not throw himself into her arms. Instead he slumped to the ground, and embracing her knees, looked up at her pleadingly: "Help me, Miriam! You're my only spark of hope! Help me save Samborik! Samborik's under arrest. He's being tried tomorrow for sabotage! They'll execute him! They mustn't! 

He's innocent! You can help me save him! It's in Smirnov's hands and from Smirnov, you can get anything you wish!" 

Miriam was stupefied. Her heart felt numb; her eyes were cold and dry. A lump rose in her throat, choking her. She was barely able to catch her breath, and with all her might 

210

burst into an ear-piercing hysterical laughter! "The holy Rabbi has come to pronounce my sins ritually clean!" 

Clutching the hair of his head with both hands, she raved into his upturned face: 

"I polluted and prostituted myself! Do you remember? 

You've plucked me out of your heart for that — spat upon me and cast me out! Now you've found a salvation for me? You could exalt, you could even sanctify my sinfulness, if it would only save your Samborik! Well, I don't sell my body over the counter! Do you hear that? It's not for sale at any price - not for you and not for your Samborik!"

The theatre auditorium of the Polipovka People's House swarmed with townsmen anxious to witness today's session of the Military Tribunal. Seated in the center of the podium, at a rectangular table covered with beige cloth, were the three judges headed by Commissar Stephan Smirnov. The Commissars Gruzinov for the prosecution, and Shneierson for the defense, were seated at two small tables to the right and left, respectively. The prosecution bench, long enough to accommodate six persons, was now occupied by Samborik alone, flanked by two armed Red Guards. Samborik's long flaxen hair was streaked with gray, and the furrows on his face appeared deeper. His eyes, however, had not changed: he had the same clear, kindly, peasant-naive gaze that had always mirrored his innate simplicity and honesty. The two Red 

Guards stood erect staring at the judges unblinkingly. 

Throughout the period when the Secretary of the Tribunal was reading aloud the bill of indictment, Samborik sat calmly and indifferently as though this did not concern him. Each time the Secretary referred to "The Struga peasant, Timofey Samborik," the accused evinced curiosity only for a fleeting moment, because he failed to grasp what was said about him and made no special effort to understand. The entire procedure of his imprisonment and arraignment seemed silly to him, and he was greatly astonished that so many people had quit their chores in the middle of the week just to flock here and listen to all this foolishness. He looked up at 211

his guards every few moments, concerned about them standing all that time. Tugging at the sleeve of one of them, he pointed at the bench and remarked: "Why stand up, doves? There's plenty of room - why don't you sit down?"

The Red Guard drew back his sleeve angrily, without looking at the prisoner. The chairman of the Military Tribunal rapped out harshly: "The accused is ordered to remain silent. He'll have an opportunity to speak when the Court calls upon him." 

The Secretary read the concluding lines of the indictment: "Taking into consideration these premeditated acts of sabotage, the Struga peasant, Timofey Samborik, is herewith pronounced a dangerous enemy of the people, and is being indicted for high treason against the Soviet power." 

Samborik stood up, and scratching the back of his head, looked confusedly now at the judges, now at the assembled throng, unable to understand what was going on. "Lord have mercy! What are you talking about, little doves? What sort of an enemy am I? I am no one's enemy - neither yours nor the people's!"

The powerful hands of the two Red Guards pushed Samborik back to his seat. The chairman repeated his stern admonition. "The accused is ordered not to interfere with the procedure of the trial! Comrade Prosecutor proceed!"

Gruzinov stood up, adjusted his pince-nez and called out: "Comrade Serebryak! the Prosecution calls for the witness Serebryak!"

There appeared before the Court the former Czarist gendarme, Serebryak, who had ransomed his life by enlisting in the service of the Red Army, and by betrayal of his personal friend, Kravlenko. "Comrade Serebryak," the Prosecutor addressed him, "were you in command of the Red Army squad that arrested the accused?" "It was I." "Please tell the Court of the circumstances under which you carried out the arrest." 212

"We were hiding in the bushes of his orchard, on the bank of the Dniester, and secretly kept an eye on all his movements." 

"When you say 'his' — who are you referring to, Comrade?" "That peasant, Samborik — the one sitting on that bench." "Why did you watch him especially and no one else?" 

"Because that was the order, 'To watch the peasant, Samborik, because he deals in contraband goods and carries on sabotage.'" "Very good, Comrade. Continue." "It was around nine o'clock in the evening. I saw the peasant Samborik carrying a heavy sack on his back. He was accompanied by another man, also a peasant, who also carried a heavy sack on his back. They were walking along without saying a word. They were already at the river bank. 

They looked about, then dragged a boat out from among the bushes. They quickly tossed the sacks into the boat and then the other man hopped in and was off down the river. We came out of our hiding place, and I commanded: 'Halt! 

Don't you move, or I'll shoot!' Too late - the other fellow was already halfway across the Dniester, and this one we arrested. " "Didn't you make any effort at all to halt the boat?" "Of course we did - we even fired a shot. But in the next minute the Rumanian sentry across the river let loose with a machine-gun, and so we withdrew, taking this prisoner with us." "Very good, Comrade Serebryak! Your witness, Comrade Defender!" 

Yosef rose slightly and remarked: "The defense has no objections against the facts brought out by the witness."

Smiling triumphantly, Gruzinov adjusted his pince-nez and called out loudly: "The prosecution calls the arrested peasant of Struga, Timofey Samborik, to the witness stand."

Samborik advanced toward the judges' table and started to argue with outspread hands: "Lord, my Master, what sin is it to help a brother? He was 213

on his way home from work and I was helping him. I've done it all my life!" "Silence please!" Smirnov admonished him. "Just answer the questions put to you by the members of the Tribunal!" "What did those sacks contain?" Gruzinov asked, approaching Samborik. "What should they contain? There was no gold! There was wheat in one, and rye in the other." "Who was the second man?” "Who should it be? My brother Andrey, of course.' "Is he a Soviet citizen?" "No, he's my brother Andrey!" "9 The throng burst into laughter, and Samborik, shrugging his shoulders, turned his head from side to side, unable to understand the cause of the sudden laughter. "Timofey Samborik, you'll not deceive this Court with your wily devices," Gruzinov remarked sternly, and proceeded with his cross-examination: "Is it not a fact that your brother is a Rumanian citizen and resides on the Rumanian shore? And is it not also a fact that your brother visits the Soviet shore every day, and that you help him smuggle sacks of grain and other products across the frontier? Is it not a fact that you are serving the

Rumanian Government, and that you are receiving full military protection for it while crossing the frontier unmolested, whenever and wherever you so desire? Will you deny that the Rumanians fired upon our soldiers when the latter tried to stop the boat in which your brother, with your cooperation, was transporting contraband goods across the Dniester?"

Samborik turned to face the assembled onlookers and said in a pleading tone: "Good people, I'm asking you, what sort of Rumanian is 

Andrey? Andrey is, after all, my brother! Our fields are on this side and his hut is over on the other side. He's working here, and living over there. Where does Rumania come in here? We are peasants - honest, Christian peasants! We don't deal with sabotage, as you call it. We don't buy it or sell it. 

What's true is true — wheat and rye Andrey does take home; 214

his wife and children have got to eat. But sabotage?"

Samborik again scratched the back of his head, then resumed his defense-directed not at the Court, but at the spectators: "Forgive me, good people, but do a favor to a Christian! 

What is sabotage? If it's a treasure, we've never found it; if it's a food, we've never tasted it!"

The throng laughed again. Samborik's eyes, filled with wonder, scanned the grinning faces, trying to fathom why his truthfulness brought forth laughter, and he was instinctively afraid. "Comrade Chairman," Gruzinov shouted, "the Prosecution does not deem it necessary to continue the examination of the accused. His provocative behavior and arrogant replies demonstrate the dangerous type of counter-revolutionary that he is. The Prosecution is now ready to address the Court." "Very well, Comrade Prosecutor," Smirnov concurred. "The

Defense may now take over in behalf of the accused." "Comrade Chairman," Yosef, half-rising once again declared fitfully: "The Defense has no objection to the statements of the Prosecution." "In that case, the Court will now hear the Prosecution's summation of the charges against the accused.” "Comrades of the Tribunal!" Gruzinov began speaking with fervor. "The accused peasant, Timofey Samborik, personifies the most dangerous type among our hidden, internal enemies.

You see here the diabolic face of a cunning and shrewd saboteur, who manipulates his transactions so cleverly as to give the impression of innocent activity, disguised by a mask of artificial naivéte and simplicity. We have established and he did not deny it that he is engaged in smuggling across the frontier for speculative purposes! The poor soul suddenly pretends not to know that the opposite shore of the Dniester belongs to a foreign power! Suddenly he doesn't know why the Rumanians start shooting the moment they behold the shadow of a Soviet citizen on the bank - but never fire at him! On the contrary, they let him and his brother cross the frontier unmolested - where and when they wish to do so! "Samborik will have us believe that the Rumanian sentries 215

do so simply because they are generous and sympathetic. 

Comrades of the Tribunal! The reason why the Rumanians do so is quite clear and self-evident. They do so because it's to their advantage! They're doing it because the price is a lucrative one! With the cooperation of the Samborik brothers, they can indulge in espionage and sabotage on one of the most important frontiers of our country! "Comrades of the Tribunal! Our brother revolutionaries rotted in Czarist jails and shed their blood in the civil war in order to secure for the working class of our country, and for the proletariat of the entire world, a new life, a life that should be free from oppressors and exploiters! It's our sacred duty to make sure that these hard-won freedoms are not betrayed or bartered away by such spies and saboteurs as the accused peasant, Timofey Samborik! "In the name of the Revolution, and in the name of the Soviet power, I demand that Timofey Samborik receive the most extreme punishment! I demand that he be shot!"

Samborik stood up again and turned around to face the spectators. An uneasiness crept over his face, as he felt an instinctive premonition of evil, like the uneasiness manifested by an animal when it senses a lurking danger. His head swayed to and fro, and his eyes reflected incredulity and horror. The two Red Guards again roughly pushed him back to his seat. "The Court will now hear the Defense," Smirnov declared, looking at the table at which Yosef sat. "Comrades of the Tribunal! The Defense concedes that the guilt of Timofey Samborik has been established without the least doubt. Therefore, our only recourse is to appeal to the 

Court for clemency - for a milder sentence, perhaps. . .”

Silence ensued in the courtroom. Everyone was watching the judges' table although the fate of the accused was a foregone conclusion. The War Commissar would stand up in a moment to announce the horrible verdict.

There is a profound mystery in the act of death, and people are impelled, as if by a magic magnet, to come and witness someone else's untimely end. Even though everyone was certain of the verdict, no one so much as stirred in his seat 216

as they all anxiously awaited the terrible words... Their expectations were fulfilled within a few seconds. Smirnov rose, and in keeping with his usual procedure, read from a paper: "The arrested peasant from Struga, Timofey Samborik, has been found guilty of treason against the Soviet power by the Polipovka Military Tribunal, and is being condemned as an enemy of the people, to the greatest degree of punishment - to be shot! The sentence is to be carried out at once! No 

appeal!"

At a command from an officer, twelve Red Guards immediately surrounded Samborik. The spectators were ordered to remain in their seats until the accused had been ushered out of the courtroom. Samborik's eyes widened and he stared about wildly. He made the sign of the cross several times, and his pale, quivering lips muttered incoherent words.

The death-like silence was suddenly shattered by David's desperate cry: "He's innocent! An innocent man is being led to death! Let me go! Let me go!" 

He broke through the cordon of Red Guards, and threw his arms about Samborik. A powerful hand seized David by the scruff of the neck, and tearing him away from Samborik, hurled him back with such an impact that he landed some ten paces away, his head hitting one of the benches. Everything turned black, and the courtroom seemed to be swaying under him like a ship on a tempestuous sea. He barely got to his feet, shook his head vigorously, and tried to throw off his semi-stupor. Then, his legs unsteady, his eyes blinded by tears, he left the courtroom and tagged along after the convoy. 

David's unexpected appearance and his public, self-endangering outcry for justice had animated and inspired Samborik like a divine solace and encouragement. Wiping the sudden tears from his eyes with his sleeve, he breathed: "Davidka my Davidka! Remained with God in his heart! 

No the world of blood is not his world — not Davidka's. . . 

He became completely calm, and with his head erect, set out on his final march, ringed by his guards. 

The convoy halted at the edge of the Polipovka Forest on the northern outskirts of the city. The ring of soldiers now 217

formed a straight row. The officer in charge led Samborik to the first tree of the forest, and taking out a handkerchief, tried to blindfold the prisoner. last "No, no need for it," Samborik said quietly, as he pushed the handerchief gently away. "Let my eyes be open for my prayer to God."

He gazed heavenward, made the sign of the cross, and with a fervor that seemed to raise him to a higher world, began to pray with heart and soul: "Almighty God, Father in Heaven, Master of the World..."

A volley of twelve rifles resounded. Samborik slumped, first to his knees, and then face forward. Only his shoulders trembled gently, then he lay motionless. . . 

A covey of swallows, frightened by the barrage, scattered in distress over the tree-tops. Far at the other edge of the forest, a peasant tilled the soil, diligent and undisturbed. The twelve Red Army guards, as well as the curious spectators, trudged away quietly with hidden sadness. The trees swayed languidly, setting in motion a cool, refreshing breeze that caressed and bent the slender stalks of grain and grass. 

Samborik's body lay alone and a black crow on a nearby tree croaked in anxious expectation. ...

David's public outburst, "He's innocent! - An innocent man is being led to death!" was regarded by the Soviet authorities as an incontrovertible act of provocation and counterrevolution, especially since David had shouted this after the Soviet Military Tribunal had rendered the verdict of “guilty,” and had pronounced sentence.

There was no need to substantiate David's violation of the law; there was no lack of witnesses in this instance. David had challenged the decision of the Court in the presence of a capacity crowd.

Without wasting a minute Smirnov signed an order for 

David's arrest. "Where is Serebryak?" he inquired of the Secretary, as soon as he had returned to his office after sentencing Samborik. "I want Serebryak to carry out the arrest of David 218

Shanan. Serebryak can sniff out Struga scoundrels like a bloodhound!" "Pardon, Comrade Commissar! Serebryak bruised one of his feet by wearing such tight boots, and went to the hospital before the session of the Tribunal was over." "Dispatch a courier to the hospital at once to give my message to Serebryak. It's urgent!"

The courier found Serebryak in the hospital awaiting his turn, handed him Smirnov's order, and said: "The Commissar is angry. He wants this bird in the cage as soon as possible. You'd better hurry!"

Serebryak glanced at the paper, and an expression of joy and satisfaction came over his face. "It's a shame," he mused, "that Vaska isn't alive to see me, Serebryak, finally hook young Shanan. "The Jewish bastard,' 

Vaska used to call him. Well, brother - you'll not slip out of my clutches now. When they lead you to the edge of the woods, I'll see to it that I'm in the firing-squad. I'll pump the first bullet into you." 

Grasping the note, he limped toward the hospital exit. 

Anastasia Bogatina, the nurse, restrained him: "Where are you rushing, Comrade? You've waited so long, and now when it's your turn, you're leaving?" "Beauty," Serebryak beamed, "it's a pleasure to wait here! 

Where you are, I could wait day and night. But I must be on my way — a strict order!” 

He waved the paper several times before Anastasia's eyes, smiling meaningfully at her. Accidentally, Anastasia caught sight of the word "Shanan" on the paper and her heart began to pound. Grabbing Serebryak's hand, she managed to scan the crudely scribbled line: "Arrest Shanan at once, with no loss of time!"

Serebryak sensed a pleasant warmth from Anastasia's touch, and pressed against her. "Beauty, you mustn't poke your nose into secret government papers," he said reprovingly. He glanced about him, and whispered to her: "Because of this, I'll come to arrest you ... at night in your bed. . ." 219

An idea suddenly flashed through Anastasia's mind. She drew a deep breath, and coming closer to him, pressed her firm breasts against his overcoat. Smiling coquettishly, she murmured in a low voice: "Comrade, you needn't be in a hurry. The man you're looking for can be found right here. He works in the hospital every afternoon; he helps out in the bandage room. Usually he shows up at one o'clock. It's almost twelve, and within an hour, your little bird will fly into the cage by itself."

Anastasia's manner and apparent willingness to help him carry out the arrest successfully, were interpreted by Serebryak as proof that she had accepted his suggestion to become intimate with him. This thought excited him, and his red pock-marked face became even more flushed. He took hold of both her elbows, and breathing heavily and loudly through the distended nostrils of his flat nose, drew her toward him: "I like you, beauty! Remember - I'll show up at night. in bed. . ."

Anastasia freed herself from his grip, and remarked with feigned severity: "Be careful, Comrade - people are looking. . . You'd better sit over there opposite the entrance, and the moment Shanan appears, you'll be there to "receive” him. I must go now — patients are waiting. 

.. 

Anastasia left through a back door and hastened breathlessly to David's apartment. As she entered the kitchen, she found the landlady in tears. "Malvina Petrovna, where is Comrade Shanan? It's a matter of life and death! Where is he? I must find him at once before it's too late!" 

The landlady wiped her tears with her apron and blew her nose. She was wailing loudly: "My poor boy! It's a pity to see him suffer. An old friend of his is being tried before the Tribunal. He didn't sleep a wink all night. You wouldn't recognize him. He's eaten practically no food in the last twenty-four hours. He'll go out of his mind. He kept on pacing his room, saying over and over, 'He's innocent - They'll kill him - He is free of any sin.' I can't bear seeing him suffer. Have mercy, O Lord!” 220

Anastasia dashed out of David's apartment without saying goodbye, and hurried toward the People's House. She had gathered from the landlady's words, that David was attending this session of the Tribunal. She had to find him as quickly as possible, she had to warn him, and then hurry back to the hospital. . . The minutes were flying by fast; her absence from the hospital must not be discovered. . 

When she reached the People's House, she learned that the session was over. A huge crowd in a compact mass was following after the prisoner and his convoy. She elbowed her way through the throng, her eyes darting in all directions, growing more desperate each moment.

Suddenly she caught sight of David. He was scarcely recognizable. His hair was disheveled, his clothes crumpled and dusty, his gait wobbly like a drunkard's, and his face deathly pale.

She took hold of his arm. He did not look back, but walked along as though he had lost his mind. His eyes were cloudy, as though covered with frosted glass. 

The throng neared the prison. A few more steps, and the marchers would reach the wide sprawling area, leading to the edge of the woods. Before long, the crowd would disperse and then she and David would be noticed. . . The danger was increasing. . . She must not lose another second - it was now or never!

She hastily pulled David after her, and both disappeared behind a massive, sprawling acacia tree. Forcing him down to the ground, she panted: "Shanan, an order for your arrest has been issued. They're searching for you high and low. You must leave town at this very minute. once 

· • "He is innocent. He is free of sin. They are murdering an innocent man. . ." She grabbed him by the shoulders, and shook him, screaming: "They'll do the same thing to you! Within twenty-four hours you'll be led along the same route! They'll destroy you! Get away - save yourself!"

A volley of shots resounded from the edge of the woods. 

David's forehead fell against the tree-trunk, and without 221

uttering a word, he cried bitterly. Anastasia stroked his hair gently: "Cry, my dear; pour the bitterness out of your heart."

Little by little, his crying subsided. He sobbed several times like a child, and then fell silent. Anastasia clasped his pale, haggard face, gazed into his tear-stained eyes, and asked tenderly: "Who is he that man they condemned?"’ "Samborik," David answered. "Samborik? Who is Samborik? What's he to you a kinsman?" "No - just Samborik. . ." 

Still clasping his face with both hands, she observed compassionately: 

"How much you love him! Your heart overflows with love for him it can be seen through your eyes as through a window revealing the depths of your soul. He was killed innocently, and now you want to follow him? Do you really think that this would be your Samborik's wish?"

Through David's mind flashed the last words Samborik had uttered to him after he had rescued him from Vaska's persecution and helped his escape from Struga: "The world of Vaska will disappear; but the world of God in one's heart will live forever. . . Acquire the wisdom of the world, my child, and be a good honest man like your father of blessed memory was. . ."

With a sense of relief, like a stray person who finds his way, he now answered Anastasia: • • 

"You're right: Samborik's wish was that I and my world should not disappear. Yes, you're right; I must escape but where to? It seems as though all my bridges have been burned. . ." "Then swim, if your bridges are burned - but save yourself!" "To swim! Yes, that's the only way! The Dniester! I must get back to Struga! I know every tree, each blade of grass. . 

Yes, the Dniester is my only hope... 

Anastasia removed a small gold ring from her finger relic of the good old times - and shoved it into David's hand: "In an emergency you'll be able to sell it or exchange it 222

for food. . ." Then she embraced him and kissed his forehead: "Go- and may God be with you."

David rushed off, heading away from the north side of the City. With mixed emotions of longing and happiness, Anastasia stood alone, her brimming eyes following David's rapid strides until he disappeared into the mysterious darkness of the dense Polipovka woods. 

At five minutes to one, Anastasia was back at her hospital post as though nothing unusual had happened to her. On seeing her, Serebryak could not restrain himself, and approaching her took hold of her hand familiarly. To his astonishment, she pulled back her hand like an enraged cat, and screamed: "Don't you dare! Keep your dirty paws to yourself! One word from you and I'll prefer charges against you, for being fresh with a nurse while she was performing her duty in a 

Soviet hospital!"

Serebryak's experience as a skilled Czarist gendarme immediately told him that Anastasia had misled him. He instantly understood her plot and was certain that David 

Shanan would not return to the hospital.

The fact that he could not even breathe a word to anyone about his unsuccessful plans to have a romance with this nurse, made his vexation even more intense. If Commissar 

Smirnov were ever to learn of this, he would really find himself in trouble. The devil knows those "Bolshevist masters" might even shoot him for this! 

Serebryak felt instinctively that it would be a waste of time to hunt for David within the city. By now he must be on one of the main or side roads leading out of Polipovka. 

By right, several posses ought to be dispatched at once. But he could not tell the Commissar that he had lost a precious hour, that he had dilly-dallied in the hospital in the hope that "the bird would fly into the cage itself." He must convince the Commissar that he had made a thorough search for David and that the latter had evidently vanished from the city. 223

Flushed with anger, Smirnov pounded the table furiously and shouted at Serebryak: "Why didn't you arrest him when he followed the convoy to the execution?” "He did not attend the execution, Comrade Commissar. No one saw him there. He must have made his get-away straight from the courtroom." "I don't want any alibis! I want that fellow in custody!

Pry into every hole and corner and bring him here dead or alive!" 

A thought popped into Smirnov's head: "Where was Samborik buried?" "Prisoners are now digging a ditch on the edge of the woods where he was executed.” "That's good. Post a secret guard at the edge of the forest. 

I'm sure that this sentimental fool, Shanan, will come out of his hideout to visit the grave of his innocent friend. And then we can pounce on him!"

For two days and two nights they kept vigil over the little mound of sod where Samborik lay buried. By now, Smirnov, too, knew that David must have gotten away. "The devil with him!" he said to Serebryak. “I got rid of him — that in itself is a relief. I'm bound to stumble upon him some day - and I'll shoot him down like a dog!" 

Serebryak, however, regarded the matter differently. He was determined to capture David now come what may! It seemed to him that in so doing he would absolve himself of the guilt he felt over his betrayal of Vaska. "Comrade Commissar," he asked Smirnov, "where do you think David Shanan is now?" "The devil knows! Why do you ask such idiotic questions?" "It's not so idiotic, Comrade Commissar. If I were a native of Struga and knew every hidden corner of the Struga Carpathians blindfolded, and were familiar with each centimeter of the Dniester shore in Struga, where would I run to escape from the firing squad? Naturally to Struga! I'll bet you he's heading there on foot through some roundabout way. I could get there sooner on horseback. My name isn't 

Serebryak, if I don't bring him back - dead or alive." 224

His revived instinct of self-preservation and his reawakened will to live were now David's dynamic driving forces. He felt that he must not and would not perish! Like a hunted deer he now gathered all his effort and sharpened his senses in order to escape the peril of death as fast as possible. 

After his hasty parting from Anastasia behind the shield of that massive acacia tree which grew near the north prison wall, David had hurried along the open terrain leading to the edge of the forest. He did not look back even once, and restrained himself from glancing in the direction where Samborik's bullet-riddled body must have lain, not far away.

He was driven by a superhuman urge to get away from this place where he might be caught at any moment. Entering the dense Polipovka forest, he penetrated deeper and deeper into the bewitching shadows of the numerous thousand-yearold oaks.

The emotional strain he had experienced during the past two days, his lack of food, drink and sleep, and his present running through the jungle-like prickly weeds and bushes produced a state of total exhaustion in David. His mind was a blank, he felt dizzy, and in his ears there buzzed the faint echo of a barely audible, distant noise. His eyes grew dim, his knees buckled, and completely spent, he finally slumped to the ground. Submerged in the tall weeds, he fell into a deep, sound sleep which tore him away from the world and its problems.

David did not know for how long he had slept, but when he awoke it was dark and stars twinkled through the treetops. He quickly got to his feet, and felt a wave of nausea rise in his empty stomach. Plagued by hunger and thirst, he was frightened by the mounting weakness which was overcoming his body. 

David was well aware that his survival depended upon two factors: energy and caution. To obtain energy, he must get some nourishment; as to caution — he must advance along out-of-the-way wood and field paths, and only at night. 

During the day he would have to keep out of sight and rest. . . 

He must not lose courage, he must collect his thoughts and be on the alert. Where does one get nourishment? How 225

and where does one find the right road to advance instead of drifting back? . . . 

In dense woods there are not any trodden paths to be found even in broad daylight, let alone in the dark of night. 

The forest is mystifying at night. The trees of the forest slumber during the day, but at night they wake up, and their black shadows look like bizarre, gigantic creatures of another world. The rustle of their branches is like the clamor of some alien, incoherent tongue, quite distinct from the odd jumble of other sounds that fill the woods at night — chirping of crickets, hooting of owls, flutter of bats' wings, howling wolves, and the distant barking of hounds in neighboring homesteads and farms. 

The greatest mystery of the forest at night, however, is that in spite of the diversified hubbub of trees, birds, and animals, there still prevails an absolute and incredible silence. 

It is an awesome silence in which one's own heartbeat sounds like the thumping of a drum, and even one's unexpressed thoughts seem to be calling aloud. 

In the midst of this mysterious nocturnal hush, the familiar, plaintive chant of Psalms suddenly reached David's ears. An icy chill permeated his limbs. He must be imagining things, he thought... How could such a chant reach this jungle at midnight? He had heard that a person who was tormented by prolonged hunger and thirst might become mentally deranged, and start seeing and hearing things that were non-existent. . . He pressed the palms of his hands against his ears with all his might. The chant grew fainter, but it 

I was still audible.

As though in a trance, David proceeded cautiously in the direction of the chant. The darkness was so intense that he advanced like one blindfolded, his outstretched hands groping at the trees. Now and then he would halt and strain his ears. No, it couldn't be! . . . It was the low plaintive voice of

Reb Zechariah, the teacher! He recognized it; there was no doubt. His hair stood on end, sticking him like needles 

No longer able to restrain himself he cried out hysterically: "Teacher, teacher, where are you, teacher?" 226

The chant ceased abruptly. David clasped the nearest tree, shaking feverishly. He felt as though all his limbs were shrinking and spasms choked off his breathing. Cautious footsteps approached. "Who's there?" a man's voice asked. "I can't see it's too dark. Who's there? Have no fear answer." 

Reb Zechariah's groping hands finally grasped David's trembling body. David tore away from the tree and flung himself into Reb Zechariah's arms. In a voice faint from exhaustion, but full of happiness, he said: "Teacher, it's I, David - David of Struga. . . I can hardly stand up - I haven't eaten for the last two days. God Himself has brought you here. . . But Teacher - you in the forest? 

In the middle of the night? What's happened? What are you doing here?" 

"Not now, my son — later, later. . . David of Struga! God in heaven, what have we come to? David, the son of Reb 

Avrum Shanan, the son of a lord, a magnate — almost starved to death! The world is coming to an end! . . . Come with me not far from here, just a few steps - I have my family there. . ." 

Zechariah's frightened wife and children were huddled in the tall underbrush together with their meager belongings. "Zechariah, who's with you? Father, what's happened?” several apprehensive women's voices asked at the same time. "Have no fear, relax. It's no highwayman - it's David, my former pupil from Struga. Nechama darling, he's famished, he hasn't eaten for days. Give him some food."

From one of his bundles, Zechariah took out a bottle and poured a drink for David. 

“That's raisin wine — drink it, it'll brace you, and refresh the dryness in your throat.”

Zechariah's wife, Nechama, shoved some bread and a piece of dry smoked goose into David's hand. He ate and drank ravenously, relishing the bread and meat spiced with salt, pepper and garlic. With every bite, he seemed to regain his normal, youthful vigor and buoyancy. 

Even the inky darkness became less opaque now. David's eyes were growing accustomed to the environment; now he could make out Zechariah's face with its short gray beard, 227

and the spectacles that kept moving up and down on his long nose. He also saw Nechama - a small, thin, sprightly woman, obedient and deferential to her scholarly husband. "She looks like a child, disguised in the clothes of a granny," David mused, and he began to feel at home in her presence. 

He saw Zechariah's oldest daughter, Ziporah, who at the age of twenty-three, was a widow. Her husband, a grainmerchant, had been killed by Ukrainian bandits during one of his journeys through the Verbivka Forest. She was left with a five-year-old boy named Menashke, now sleeping peacefully in his mother's' lap, with his thumb in his mouth. "How fortunate you are," David reflected; "there are no boundaries to the happiness hovering over your serene, childish face." 

He also saw Zechariah's younger daughter, the fourteenyear-old Esther. She squatted, clasping her legs and leaning her head on her knees. She kept looking at David and her young heart filled with a warmth that transformed her previous sadness into a new, hidden joy.

Reb Zechariah's family learned from David the events that had led to his escape, and David in turn, found out how they came to be in the woods. "Yes my child, there is no longer any room here for God's word and His Torah... the new power is waging war against the Lord, and also persecuting those who cannot give up their faith. A Jew deprived of his Torah is, alas, no longer a Jew. I had to choose therefore, between two alternatives: 

To forsake God and His Torah, and cease to be a Jew, or to take the wandering staff as our forefathers have done so often throughout our history, and look for a haven elsewhere. 

America is that haven! Huge Jewish settlements are blossoming in America! I have a brother over there, and should we get there, the Lord willing, I'll still be able to teach Jewish lads and guide them in their study of Torah. "I've arranged with Ukrainian smugglers to lead us across the frontier of Bessarabia to the Rumanians for the little bit of silver that I still have. The road from there is already open, and the world, free. "When dusk set in today, the smugglers brought us here 228

and told us to wait until they call for us. So we're waiting in the dark, and from our sad hearts come the words from the Psalms: "O thou that dwellest in the covert of the Most High, and abidest in the shadow of the Almighty. . . . Thou shalt not be afraid of the terror by night, nor of the arrow that flieth by day." "And so, suddenly - "Teacher, teacher,' I couldn't understand where that voice came from. Well, praised be the Lord - perhaps it was the will of Providence that we meet thus in our wandering."

Reb Zachariah's references to wandering depressed David. 

He thought for a while and then asked: "Teacher, why must Jews always wander? Why must 

Jews forever look for still another haven?" "Because this, my child, is the fate of the Jew." "Fate? Is it fate to be exiled from a country where one has been rooted for generations? Is it my fate to be driven out of Struga? Struga is mine! Mine no less than it is the peasant's! My ancestors poured their sweat into the black soil of Struga! My father soaked the Struga earth with his blood! No one can tear my roots out of the Struga earth!

Struga is mine through flesh and blood, and no one can chase me out of there!" "Your argument, my son, is just. It's unfair to chase you out of Struga. Your people served Struga with heart and soul for generations, and contributed to its growth and existence. No, it's not fair or human to chase you out of 

Struga - but nevertheless, Struga is not yours." "Why not?" "Because you're a Jew!" "What has my attachment and allegiance to the land where I was born and raised got to do with my being a Jew?" "A great deal, my son. The heart and soul of the Jew are, according to Jewish tradition, faith and history, bound to another land the land of Jewish origin. The Struga peasant calls his land 'Mother Earth,' and you cannot call it that, because, as a Jew, you're bound through blood ties to another land. 

"The Jew is the only one in the world for whom his 229

people, his faith, and the land of his origin, are interlinked and inseparable, like body and soul. And here is how it came about: "The Almighty made a covenant with the first Jew, Abraham, and sealed it with blood. The covenant was this: 'Walk before Me, and be thou whole-hearted. Spread my mission of justice and thy reward shall be. . . I will give unto thee, and to thy seed after thee, the land of thy sojournings, all the land of Canaan, for an everlasting possession. . .' This covenant between God and Jew is permanent; Jews observe it to this day and will continue to observe it forever. "Yes, my son, on the eighth day after your birth in your beloved Struga, your parents pledged that you would follow that covenant. They had you circumcised and pronounced the benediction "Bring him into the covenant of Father 

Abraham." That means that your fate was sealed from the moment you emerged into this world - your destiny is to carry God's word, to learn and spread His Torah, and to yearn after the Promised Land. . . Your parents brought you up in that tradition even as their parents had done with them; and you will carry on the same way with your children no matter where you are, no matter in what country you take root once again. . "Honestly speaking, how can a Jew deny his soul's bonds to another land, when all of our prayers from first to last, are filled with sanctification and nostalgia for the land of our origin, Zion, and for our holy city, Jerusalem. Each time the holy scroll is taken out of the Ark to be read, the worshippers. exclaim fervently: 'For out of Zion shall go forth the law, and the word of the Lord from Jerusalem.' "A Jew, my son, must be a loyal and devoted citizen to the country of his adoption; but as long as he is a Jew he is spiritually attached to the Jewish people, to the Jewish faith, and to the land of his Biblical ancestors. No matter how much one tries to argue one's way out of it, one can't! 

No my son, to chase you out of Struga is certainly not fair,. but your claim that Struga is no less yours than the peasant's, is also unfair." "In that case, must we be driven like sheep from place to, 230

place? Is this the reward for a Chosen People?" "We are the Chosen not because we are the best people in the world, but rather because we are the preachers of a holy mission, of God's word and God's Torah. A Jew who doesn't fulfill that mission is not one of the Chosen. We undertook long ago to observe the precepts of the Torah. 

Therefore, we have to carry out our duty wholeheartedly whether we find ourselves in Struga, Polipovka, Bessarabia, or America. As long as we continue to study the Torah, and as long as we observe its precepts of humaneness and justice we'll survive, no matter how much they persecute us! The secret of our survival is not that we are extraordinary creatures, but that the Torah which was entrusted to us is everlasting and imperishable. . 

Reb Zechariah breathed a deep sigh, and fell silent. The nocturnal hush of the forest fell on this small wandering group. Judging by their even rhythmic breathing, they all seemed to be sleeping soundly. Stretched out on the ground, his head propped on his hands, his eyes open, David was steeped in reveries.

His childhood years in Struga flitted across his mind. He recalled incidents that had taken place before he left Struga "to seek God's word, and to study God's Torah." He could remember an instance, while listening to his father tell about the Jews wandering through their two-thousand-year-old exile, and waiting for a redeemer to bring them back to the 

Promised Land, when he had remarked: "Struga is the best land in the world; let the redeemer bring all the Jews to 

Struga." He could still hear his father's answer: "No, my child - Struga is a part of the exile. It is not the land of our forefathers."

Then, he had not been able to grasp the meaning of those words; he had revolted against such an attitude toward Struga. It was only now, after listening to Reb Zechariah's true, forthright words, that he understood what his father had said. "Struga is a part of the exile. It is not the land of our forefathers. 

They heard the thud of heavy footsteps coming towards them. The noise of crackling dry twigs, and the rustle of 231

foliage and underbrush, alerted everyone. With palpitating hearts and bated breaths, they sat crouched, straining their ears to listen to the approaching unknown. David sat up quickly and whispered: "Teacher, someone is coming." "Shh it could be the smugglers." “Teacher, I'll leave now. My presence here might hinder you." 

"No David — our meeting in the woods was an act of Providence. Remain with us - and let God's will be done. . .”

The figure of a peasant emerged from among the trees. 

Zechariah recognized him as one of the smugglers. The peasant slowly scratched the back of his head and said, 

"Come on. Time doesn't stand still let's get going. We've got to reach the Verbivka Forest before daybreak. We'll walk in single file, five or six steps apart from each other. I'll lead, and the one following me do what I do if I halt, do the same, if I drop to the ground, do it too. The rest do the same. 

Remember no noise, not a sound! You want to cross the 

Dniester? Then keep quiet and do as I say!" 

The peasant again scratched his head and squinting keenly, riveted his cat-like eyes on David and asked in amazement: "And who's he? How did he get here? The agreement was a family of four and a child; three women, an elderly man and a child. We didn't bring this fellow here. Who is he? 

What's he doing here?" “I too, am a refugee," David explained. "I lost my way in the woods and stumbled upon my teacher's family. I'm also anxious to cross the border." "Yes, indeed," Reb Zechariah quickly corroborated. "He's now one of us. You understand? We were a family of five, and now we're six. What difference does it make to you? 

We don't want it for nothing." "What are you babbling there, old man? To smuggle women and an old man isn't the same as smuggling a young fellow.

He could be a deserter from the Party, or a spy! For that, brother, they shoot you down on the spot! Smuggling a young fellow is a great risk. For that, one has got to get well paid. You, old man, your wife, the daughters and the child 232

- I'll take. That's what we agreed - that's the way it'll be. 

Let him go his own way!"

When Reb Zechariah had decided to leave Polipovka and steal across the border with his family, he had secretly sold all the belongings he had acquired during a lifetime of hard work. Then he had equally divided the silver and gold coins thus obtained among all the members of the family. "Let each one sew his share of the money into his clothing," he had instructed. "No one can tell what's in store for us on such a hazardous journey. If, God forbid, one of us should become separated from the rest of us, it will certainly help for that one to have a little money; your life could depend on that.”

And so each one sewed his coins into a concealed place on his or her garments, and guarded them carefully. Reb 

Zechariah had emphasized the importance of such security, and the members of his family understood and carried out his wish.

When the smuggler refused to take David along, none of Zechariah's family budged. It was obvious that they were determined not to abandon him, and would not proceed without him.

Esther was the first to express that thought concretely. 

Tearing one side of her dress, she took out a tiny bag filled with jingling coins, and stepping over to David, softly said: "Take it - it's filled with silver and a few gold coins and hand it to him, so he'll let you come along."

David stood bewildered, without touching the money. "Take it David," Reb Zechariah encouraged. “I intended to do the same thing but Esther, bless her, was ahead of me.”

The peasant took the money, counted it, shoved it into his bosom, shrugged his shoulders in wonder, and mumbled: "You Jews are strange people. I'll never understand you. 

Well, let's get going!"

The peasant walked ahead, halted now and then, looked about him, listened carefully for a moment, and then went on. Behind him, walking in single file, a few steps apart, trudged the wanderers of a Jewish family. Bowed down with grief and fear, they summoned courage and faith with every 233

advancing step to steer through the perilous journey towards a new life of humaneness, justice and integrity. 

For three days and three nights, David and Reb Zechariah's family traveled along out-of-the-way paths through fields and forests. On the fourth day they found themselves in a thicket at the foot of the Carpathians, only a few miles from Struga. This was the last and most critical step of their journey. Later at night, when the surrounding inhabitants would be soundly asleep, the wanderers would make their way to the bank of the Dniester where they would be smuggled across in a boat that was prepared for them. 

David became restless. The nearness of Struga aroused in him emotions that stirred and drew him to the home of his childhood. With closed eyes he now envisioned every lane, every tree, every garden fence. So near, so very near. . . He was practically in Struga! How he yearned to glimpse his old home-locked and deserted since his father's death. 

How anxious he was to look at that massive apple tree beside which was his father's grave. How much he wanted to run through the orchard, to sprint over the fence to Samborik's dwelling. This last thought tore at his heart. . . Esther felt David's restlessness. Moving cautiously over to him, she touched his hand and asked: "What's happened, David? Why are you so sad? You've changed since we entered this thicket. You haven't taken my hand, not even once. Even with father, you're not the same. Before, both of you were forever talking about Torah, but now he's doing all the talking and you're so silent. Your eyes stare ahead so dreamily; you're hiding something from 

What's the matter with you, David?" us.

David picked up Esther's hand and kissed it tenderly, then pressed his cheek affectionately against it.

In the course of the past three days and three nights of wandering together, David had become so close to Reb Zechariah's family, that it seemed to him as though he had spent a lifetime with them. He became especially attached to Esther. She had fascinated him since their first meeting in 

Polipovka Forest, when she had acted with an independence 234

and courage more in keeping with an adult than a fourteenyear-old girl. He had noticed the sharp contrast between her mature mind and behavior, and her undeveloped, almost childish physical appearance. Nature had not, as yet, endowed her with her share of entrancing mellowness that creates and shapes alluring femininity. . 

She was rather small. Her smooth chestnut hair was combed neatly into two hanging braids. Her thin, delicate face had even, charming features. Her eyes were of an indeterminate color a blend of gray and blue, which reflected a compassionate goodness. Her figure was erect, slender and childish.

It was remarkable, that although Esther's body still lacked the enticing contours of a mature woman, her spiritual intensity, sensitivity, warmth and kindness were those of a fullgrown, motherly person.

It had happened on the second night of their wandering. 

They had been in a hideout in the Verbivka Forest, not far from the White Inn. The smuggler had ordered them to remain calm and quiet, as he proceeded to reconnoiter the territory. "For God's sake, not a squeak out of you! The

White Inn is full of Red Army men. Voices in the forest echo very loudly and far away. If they hear you - all is lost!"

Little Menashke had been sleeping peacefully, snug in his mother's arms. He must have had a dream, for he suddenly awakened and cried out: "Mother, mother, the dog - take him away, I'm afraid, I'm afraid!" 

The youngster's voice echoed shrilly through the woods. 

The wandering group recoiled in fear; Reb Zechariah clutched his head, pleading “Quiet him, hush him up, or heaven forbid, we may all perish!" Nechama wrung her hands, trembled, and wept. Ziporah snatched the shawl off her shoulders and pressed it against the child's mouth. Choked by this gag, Menashke began to gasp and flounder spasmodically. 

Esther quickly yanked the shawl from Ziporah's hand, cast it aside, and picking up the child, let him cry softly against her slender throat. Rocking him in her arms, she started to hum a sweet lullaby. Menashke stopped crying and asked half-sobbing: 235

"Esther, why did Mother want to choke me?” "Because you were shouting, little one. One mustn't shout in the woods. Bad men may hear you, come here, and hurt all of us - Grandpa, Grandma, Mother and you." "And you, too? And David, too?" "Yes, my dear - everybody." "How can they hurt mother?" "They can take her away from you- and leave Menashke without a mother." "Esther, I want to go back to mother; I want to sleep."

Esther returned the youngster to his mother. Ziporah took him into her arms, hugged him, and leaning her head against his, let her silent tears flow freely. . .

When everything quieted down, and Reb Zechariah's family was once more enveloped in the mysterious, nocturnal stillness of the woods, David edged his way cautiously towards Esther. He felt for her gropingly, and gently caressed her delicate face, thin throat, and narrow shoulders.

Esther did not resist, and as she let David's hands rove along her body she sensed a delightful warmth spread through her limbs. Entwining her fingers with his, she pressed his hand against her small breasts, feeling the wonderful thrill of the dawning of her first true love. . . 

And now on the fourth day of their wandering, when their proximity to Struga was upsetting David so much, and when the memories of his birthplace and childhood were filling him with longing as well as sorrow, Esther appeared at his side ready to share the burden of his sadness. . . · • "Esther, my darling," David whispered to her, “I'm not hiding a thing from you. . . I just can't seem to believe that 

I'm back in Struga. Do you see that trail? It's a shortcut leading straight to our orchard. . . A few short minutes and

I'd be in my home in the house where I used to cuddle close to my mother; where my father's voice while he was studying Torah used to echo in all the rooms; where my sisters and I enjoyed security and happiness. Yes, only a stone's throw away along that trail. . .” 

236

David's expression of nostalgia for Struga did not impress 

Esther. Shrugging her shoulders, she remarked: "All that's dear to you in Struga is no more; it's dead!

When someone near to you dies, it hurts badly, but you don't die with him. . . David, the road for you doesn't lead to Struga any more, just as mine doesn't lead to Polipovka. . .' 

David gazed at Esther in amazement and said: "Esther dear, how right you are! How true your words are!

We can't pursue yesterday's road. The road of tomorrow is hard and dangerous but it's the only straight road for you and me."

Esther moved closer to David. He felt her warm breath on his face. Enchanted with happiness, she whispered in his ear: "David my darling, let's you and me, together, always follow the straight road of our tomorrow. I'll devote my whole life to you. Your happiness and peace of mind will be my reward.

David turned to face Esther. Their eyes met and reflected mutual pride and understanding. Then he called out: "Teacher, this is the only right way," and hurried off along the trail leading to the orchard of his childhood home in Struga...

Dashing along the narrow winding forest trail, he seemed to hear the echo of Esther's words, "Everything that's dear to you in Struga is no more, is dead. The road for you doesn't lead to Struga any longer. ." And, indeed, the closer he drew to Struga, the more removed from his heart it became. 

David sensed a strange exhilaration, and for the first time in his life an indiffference toward Struga. He was completely convinced that his bond of generations to Struga had torn, rotted, and fallen apart. There wasn't the least doubt in his mind that his destiny was now linked to Reb Zechariah's wandering family. His impulsive resolution to return to Struga in order to divert the attention of the 

Dniester sentinels filled him with pride and joy. "Let them cross the border first of all," David thought. "I'll follow them later. Nothing in the world will stop me - 238

not the earth of Struga, not the enchantment of the Carpathians, and not the trap set by Serebryak!"

David halted at the edge of the woods. A few steps to the right, and he would find himself at the north fence of his father's orchard; a few steps to the left, and he would be on that wide Struga highway that bisected the entire length of the village. Yonder, the round copper church cupolas were visible, and over there he could discern among the trees the tilting hut of Vassilii Kravlenko's father, Ivan the drunkard. 

An idea suddenly flashed through his mind: “Ivan the drunkard!" I could be helped through him if he only knew that Serebryak is actually the murderer of his Vaska! 

Yes, I must reveal Serebryak's betrayal to Ivan! That's my only chance to fight Serebryak through Ivan's revenge." 

Clambering up trees and springing from one bush to another, David managed to reach Ivan's hut unobserved. Ivan sat dozing on the earthen ledge alongside his hut. “Ivan, Ivan," David called out in a low voice. Ivan looked about dazedly, and seeing David, asked: "Who are you! What do you want here?" "Come here, Ivan - right here behind this tree. I want to talk to you about something very important." "Say who you are the devil with you!" 

"I want to talk to you about Vaska; I know who killed him." "Is that anything new? Everybody knows - the Jews, those anti-Christs murdered him! Trotsky's sons-of-bitches, the devil with them!" "No, Ivan - I know who the guilty one really is."

Ivan rose from the ledge, and narrowing his bleary, colorless eyes, advanced slowly and suspiciously toward David.

The peasant's head and face were covered by disheveled gray hair. His skin was furrowed with deep wrinkles, folds of skin dangling from his jowls, His red-blue nose was covered with a net of thick purple veins. His stooped, gaunt figure and entire appearance clearly showed his physical degeneration due to excessive consumption of alcohol. Scrutinizing David, he remarked: "Seems I know you, don't I? But I can't place you. With- 239

out a drink my memory doesn't seem to work at all. Nothing clicks with me without a drink. The devil with it! It's hard to get a good mug of liquor now - there's only stinking homebrew that burns your insides out. And even that's hard to get unless you trade away your household. I drank everything up already, the devil with it! I even traded the icons for a drink. The house is empty now. . . and I'm dying for a drink. I'd kill for a drink... Well, speak up - who are you? 

And what do you know about Vaska?" "I'm David Shanan, the son of Avrum Shanan, and I know who betrayed Vaska." 

These words jolted Ivan out of his dream-state: "You? Yes, now I recognize you! Well, you've certainly grown up. Only a runt a while ago - and now a big bruiser! 

I heard you're a big shot among your Jewish commissars. 

What are you doing in Struga? What the devil are you up to here?" "I've come to tell you that the man who was responsible for Vassilii's death is in Struga right now. You know him very well he was Vassilii's best friend." "Don't keep me in suspense - speak plainly." "Do you remember Serebryak and Fedka? The Count sent them both on a mission to Polipovka. They were caught there by the Red Army and accused of espionage. The War Commissar, Smirnov, offered to free them if they would join up with the Bolsheviks - and would agree to betray the Count and Vassilii. Fedka refused to be a traitor, and was shot down. Serebryak saved his own life by accepting their offer. . .” 

Grabbing the lapels of David's jacket, Ivan pulled him toward himself and, boiling with fury, raved: "You're a liar, you Jewish dog! Say it's a lie, or I'll kill you on the spot!"

David gripped Ivan's wrists with such force that the old man writhed in pain, and let go of his lapels. The peasant sensed David's determination and courage, and was afraid of him. The young Jew's powerful hands surprised him, and he cowered before him. The muscles on the peasant's face and limbs quivered spasmodically. Pressing his fists 240

against his chest and trembling with hate and wrath, he bellowed hoarsely: "Serebryak - the Moscow swine, the son-of-a-bitch! Sold 

Vaska to save his own mangy hide! Killed Vaska and killed me! When Vaska was alive everyone knew Ivan Kravlenko!

It was an honor to drink with Ivan Kravlenko. Since my 

Vaska's been killed, no one pays any attention to me! Ivan

Kravlenko has to beg for a drink and everyone spits at him now... Killed Vaska and killed me. Where is he - that murderer of my Vaska? Lead me to him - I'll stick a knife into his belly! Where is Serebryak?" "He's at the Dniester. He's guarding the shore; he's in charge of a whole detachment of Red Army men. He became a commander that's the reward he got from the commissars for Vassilii's death." 

"He'll pay - he'll pay with his blood. . . A drink - I must have a drink. . .'

David took the ring that Anastasia had given him, and handing it to Ivan, said: "Take it - it's pure gold. You'll be able to get all the homebrew you want for this. You won't have to beg for a drink anymore, and you'll be the same Ivan Kravlenko you used to be.. 

For the past three days and three nights, the Struga bank of the Dniester had been under strict guard. By a special decree of War Commissar Smirnov, the complete command of the permanent military squadron at Struga had been assigned to Serebryak - until his "important and secret mission" had been carried out successfully. 

For Serebryak, this assignment was the most noteworthy event of his life, and the most stirring adventure of his entire career as a police gendarme. To him, the task of capturing 

David was more than a mere military assignment or duty; it was a challenge, and also, in a way, a sort of race between life and death. He visualized how the success of this mission would bring him prestige, promotion in rank, and excellent prospects for the future. He did not wish to dwell on possible 241

failure, for he knew that that would plunge him into complete ruin - and perhaps lead to a fresh indictment before the 

Tribunal. 

Consequently, Serebryak was putting his whole heart and soul into this assignment. Armed Red Guards had secretly been posted all along the shore line. Strict orders had been issued to detain, without exception, everyone approaching the river bank, and to bring him to Serebryak for the latter's personal interrogation.

At first the commander of the local squadron had urged Serebryak to deploy half of the military force to the shore line, and dispatch the other half to the village for a thorough search of the huts and orchards. Serebryak, however, considered the shore line the one place that Shanan must inevitably come to. Therefore, he concentrated the entire squadron there. "Shanan is too shrewd to hide in the village for very long," he said to the local commander. "He knows that sooner or later he'd be noticed and betrayed. His only way of escaping would be to smuggle himself across the frontier and join the Rumanians.”

Serebryak himself did not leave the shore line for an instant. He made the rounds from one soldier to another, and whenever he suspected one of them of not being sufficiently alert, he clamored, "It's not to catch a nap now and then, comrade, that you've been assigned here! Your duty is to be alert and watch! Watch every shadow of every suspicious thing. Keep your eyes and ears open! This isn't just a game- it's a command from the Soviet power!...'

A young officer came running from the local military headquarters and breathlessly reported to Serebryak: "Comrade, important news! A peasant just passed by headquarters and told us that he saw Shanan hiding among the trees in his father's orchard. I believe that we ought to withdraw the squadron from the shore and throw a cordon around the orchard. Shanan wouldn't escape that trap!"

Serebryak regarded the courier contemptuously and began firing him with questions: "What's your hurry Comrade? How do you know the news 242

is true? Who is the peasant? What makes you think he can be trusted? Why haven't you asked him how he found out that we're on the lookout for Shanan? Hasn't it occurred to you that this fellow could be some slick provocateur, who gave you this report just to trick us into recalling the guard from the shore, so that while we were away Shanan could cross the Dniester without anyone noticing him? It's quite likely that Shanan is hiding near here in one of the caves or crevices of the Carpathians. Does a Soviet officer change military strategy because of a word from some unknown suspicious passerby? Comrade, go back to headquarters to your records, and leave the planning of police tactics to us!" 

A tumult of voices suddenly echoed along the guarded shore line. Shading his eyes, Serebryak tried to discern what was going on. Several Red Guards were struggling with someone, forcing him to come along. Serebryak thrilled with joy, and in his mind there flashed the thought: "Bravo! They've nabbed him. I've trapped him at last!"

Serebryak hastened in the direction of the commotion, but suddenly halted in his tracks, flabbergasted. He caught sight of Vassilii's father Ivan, dead-drunk, throwing himself around, cursing and shouting. The soldiers held him with force, dragging him hand and foot, until they brought him to Serebryak. "Let go of him!" Serebryak commanded. "He's got nothing to do with our Shanan. Leave him alone!" And to Ivan he said: "Still the same! I thought that the liquor had burned you to a cinder by now. Well, go your way, and don't interfere with the government in the performance of its duties." 

Ivan tried to steady himself, and looking at Serebryak through his watery eyes he shouted: 

"You murderer, you traitor! You betrayed my Vaska it's because of you he was killed! You thought you'll get away with it, that no one will find out about it - but Ivan knows all! The whole world will know all now. Soldiers, he's a 'traitor! He's a murderer! Shanan himself told me the whole truth!" 

Serebryak leaped at Ivan, clutched his gray mane of hair, and foaming at the mouth, yelled: "Where did you see Shanan? When did you speak to Shanan? 243

Where is he? What did he tell you? You're drunk - you've made up all this!" "Take your murderous paws off me! Drunk I am, but I don't make up things. You and Fedka sat in the Polipovka 

Prison. Fedka let himself be shot because he wouldn't betray 

Vaska. But you - you've killed Vaska! Murderer! Murderer! Murderer!" 

Tightening his grip on Ivan's hair with one hand, Serebryak smashed his other fist into the peasant's face. "Shanan! Tell me where Shanan is! Where and when did he tell you all this? Speak up you damned drunk!"

Ivan pulled out a sharp, crooked shoemaker's blade from his pocket, and thrusting it into Serebryak's belly with all his might, he raved wildly: 

“Shanan's at my home! He told me everything — the whole truth! Pay now! Pay for Vaska's blood!" 

Serebryak growled, and crouching in pain, groped for the wound from which his blood gushed forth. With a trembling hand, he pulled out his revolver and emptied all of its five bullets into Ivan. "Comrade Commander," Serebryak gasped faintly between groans of pain, "Withdraw the entire garrison from here at once, and surround this drunkard's house and orchard! Don't lose a minute! Shanan must be captured dead or alive!" 

"But you, Comrade — you've been seriously wounded. You're losing too much blood. You must be taken to a dispensary at once!" 

"First Shanan! I can wait. . . Withdraw the guard and surround Ivan's home at once this very instant!" 

The shore line was deserted and quiet. Serebryak, semiconscious, squatted in a pool of his own blood. Near him lay the dead body of Ivan...
