Because of his profuse bleeding, Serebryak's ears rang strangely. His head felt empty, and the world about him rocked and reeled. David emerged from the underbrush. He advanced toward him with slow but firm steps. Serebryak opened his glazed eyes wide, tried to cry out, and could not... 

He toppled over, his face falling on Ivan Kravlenko's motionless chest. .. 244

The Dniester was as smooth and placid as the surface of a mirror. David was swimming away, the Struga shore line receding further and further behind him. The clear, refreshing water of the river cooled his feverish mind, still milling with the thought - "Struga, Struga, the end of Struga The kindness of mother; the self-respect of father; the 

Torah of Reb Itzikel; and the humaneness of Samborik my legacy from Struga... I'll never forsake this legacy; I'll cherish it deep in my heart, as long as I live. . .”

David turned his head and looked at Struga for the last time. It seemed to him as though the ridges of the surrounding Carpathian Mountains solemnly looked down on the Dniester shore, and lamented the gruesome shedding of human blood. 245

Land of Freedom 

Like a bough torn from a tree trunk by a tempest and tossed onto alien land—that was how David felt when he had been uprooted from his native soil of Struga and Polipovka, and flung into strangeness. 

Swimming across the Dniester, with his spiritual legacy in his heart, David became a homeless refugee. For the first time in his life he sensed that he was a wanderer, belonging to no one and cared for by no one. He was seized by an overwhelming sadness and thought: "What am I? Who am 

I? Whoever misses me? Who needs me? Of what use is a life like this?" David's grief was dispelled like the dissipation of a dense morning fog by the illuminating rays of the sun, when he was reunited with Reb Zechariah's family at the East Rumanian frontier town of Britchan, in Bessarabia. . In order to cope with the ever-mounting influx of Jewish refugees that kept flocking in from the Ukrainian-Russian side and were flooding the township, the community elders of Britchan had set up a huge barracks, as well as a "people's kitchen." The hapless refugees found a temporary haven there, until such times as their relatives in America, or the Philanthropic institutions of that country, could come to their rescue with visas to that legendary land where they hoped to find freedom and happiness.

David squatted in a far corner of the barracks, sadly eyeing the great throng of people. Amidst an uproar of bickering, laughter and tears, men, women and children crowded about 246

on the dirt floor, each with his bundle of rags, each with his pack of poverty. They appeared to him like heaps of rubbish. 

A cauldron of soup and a big tray filled with sliced bread had just been brought in from the "people's kitchen." The crowd pounced on the food like a pack of starved animals devoid of human dignity, ready to kill one another for an extra piece of bread. "My God," David thought in bitter despair, “what man can become almost overnight! Only yesterday these men were dignified citizens, property owners, with social responsibilities. How insignificant man must be, if he can stoop so low! 

Are these the proud sons of Israel? Are these part of the "Chosen People?"

Suddenly, something unusual took place. A middle-aged 

Jew, with a long black beard and dark, sparkling eyes, dashed into the barracks, mounted a crate, and called out: "Fellow Jews, one of our refugees, Reb Zechariah, from the Polipovka Yeshiva, will teach the Holy Torah every afternoon beginning today, in the old Synagogue of Britchan!" 

Upon hearing Reb Zechariah's name, David cheered up at once. He rose to hasten toward the Synagogue, but his progress was impeded by others elbowing their way to the exit of the barracks. "What's happened?" David wondered. "They're turning away from the soup bowls! They're dropping the chunks of bread they were snatching just a moment ago! Where are they rushing to? What bewitched them so suddenly? Was it the burning eyes and black beard of the Jew? Was it the name of Reb Zechariah of Polipovka? Or was it the Torah?"

The Synogogue was packed. The assembly hung on Reb 

Zechariah's every word. David could not make his way to the dais, and leaning against a wall he became part of the throng, engrossed, oblivious to all else. . A chill of reverence swept over the crowd as Reb Zechariah intoned the verse from Exodus "and ye shall be unto Me a 

Kingdom of priests, and a holy nation.” "God has chosen us," he continued, "not because we are stronger, better or nicer than other nations, but because we have accepted His Torah. We have vowed to live according 247

to the laws of holy Torah among ourselves, and among the other peoples of the world. God's Torah is everlasting and indestructible. As long as we continue to devote ourselves to the study of the Torah we shall never perish, and no human force in the world will be able to crush us. When the Lord gave the Torah to us on Mount Sinai, He said to us that if we would guard the Torah, study it continually, and follow its tenets, then 'Ye shall be unto Me a Kingdom of priests, and a holy nation. 

Remarkable! David scanned the faces of the congregation, and no longer saw in them the abasement and despondency of wandering beggars milling about on the earthen floor of the barracks. He saw pride and dignity in them. These were the children of a nation whose heritage was the legacy of the Torah.

David was happy to be one of them. "What does it matter where we are, where we are driven to, or how we are tortured? As long as we have the Torah, we belong to the 

Lord's 'kingdom of priests and a holy nation.' No one can take that from us. That is the secret of our endurance, and of our integrity. 29 After many months of wandering, Reb Zechariah, his family, and David, arrived in New York. They settled on the East 

Side, on Canal Street. Here Reb Zechariah found fresh sources of active Jewish life, and was in his element again. 

He lost no time in establishing a small Yeshivah-academy, naming it "Beth Itzhok” — in memory of the Polipovka Rabbi, Reb Itzikel.

From time to time, David would come to Reb Zechariah's Yeshivah on Canal Street and study a page or two of Torah and Talmud with him. He loved it because it made him feel that Canal Street harboured the connective links with the legacy of his past.

David loved Canal Street for another reason: it was here that he and Esther had struck roots in their adopted land and had felt the warmth of their first happiness together. Ever since that terror-haunted night, when David in the course of his perilous escape from Polipovka had crept 248

stealthily along in the woods and had stumbled upon Reb

Zechariah's family there, he had been like one of them. Reb 

Zechariah and his wife, Nechama, were as fond of him as though he were their own son, and Esther's instant love for him had contained all the purity and intensity of her youthful innocence. 

On the morning following their arrival to New York,

David left the house while all the others were still sound asleep. No one knew where he had gone. He returned that evening, and depositing three dollars and fifty cents on the table, said merrily: "America is a wonderful country! Everybody works! Most of the American millionaires started as working men - for a couple of dollars a day - just like me." 

Reb Zechariah smiled naively, without saying a word. 

Nechama's face crumpled into a look of disappointment.

Esther stared at the enthusiastic David, and later in the evening as they sat alone on the narrow balcony, she said to him: "David dear, you seemed so happy and contented with your earnings today - you spoke so admiringly about millionaires I had the feeling that you do not wish to become a doctor any more." 

David embraced Esther tenderly, pressed her head against his chest, and kissing her eyes gently, replied: "No my darling, medicine is no longer a question of wishing or not wishing. It's part of me. I can't imagine my future life without it. Medicine is the dream of my dreams.

But America is a very practical country. Without means, it's very here." hard here. Without means 

· one can't study medicine

Esther snuggled up closer to David. "David dear, promise me that you won't go back to work tomorrow." "Why?" "Because I want you to start preparing yourself for entrance to the University. I'll be very unhappy if you force yourself to do something you don't want to do." "Entrance to the university — without means?” 

249

"I found a job today. 'How old are you?' the employer asked me. 'You look too young-in my shop I don't hire children under the age of fifteen.' - 'I'm not as young as

I look,' I told him. 'I'm already engaged to be married and my fiance is studying medicine." I must admit that I felt so proud when I made that last statement." "You - working in a factory? No! I won't permit it. I couldn't stand that!" David protested. 

Esther put her hand over his mouth: "Do you remember what I told you in the forest? You seem to have forgotten, but I haven't. For me that became the most important commandment of my life. I recall every word I said: 'Let's you and me together, always follow the straight road of our tomorrow' .. Yes, darling - you go on with your studies, and I'll worry about the means. I'll have the feeling that I'm sharing in your happiness in healing the sick." 

David pressed Esther's hand tighter to his lips in deep gratitude.

They found their first year in America the most difficult one. From early morning until late at night, each would be engrossed in his separate chores: Esther busy at her job, and David at his studies. 

David would feel very upset each time he saw his tiny, delicate Esther come home exhausted after a hard day's work in the factory. She would invariably greet him with a smile of boundless happiness: "David dear, come and tell me how your studying went today. You're so clever the more you talk, the more I love to listen to you." 

One evening David said: "Esther, my darling - Esther it's not right! You're sacrificing too much - and this is only the beginning. Even if 

I'm admitted to the university in the Fall, it still means five years of study, and then a few years of training in a hospital. And you - in a factory all that time? The mere thought of it scares me. And then, I'm afraid we'll drift apart." 250

"Are you thinking of leaving New York?" she asked alarmed. "No, my dear. Don't you understand? The road to my medical career will lead me into another environment, another society. While you go on working in the factory for years to help me out we'll be growing further and further apart because we'll be leading such different lives."

Esther pondered on this for a moment, and then asked: "What's the remedy doctor?" "To study! I know it's hard to do both, but I'll help you.

It's never too late to study. First we'll study English together, then we'll read books, and attend lectures, concerts, opera, theatre. We'll criticize, discuss, and in this way we'll create a common cultural background for both of us, together." 

Esther threw her arms around him, showering him with ardent kisses, and exclaimed with childish joy: "From now on my work will seem sweeter than honey!"

On the day when David was admitted to the Columbia University Medical School, he went about in a daze mumbling to himself: 

“Medicine I'm a medical student! At last I'm on the path that will bring me to the realization of my life's dream!” 

His head in the clouds, David sauntered aimlessly through the noisy streets of New York. "It's strange - so strange" he mused, "the wish to study medicine came to me in Struga and Polipovka - in such a different world, and so far away. 

Who would have imagined then, that my ambitions would be realized here in New York?"

He remembered his mother and her wondrous tales about the Angel Raphael; his father, with his great love for study;

Samborik, with his infinite goodness; and Reb Itzikel, with his interpretation of the doctor's true mission. . . Now, more than ever before, he felt that these shadowing figures of the past were the pillars upon which his entire life had been erected and would be sustained. He was seized with a lonesomeness for them.

He felt an urge to see Esther, as if she were the embodiment of those whose legacy he carried in his heart. He entered 251

the long corridor of the factory where she worked, and proceeded toward the wing from which the humming of sewing machines resounded. He often came to meet Esther after work, and was well known at the factory. However, his appearance in the operators' section this time surprised everyone. 

The forelady came over to him and asked: "Shanan, what are you doing here?" "I want to see Esther it's urgent." 

"Esther? I don't quite understand." "What's there to understand? I want to see Esther." 

The forelady eyed him suspiciously: "Don't you know that Esther doesn't work as an operator any more?" “No, I didn't know that. Where does she work?" "She's been working in the office for over a month. Didn't she tell you that?"

Without replying, David wheeled around and hastened in the direction of the office. "David dear," Esther cried out when she saw him, “I wanted to surprise you. . . the reward of learning. I wanted to tell you when you would bring me the news of your acceptance to the University. You look different today. Has anything happened?" "Yes, my darling, it's happened." "You've been accepted?" "Yes." "Thank God, thank God," she whispered, closing her eyes.

David took hold of both her hands and said: "Come, I've got so much to talk to you about. My heart is full- I can't wait. Drop everything and let's go."

Before Esther had a chance to ask where they were going, David hailed a cab, and called out to the driver: "Columbia University Medical School, please." 

While they were riding along, she asked in amazement: "Columbia – now? Do you register on the same day of your acceptance? Isn't this sort of unusual?" "Of course, dear. According to the letter of instructions from the school I have to register two weeks from today, but 252

I want to take that first step across the threshold of Columbia with you."

He put his arm around her and they remained silent, swaying gently in the swiftly moving cab, each immersed in his own thoughts.

David held Esther's hand tightly in his with religious awe, as they both ascended the stone steps leading to the spacious entrance of the Medical School. Their eyes were riveted to the space over the gigantic door where between two huge marble columns, the inscription "College of Physicians and 

Surgeons" was carved in stone. 

"This is Columbia University!" David spoke with fervor, as Esther listened spellbound. "It's a hundred and seventy years old. Yes, my dear it was founded in 1754, by decree of the English King, George II. At that time it was called 'King's College.' Thirty years later, in 1784, after the Revolutionary War it became 'Columbia College.' The institution carried that name for a hundred and twenty-eight long years. 

It was only in 1912 that it was renamed 'Columbia University.' In 1767 when it was still called 'King's College,' it established a medical school the first in the North American colonies; and the first two doctors to graduate from that school were Robert Tucker in 1770, and Samuel Kissam in 1771. In 1807 another medical school was established, under the name of 'College of Physicians and Surgeons.' Seven years later, in 1814, that school merged with Columbia College, becoming the medical school of Columbia University. And the name 'College of Physicians and Surgeons' has remained to this day."

After a momentary pause, and without removing his eyes from the inscription David went on: 

"Do you know, Esther, it was only seven years ago in 1917 that the Columbia University Medical School started to admit women on an equal footing with men.” 

Esther turned to David with a puzzled look: "Are you implying that I should start taking up medical courses, too?" "No dear, in this instance I thoroughly approve of the 253

pre attitude. I have nothing against the emancipation of women, but there are certain responsibilities that aren't suitable for women and I consider medicine one of them. 

The woman's role is to create ideal happiness at homejust as you're doing for me. Without you by my side, I couldn't become a doctor." "That's silly. My father says that you're a genius and that you know the Talmud well enough to be a rabbi. Just look at what you've accomplished in such a short time. A refugee from Russia, and after less than a year in this country, you're accepted at Columbia University on a par with native American students. You've got an aptitude for studying and you'll master the medical course without any trouble, too. 

I'm sure you'll be at the top of your class." "Mastering the medical course is not the most important thing." "No? What's the most important thing?" "To become a true doctor." "What is a true doctor?” "A true doctor tries to cure his patient not just with his medical knowledge, but with compassion as well... While knowledge is acquired at the University, compassion cannot be learned from books. One has to be born with it and then see that one doesn't lose it. With you by my side, I'll try to be that kind of doctor. With you by my side, it will be easier for me to fulfill the mission of 'Healer of All Flesh. . .'

Esther decided that David's medical career, his ideals and his happiness would become the purpose and content of her life. 

She felt that David's virile body and seething young blood, was restless and hungry. Each time they were alone, he would crush her to him and cover her with kisses. He would bury his feverish forehead in her bosom and in his sensual desire, mumble amorous pleas. And yet he idolized her so, and his love for her was so great that he would endure any torment rather than violate her innocence. She knew this and was deeply grateful to him.

Her sixteen-year-old body was ripening into maturing femininity, and she yearned for greater intimacy with David. 254

A few days after David had been accepted at the university, during one of their loving interludes, Esther suddenly clasped his face between her hands, and gazing into his half-closed eyes, said: "David, dear - this is no good, either for you or me.

Tomorrow I'll go to work feeling irritable and confused and miserable, because all this disturbs me. . For you, it must be worse - you're so strong, so virile. "Esther dearest, let's get married. If you want, I'll even talk to your father today." 

Reb Zechariah was pouring over a Talmudic tract. When 

David and Esther appeared in the doorway, he looked up and said with his usual amiable, naive smile: "David, Esther - it's so nice outside and you're indoors? 

Why don't you take a walk? God's wonder! New York - the biggest city in the world, with its endless stone and steel; with its automobiles, and trains racing below the ground and above roofs; with houses and buildings whose peaks scrape the clouds and just a few steps away, a magnificent park with trees, flowers, grass and lakes really a delight!" 

It dawned on Reb Zechariah that David and Esther were oblivious to his words. He suddenly realized that their joint appearance was not casual that they had something to say to him. 

"Sit down my children," he said "why are you standing?" "Reb Zechariah,” David began hesitantly. "We've come

I mean, Esther and I have come . . Esther and I have. decided to get married." 

A weeping voice was heard behind the door. Esther's mother, unable to restrain her emotions, expressed her joy in her most natural manner through tears. She rushed into the room, embraced Esther, and began to congratulate her through her tears. "Esther my child, mazel tov (good luck). God Himself found the right match for you! May you live happily with him to a ripe old age! Mazel tov!"

Reb Zechariah kept blowing his nose in his big handkerchief. Behind the thick lenses of his glasses his eyes were wet. Extending his hand to David, he said: 255

"Mazel tov, David! I'm grateful to the Everlasting One for the grace He has bestowed on all of us. It's too bad that your parents have not lived to see this joy. Nechama," he turned to his wife, "we'll have to arrange some sort of a wedding celebration. And perhaps we should let them have our room - it's bigger and brighter." "No, father," Esther ventured in her customary practical, logical manner, "we'll only stay here temporarily — say, for a few months- until we get suitable living quarters for ourselves uptown. David will be studying at Columbia University, and he ought to live closer to his new environment." "Why not remain here?" David asked in surprise. "I like the East Side - I like Canal Street." "It isn't because we've stopped liking the place where our parents live that we'll move away," Esther persisted; “it's because we also have to get to like the other environment.' ?? "Esther's right," David observed reluctantly, looking pleadingly at Reb Zechariah. "Medical School will take me into another world, another kind of society- but in my heart, I'll always love this place."

Standing beside David and holding his hand, Esther said to her parents: "I, too, will love this place."

Esther felt David's fingers tighten around hers, and sensed his gratitude for her readiness always to stand by his side. 

On the first day he attended classes at Columbia, David experienced an unexpected shock which almost shattered his life's dream of becoming a doctor. It was Esther who came to his rescue.

The freshmen had assembled in the amphitheatre for their anatomy class. The hubbub and laughter ceased abruptly as the professor appeared in the doorway. He was a tall lean elderly man, with long gray hair, a thick moustache, and stooped shoulders. His eyes were watery and colorless. Glasses perched on his aquiline nose. His face was that of a man immune to surprise and to change. "Gentlemen," the professor began in a monotonous voice, "medicine is the art of healing. In order to understand how 256

to heal the sick, one must first of all be thoroughly familiar with the structure of the healthy body. Anatomy is that study of the human body. "I must warn you, gentlemen, that studying anatomy textbooks is not like reading light fiction. Anatomy is a dull subject - it deals exclusively in facts. When you are told that the human body consists of the head, neck, four limbs and a torso, these are simple facts. There's nothing to philosophize about. The same applies to the structure of each limb and organ, from the tiniest to the largest. "Anatomy, gentlemen, is a subject to memorize. It doesn't require great wisdom. If you're asked: How many muscles does the hand possess and what are their names, you can answer correctly only after you've memorized this information. 

"A knowledge of anatomy is acquired from two sources: textbooks and dissection of cadavers. Please, gentlemen, remember dissection doesn't mean carving! It means the careful, precise taking-apart of the various structures of a given part of the body, just as a skilled mechanic takes apart a highly complicated machine. The textbook tells you what the anatomic structure of an organ is, and you have to convince yourself of this through careful dissection of the cadaver. "The class will be divided into three groups. The first group will carry out the dissection of the upper extremities; the second group, of the lower extremities; and the third group, of the neck and throat. The names of the students of these respective groups, along with their instructors, are listed on the door of the dissection-theatre." as- David's name was among those in the third group signed to the dissection of the neck and throat. The moment he entered the dissecting hall, he broke out in a sweat. He was appalled by some unknown, obscure horror. The drab gray stone walls of the large rectangular room were hung with brightly-colored anatomical illustrations of huge dimensions. The blood vessels looked like heavy red and blue cords; and the muscles, like thick brown braids. Here and there, skeletons suspended from their iron rods dangled loosely, their bones producing a disconcerting dry clatter. Narrow 257

wooden tables were ranged in three long rows. On each table lay a dead human body, covered with a sheet. 

David proceeded to his table, number 28, in the third row. 

His feet felt like blocks of wood; his head was in a whirl. 

Reawakened in his mind was the horror of death that he had gone through in Polipovka and in Struga. As he took up his post at the table, his heart pounded and his body felt numb. 

The instructor pulled off the sheet, revealing the corpse of a man. David gasped for air and shivers ran along his spine. 

The dead man reminded him of drunken Ivan, Vassilii 

Kravlenko's father, whom Serebryak had shot down on the

Dniester shore. David had the feeling that in one more moment the cadaver would open his eyes and start to growl. . 

The instructor's voice sounded to him like a remote echo, the words barely audible: "The skin is the first layer of the body. It has to be incised in such a way that the scalpel does not damage the other layers of structures right underneath. Like this-" 

The instructor made a long incision across the throat of the cadaver. David felt dizzy. The huge posters started to sway, as did the walls, and the skeletons, baring their teeth like fangs, launched into a "danse macabre"...

David backed away from the table and staggered out of the dissecting hall. The fresh outdoor air revived him a bit. He hailed a taxi and rode home. He slumped down on his bed, buried his head in his pillow, and lay there in misery and despair. "What's happened to me? I don't have the strength to be a doctor! What will happen now? My dreams are shattered! 

I'll never be a doctor! My life has lost all its meaning. . ."

Esther could not concentrate on her work in the office that day; she was too happy and too excited. “David started his studies at Columbia today," she kept thinking joyously. 

She was anxious to get home before his return; to welcome him and listen to his enthusiastic impressions of his first day at Medical School. "David dear, what's happened?" Surprised to find him already at home she asked, feeling frightened and alarmed. 258

"Were you expelled?"

Without raising his head from the pillow, David answered, "No, the University didn't expel me - I expelled myself.” "What are you saying dear? What nonsense? Are you sick? 

Have you a fever? What do you mean you've expelled yourself?" • • "I ran away from the dissection room. I couldn't stand them cutting up a dead man. One more minute and I'd have collapsed right there among the cadavers on the tables. . ." 

David sat up. His face was pale, and his eyes haunted with fear. He was shivering violently and his teeth chattered. "I always hoped to become a doctor. Today though... today 

I was convinced that I don't have the stamina for it." 

Esther put her arms around him, her face close to his, and said in a soft, motherly tone: "My darling, my beloved. You couldn't stand the dead bodies because the 'vale of death' you just escaped from is still fresh in your memory. Only last night you cried out in your sleep, 'He's innocent! They're condemning an innocent man to death!'" "Yes, I have nightmares so often," David murmured. "I saw Samborik again last night - good kind Samborik. I can't seem to forget him. . "Yes my darling, your fright in the dissecting hall of cadavers has nothing to do with your will and your strength to become a doctor." "Do you really think so?" 

"I don't think so I'm sure of it and I'll prove it to you right now!" "Prove it? How?" “You and I will go right back to the University now. You will return to the dissecting room. You will go to the table you were assigned to, and you will begin your work. . ." "I? Now? It's impossible, it's late, the school is closed by now." "David, everything is possible for the one with the will and the power. All locked doors spring open for him."

The high, wide corridors of the Medical School were deserted and dim. The massive doors of the various depart- 259

ments were locked. David and Esther walked slowly along the stone floor, their measured footsteps echoing and reechoing in the empty halls.

They halted in front of the dissecting room and David tapped on the door. The thud of heavy steps resounded, then the turn of a key in the lock. The janitor, a middle-aged man wearing a soiled smock, stuck his head out, and disgruntled by the intrusion, snapped: 

"It's after hours — it's closed! You must have made a mistake; this isn't the college office - this is Anatomy." "Yes I know," David replied. "I'm a student; may I come in?" "What for? Have you forgotten anything? It's against the rules for students to come here after hours unless they get special permission from Professor Stevens."

Esther took two quarters out of her purse, and unobtrusively slipping them to the janitor pleaded: "Please let him in. It's very important."

The janitor closed his fist around the two coins and looking at Esther with surprise, asked: "And who are you?" "I'm his wife." "You? His wife? Hallelujah! So young and already married!” "Please let him in," Esther persisted. "Our happiness depends on it." 

The janitor shrugged his shoulders: 

“All right — but not for long; and only him, the student. 

You, ma'am, will have to wait here."

He admitted David and closed the door. He was puzzled by the entire matter, and scratching his head, muttered under his breath, "Our happiness depends on it' How come? What connection could these stiffs have with their happiness?"

Shrugging his shoulders again, he left David alone among the thirty-six dead bodies and shuffled out of the dissecting room through a side door. 

the David looked about him. He felt like an intruder only living being in this world of the dead. The skeletons in the corners seemed to frown upon him for his arrogance in thus disrupting the eternal rest of death. 260 1

David sensed a glow of happiness. He felt the resurgence in him of that inner strength which only a short time ago had failed him. He stepped over to table number 28 and uncovered the cadaver. On the right side of the neck, most of the anatomic layers were already dissected. The left side was untouched. He picked up a scalpel, and without the slightest hesitation made a long incision on the skin... 

Before long, David's unusual aptitude for study, his keen mind and his prodigious memory, attracted the attention of both his classmates and his professors. He advanced from one school year to the next with highest honors, and was awarded the monetary prizes and scholarships in each grade. This lightened the burden Esther had assumed of providing for both of them financially. They moved into a small three-room apartment on West 96th Street and continued to work as a team toward the most important goal of their lives - David's medical degree.

Esther constantly strove to create that wholesome home atmosphere which was so important to David. She studied at first with David's help, then alone, perfecting herself in the English language. She read the best of the world's literature and in addition, acquired a considerable knowledge of history, philosophy, political economy and art. 

In their new social circle of students, young physicians, and professors, Esther came to be regarded with the same esteem as her brilliant student-husband. David was overjoyed watching Esther hold her own in various cultural discussions. 

sex 

Esther felt that an idyllic married life could not thrive on culture alone. There was another equally important factor - and she did not intend to ignore it. With her inborn keenmindedness and intelligence, Esther knew that David's handsome looks and magnetic manhood would be noticed by the women in the sphere of his profession. She was aware of the possible rivalry awaiting her from other women who were bound to use all their feminine charms to lure David into a web of sexual-romantic adventures. She also knew that 

David was no angel; that as a virile young man with human frailties he was likely to succumb to temptation. 261

- Esther decided that there were only two ways to react to, and perhaps avoid such situations — either to indulge in intermittent scenes of jealousy, suspicion and accusations, or to make herself strong competition for any rivals. The former solution was distasteful to her. She chose the latter - to provide for David at home an environment of romanticism and feminine charm which would always be one degree better than anything he might find elsewhere. . . 

She had sensed that during the first months of their marriage no other woman could possibly deprive her one iota of David's love. "Why?" she had asked herself and had immediately answered, "Because he finds in me all that man desires in woman - freshness, cleanliness, and desirability!” 

She was careful to preserve these qualities. The more she blossomed and matured, the more conscientious she became about effectively using the power that a woman possesses over men in their greatest weakness sex. 

She was not interested in men. She was only interested in one man - her man David. She never let David see her looking tired, disheveled, or slovenly. He always saw her wideawake, immaculate, and dressed up as if she were anticipating a distinguished guest. Catching sight of her, he would close his eyes lightly, draw a deep breath, and sigh: "Esther, my love, you're as fresh as a fragrant rose. . ."

Esther was well aware that the best things in life, especially those connected with physical intimacy, could become commonplace and dull if pursued simply as a part of a daily routine.

She did not want her marriage to deteriorate like that. 

Upon his return home, David would often find her dressed in a revealing negligee, looking enticingly feminine. He would lift her high in his arms and kiss her passionately. "Esther darling," he would say, "Your body is always so fresh and alluring." And Esther would listen and be in ecstasy.

Her "woman's strategy" succeeded. She created an ideal home life for David and herself spiritually, culturally and romantically. This was Esther's most important contribution to David's medical career. With it she helped him maintain 262

the integrity of his personality. In his new environment, her effect on him enabled him to achieve as high a social-cultural level as any of his American-born acquaintances. The home she established mirrored the ideological scope of David's life as a man, a citizen, a doctor and a Jew. 

David became a doctor and a naturalized citizen on the same day. In the morning, at the colorful ceremony of the 

Columbia University graduating class of 1929, he received his diploma of Doctor of Medicine. In the afternoon of that same day, at a modest ceremony in the Federal Court, he was sworn in as a citizen of the United States of America. 

When he came home that day he spread out his medical diploma and his naturalization certificate, side by side, gazing reverently at them both for a long while. As Esther came over to him, he took hold of her hand and said: "Here they are- the certifications of two great values profession and citizenship!" "Both on the same day, too," Esther added enthusiastically. "Yes, on the same day. That's just coincidence, but for me it's symbolic. Medicine is for all mankind; yes my dear, for mankind throughout the entire world without regard for race, color and creed. And America is the freest country in the world. From now on I'll dedicate my life to these two values - to my profession and to my adopted land of freedom." 

David's outstanding record in all his subjects during his five-year medical course, opened the doors of the best hospitals in the country to him. He made his choice and was accepted as an interne under the supervision of Dr. Eugene 

Taylor at the Universal General Hospital. 

263

Dr. Eugene Taylor 

The surgeon-in-chief, Dr. Eugene Taylor, completed his final operation of the morning. He removed his rubber gloves, untied his mask and used it to wipe the perspiration from his forehead, while he addressed his first assistant: "I think that the new internes are due to arrive today. 

How many of them are assigned to the surgery department?" "Six, sir." "Would you please convey to them my invitation to dinner at my home tomorrow night at seven. This has been my practice with every group of new internes, for years. Mrs. 

Taylor and I regard it as a sort of tradition." "Yes sir," the assistant acknowledged with a smile and a deferential nod of his head.

As was his custom, while in the surgeon's dressing-room, Dr. Taylor was prepared to discuss the most interesting operation of that morning. Both those genuinely yearning for knowledge and those who simply wished to show proper respect for the chief surgeon, would ordinarily listen attentively to his enthusiastic description of the case. Today however, an unusual agitation permeated the room. The discussion going on was loud and vehement. Some had their heads buried in widely opened newspapers, while others paced up and down, reiterating: "Incredible! It's a catastrophe for the entire economy of the country!" "What's happened? What's all the excitement about?” 

Dr. Taylor inquired. "Eugene, what's the matter with you? Are you a stranger here?" one of the older surgeons demanded. "The stock 264

market has crashed! Wall Street has collapsed! A panic is sweeping the entire country - and you're asking 'what's happened?' Millions of people have gone bankrupt overnight.

The newspapers are full of reports of suicide attempts, and you're asking 'what's all the excitement about?"" Then he came over and whispered in Dr. Taylor's ear: "The Taylor 

Realties Shares are now worthless, wiped out!"

Dr. Taylor went into his private office, closed the door behind him and slumped into his deep armchair. He felt tired, and leaning his head against the back of the softly upholstered chair, he closed his eyes and became engrossed in thought. 

Across his mind flitted thoughts of his deceased father,

John Taylor, founder of the giant real estate firm that owned innumerable skyscrapers, hotels, and apartment houses in New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, Philadelphia, and the leading cities of Canada, Latin America and Europe.

John Taylor had ruled an empire. He had ruled with uncompromising rigor, and had recognized no other value in life save that of the might of wealth. He had acquired his kingdom alone, starting from the bottom, and reaching the heights. 

At twelve, John Taylor had worked as an errand boy for a real estate firm; at eighteen he had already negotiated mortgages of small and large properties in and around New 

York. At twenty-two he became the youngest vice-president of the firm; and three years later all of Wall Street was in a dither about the phenomenal rise of the twenty-five-yearold financial wizard, John Taylor, who had taken over the old established real estate firm, converted it into the new "Taylor Realties Corporation," and had begun to rule over it with an iron hand.

As is generally characteristic of autocratic, absolute rulers, John Taylor had trampled mercilessly over every stumblingblock on his march to success. He stifled every vestige of sentimentality within himself that could lead to weakness and submission. His marriage to Clarissa Walsh the only daughter of the Pittsburgh steel tycoon, Walter Walsh, was a coolheaded practical move. Skyscrapers and steel - an amalgamation of indestructible force! 265

Clarissa Walsh brought with her the fortunes of her father's steel. She herself, however, was physically and emotionally far from the concept of steel: with a thin, frail body, dreamhaunted eyes; soft, sentimental, pale and weak.

When Clarissa became pregnant, John Taylor felt confident that she would present him with a son. This is what he planned, this is what he wished - and therefore, it had to be so. But it was a daughter he got and John Taylor did not try to hide his disappointment and displeasure. In his dominion someone had dared to play a prank that had thwarted his will. He had no alternative but to accept the prank, but did not feel duty-bound to delight in it. . . 

When the second child also proved to be a girl, John Taylor regarded it as a challenge to his authority. He blamed his wife for it she was too delicate and frail to bear a son. 

The two pregnancies had undermined Clarissa's none-toorobust health. Her anemia became persistent. She became even more emaciated, and her entire appearance was like a withered twig likely to snap in the slightest breeze. Her physicians warned her: "Mrs. Taylor, you must not become pregnant again! Your blood is too poor."

Clarissa would not heed their advice. It is hard to understand the actions of the weak. Not only do they permit the strong to domineer and ride roughshod over them, they even seem to derive a certain inner satisfaction from it. They idolize the strength that tramples them and kiss the rod that trounces their bodies. . . Clarissa loved John Taylor - she loved his brutality and rudeness, and felt blameworthy for not having given him a son. 

Her third child was a son. John Taylor triumphed at last - but Clarissa paid for it with her life. After the birth of her son, she was almost continuously bedridden for six long years. There was an endless procession of doctors and nurses daily. Choking, pungent medicinal odors forever wafted from her bedroom. She spent her days in a wheel-chair, and at night she lay awake, her roving eyes searching the darkness for a spark of that light for which her wasted body so yearned. 

John Taylor showered Clarissa with all the comforts and medical attention that money could secure. But what her 266

heart longed for most - his love - she did not get. She languished and pined for it, wasting away. 

Clarissa quenched her feverish desire for love, and appeased the pangs of emptiness in her heart by coddling her tiny son, her Eugene. . . like a stray wanderer in a forsaken, torrid, sandy wasteland, who stumbles upon a spring, and thirstily gulps down the water.

She would snuggle him to her sunken chest, press him against her empty shriveled breasts, and sense her dry nipples becoming firm, exciting within her a thrill of gratification. 

She would close her eyes and kiss her child fervently, hugging to herself the only bit of happiness in her wretched life. "My boy, my son, my little man," she would say lovingly: "you'll grow up to be husky and strong just like your father.” “Mama, why do you always stay indoors?" the youngster would often ask. “Why don't you ever go for a walk with me like the other mothers do?” "Because I'm sick my dear - very sick, and I don't have the strength to walk." "When will you get well?" "Soon, very soon - that's what the doctors say." "When I grow up I'll become a doctor, and I'll make you well right away." 

Clarissa's eyes would fill with tears, and stroking the thin blonde silken hair on the child's head, she would reply: 

"Yes my son, my darling — that would be wonderful!"

John Taylor frowned upon the mother's endearments toward the youngster. Wresting the child from her, he would lift him up almost to the ceiling and exclaim half-seriously, half-jestingly: "Why do you cling to Mama's skirt? Be a man, as behooves the future president of the 'Taylor Realties Corporation!'" And to his wife John would say disdainfully: "Clarissa, Eugene is not a girl; he's a boy and boys are not brought up with caresses and kisses. That's not the way to build a strong character in a man. Because of your pampering he'll grow up to be a 'chicken-hearted sissy!' "' 

John's words hurt Clarissa, not because of his derisive taunt, but because she had a premonition that she would not live to see her son grow up. 267

When Eugene had turned six, Clarissa wanted to have the nursemaid take him to a private school every day, where "the child will be under better supervision." "What nonsense!" John argued. "Private schools, indeed!

Let him attend a good American public school that caters to all American children. Is he a cripple who needs special supervision? Thank you for your good intentions! A future president of the Taylor Realties Corporation needs no favors from anyone. He'll take care of himself without special supervision!" 

It was John Taylor, naturally, who had had the final say. Eugene was indeed fond of the public school, and of his schoolmates and teachers - all of whom were amiable and friendly. However, he missed his mother very much and would wait impatiently each day for the last bell. The moment he got home he would run to her room, let her kiss and fondle him, and then share his experiences of the day with her. 

On Fridays he would be especially happy because he knew he would not have to attend school until the following Monday. His mother, too, appeared more jolly on Fridays, and would even laugh aloud — something she rarely did now. 

There came a Friday which began as cheerfully as had all the other Fridays. Returning from school, Eugene proceeded to his mother's room. His mother, as usual, welcomed him with open arms and hugged him, resting her pale face on his blonde head. The youngster, as always, sat down at her feet spinning a long yarn about his day's experiences. 

After a short time, he was surprised that his mother was not reacting to his chatter in her customary manner. She was so unusually quiet. He couldn't even hear her breathing. He got up slowly and saw her as he had never seen her before: her head had fallen back, her eyes were wide open and lifeless, as though they had been stunned by some sudden fright. Her parched blue lips were twisted as though she were in excruciating pain. Her arms dangled from the sides of the wheel-chair like artificial limbs. "Mama, Mama," 

Eugene called to her, and nudged her lightly. Her body swayed, and toppling from her seat, she dropped to the floor 268

and lay there motionless. . . Eugene never forgot that last look in the stunned eyes of his dead mother. . .

John Taylor's son proved to be a disappointment to him. 

Although Eugene had inherited his father's height and robust physique and looked very much like him, he had on the other hand, inherited his mother's soft dreaminess and sentimentality. During the period immediately following Clarissa's death, John Taylor assumed that Eugene's preoccupation was the normal reaction of a child longing for his mother. 

Before long, however, he became convinced that this went far deeper, and he set about to uproot what he considered a decided weakness in his son.

Eugene turned out to be an excellent student in the elementary grades and later on, in High School. He won all the school prizes both in academic competitions and in various other school activities. John Taylor, however, felt that the best foundry for the forging of a steel-like character in his son was the Taylor Realties Corporation - under his own. supervision. He would take his son along to all the important conferences of the board of directors, so that he might absorb a sense of the dynamic use of inexorable authority which supplied the propulsive power for the administration of this gigantic real estate empire. 

Eugene graduated from high school at the head of his class. At the commencement exercises he was awarded a gold medal, and the principal, in his presentation to Eugene, remarked: "As long as the United States of America will continue to raise youth of Eugene Taylor's caliber, our country's existence — culturally, socially and economically will always be bright and secure!" 

On their way home from the graduation ceremonies, John Taylor placed his arm around his son's shoulders and said warmly, "Son, I'm proud of you! You've proved yourself to be a real Taylor! I have a surprise for you - a graduation present."

When they got to the main building of the Taylor Realties Corporation, John ushered his son into a spacious, richly 269

furnished office, with the following words inscribed in huge gilded letters on its door: "Eugene Taylor, Vice-President." "Like father, like son," John Taylor said proudly. "Both of us have been the youngest vice-presidents of the firm 

I, at twenty-two, and you, at eighteen!" "Thanks, Dad - thanks indeed," Eugene, though visibly stirred, answered calmly. "This is very magnanimous of you, but Father, it's not what I want." 

John Taylor paled, and his lips quivered: "What's that? What do you mean by 'it's not what I want?' 

Don't you like the office?" 

"On the contrary, Father, the office is splendid — but not for me. I have a different aim in life." "What do you mean by a different aim in life'" John 

Taylor angrily demanded. "I want to study medicine." "Medicine? Are you out of your mind? You want to swap an empire for enemas and bedpans? You want to dash out with a little black bag at midnight to ease somebody's bellyache for half-a-dollar- and for that privilege you'd forfeit a multi-million dollar fortune? You're joking - you couldn't be serious!" "Dad, I know it's hard for you to understand but medicine is my vocation. I want to be a doctor." 

John Taylor was beside himself. "How much does a physician earn?" he shouted. "Take the most eminent! Ten thousand a year — twenty thousand! You can buy and sell them all today! The most prominent among them consider it a stroke of good luck if they can get a few shares of 'Taylor 

Realties.' You have it all in your hands and you're throwing it away?" "Medicine, Dad, isn't measured in terms of dollars. I don't think decent doctors expect to reap a fortune out of their profession." 

John Taylor mused for a moment then said, "Tell me how come up to now you never so much as hinted at your wish to become a doctor? When did you get this brilliant idea of yours?" 270

"At mother's wheel-chair- sitting at her feet and looking at her pale face. . ." 

Dejected, John Taylor fell silent. The weak Clarissa had conquered - twelve years after her death.

In the course of time, father and son drifted more and more apart, heading toward diametrically opposed worlds. 

John Taylor ruled ever more intensely over his kingdom of power and wealth, while Eugene Taylor devoted himself completely to the task of assuaging human pain, tribulation and despair in the realm of medicine. 

It looked as if the respective worlds of father and son were racing with one another. The Taylor Realties Corporation acquired more millions, year after year, and John Taylor's fame in the world of finance mounted ever higher. Dr.

Eugene Taylor did not lag behind in his world. His prestige as an eminent surgeon in the United States also rose higher in time.

Suddenly both worlds met. Father and son found one another again:

Presiding at one of the sessions of his Board of Directors, John Taylor, as was his custom, asked each member: "What do you say Jim?" - "What do you think Tom?" - "And you 

Charlie - do you concur?" and so on until he had made the rounds of all the participants seated about the big round table. The answers were concise because everyone knew that these questions were just a matter of formality. John would have the final word anyhow, and the members of the Board of Directors seemed to be satisfied with that. "John's all right," they said. "What's good enough for John is good enough for us." 

John stood up and remarked: “Gentlemen, I'm submitting for your approval the following definitive plan. . ." Then something happened - the speaker's voice grew so faint that it was barely audible and his lips began to quiver. He reeled, his tall figure bent forward, and he clutched the table with both hands. His face turned pale and beads of perspiration appeared on his forehead. Before the assembled men could 271

regain their composure, John slumped limply into his chair his head thrown back, breathed deeply several times and fell unconscious. 

Everyone was panic-stricken. Most of them were at a loss as to what to do next. Only Tom Nixon, an old intimate friend of the Taylors, preserved his equanimity and proceeded unruffled to administer first aid to John. He lay the stricken man on the leather divan, unbuttoned his shirt collar, loosened his other clothing and applied a cold wet towel to John's face and forehead. John revived enough to breathe rapidly, staccato-fashion, for a few brief moments and then lost consciousness again. Nixon dialed a number on the telephone: "Hello! I wish to speak to Dr. Eugene Taylor. Yes, yes very urgent. I must speak with him at once it can't be delayed. Hello Eugene is that you? Tom Nixon speaking

We're at a board meeting - your father was suddenly taken ill. Come at once — I think it's serious. No, no, I won't leave him. I'll wait till you arrive." ... 

When John Taylor opened his eyes again he was in a hospital and Eugene was standing over him. "Hello Father - how do you feel?" "Where in God's name am I? What am I doing here?" "You're in the hospital, Father. I brought you here for a few days. You'll rest and I'll take care of you.” "In the hospital? Am I so seriously sick that I need hospitalization?" 

"No Father, you're not that sick at all just exhausted.

This can happen to the healthiest people." 

"Yes something happened to me during the meeting. Everything in front of me went black and after that I don't remember a thing... Eugene, give me your hand. . . It's good to be near you my son." "It's good to be near you Dad.” "Eugene. I'm afraid - I'm afraid to die. I. I want 

I've never been so afraid. . . Help me Eugene don't let me die!" to live. .. "John Taylor I'm ashamed to hear such words from you. 

You're good for at least another fifty years. Well, enough chatting you've got to rest." 272

The examination revealed that John Taylor had suffered a severe internal hemorrhage caused by a tumor in his stomach, which had corroded an artery. The only possible prospect for saving his life lay in an immediate operation. "Dad," Eugene addressed him with feigned cheerfulness, "I've got very good news for you: You'll recover completely.

They've established what was the matter with you - you've developed a small stomach ulcer. It has to be operated on but in less than two weeks you'll be like a new-born person!”

John Taylor was lost in thought for a while, his gaze riveted upon Eugene's eyes. Finally he asked: "Who's going to cut - you?" “No, Father — not I.” "Why not?" 

"Because you're my father and I love you. Cutting you would be like cutting myself - and one doesn't cut oneself..."

Eugene asked one of his colleagues to operate and John Taylor underwent surgery. The operation was short. He was opened and closed. The tumor proved to be malignant and had already spread to the liver, spleen, and other internal organs. One word - "inoperable" — and the fate of John Taylor was sealed. 

Eugene hardly left his father's bedside. At those moments when John was suffering excruciating pain, high temperature, nausea and persistent vomiting, his son invariably found a suitably soothing word to calm him- often a witty remark that would cheer the patient and even evoke a smile. 

A few days prior to his death John Taylor said to his son: "Eugene my son, I wish to make a confession to you." "About what Father?" "About your 'aim in life.' Remember? I didn't understand it then... Now I admit that compared to your shares in life, the shares of the Taylor Realties Corporation are worthless. 

Dr. Eugene Taylor came to with a start and glanced at the clock - it was three in the afternoon. He must have dozed off for a whole hour. The noisy voices of the arguing physicians still echoed from the dressing-room. The words "The 273

Taylor Realties shares are worthless" that the older surgeon had whispered to him still buzzed in his ears. . . He smiled wistfully and felt a warmth and longing for his father. 

Restless with curiosity, six young men stood at the entrance of Dr. Eugene Taylor's home and waited for their ring to be answered. Their eyes scanned the towering white pillars which supported the broad facade of the old sprawling, twostorey colonial-style structure. 

The gigantic growth of New York City and its climb toward the clouds had as yet failed to budge that upper section of the metropolis that dovetailed with Central Park. It seemed as though the long-established, tradition-steeped, individual one-and-two storey dwellings had defied the wild upsurge of the skyscrapers, with pride and stubborness, and refused to eclipse the grandeur of the park surrounding them. 

The door was opened by an elderly Negro in a short white jacket who bowed low and with servile courtesy, declared: "Please come in gentlemen. Dr. and Mrs. Taylor are expecting you."

Upon entering, one immediately felt the harmony between the outward architecture and the artistic interior decoration. 

The windows, extending from floor to ceiling, faced the park and were hung with richly pleated silk curtains. On the walls were valuable oil paintings by eminent artists; sparkling crystal chandeliers were overhead; and one's feet sank into colorful Persian rugs as into the softness of fine fur. There was massive furniture, with fine wood carving, and indeed the entire atmosphere testified to a life of rich tradition and financial security.

As soon as the young men had crossed the threshold Dr. 

Eugene Taylor came forward to greet each one with as much ardent hospitality as though he had known them for years: "Very glad you came. Please make yourselves at home." 

Then turning to the Negro servant, "Thomas, tell Mrs. Taylor that the internes have arrived. Wonderful weather, gentlemen so unusual for October 30th. This has been like a warm summer day. . . A terrible thing — that stock-market crash. . . Well, one more day - November first — and you'll 

274

take charge of the surgical wards. . . Let's hope you'll perform your duties well. Ah, here's my family! Patricia my dear, here are the new internes. Gentlemen may I present my wife, Mrs. Taylor, my daughter Elizabeth, and my son, Charles. And now, please let's go and eat. You must all be starved. When I was an interne I was forever hungry and I doubt that internes have changed very much in that respect..."

During the course of the meal Dr. Taylor spoke incessantly. He evidently wished to put his young guests at ease and make them relax and he carried on the conversation in a light vein, touching upon diverse personal topics of daily life. "Patricia my dear, the roast beef is excellent. I'm saying this only to you - the cook mustn't hear it or she's likely to become independent and ask for a raise!” 

Mrs. Taylor smiled good-naturedly, and glancing at her husband seated at the other end of the table she replied, as though addressing a child: "I'm very pleased my dear, that you've enjoyed the roast beef." "Thomas is in seventh heaven! Gentlemen, Thomas is our servant and he's particularly pleased to serve us tonight because our guests are young doctors. A nephew of his is studying medicine and he considers it such an honor, that tears come to his eyes at the mere mention of it. Good old Thomas." "Elizabeth darling - are you making any progress in your music? Gentlemen, Elizabeth is a very fine pianist. After dinner we'll insist that she play something for us. Yes sir, we'll insist and we won't take 'no' for an answer." 

The pretty blonde fourteen-year old Elizabeth blushed, and as her blue eyes scanned the faces of the young internes, she answered coquettishly: 

"Oh Father, stop it! I feel terrible! You're always putting me on the spot! Dr. Taylor, I don't love you any more. "Charles, my son - what's the matter with you? You've been very quiet. How's the movement getting along? Gentlemen,

I'll have you know that Charles is a Communist. He's posi- 275

tive that given time, the proletariat will seize power throughout the world. A united great international family of the proletariat! Absolute happiness will prevail on earth then! 

Not bad eh?"

The sixteen-year old Charles – tall, well developed physically, and looking very much like his father, spoke up now without raising his eyes from his platter: "Why that sarcasm? What happened yesterday on Wall

Street is the best proof of how decayed the entire capitalistic system is. Only a structure that has no enduring foundation crashes like that." "Well what did I tell you, gentlemen - my Charles is a genuine agitator! When he discusses the plans the Communists have for handing over power to the proletariat, I get the shivers. Luckily though, we physicians aren't included among the "condemned bourgeoisie." Believe it or not gentlemen, we have the honor of being considered part of the "privileged proletariat.”

Charles pushed his platter away abruptly and drawing a deep breath, was about to reply. Patricia Taylor however, like an adroit chess-player, forestalled him by making the right move. She announced with a blend of authority and courtesy which complemented her youthful but majestic appearance: "Eugene dear, Charles' political views can hardly interest our guests. I don't mean to be insulting but so far you've monopolized the conversation. Don't you think, my dear, that our visitors would also like to say something?" "A thousand pardons, gentlemen! My wife is completely right as usual. How silly of me to have kept babbling like this. Of course we're very anxious to get to know you. 

Patricia dear, why don't we carry on with that game? It's a very interesting game, gentlemen! Some years ago when a new group of internes had honored us by coming here for dinner Mrs. Taylor hit upon the idea of this game. Each interne was given five minutes - less if he wished, but not a second beyond that time-limit- during which he had to give us a few autobiographical items and tell us what he thought was the most important event leading to his de- 276

cision to study medicine. This little game turned out to be such a success that we've played it ever since, at similar gatherings. Patricia dear - you have the floor."

Patricia rang a small crystal bell and when the Negro servant appeared she asked him to serve coffee and liquors in the parlor in a half-hour. 

Dr. Taylor, his family and the six invited internes retired to the spacious parlor. The soft comfortable silk and velour-covered chairs and divans were placed in a semi-circle. 

Everyone sat down and the game began. "Peter Sparks, twenty-three years old, Atlanta, Georgia.

My father is a pastor, and I was supposed to follow in his footsteps. On the day that I was to register in the Institute of Divinity I happened to pass the Medical School. I can't account for it—I had no specific reason to do this - but something impelled me to enter the huge corridor. The students were milling about near the wall where the classes were posted and I stopped and looked too. The headings, 'anatomy,' 'physiology,' 'bacteriology,' 'pathology,' fascinated me. As a matter of fact I don't remember how it actually happened, but I queued up along with the rest of the registrants there and in less than fifteen minutes I came out with an entrance card as a student of first year medicine. "At home my people were shocked at what I had done. 

At first they thought I was joking but after seeing my card, they looked at me suspiciously as though I had gone berserk and they just kept quiet. "My father broke the silence. "Congratulations my son! Lots of luck to you! Actually there's no great difference between a pastor and a physician: one is concerned with man's soul; and the other with man's body. Body and soul are inseparable anyhow.' And now here 

I am, a physician.” "Wonderful, Sparks!" Dr. Taylor exclaimed enthusiastically. "That's more than an episode - it's a whole philosophy of life. Wonderful indeed! . . . Patricia dear, I'm getting to like your game more and more! Sparks starts us off well, gentlemen continue." 

"Robert Young III, twenty-five, Philadelphia. My grand- 277

father, Robert Young I, left an estate valued at fifty-million dollars. My father, Robert Young II, administered that estate with great skill and in a matter of a decade, doubled the assets. I, Robert Young III, didn't show the slightest inclination to follow in the footsteps of my father and grandfather.

So my relatives decided that the best way to safeguard the family's fortune, was to have me study medicine. This profession would involve no great financial risk and was a noble calling. The idea was to create a "Robert Young Medical 

Center," which would perpetuate our name in a lasting philanthropic enterprise. Besides, most of our great wealth would remain undisturbed until a Robert Young IV or V would arrive who might be more competent than I to resume where his forefathers left off." "The 'Robert Young Medical Center' is not a bad idea," 

Dr. Taylor broke in, "not a bad idea at all. . . Pardon my introducing a sad note into our game, but would you be good enough to tell me how yesterday affected the Robert Young fortunes?" "Dr. Taylor, you don't know my father, Robert Young II.

Wall Street crashed - but not Robert Young II! He belongs to the chosen few who foresaw this crash even though other Wall Street financiers didn't have the slightest inkling of it. 

Yes sir, my father is a type — there aren't many like him! . . .' 

"Julius Klein, twenty-four, Manhattan, New York. My father worked in the clothing industry all his life, but it was always his fond dream that I and my twin brother, Richard, should become doctors. Ever since we were kids we were urged to be good students because otherwise we wouldn't be able to become doctors. Even our toys were related to medicine: tiny doctors' kits, toy ambulances, vials, glasses, and little jars containing sugar tablets with labels on them of all sorts of popular medicines. When we started studying 

Latin in High School my father considered it a terrific step forward. 'Boys,' he told us, 'you're well on the way now.' 

Latin is the international language of physicians!' “When Richard and I grew up we asked our father why he insisted on our studying medicine instead of helping him in his business ventures which incidentally had increased quite 278

a lot by then. His reply surprised us. "He told us that all his life he had harbored a dream of becoming a doctor. However, he had to give up that ambition when his father, my grandfather, died suddenly leaving his wife to manage alone with six young children. The entire responsibility of providing for the family rested upon my father. He was only fifteen then, and the oldest child. "Since his dream of becoming a doctor had been dissipated he vowed that his son must achieve the goal that he had failed to reach. Then when he had twins instead of an only son, he decided to send both of us to med school. Richard couldn't stand the med school grind for more than a year so he chucked it and became my father's right-hand man. But I guess I succeeded in fulfilling my father's childhood dream for him. . .” "Pardon me Dr. Klein," Mrs. Taylor interposed, "do you mean that you really consider your medical career as just the fulfillment of a duty to your father? How do you feel about it deep down in your heart? What's your own personal attitude toward medicine?" "Medicine is fine! I think of it as a career on a par with all other careers where 'success' is the end-goal. Whoever is successful in whatever he does has chosen the right vocation for himself. I assure you that I'm going to do my utmost to be successful in medicine.” "Practical - most practical!" Dr. Taylor observed amiably. "There's also room for the practical in the medical profession. It isn't given to all to be idealists. If all were idealists in a practical, materialistic world like ours it would really be a Utopia. Well gentlemen, who's next?" "William Ross, twenty-seven, Washington, D.C. I hail from a family in which medicine is a tradition. My father is Dr. 

Walter Ross, my grandfather was a doctor too, my mother is the daughter of a doctor, and my sister is married to a doctor. 

My older brother graduated from med school three years ago and has just returned to Washington where he'll specialize in obstetrics and gynecology. I can't point to any events that suddenly impelled me to study medicine. It looks as though I was just destined to be what I am from birth." 279

"A born doctor!" Dr. Taylor exclaimed. "Not bad! A doctor in whose blood medicine has been instilled for generations! Not bad! Not bad at all!" "Alfred Mueller," began another interne, "twenty-four, Detroit. My father is a mechanical engineer who came over from Germany at the turn of the century. My two sisters and 

I were born and raised in Detroit. "Six years ago my father went back to Germany to visit. his very old parents and he took me along. I was greatly impressed by the Germans - they're very different from us. 

They have the endurance of steel and indomitable characters! 

I heard so much about racial purity in Germany, that I decided to study medicine and do research in that field." "Dr. Mueller," Mrs. Taylor remarked, "I presume you're a

Christian and are aware that the essence of Christianity is that God created man in His image, and that all men are equal in the eyes of God." "Pardon me Mrs. Taylor, but I didn't hear anyone say that our Americans in the South aren't good Christians, and they certainly don't think the colored people were created in the same image as they were or that they are their equals.” "Dr. Mueller," Dr. Taylor broke in, "I'm interested in your medical approach to the racial question. Tell me please, what do you intend to prove in your research? Do you wish to study how certain diseases affect various ethnic groups?" "Partly that and something else besides." 

"Something else? May I ask what that is?" "The greatest experiments in biology and in medicine are usually carried out on a variety of different animals, like guinea pigs, rabbits, dogs, monkeys and so on. The knowledge acquired from these experiments on animals is applied to improve the well-being of humans. The purity of race in thoroughbred dogs and horses is an established fact that no one disputes. In fact, pedigreed dogs and horses are worth fantastic prices because their pedigrees imply better quality in every respect, physical and mental. If pure-bloodedness is possible in animals why shouldn't it be possible in —?” "Dr. Mueller," Dr. Taylor interrupted him, "the concept of pure-bloodedness in dogs and horses does not apply to human 280

-- beings. Men and the society they live in are subject to ethical laws. Ethics is the quintessence of the existence of mankind – from its inception to this day and to the end of time. Dogs and horses are akin to man biologically, but not in the ethical values. . . Yes Dr. Mueller, what you've just said is interesting, very interesting, but it has little to do with 'ethics.' Ethics, doctor, human ethics! . . . If it's disregarded, forgotten and trampled upon - it would mean the complete extinction of mankind, the pure-blooded Germans included.” 

An embarrassed silence ensued. For a while it looked as though this time the game would wind up as a most unpleasant affair. However Patricia Taylor once again came to the rescue and declared half-jestingly: "Why have you all grown silent? There's an old superstition that when a social group suddenly falls silent, at that moment a fool is born. It would certainly be too bad if such an intelligent and 'highly philosophical' group as this were to cause the birth of one more fool in our world. 

Anyway, our game isn't over yet. We haven't heard from that quiet pensive gentleman over there. Please doctor, let's have your contribution to the game." "David Shanan, twenty-three, Russia." "Russia?" Charles, until now a silent spectator, jumped up. "When did you leave Russia?" "In 1923." "In 1923? In that case you must have witnessed the whole 

Russian Revolutionary upheaval! You've seen with your own eyes the Dictatorship of the Proletariat in full action!" "Yes, I've experienced the full impact of the revolutionary upheaval. . . I've seen with my own eyes the Dictatorship of the Proletariat in complete power." "Charles dear," Mrs. Taylor interposed, "I don't see why your enthusiasm for the wonders of the revolution should make you forget elementary courtesy. Please don't interrupt 

Dr. Shanan and let him finish what he was going to tell us." "It really doesn't matter Mrs. Taylor. As a matter of fact, I'm delighted at your son's questions. My answer to him may prove enlightening and help him in the future." "Thank you Dr. Shanan - thanks indeed!" Charles re- 281

sponded enthusiastically. "I'll hold you to your promise. I'll most certainly make the best use of what I can learn from your personal experiences in Russia. It's really wonderful! 

I hope you'll be able to come to one of our meetings. You can hardly imagine what this means for the movement!" "Charles my boy," Dr. Taylor at last remonstrated, emerging from the reverie, which followed the brief discussion with Dr. Mueller, "Dr. Shanan did not come to our home as an emissary from Moscow, but as one of the new internes who will start working in my surgical department the day after tomorrow. You can ask him your political-ideological questions on another occasion. Incidentally, don't forget to ask him why he left the land of the proletarian dictatorship. I have a feeling that you'll get quite a revealing and interesting answer."

David fixed his deep black eyes upon Dr. Taylor and said in a barely audible voice: "Yes sir 

a very revealing answer. . . "What prompted you to study medicine?" Dr. Taylor asked. "My mother - the most beautiful mother in the world. . .

I was seven years old when she became incurably ill. While 

I was nestling close to her and looking at her pale face, I felt a strong urge to become a doctor and to make her well. She died three years later. But my childish ambition to become a doctor never weakened." "The pale face of the ailing mother," Dr. Taylor murmured as though to himself. "Strange - strange, indeed," Alfred Mueller remarked. “I can't understand how a sentimental experience during childhood can determine the vocation of an adult! What branch of medicine do you intend to specialize in Shanan? To cure ailing mothers and to make up to yourself in that way for your own mother's death? Don't you think that's a pretty naive and narrow attitude toward such a science as medicine?" 

The dreamy expression of David's eyes disappeared, and a glint of fire sparkled in them. "I had a rabbi by the name of Reb Itzikel, who was a very wise sage. And he told me: "True, medicine is a science, but 282

the doctor must be more than a scientist. He must possess soulfulness. He must sense God in his heart. . . Only then does he deserve to be a true emissary of the Healer of All 

Flesh... That's the sort of medicine I intend to practice." "Pardon me," Juius Klein spoke up with a grin: “When it comes to rabbis, I, too, have something to say. Rabbis have a hard task trying to persuade the members of their congregations to attend the synagogue services. In most cases they preach before empty benches. I doubt very much if they could accomplish more with the doctors than they do with their congregations. Let them leave medicine to the medical faculties!"

The others smiled at this pronouncement and eyed David curiously. "Tell me Julius," David asked, also jestingly, "what's your reaction when you see a magnificent dress on a wooden mannekin?" "What kind of question is that? What relevance does that have to our discussion?" 

"It's an important question, Julius — and very relevant. 

Didn't you ever think about how this same magnificent dress would appear on the body of a beautiful young lady? No?

It's a pity for then you would understand that the medical faculty gives you only the diploma - the magnificent dress. . .

The essence depends on who's wearing it. And this essence can't be determined by the faculty." "What are you driving at? Are you trying to imply that the rabbinate determines it?" "No - neither the rabbis, the priests, nor the school faculties, but each person for himself, with the aid of his mind and heart... The rabbi, the priest, the teacher, are all like clocks. They don't control the march of time, but just indicate the hours of bright day and the hours of dark night. 

The rest depends on us." 

Thomas appeared in the doorway, carrying a big silver tray with fragrant hot coffee, decanters filled with various liqueurs and little crystal glasses. He put the tray down on a low coffee table and said to Mrs. Taylor: 283

"It's a half-hour now maʼam.” "Thank you Thomas, you're just in time. We are ready to have our coffee now." 

Mrs. Taylor was pleased with Thomas' arrival at this moment not so much because it was time for coffee as because this would help to break the tension brought about by the unexpected clash of words and ideas among the participants in her innocent game. The coffee and liqueur did, in fact, restore to the group the pleasant atmosphere of a normal social gathering. 

Holding his demi-tasse, Dr. Taylor walked about the room smiling at one person, addressing a few words to another, until he got to his daughter, who was sitting in a corner gazing pensively through a window which faced the park. "A penny for your thoughts," Dr. Taylor whispered into her ear. "Papa dear, please tell me which of the internes do you like most?" 

"That my dear I'll find out later when I see them at work." "I don't mean that; I mean whom do you like most just as a person tonight?" "It's hard to say, my child. They all seem to be fine young . . And you - whom do you like most?" men. 

"Dr. Mueller's the most handsome - tall blonde and strong, manly features; even white teeth, blue eyes the nurses will go mad over him. . . I like the one with the foreign accent too I don't remember his name." 

"Shanan." "Yes, Shanan. Somehow he fascinates you with his talk. 

He looks more like a clergyman than a doctor. What is he - a Jew?" "Yes." "Papa dear, I heard you sort-of suggest to Dr. Mueller that he's against human ethics. Just what is ‘ethics,' anyway?" "Human ethics is. 'soulfulness and God in one's heart.'” "But those are Shanan's words.- I think that's what he said were the requisites for a good doctor." .. "Yes my child, being a real physician is an ethical mission." 284

"Thanks papa." "Thanks for what?" 

"For being so patient with such a dummy as me - and for explaining all those terribly difficult things!" "In that case, will you do something for your patient father?" "At your service, Dr. Taylor. I'll do whatever you ask."

Dr. Taylor took hold of her hand, led her to the piano and said, "Play something my dear.”

Elizabeth sat down on the piano stool and ran her slender, flexible fingers over the keys several times. At the sound of the piano, the conversation in the parlor stopped. She straightened her shoulders and her face assumed a serious mien, adding to her beauty the impression that she possessed determination and mastery over self to interpret the work of the composer not as a child but as an adult. She paused a few seconds to make certain that she had the undivided attention of the group, and then began to play. 

A curious change came over the young girl at the piano. 

From the billows of musical sounds, her gleaming face emerged like a serene moon after a stormy summer eve.
