She was now untouchable, like the stream of wondrous melodic waves that rose under her rippling fingers. She neither gazed at anyone nor seemed aware of their presence. Now she was in another world a world of exaltation and ecstasy. 

The atmosphere of the room was pervaded by those pleasantly mellifluous tones, which penetrated the souls of its listeners. In David the tenderness of the music reawakened dormant memories. He sat enchanted, eyes closed, absorbed in a sudden recreation of bygone times. . .

The piano strains wafted through the open windows breaking the nocturnal hush of the park. . .

If any one of the six internes had ever been dubious about the possibility of a single individual possessing a double personality, none of them had the least doubt about it after they had started working in Dr. Eugene Taylor's surgical department on the morning of November first. They were all astonished at the complete contrast between Dr. Taylor's behavior 285

in the hospital environment and his manner when he was at home with his family. Where was his former geniality? His volubility? His cheerful smile? His keen sense of humor?... "Good morning sir" Robert Young III greeted him cordially. "I certainly spent a pleasant evening at your home, sir." 

Clearing his throat, Dr. Taylor answered indifferently: "Dr. Young, your private social life doesn't interest me. 

Please bear that in mind during all your hospital hours!”

The assembled internes were stunned by these words and tried to avoid each other's eyes. Julius Klein then hurried in, panting, too late to hear the "welcome" that had been accorded the other internes. He, too, was jolly in his greeting. "Good morning sir. I hope I'm on time. The hospital corridors wind around in such a complicated maze that I almost got lost. I swear one needs a map to find one's way around here."

The rest of the internes held their breaths. Julius was the only one to laugh at his own joke. Dr. Taylor looked at him for a moment, then remarked: "Dr. Klein, I believe?" "Yes sir, Julius Klein." "How late is it Dr. Klein?” "Eight sir - eight on the dot!" "Dr. Klein, in the future you'll be so good as to wait for me. Today I've waited for you and that must not happen again!" "I'm sorry sir, but I was under the impression that we're supposed to report at eight and it's exactly eight now." "Doctor, you're not working by the hour. You're a physician now, and the duties of a physician aren't fulfilled by punching a clock." "I beg your pardon sir. Is there a definite time when we have to meet you in the wards or in the operating room?” "Yes - when and where I arrive, you are to be there waiting for me." "When do you arrive, sir?” "When duty calls. That's one thing you should never forget!"

Julius lowered his eyes in silence. 286

"Does anyone wish to ask any questions?" Dr. Taylor inquired brusquely. "If not, we won't waste any more time, but get down to work. Sparks and Ross, you'll work with

Dr. Clark today. Young and Klein - with Dr. Newman. Muel ler and Shanan — with me." 

Dr. Taylor headed for the operating pavilion. As soon as he appeared in the doorway, the customary hubbub of hospital personnel hurrying to and fro, died down. Although each one continued to pursue his or her particular chore, it was done with restraint and without any noise. This was the effect of the presence of the surgeon-in-chief, Dr. Eugene Taylor. 

The superintendent, Miss MacDonald, advanced towards him. She was a stern young woman who ruled over the administrative set-up of the operating pavilion like an absolute monarch. Discipline, promptness and the rigid following of all hygienic principles, were laws that none dared violate in her presence. Orderlies, nurses, internes, and even the older surgeons, were afraid of her. 

Nurses of this type are not unique – they can be found in almost all hospitals. They are usually elderly women not especially endowed with feminine charm, disillusioned in their private lives, and they pour out their bitterness in their strictness towards those working under their supervision. 

Ruth MacDonald was an exception. She was young - only twenty-seven - beautiful, with light blonde hair and deepblue eyes. Her peremptory strictness in the operating pavilion added an air of majesty to her charming, sculptured features, and her tantalizing figure appeared even more attractive in her well-fitted uniform. Both young and middle-aged physicians often cast admiring eyes at her, but her inflexible authority created a barrier which the most valiant did not dare to cross. 

Dr. Taylor was the only one whom Miss MacDonald feared although he had never so much as raised his voice to her. 

His very appearance in the operating-quarters filled her with awe. When he was operating she would pace about outside his operating-theatre, and hushing the passersby would repeat, "Shhh Shhh - Dr. Taylor is operating. Quiet please." 287

This time, too, she approached him with deference and said meekly: "Good morning sir. We are at your service. As you have ordered, I've sent for the first patient- the one with the ruptured kidney." "Yes, yes indeed. The most urgent case precedes all the others... Miss MacDonald, these are the two new internes, Dr. Mueller and Dr. Shanan. They will assist me today." "Yes sir, I will see to it that they get what they need."

The patient was a young man of twenty-four who had been brought to the hospital on the previous night in a state of unconsciousness. He had been thrown by an automobile and his right side had hit the point of a hydrant. This blow had ruptured his right kidney, causing a massive internal hemorrhage and shock.

The patient's condition was critical and presented a problem. In order to save his life an immediate operation to stop the bleeding was indicated. The patient's physical condition however, was so poor in spite of the emergency treatment all night, that it was highly doubtful whether he could survive an operation. Both steps were dangerous. What was to be done? . . . Dr. Taylor decided to operate. . . This first operation with Dr. Taylor made an unforgettable impression on David...

The patient lay on the operating table, covered in white. 

The right loin thickly colored with iodine was exposed, and created a sharp contrast with the surrounding whiteness under the concentrated glare of the powerful electric lamps. 

A solemn silence reigned in the operating room. The only sounds were of the labored breathing of the patient, and the strange hissing of the anesthetic gas flowing through the tube of the mask which was clamped on his face. 

Dr. Taylor, his assistants and nurses clad in their surgical garb, waited watchfully about the table. David stood spellbound... He felt as if he were standing among priests at the altar of a temple, where holy services were being conducted in a spirit of sanctity. . . 

The voice of the anesthetist could be heard: 288

-- "Blood pressure 110 over 85; pulse — 90, quality — not bad.

Transfusion flowing normally; the patient is asleep; ready for surgery."

Dr. Taylor stretched out his hand. The scrub-nurse handed him a scalpel, and the operation began. Like a skilled artist who guides his brush across the finest lines of a portrait, Dr. 

Taylor guided the edge of the knife across the tissues of the patient's body.

The skin had been cut open; the muscles were being severed one after another and retracted, revealing a deeper approach to the ruptured kidney. From beneath the capsules in which the kidney was enveloped, there came into view a large blue, protruding mass bursting under tension. 

A cut over the capsule and masses of clotted blood gushed forth the patient's white cover turned red and streams of blood flowed on all sides. The anesthetist became restless: "Blood pressure dropping rapidly-95, 90, 70. . . Pulse weak, barely perceptible. Respiration shallow. The patient is in shock. Blood pressure not obtainable. . .” Dr. Taylor frowned, and big beads of perspiration gathered in the wrinkles of his forehead. Without pausing for a moment, he commanded: "One cc of adrenalin intramuscularly! Accelerate the transfusion! Start infusion of 5% glucose in physiological serum in another vein! The kidney must be removed. That's his only chance. . ."

He plunged both his hands into the interior of the hemorrhaging wound, and groped his way to the main vessels of the kidney. He clamped the vessels with a metal clamp.

The torn kidney was removed; the bleeding stopped. The warning voice of the anesthetist resumed: "Patient's condition not better; still in deep shock. Pulse weak and hard to discern. Blood pressure not perceptible. 

Respiration short and superficial. He is becoming cyanotic. 

I doubt whether he can last much longer. . .” 

Dr. Taylor raised his head and said calmly: "My forehead, please.' 

Miss MacDonald appeared, and with a moist sponge wiped 289

the perspiration off those parts of his face which were not covered by the surgical mask. He drew a deep breath and continued: "The wound can't be closed now - the patient's condition is too critical. We'll do it in a day or two if he survives. 99 The wide yawning wound left by the removal of the kidney, looked like the open toothless maw of a monster. Dr. Taylor asked for long packing bandages and as he proceeded to fill and pack the wound, he commanded: "Keep the patient in the side recovery room. Apply all emergency measures against shock. Raise the foot of the bed. 

See to it that the patient is kept as warm as possible with covers and hot-water bottles. Give him another cc of adrenalin intramuscularly. Install an oxygen tent. Continue the intravenous administration of blood and glucose in serum. Miss 

MacDonald, please assign a special nurse to the patient. Dr. 

Shanan, you'll take charge of the case."

Dr. Taylor's orders were carried out promptly with the greatest efficency. David stood over the sick man and for the first time had a chance to observe him closely. The patient lay motionless, his head thrown back, his face blue and swollen. The glazed whites of his eyes were visible under his half-closed lids. Only occasionally could one discern a light short gasp of breath, like that of a fish trapped on dry land. 

David was at a loss as to what to do. He kept his hand on the patient's pulse but could feel nothing except the moribund man's unpleasant, clammy perspiration. There was no pulsation in the vessels of the patient. "That's it, doctor," the nurse remarked calmly and matterof-factly. "Very little can be done for him now. Poor devil... And so young, too. . . Well, it's all in the game. . .' "Do you think he's dead?" David asked shakily. "As good as dead! Won't be long now. . . He's done for all right..."

David turned away from the patient and headed back for the operating room where Dr. Taylor was operating on his second case. Without interrupting his work and without looking up from the operating wound, he asked loudly: "Is he dead?" 290

"Almost sir." "Almost?" Dr. Taylor shouted. "What's the meaning of 'almost dead?'" "He's dying sir. He's just about breathing his last." "How dare you leave your post! Back - back to the sick man and stay there! If you can't save him, stay by his side and wait until he's dead! Not 'almost dead,' but dead!"

David's heart contracted painfully. Pale and wobbly, he made his way back to the patient. 

When David reentered the side recovery room, the nurse there was scared by his appearance. She could not understand this sudden change in him, and eyeing him in amazement said: "Not yet, doctor. . . He's dead, but not quite. . .” 

David paid no attention to her. His ears were still ringing with Dr. Taylor's screaming words: "If you can't save him stay by his side until he's dead! . . ." He glanced at the pressure indicator of the oxygen tank — it didn't stir. . . He thrust his hands under the tent, and feeling the patient's chest, he compressed the ribs from both sides and then released them. The lungs drew in the air and the pressure indicator of the oxygen tank began to flicker. “One. . . two. . . three. . . four. . ." David counted slowly, and on reaching "five," he again compressed the chest of the "almost dead" patient. He continued this artificial respiration for a long time and listened to the hissing of the oxygen which now found its way to the spongy inner structures of the patient's lungs.

Drenched in perspiration, David paused for a moment, felt the patient's pulse, and began to tremble. He cried out in a joyous voice: "It's pulsating! It's pulsating! I can feel it! He's alive! 

He's alive! Nurse, let me have a syringe and a needle! Hurry a syringe and a needle! I forced his lungs to breathe! Now 

I'll force the flow of blood into his veins!"

The transfusion and the glucose in serum had until now barely pushed a few drops through the moribund patient's veins, which had collapsed in exhaustion, paralyzing the stream of life. 291

David filled the syringe with blood from the flask, and with the pressure of his own fingers, forced it into the patient's veins. For hours on end he stood bowed over the ailing man and continued thus to inject the blood and glucosed serum.

It was already late afternoon. Exhaustion, hunger and thirst did not bother David. He was concerned with only one thing to keep this man alive. And then suddenly it happened - the patient opened his eyes, and in a faint sighing voice mumbled: 

"Water. . . dry. 

A little water. 99 dry in the mouth... Water please..

For David those were not the ordinary words of a patient awakening from an operation. They were words from heaven, letting him know that his mission - the mission of the 

Healer of All Flesh - had been achieved. 

Tears of gratitude and of inner spiritual achievement trickled from David's eyes. From the depth of his heart came forth the words of Psalms: "Let the words of my mouth and the meditation of my heart be acceptable to Thee, O Lord, my Rock, and my Redeemer." 

Engrossed in his thanksgiving prayer, David felt the touch of a hand on his shoulder. Turning his head around slowly, he saw Dr. Taylor. "Pardon sir," David stood up quickly and wiped the tears off his face with his hand. "I didn't hear you come in."

Dr. Taylor looked at David in silence for a while and then remarked: "To save a human life, Shanan, is like giving birth to a child. First one endures anguish and fear; then one gives way to tears of joy. . ." "Yes sir. Thank you sir." "Thank you for what?" "For teaching me not to forsake the last spark of a human life..." 292

Ruth MacDonald 

Ruth MacDonald was born and raised in a suburb of Pittsburgh. Her father, Frank MacDonald, had been employed by the Horner Steel Company since his early youth. His employers held him in great esteem because of his industriousness, loyal devotion, respectable behavior and personal integrity. 

Mr. MacDonald had started his career in the Horner Steel 

Company as an ordinary errand boy and had gradually advanced through the years to the responsible post of chief supervisor of the factory. In due time he had married the daughter of a simple upright worker and had led a very tranquil and happy married life.

He had four children, two sons and two daughters, who were reared properly, as behooves a fine, hard-working, American family. The MacDonalds owned their own modest home not far from the factory. Their house was surrounded by a well-kept lawn and a small garden of fragrant, multicolored flowers. Mrs. MacDonald was an ideal housewife. She worked tirelessly in her home — cooking, baking, washing, and keeping her house and children spotlessly clean. 

When he returned home from his work in the evening, Frank MacDonald always entered a clean, cozy dwelling filled with the savory aroma of cooking. The atmosphere created by his wife's smiling affection and the hearty noise and laughter of his children made him feel certain that in the whole wide world, there wasn't a happier man than himself.

Frank was very fond of his four children, but above all, 293

he adored his oldest daughter, Ruth. He showed sufficient warmth and love to the three other children but when he embraced and caressed Ruth, he felt an entirely different affection. Occasionally he thought it necessary to justify his partiality for Ruth. "She's the oldest," he would assure himself. "She was the first to give me the thrill of being a father and I can't seem to forget it to this day."

His children were beautiful, obedient and good, but Ruth surpassed them all. At any rate, that was how Frank Mac- 

Donald felt.

Coming home from work one evening, Frank, as was his custom, hugged Ruth, lifted her up, and buried his face in her soft white throat. She would usually become convulsed with laughter as she felt the tickling of his unshaved chin, but this time something unusual happened: 

She wriggled out of his arms and said, blushing: “Papa, please don't do that any more!" Then she dashed out of the room. 

Frank stood agog. He absent-mindedly stroked the heads of the other children, and then went to the bathroom to tidy up for dinner.

Ruth sat at the table close-mouthed, blushing, avoiding everyone's eyes and eating hardly anything. "What's the matter with you, Ruthy dear? Why don't you eat?" her father asked. "I don't know - I think I'm not hungry," she replied nervously and impatiently. "Has anything happened at school? Has anyone insulted you? It's not like you to act this way without some reason. 

Something must have happened. . .” "Frank," Mrs. MacDonald spoke up, “Leave the child alone and eat! Ruthy will be all right."

Later, when husband and wife were alone Mrs. MacDonald remarked, somewhat embarrassed: "This has been a hard day for Ruth. She's become a woman today. She's got her menstrual period for the first time. 

Maybe you think she's quite a scholar and knows a lot from books, but when this happened, she came running to mother, crying and scared..." 294

Frank MacDonald felt as though a millstone was pressing upon his heart. Eyeing his wife sadly, he said: "Ruthy, a woman... Ruthy grown up. . . too quick - yes, too quick. Before you know it, some strange devil will come along and put his paws on her and - Ruthy just won't belong to us anymore." "Frank MacDonald I'm amazed! You don't talk at all like a good Christian... You've got silly thoughts in your mind. . . 

What did you think that Ruthy would keep on being a baby forever? Children grow up, parents get older, and that's how God's little world goes on. 

99 Ruth blossomed. The new fresh sap coursing through her veins quickly unfolded the dormant buds of her womanhood, and she could sense within herself the mysterious power of sex the power before which the strongest of men bow. 

She became tall, slender, and full of charm. Walking in the street, she was aware of the male heads turned in her direction watching the rhythmic motions of her body, noting how her light blonde tresses swayed against her smooth neck and shoulders, observing her rounded hips and the easy gait of her long slim legs. In the high school, not only the students, but also middle-aged and even elderly teachers would gaze at her with eager eyes. 

Frank MacDonald, likewise, regarded his beloved daughter with different eyes now. The prettier she became, the prouder he felt. But somewhere in the inner recesses of his heart a tiny worm of fear was gnawing, an intuitive, premonitory fear which he tried his utmost to banish. 

It happened in June, 1918. Ruth had graduated from high school at sixteen. She was the Queen at the Graduation Ball.

This customary celebration had, however, been darkened by the clouds of World War I. The American people were beginning to feel the yoke of responsibility and the grief of human sacrifice.

It was the most crucial phase of the War. Russia had parted from her allies, and her Revolutionary leaders had concluded a separate peace with the Germans. The great powers, England and France, were drained and pathetically 295

anemic from the past four difficult war years. America's fresh troops and inexhaustible natural resources constituted the only hope for a possible victory on the part of the democratic world and President Woodrow Wilson made this hope a reality.

The blood of America's youth was being spilled on the battlefields of France. The people back home throughout the forty-eight states, were being exhorted not to relax in helping to achieve victory in this war for “freedom and democracy.” 

Young and old, little and big- all had become "war-conscious." There was hardly a single private or public affair of whatever nature that was not converted into some form of aid to further the cause of the war. 

That year the annual Graduation Ball at Ruth's high school was turned into an affair "to help the boys overseas.” "Ladies and gentlemen," the principal said to the audience, "tonight's festivities mark an important occasion because graduation from high school is a big stepping-stone in the life of youth. However, we cannot at any time forget those who are sacrificing themselves on our behalf overseas so that we can continue to enjoy freedom and democracy. It is our duty to participate in the general war effort even during this local celebration, and the graduates have volunteered to make a collection for the Red Cross. Please respond warmly and give generously."

Ruth MacDonald was intoxicated with happiness. She was the most popular girl at the ball, and had been dancing continuously. The moment the orchestra struck up a tune she was surrounded by young men, jockeying to be her partner. How wonderful! How happy she was! If only she could dance with two, three or four simultaneously! 

The "lucky" one kept whispering to her during the dance, telling her how beautiful she was, how lovely and alluring. 

As Ruth continued to glide along to the rhythm of the music, the young men's compliments exhilarated her more and more and she was in a transport of delight. ..

Suddenly the drummer of the orchestra beat a rapid roll on his drum, ending with a single loud bang. The hubbub 296

died down and the principal proceeded to make his appeal. 

Ruth did not recall ever having "volunteered" to collect money at the ball but who cared - why not? What difference would it make to her boundless happiness?

Instead of collecting the contributions in a plate as the other students were doing, Ruth deftly picked up the sides of her silk gown with both hands, forming a groove, and gaily flitted among the guests laughing coquettishly and pleading: "For the Red Cross, sir! The boys overseas need our help, sir!"

A twenty-dollar bill suddenly fell into the groove of her gown. In the midst of the accumulated twenty-five and fiftycent coins, the appearance of such a large sum was quite astonishing. She quickly raised her eyes and found herself looking at a strange young officer. His eyes were dark as coal. Ruth stared at him and felt her heart beat faster. . . "Pardon sir, I don't think I have enough change for you," she said in wonder, without taking her eyes off him. "I didn't expect any change, Miss-" "Ruth MacDonald." "Very pleased to meet you, Miss MacDonald. I'm Captain 

Andrew Mills, from Columbus, Ohio. Came to Pittsburgh for a few days - military matters. Jim Harrison, your math teacher, is an old friend of mine - we went to school together. 

He invited me to come to this ball. But he's been busy all evening and poor me, I'm dying of loneliness." "Captain, your contribution - it's very generous of you. 

I'm so surprised. . . I don't know what to say. Thank you many thanks. You certainly appreciate the importance of the Red Cross!" "Not just the Red Cross - also your wonderful beauty.” "Thank you, Captain. How gracious of you... But I must be on my way. Thanks again - and may God bless you. "Wait, please wait Miss MacDonald. Don't leave me alone. 

I haven't taken my eyes off you all evening. . . There have been so many adoring young men around you, I didn't have a chance. . . Tell me, how long would it have taken you to collect twenty dollars tonight?" 297

"My God, centuries!" "And I only want one hour from you.

For the rest of that evening, Captain Mills and Ruth Mac- Donald became oblivious to time. From their first dance together, when his strong arms gently clasped her waist Ruth sensed a wave of strange sweet weakness enveloping her. . . "Ruth... my dear, dear Ruth," he murmured, "Ruth, my darling. I'm in the middle of a wonderful dream. O 

God, don't wake me- please don't take this dream away from me. 99 

Ruth did not utter a word but her lips quavered, as though in silent prayer. Her eyes closed; she continued to nestle against this stranger to whom she was drawn by a mighty force of belongingness.

The night was mysteriously silent. The Allegheny, Monongahela and Ohio rivers flowing through and around the city like gigantic blood-vessels in a stupendous organism, exuded a dense gray mist that hovered and shrouded the terrain. 

From their wide yawning mouths, the tall awesome chimneys of the numerous factories and mills belched forth dense clouds of black smoke that hung over and screened the summits of the surrounding hills. Sandwiched between the smoky canopy above and the veil of mist below, Pittsburgh the "city of smoke," the "city of steel," sprawled in nocturnal repose. . . 

Ruth was shivering; the chattering of her teeth echoed in the prevailing calm. Andrew held her closer to him and asked, "Ruth my love, why are you shivering so?" 

She did not answer. She threw back her head and by way of reply, pressed her burning half-open lips against his mouth.

The two merged into one one breath, one soul, one life. . . 

They sank down onto the soft grass. Seething passions raced in their blood, and two love-hungry beings found one another and yielded to the overwhelming force of mutual desire. 

The ball had long been over. The sounds of the orchestra had long since ceased. The tumult of the celebrants had long since been swallowed by the stillness of the night. 

Ruth suddenly returned to reality. "How late is it, Andrew?" she asked. "Four, my darling. Pretty soon the stars will begin to 298

pale; they're afraid of the rising sun, you know.. "My God! I must get home right away. My parents will be terribly worried." 

Walking Ruth home, Andrew held her hand tenderly. He kept bending his head to gaze at her, his eyes filled with true and sincere love. 

"Ruth, my dear. . . my only one forever! I love you so very much! Today I'll come to your home. You'll introduce me to your parents and I'll tell them: I've come for my beloved. I've come to claim what belongs to me - my Ruth! 

Please give her to me with your blessings!" 

Drawing a deep breath, Ruth said: "Andrew - you won't laugh at me?" 

“Laugh — at you? Why?" "I want to repeat something that you said when we were dancing." "I said so many things! Do you remember everything? You want to repeat everything?" "No - not everything; just this: “O God, don't wake me. . . please don't take this dream away from me. . .” 

Captain Andrew Mills did not show up that afternoon. A messenger from the hotel where he had been staying delivered the following note: "My dearest Ruth, Ever since midnight there's been a communique here for me, ordering me to rejoin my detachment.

It looks like we're off. God knows where to. I hardly have time to jot down these lines. I'm awfully sorry not to be able to say goodbye to you. 

I love you, Ruthy dear, and from now on I'll be counting the minutes and impatiently waiting for the happy moment when I can be with you again next time forever and ever. · • Andrew" 

Ruth was stunned. Her face turned deathly pale. Her heart shrank painfully within her; her head was in a whirl; her knees buckled and she collapsed.

When she came to, she was lying in her bed. Her mother 299

sat on a chair facing her - sad and dejected. Clutching 

Andrew's note in a shaking hand she asked in a muted voice: "Who is he? How long has it been going on? Why haven't you told your own mother and father about it?” 

Ruth lay with eyes wide open not saying a word. Mrs.

MacDonald rose from her chair, and sitting down on the edge of the bed, asked anew: "Who is he? What's happened between you two? You can tell all to a mother. . ." 

Ruth threw her arms around her mother, and burying her face in her neck wailed bitterly: "Mother, mother! I love him so! I love him so terribly much! Why was he taken away from me - taken away so soon?"

Mrs. MacDonald had always been the strict parent, while her husband had been the more easy-going and affectionate one. "You're ruining the children!" she used to admonish him. "If I depended on you they'd grow up to be God knows what." Her practical common sense, her keen mental vision and austerity had counteracted her husband's leniency and joviality, and thus struck a balance in the raising of their children. No one had ever seen their mother being sentimental. .

Now, however, Ruth's tearful lamentations over the loss of her first lover stirred dormant feelings of sympathy in her mother's heart. She hugged her grown-up daughter to her, stroked her hair and kissed her tear-stained eyes: "My poor child. . . That's the fate of woman: to love and to suffer. The war will be over and he'll come back to you. Yes my dear, he'll come back. Pray to God for him and wait wait patiently; wait and hope. . ." 

Her mother's unusual sentimentality had the effect of cool, soothing balm on a burning wound. Ruth calmed down and began to wait. . . She prayed to God and waited. She cherished Andrew's one note like a sacred amulet. She read it over and over and waited – waited and hoped. 

The days were long and dreary; the nights even more so. 

Ruth's gaiety and mirth vanished. Her youthful impetus to sing and flit about died down. Her former deep laughter was 300

no longer heard. An unusual stillness of despair and melancholy prevailed in the sixteen-year-old girl.

Her eyes became lustreless; her bloodless cheeks became thinner; she had no appetite and the mere thought of food made her feel nauseated. She became weak and listless, and felt drawn to bed. She could not sleep, but her lead-weighted limbs felt better when she was not on her feet. . . 

Six weeks went by. Then the newspapers reported the tragic news that an American transport ship had been sunk by a German submarine; Captain Andrew Mills was listed among those who perished.

Frank MacDonald became nervous. He looked for a place to hide the newspaper, and kept saying to his wife excitedly: "Ruth mustn't get hold of this paper! She looks like a ghost as it is. This terrible news will destroy her. I don't want her to learn about this! Do you hear me? I don't want her to know!" 

"No Frank that's not the proper way," Mrs MacDonald replied in her usual tranquil, rational manner. "Such things can't remain hidden for very long. It's better for us to break the news to her, rather than for her to learn about it from strangers. Give me the paper, Frank."

Frank lowered his eyes and meekly handed her the crumpled newspaper. Ruth received her mother's tragic information without the least emotional reaction, as though she had already known it for a long time. She remained lying on her bed not even making any effort to turn. "Ruthy dear are you sleeping?" 

"No, Mother, I'm not sleeping." "Would you like to look at the newspaper?" "No, Mother, I believe you." 

This indifference surprised Mrs. MacDonald so, that she did not know what to say or do next. Finally she ventured to comment: "This, my dear, was the will of the Lord." "Yes, Mother." "You ought to thank the Lord - He has been gracious to you. It might have been worse. 

301

"Worse? How?" "You could have remained a widow with a child. Don't you think that would be worse?" "Yes, Mother." 

"I'm very glad that you're taking this so reasonably. Of course it's a pity- a great pity - but it was God's will... 

Ruth suddenly sat up and clutched her stomach with both hands. Her face turned pale. "What's the matter with you, Ruth?" her mother asked in alarm. "I don't know, Mother. I feel as if I were going to die."

She got out of bed and rushed to the bathroom. Mrs. Mac- 

Donald followed her. Ruth bent over, retching and vomiting.

Her mother was stupefied. She began to tremble as the shattering truth dawned on her. Ruth's misfortune was much greater than she had imagined. She was pregnant. . . 

Mrs. MacDonald immediately regained her composure, and decided upon a plan: Frank could never survive this misfortune of his beloved daughter. This must not be divulged to him. Ruth must leave Pittsburgh. She could be sent off to 

Mrs. MacDonald's younger sister, Gloria, in New York. Yes, that seemed to be the best way out. Gloria was a nurse, unmarried, and employed in one of New York's leading hospitals. Gloria would know what to do. . . 

Upon emerging from Ruth's room, Mrs. MacDonald was confronted by Frank who anxiously showered her with questions: "What's Ruthy doing? How did she take it? Poor child, poor little girl! How is she? Shall I go in to see her? Do you think I could be of some help?" "No, Frank - not now," Mrs. MacDonald answered with a tone of finality. "Ruthy is fine; it's best to leave her alone for a while. . ." "Do you think she can be left alone? She won't. Is she likely to harm herself? You know what I mean. . . "Yes, I know what you mean. Frank, Ruthy won't be able to recuperate here. We've got to send her away to some place where her broken heart will heal more easily. She can't stay 302

in Pittsburgh.'

Frank remained speechless. His wife's decision fell upon him like a heavy sentence. He left the house and returned home late that night dead drunk for the first time in his life. He threw himself into bed fully dressed and sobbed: "My little Ruthy, my poor little girl. . To send her away!

Where to? She's so young, so broken up. I could feel this coming. My Ruthy, my little Ruthy.. Mrs. MacDonald undressed her husband without uttering a word and with motherly warmth covered his trembling body. . .

God's wonderful world and the mysteries of life are hard to fathom. Unknown forces govern God's creatures from the most insignificant little worm to the biggest, cleverest man. 

One of these abstruse forces is motherhood. 

The planting of a seed in a creature elicits an unusually strong feeling of attachment and boundless love for the growing offspring. The magic of motherhood leads to the greatest and most fearless self-immolation on the part of the mother regardless of whether she be a crawling creature, wild animal, cattle, or human being. 

Ruth, too, was affected by this magic of motherhood. Her "great misfortune" which harbored the danger of social ostracism both for herself and her family, seemed to her trivial and meaningless in comparison with the wonderful delight she felt over the child growing within her. A pleasant sweet warmth suffused her body and soul.

She gladly accepted her mother's proposition to go to her 

Aunt Gloria in New York. The hints about Aunt Gloria 

"knowing what to do," were ignored completely. Ruth yearned for this child as though it were her own life, and would shudder at the thought of losing it. She would often close her eyes and indulge in enchanted reveries, in which she visualized a new life a life of mother and child, a life of purpose and meaning, a life of stimulation and achievement. She would work and bring up her child. She would give her heart and soul to the child. No one, no one could bar her from this heavenly bliss. . . 

303

Ruth and her Aunt Gloria got along very well together from the start. Both were surprised to find in each other the exact opposite of what they had anticipated. Ruth had pictured her mother's sister as an old maid, a stern nurse, embittered against the entire world. "Aunt Gloria will know what to do about it," had been ringing in her ears. Aunt 

Gloria, Ruth had assumed, would probably try to bring about an abortion. She would give her various bitter potions, induce her to take special baths, or perhaps try to puncture her womb with knitting needles or something. ..

To Ruth's great amazement and relief, Aunt Gloria turned out to be not at all what Ruth had expected. She had a comely, youthful, sympathetic face, with soft, mild features, and pleasant light-blue eyes. She was of medium height, slender, cleancut, elegantly dressed, and very amiable.

On the other hand Gloria had imagined her niece as an overgrown, provincial, stupid girl, who at the age of sixteen had gone to bed with the first man who touched her. Now this niece was arriving in New York with a swollen belly, heavy feet, bloated face, and cow's eyes - coming to the big city so that her Aunt Gloria could help her get rid of the bastard. . .

To her surprise, the niece she met was a very beautiful girl full of youthful charm and animation, whose eyes gleamed with natural warmth, a sharp awareness, and intelligence. 

Aunt and niece immediately felt drawn to each other. “Aunt Gloria, I'm so glad to see that you're so nice. Somehow I was afraid you'd be altogether different." "Not 'Aunt Gloria' - that makes me feel old - just call me 'Gloria.' Ruth, do you want to know the truth? I too, am happy to see you as you are. I was kind of afraid that you'd be different."

Both women were laughing now, and dabbing at their moist eyes. They opened their hearts to one another. With breathless interest Gloria listened to Ruth's story about her short but intense love affair which had ended so abruptly and heartrendingly. She was especially fascinated by Ruth's inexorable determination to continue her pregnancy and never to part from her child. 304

Ruth, in turn, learned about Gloria's life 

a well-rounded life, quite absorbing and stimulating when she was busy at work making the rounds of the various patients in the hospital wards; but a life that could be sadly empty and lonely when she returned to her small one-and-a-half-room efficiency apartment. She also found out that every Tuesday evening, Gloria's drooping spirits vanished because it was then that she had her weekly date with Steve Brooks. "Is he handsome?" Ruth asked eagerly. "Yes, very handsome, tall and strong. He's blonde and full of life. When he laughs, everybody laughs. You'll like him the minute you see him; you'll fall in love with him. 

They all fall for him.” "How long have you known him?" "Four years." "Four years? That long? What are you waiting for? Why don't you marry him?"

Gloria lowered her eyes and said with a deep sigh: "Steve is married. . . For the time being his wife doesn't want to divorce him, so we're waiting. Yes, I know what you think, how long can a girl go on waiting? I'm thirty-two years old. I've broken with him several times and sworn to myself that I wouldn't see him any more. A couple of weeks pass by, and then the moment I hear his voice I run back to him as though I were hypnotized. And when I see him 

I weaken and flop into his arms. What can I do? I love that guy... 99 Gloria and Ruth chatted until late that night. Several times Ruth tried to steer the conversation toward a discussion of how she could solve her own problem, find a job, and begin to adjust herself to her new life, but Gloria would wave this question aside: "You've got plenty of time dear, you'll be my guest for the next couple of weeks. Relax and get used to the atmosphere of our big city. We'll go out and have some good times together; later on we'll work out your problem. . 

On the following day, Tuesday, Steve Brooks showed up.

He appeared at the entrance of the apartment, lugging two big paper bags that hid half of his head. The bags were 305

filled with all sorts of food and delicacies - bread, butter, cheese, coffee, cocoa, sugar, imported sardines, smoked fish and meat, several varieties of canned fruit, candy, and a bottle of wine. "Steve Brooks!" Gloria exclaimed. "What did you do rob a warehouse? Where did you get all this? What's happened? Has the war ended? Who won?" "Very funny! Very funny! What are you - an agent of the secret police? Is that the thanks I get for all these good things? Come here, you little dictator, and give me a kiss!”

Steve pulled Gloria toward him and caught her in his arms. He suddenly saw Ruth, and asked in astonishment: "And who's this?" "This is my niece from Pittsburgh, Ruth - Ruth Mac- 

Donald, Mrs. Ruth MacDonald. . . her husband died in action." "I'm sorry, very sorry. I'm Steve Brooks, a serf of this dictator here who pretends to be your aunt." 

Steve's vivaciousness, sense of humor, and even his physical appearance reminded Ruth strongly of her father. As she gazed at him, smiling, she was overcome by a nostalgia for home. "Pittsburgh, eh?" Steve exclaimed, slapping his forehead with his hand, “and I thought that nothing but steel came from Pittsburgh — cold, hard steel! Gloria, are you sure that she hails from Pittsburgh, and not from heaven? She's as beautiful as a goddess!" 

"Yes, indeed, Steve, she is very beautiful."

Steve took hold of Ruth's hands, spread them wide and regarded her for a moment like a connoisseur appraising a valuable object. "Look at what comes out of Pittsburgh! What am I doing in New York? Pittsburgh is where I belong!"

Gloria and Ruth giggled. The room was filled with Steve's resonant voice and gay, joking talk. "Mr. Brooks," Ruth remarked at last, "you remind me very much of my father." "Hold it! Hold it! What nonsense is that? Mr. Brooks! 

Steve is the name! I'm very glad to learn, my dear, that I 306

resemble your father; I only hope that he's younger than sixty." "My father is very young - he's not even forty." "Thank God for that. Gloria, what about a bite? I'm starved to death." 

Steve opened the bottle of wine, filled three glasses, and raising his own, said: "I'm proposing a toast to Pittsburgh! The city of smoke, the city of steel, shall henceforth also be called the city of beauty!"

Throughout the meal, Steve did not cease to amuse and entertain both girls. It was late at night when he was about to leave and Gloria said: "Steve, I'll walk you part of the way. Ruthy dear, leave the dishes; I'll be right back."

Alone with Steve in the elevator, Gloria stood on tip-toes and planted an ardent kiss on his lips. "I'm sorry, Steve, about this evening. It's only for a week or two. Next Tuesday we'll probably be alone again. 

I miss our nights - they're so few. . .' 

"I understand, I understand. Think nothing of it. She's a swell kid; I like her." "Steve, Ruth is in a jam. She's not married. Her friend, a captain in the Army, went down with the ship on his way overseas. She's pregnant. . . I'll need your help, Steve. 

She must have a job - you've got connections." "Poor kid - she sure is in a jam. Of course, I'll do whatever I can." "Steve, I felt that I had to tell you the truth. But when you see her - pretend that you don't know a thing. You know what I mean. .. 

"Yes of course - don't worry. And by the way Gloria - 

Ruth doesn't have to move out because of me. 

She's got to be helped. You and I, we'll enjoy many more evenings together later on." 

That night Ruth lay awake unable to fall asleep. She recalled various phases of her life from early childhood to now. She thought of Pittsburgh, her home, her father, her mother, her two brothers, her younger sister, high school, the 307

Graduation Ball, Andrew... She and Andrew in the mysterious hush of that night. A warm feeling permeated her body and involuntarily she placed the palms of her hands on her stomach. Strange - she could not picture the kind of life she would have led, had Andrew not perished. . . 

New York. At night. How does New York look? She didn't know. Maybe tomorrow she'd go out. Pale streams of light from the street lamps shone dimly through the window curtains. From time to time the room's walls seemed to reverberate to the movement of elevated trains swiftly passing. From the other bed came Gloria's quiet rhythmic breathing. "She's sleeping so soundly," Ruth mused, "She's probably dreaming about Steve. . . Yes, she loves her Steve."

When Ruth awakened, Gloria had already left. Her bed was made, and on its smoothly-drawn spread was the following note: "Ruthy dear: 

I'm late and in a hurry to get to work. You'll find your breakfast in the kitchen. Heat the coffee. 

Go out for a walk if you want to but only in the neighborhood - don't wander too far away. I'll see you at four. Gloria"

Ruth felt exhilarated. She opened the curtains and sunshine poured into the room. She stuck her head out of the window. She could hear the cacophony of elevated trains, passing autos, pushcart-peddlers with their monotonous outcries and the tumult of the children playing on the sidewalks. Especially conspicuous were the murky brick walls of the neighboring apartment houses, with their small iron balconies under which narrow fire-escapes dangled. To Ruth, these looked like cages rather than dwellings in which people lived. She was seized with a feeling of claustrophobia, and yearned for fresh air and space. She decided to go out. It was a fine day and she would have a chance to stroll through the streets of New York before Gloria returned from the hospital.

She filled the bathtub with warm water, washed herself 308

with scented soap, and played with the ever-rising, bubbling soapsuds. The soft water caressed her body and filled her with a gratifying feeling of well-being.

Ruth slipped a light smock over her powdered body, stepped into her slippers and came out of the bathroom. She was suddenly startled. Surprised and frightened, she emitted a piercing outcry. On her still unmade bed sat Steve Brooks.

She instinctively drew her scant smock tightly around her, and stood bewildered and embarrassed. She glanced down at her feet, bared to above the knees, and was at a loss as to what to do. Her heart was pounding, and crimson-faced, she gasped: 

"Steve! — you? I didn't expect you. Gloria didn't tell me that you were coming. Who let you in? My God, the door must have been unlocked and I didn't know it!" 

Steve stepped over to her, and taking hold of her face with both hands he tipped it back a little and said: • • "Don't hide your sky-blue eyes from me... How beautiful you are! Gloria doesn't know about my coming here now. 

And as to the door, my dear, I've always had a key. Understand?" 

No, Ruth didn't understand. Steve's words made no sense at all and his stare chilled her blood. His murky gray eyes bore through her like steel rapiers. His lips twisted in a cynical grin, which evoked disgust and abhorrence in her. 

Freeing her head from his grip she said angrily: "Steve, you'd better come back when Gloria is home.

Excuse me now - I'm very busy. I have to go out soon and 

I want to get dressed." 

As she turned to go back into the bathroom Steve seized her hand and roughly pulled her toward him. Her skimpy smock flew open, revealing her nude body. Steve turned wild. His eyes flashed like a flaming fire. He clasped her with his strong arms, straining her towards him in a vise-like grip. A burning heat beat down on her from his half-open mouth.

He hurled her on the unmade bed burying his head between her breasts and biting into her white body. He was oblivious to her pleading, her tears and outcries; he did 309

not heed the blows of her fists on his head, nor her sharp nails that scratched his face to the point of bleeding. He kept on burrowing evermore savagely into her. 

She weakened and could hardly catch her breath. Her limbs were becoming numb and she was in a daze when his hands fumbled about her belly. "No not that. . . O God, please help me!" She struggled with her last remaining strength; and then suddenly saw a ray of hope – his necktie!

Steve's husky body was sprawled heavily on top of her. 

With one hand he pinned her down to the bed; with the other, he manipulated adroitly ever closer to pouncing on his prey... He kept kissing and biting her throat, arms, and breasts, tossing and turning his disheveled head.

In the struggle his shirt unbuttoned, and from under his crumpled collar dangled the two ends of his loosened tie. 

Exerting her last effort Ruth seized both ends of the tie and drew them tightly about his throat. Steve hiccoughed and began to choke. The more he pulled his head back, the tighter the noose became. His face turned purple; the veins of his throat became taut to the bursting point; his eyes nearly popped out of their sockets. He bent his right leg and kicked savagely into her belly with his knee. . . 

Ruth blacked out; her grip on the ends of his tie relaxed. 

She felt as though she were falling down, down into a bottomless abyss. From that unfathomed depth she heard the faint distant echo of a choking cough, of curses, and of a banging door. . .

The acuteness of her stomach pains subsided a little. 

Through her semi-consciousness mind there flashed the thought: "I'm free. . . I'm clean. . . He didn't dirty my child .. I thank you, Lord I thank you. . ." 

When Gloria came home, she found Ruth sprawled in a pool of blood on her unmade bed. She pressed Ruth to her bosom crying hysterically and kissing her forehead and closed eyes: "Ruthy dear, what happened? Open your eyes - talk to me! Just one word! What happened?"

Ruth barely opened her eyes and made an effort to say 310

something but slipped into unconsciousness again. 

Gloria dashed out into the corridor, reached for the public telephone and put through an urgent call for an ambulance.

The blow at Ruth's stomach had brought about a rupture in the inner structure of her womb. She lost the baby. An acute internal inflammation set in. Microbes invaded her bloodstream and flooded her entire body. 

For two weeks Ruth hovered between life and death. 

Gloria did not leave her bedside, keeping vigil day and night. 

She applied cold compresses to Ruth's feverish forehead and kept moistening and refreshing her dry, chapped lips. She held Ruth against her each time the girl cried out wildly in her delirium: "Andrew! Andrew! It's a boy! Steve! Steve! 

Take care- the child!"

To prevent Ruth's outbursts from disturbing the other patients in the ward, she was isolated in a side room. At night the dim lamps cast long somber shadows across the gleaming parquet floor. The occasional sound of painful groans pierced the oppressive quiet, disturbing the stupefied and drugged sleep. Gloria was drowsy and exhausted. The door opened slowly and the surgeon-in-chief, Dr. Eugene 

Taylor, walked in. Gloria came to with a start and rose to her feet. "Dr. Taylor-you, so late at night?" "Remain seated, nurse. Please don't stand up. You must be tired." 

Dr. Taylor felt the patient's pulse. "Weak and rapid, but regular. Temperature still high. 

Poor child. Septicemia is not easy to overcome. She fights like a trooper. It's the tenth day - high time for the fever to subside. . ."

Ruth tossed and turned, mumbling incoherent words, along with the names of Andrew and Steve.. "Who is Andrew?" Dr. Taylor asked. "Her fiance he died in action." 

"The father of the child?" "Yes." "And what about Steve? Who is he?" 311

Gloria remained silent. In her confused mind, two vague thoughts were whirling about: whenever Ruth called out

Andrew's name in her delirium she invariably referred also to Steve. But where was Steve? She had not heard from him.

She had tried to reach him by telephone, and the answer always had been "Sorry, Mr. Brooks has left New York. No, he didn't leave any forwarding address. . ." "Who is Steve?" Dr. Taylor repeated his question. "I don't know sir. I haven't the faintest idea," Gloria replied, swallowing a lump. Ruth's fever subsided on the fourteenth day. She opened her eyes and could hazily see Gloria standing over her with tears trickling down her kind and smiling face. "Gloria, Gloria," Ruth mumbled faintly, "I'm so happy to see you. 

Don't leave me alone. . . Where am I? I didn't know he had a key. . . Everything happened so suddenly so horribly... He jumped at me like a mad dog. . . I was choking him I didn't let him dirty my child. . . Gloria, don't leave me alone! Where am I?” 

Gloria hugged her weak, emaciated niece. She buried her face in her thin neck and wept bitterly. "Gloria, dear Gloria, I'm sorry, very sorry. Don't cry dear." "It's nothing-it's nothing. A bit of crying will do me good." She raised her head, and half-laughing through her tears, said: "What a fool I am! I don't know whether I'm crying because I'm so glad you're alive or because of sorrow over my poor luck... 

"Gloria, my good Gloria, where am I?" "You're in the hospital, dear. You were very ill - you'll be all right. You've got to rest now; the doctor has ordered strict rest." "The doctor? What kind of a doctor?” "Dr. Taylor - he saved you. A wonderful person and a great surgeon." "A surgeon? Did they cut me?" Ruth's trembling hands began to grope for her belly under the bed cover. Sighing with relief, she said: 312

"Thank God - I wasn't cut. My child hasn't been hurt... "Ruthy dear, you - you're no longer pregnant. You had a miscarriage. . . You lost your child.”

Ruth's hand on her belly writhed spasmodically. A chill shuddered through her. Her eyes lost every spark of light; they appeared glazed and lifeless like those of a dead person whose lids remained open. • • From that moment on Ruth resigned completely. She neither spoke, ate, drank, nor showed the least will to live. "Dr. Taylor, it's all my fault," Gloria said. "I shouldn't have told her straight off like that about her miscarriage. 

I knew very well how important that child was to her. She wanted it so much. I should have been more careful. Now 

I'm afraid she won't survive. It's all my fault. Please, Dr. 

Taylor, help me! Please save her!" .. "Get hold of yourself, nurse. I understand and appreciate your emotions about your niece but that's not the way to help her. Your worrying and crying are only signs of weakness. It's natural for you to react this way; it's very dif ficult to be objective about one's own relatives. But an attitude like yours is no good around a sick person. The more ill the patient is, the stronger his healers have to be. Nurse, no one can heal with weakness. An invalid can sense which one of his healers is strong and which is weak and he instinctively has confidence in the strong. Confidence, nurse, is faith, and faith is the greatest secret of healing." "Yes sir, I know I'm weak. . . Maybe not with other patients, but in Ruth's case I can't help it." “I understand nurse. I think you had better withdraw from this case. Another nurse will take charge of Miss Mac- 

Donald. You may visit her as a relative. I'm sorry, but this change is necessary for the welfare of the patient.' 

"Yes sir. Thank you, sir. May I see her now for a minute to say goodbye?" "No, nurse, that would only lead to another emotional outburst. You had better leave now without seeing her."

When Dr. Taylor came in to visit Ruth again, he sat down 313

near her bed, felt her pulse, and holding her thin pale hand, gazed sympathetically at her for a long while. A nurse appeared in the doorway and said: 

"Pardon me sir; you are wanted on the telephone — it's 

Mrs. Taylor." "Thank you, nurse. Tell Mrs. Taylor that I'm busy on a very important case. I'll call her back as soon as possible.”

The nurse turned to leave, casting a suspicious look over her shoulder. 

Ruth's eyelashes fluttered slightly. She raised her head slowly and looked at the doctor with surprise. "Hello," Dr. Taylor said gently, smiling at her. “I'm Dr.

Taylor; you're my patient. I'm very much concerned about you... Bad microbes flooded your bloodstream and almost poisoned you... But you conquered them. For fourteen days and nights you struggled for your life and you won. . . Do you know why you won? Because you wanted to live! The strength of your will to live helped you to defeat millions of microbes. As long as you desired life I had every hope of saving you. However, when a patient doesn't want to live, the doctor is helpless - completely helpless. Yes, I'm worried... It's a pity, a great pity. . ." "I don't want to live," Ruth moaned. "I don't have anything to live for. I want to die. Let me die.” "My dear girl, dying is nothing. You don't need me for that. To live - that's something! To lose the man one loves together with the fruit of his love, is a wound that leaves a deep scar on the heart. . . Yet wounds and scars make life more meaningful. You are young, very young, and so wonderfully beautiful. You'll love again and be loved. You have a lot to live for." 

Ruth began to cry quietly at first and then with loud. sobs. She felt as though the ice floes around her heart had thawed releasing her from her oppressive burden. "Good, my dear, good," Dr. Taylor consoled her. "Have a good cry - you'll feel better. A good cry is as refreshing as the serene blue sky after a stormy rain."

Ruth's crying subsided little by little and she calmed down. 

Dr. Taylor picked up the glass of milk which stood untouch- 314

ed on the night table near her, and brought it to her lips. "Drink it," he said, "and let the whiteness of milk be the symbol of the white flag with which you are surrendering to life. . ."

As Ruth drank the milk, every swallow refreshed her parched throat, and revived the blood in her youthful veins. She looked at Dr. Taylor as at a wondrous light, beaming rays of hope and solace upon her. 

From that day on Ruth began to recover. She ate, drank, and obediently swallowed the bitter medicines which Dr. 

Taylor had prescribed to improve her anemia and to tonify her debility.

Physically, Ruth felt considerably improved, but spiritually, she was still dejected. She would sit up in her bed for hours, sad and pensive, her blue eyes reflecting a deep melancholy. "How is my wonderful patient today?" Dr. Taylor asked cheerfully, as was his custom when he made his daily rounds. 

He glanced at the inner membrane of her lower eyelids and remarked: "Good - the anemia is practically gone." He felt her pulse. "Wonderful – regular and strong." He pressed the tips of his fingers into her abdomen. "Everything normal again! In a day or two you'll be able to leave the hospital." 

Ruth sighed and kept quiet. 

"Miss MacDonald, did you hear what I said? You are well! 

In a day or two you'll be able to go home. Why do you keep quiet? Doesn't this news mean anything to you?”

Ruth restrained herself from bursting into tears; she didn't want to cry again in front of Dr. Taylor. "On the contrary, doctor," she answered faintly, "it means very much." "It doesn't seem to, judging from your reaction. Instead of looking happy, you're sighing so sadly." "I can't be overly-happy when the prospect of leaving the hospital makes me feel so sad." "That's because you're afraid to face the reality of life again outside these hospital walls. That's a natural and temporary reaction that will pass very soon. The careful attention and treatment that a patient receives in a hospital 315

during a prolonged illness often causes him to feel a sentimental attachment to this environment, where he's been taken care of so tenderly. That's natural. Yes, my dear, it often happens but the feeling will pass..." 

Ruth cleared her throat. Her heart pounded in apprehension over the question she wished to and had to ask: "Dr. Taylor, may I. . . may I ask you something?" "Of course, my dear. Please do." "Why were you so good to me? So much time and attention - for me, a stranger and poor? Forgive me if I'm being silly..." 

"No no my dear this isn't silly. . . I'm a doctor, and the duties of a doctor are divine... Man's life belongs to God, and when a doctor saves a human life he's fulfilling a divine mission. To save a human life is a great spiritual joy to a doctor. No, my dear, I haven't been good to you but to myself. Stranger? Poor? What has that to do with fulfilling a divine mission? Do you realize, my dear, that if the Nazarene had bandaged the wounds and relieved the pains only of His own relatives and only of the rich the world would never have given birth to Christianity. . ."

Ruth listened eagerly to every word. Suddenly she grabbed his hand and began to kiss it, fervently exclaiming: "Dr. Taylor, please let me remain with you! Don't send me away! Let me follow in your footsteps - help me!"

Dr. Taylor was taken aback for a moment. Gently withdrawing his hand from Ruth's grasp, he said in amazement: "Miss MacDonald, what a pleasant surprise! So you want to be a nurse! You've made a good choice! Of course I'll help - you'll be a wonderful nurse. And who knows - maybe some day we'll work together in the operating room." 

Ruth gazed at Dr. Taylor with deep gratitude. Her eyes mirrored her joy and happiness. She smiled and said: "Thank you sir. I hope so sir - I hope so... 

316

Dr. Wilfrid Newman 

Dr. Wilfrid Newman was in a bad mood. It was one of those days when everything seemed to go wrong, as though some evil spirit had contrived to exasperate him. 

It had all started in the morning in the operating room. 

As was his custom, Dr. Newman had arrived at the hospital at twenty minutes to eight. He was rested, in good spirits, clean-shaven, and dressed in a freshly pressed suit with a boutonniere in his lapel. He entered the surgeon's dressing room and began to change. 

He thought with singular pleasure about his first patient on today's operating schedule - "Milton Gleason, of Gleason Construction, Inc., a multi-millionaire and one of the leading industrialists in New York! I got him! A small right inguinal hernia. Good thing I scared him. . . Millionaires have no respect for 'trivialities' '

Dr. Newman was very pleased over the way he had handled the "Gleason Case." The entire episode flitted through his mind.

His nurse came in with the appointment book and asked: "Dr. Newman, when can you see Mr. Milton Gleason? His private secretary is on the phone." "Milton Gleason of Gleason Construction, Inc.?" "I don't know sir; it could be.” "It could be? I don't take any chances. Say that my schedule is all filled up for the next ten days. What's today - March 5th? Tell him that I can't see him before Thursday, March 15th!" 317

"It's a pity, sir, to lose a patient. Your appointment book shows some time free today or tomorrow." "Busy! - just say I'm busy - very busy! That's the way you're to answer - understand? Dr. Newman cannot see Mr. 

Gleason before March 15th." "Yes, sir; I'll do the best I can." 

Mr. Gleason came on the Fifteenth. "What's the matter with you doctors? The moment you move to Park Avenue, you become independent and we can't get near you. Waiting ten days for an appointment! It's unheard of!" "What can I do for you, Mr. Gleason?” 

"Straight to the point, eh? Well I like that. I don't like to waste time in my business either. I'm healthy, not a thing wrong. At least that's what I thought until that day when 

I asked my secretary to telephone you for an appointment.

While I was taking a shower I suddenly noticed a round swelling about the size of a walnut right here in the right groin. It doesn't hurt and it doesn't bother me, but ever since I first noticed it I keep thinking that I've got something there that wasn't there before." "Please get undressed and I'll examine you. Mm... just as I thought. Cough, please; stronger. Mm. . . that's it." "What?" "A hernia, Mr. Gleason." "How the devil did I get a hernia? Is it serious?" "As a rule it's not serious. However, it may have serious potentialities." "What does that mean in plain language?" "It means that the hernia could twist around and become strangulated. The intestines would be completely obstructed and the patient's condition could then become grave and even fatal." "What do you propose to do?" "Operate." "Is there any other remedy without an operation?" "I'm a surgeon, Mr. Gleason. When an ailment can be cured surgically any other remedy is worthless!" "You're a man of action. Well, I'm taking your advice. 318

Operation it shall be!"

The shrill ring of the telephone in the surgeons' dressing room interrupted Dr. Newman's trend of thought. "Hello! Yes, this is Dr. Newman. Who is this speaking?" "Shanan, sir." "Shanan? What's happened? Where are you?" "I'm in Ward 'C.'" "In Ward 'C'? What are you doing in Ward 'C'? Why aren't you in the operating room? It's two minutes to eight. 

You know very well that I'm operating on Mr. Gleason at eight." "I'm sorry sir. A patient with an acute strangulated hernia was brought in. His intestines are completely obstructed. 

He has to be operated upon immediately. I figured you would want to take him first and then attend to Mr. Gleason." "No! Mr. Gleason will be operated upon at the scheduled time. Shanan, you'll assist me and I wish to see you in the operating room immediately!"

Angrily, Dr. Newman banged down the receiver and proceeded to the operating room. Wrapped in a white sheet, Mr. Gleason already lay waiting on the operating table. He was dozing under the influence of a morphine injection which had been administered to him before he had been wheeled out of his room. Sensing the presence of Dr. Newman, he opened his eyes: “On time, doctor. You're a man of action - I like a man of action. Make it a good job, doctor. . ." "Yes, yes, Mr. Gleason. Everything will be all right. Don't worry," Dr. Newman reassured him, and stepping over to the washbasin he began to scrub his hands with brush and soap. He noticed that it was Julius Klein who was preparing to assist him. "Where is Shanan? I just spoke with him and ordered him here."

Julius Klein was scrubbing his hands and concentrating on his fingernails he answered hesitantly: "You don't know Shanan, sir. He won't leave an emergency case even for a minute. He asked me to assist in his place." 319

Brushing his long thin fingers furiously, Dr. Newman looked cautiously to right and left and mumbled under his breath: "Why did he have to turn to you? Why couldn't he ask an interne of another faith? Why is it that when two Jews meet they immediately have to form a holy congregation?" "Sir, you don't know Shanan. Among Jews he seems like a rabbi although he doesn't preach Jewishness; and among 

Gentiles he seems like a Christian although he doesn't preach 

Christianity... You think he turned to me because I'm a 

Jew? Believe me sir, you don't know Shanan." "Stop hammering on the idea that I don't know him. What's there to know? As far as I'm concerned he's a pain in the neck. Who's this patient in Ward 'C'?" "A homeless old Negro. The police picked him up in the park only half-conscious.” "A homeless old Negro? He wanted Mr. Gleason of Gleason

Constrution, Inc., to give up his place to some homeless Negro?" "Yes sir. It's as I told you - you don't know Shanan."

Dr. Newman operated upon the Negro at ten o'clock. Both 

David Shanan and Julius Klein assisted. The intestines which were strangulated in the hernial sac showed sings of gangrene. 

This strangulation had cut off the blood circulation causing necrosis. It became necessary to remove that part of the intestine.

The operation took three hours. Dr. Newman demonstrated exceptional skill in his surgical technique. He was complete master of the situation, and the tissues yielded meekly to the agile movements of his fingers.

After the operation he chanced to overhear a conversation between the two internes: "David, you must admit that he's a brilliant surgeon. What technique! That's real surgical art. On that he surpasses even Dr. Taylor!" "Julius, have you ever heard an automatic piano playing?

Technically the notes are played with precision and without mistakes. Still it's not good music because the soul of the musician is missing. Newman is a brilliant technician but 320

he'll never be like Dr. Taylor. He hasn't got a soul like Taylor's."

Shanan's sharp words, accidentally overheard by Dr. Newman, disheartened the surgeon and he left the hospital in drooping spirits. He reproached himself several times telling himself that it was absurd to take the babbling of some queer immigrant interne seriously. . . Today he was not justified in feeling depressed. After all, hadn't he performed an operation on Milton Gleason of Gleason Construction, Inc. . . . It was quite an achievement to have a patient like that the cream of the crop of New York. 

But it did not help. The cutting words spoken in a thick foreign accent, "He'll never be like Dr. Taylor. He hasn't got a soul like Taylor's" kept resounding in his ears. Dr. Newman was not hungry and did not eat any lunch. He arrived at his office earlier than usual, and somewhat surprised, the nurse said: "It's not two o'clock yet. Your first appointment is only an hour from now. You seem worried – has anything happened? How did you make out with Mr. Gleason?" 

"Oh fine, Laura, fine. I'm simply tired. I want to rest up a bit. Anything important for me?" "No, nothing special. Some people telephoned; they'll call back later. Oh, I almost forgot, your father has just been here he wanted to see you. He went to the corner to buy cigarettes and will be back soon." 

"Fine, Laura. Please let him in when he gets back."

The news of his father's visit dampened Dr. Newman's spirits even more. He was never too anxious to see his father - not because he disliked him but because his father's presence always reminded him of the reality of his Jewish origin. . Ever since he could remember Dr. Newman had deplored the fact that he had been born a Jew. He could blame no one but his father for this misfortune which had always filled him with a deep-seated pity for himself. He loathed his Jewishness as a hunchback loathes his hump. His father, Moses Newman, had emigrated to the United 321

States from Russia at the age of eighteen to avoid serving in the Czar's army. He had settled down on the East Side of New York and had been in his element there from the start. The land of his adoption had fascinated him. Here every person was free. Everyone enjoyed an equal chance to work and to progress. Everyone could live undisturbed, worshipping according to his faith, and in his own social atmosphere. 

Moses Newman became a patriotic American. At the end of five years he became a naturalized citizen and plunged, heart and soul, into the political activities of the masses. He was engrossed in all political elections, proudly availing himself of his citizenship rights and the privilege of voting in a truly American way. 

He was especially fond of the Jewish cultural life in New York. Here were Jewish newspapers, Jewish books, elementary parochial schools, Yeshivah academies, synagogues, Hasidic centers - a veritable paradise of flourishing Jewishness. "America is a God-blessed country," he would say with enthusiasm. "Here one can enjoy one's rights as an American citizen and at the same time practice the Jewish faith according to the holy Torah." 

Moses worked hard and diligently — first as a salesman in a dry-goods store; later as the owner of his own store on 

Essex Street with his living-quarters on the floor above. 

He married a cousin whom he had helped to emigrate from Russia to New York, and they had two children, a son and a daughter. There was none happier than Moses Newman. He loved America, he loved New York, and he particularly loved Essex Street, with its incessant deafening tumult of pushcart-peddlers and jostling throngs swarming about like locusts; with its clamor of haggling voices, and its mingling odors of herring, dill pickles, chickens, and fish. He loved his thriving little shop which gave him a sense of economic security. He loved his wife and children, and felt that he was striking roots deeper and deeper into this land of happiness and plenty. And he loved the American Jewish community with all his heart. He belonged to the Beth David 

Synagogue where he participated actively in various social and religious enterprises, supported all Jewish benevolent 322

and cultural institutions, and made certain that his children had a good Jewish education. 

Like most Jewish immigrants, Moses Newman dreamed of his son becoming a doctor and persistently fostered this ambition in the boy. When Willie was six years old Moses himself took him to his first day of public school. "Willie," he told him, "this is it! You're starting on the road that leads straight to becoming a doctor. You'll have to go a long way but just keep on going-don't stop - and you'll get there. America is a wonderful country. No one will stand in your way."

On that day when Willie just began school Moses Newman walked in a daze. He kept telling his customers and friends, "Something important has happened today! My son 

Willie has started to study for doctor."

Finally his wife, Blima, rebuked him: "Moses, said once it's a joke; said many times, it's silly. People will laugh at you." "Why silly? Why do you say that, Blima? Time marches on. Do you know, Blima - I've figured out that in only sixteen years from today Willie will become a doctor. Seven years in Public School, four years in high school, and five years in college. . . Come to think of it, Blima — it's not so far away at all. Sixteen years slip by very fast. Believe me, it's not so far away as it seems.

Moses was right. Time did race along as Willie plodded untiringly and undeterred on his sixteen-year-road to a medical career. There was one thing, however, which Moses had not foreseen and which perturbed him so greatly that his joy and pride in his son's steady educational progress was dimmed. 

Moses became painfully aware of the fact that the further his son advanced toward his destination as a doctor, the more the boy became alienated from his parents, his sister, and his traditional Jewish life. This was not a physical enstrangement, for Willie as always continued to live at home with his parents to eat and sleep there, and to enjoy the comfort provided him. His father, as always, paid for his tuition, books, clothing, and allowance. 323

The alienation was rather a spiritual one. His father first noticed it in Willie's indifference and contempt for Jewish studies. Although he had turned out to be a most diligent student in his secular school he was the most backward in the Hebrew school, and later, under private Hebrew tutors as well. "He's got a good head," his Hebrew teachers would tell Moses sympathetically, "but he's an unwilling student, and lack of will is worse than lack of ability." "Willie, you're very good in school and I'm very pleased with that," Moses would argue with his son. "That's a sign that you'll be a great doctor. But you're also a Jewish child and you should be willing to learn Jewish too." "Jewish is for greenhorns like you and mother. . . I'm a native American. English is the most important for me." 

When Willie graduated from Public School he received the highest award for English. During the ceremony of distributing the school prizes the principal praised Wilfrid Newman highly for his excellent grasp of the fine nuances of the English language. "Why aren't your parents here?" the principal asked him. "My mother is sick; my father didn't want to leave her alone." "I'm sorry to hear that. I hope she'll feel better soon.

It's a shame that they couldn't witness the honor awarded to their son."

On the following day the principal dropped in at Mr. 

Newman's store and said: "I would like to see Mr. Newman." "I am Mr. Newman. What can I do for you?" "How do you do, Mr. Newman. I am Mr. Raulston, the school principal.” 

"The school principal? My God, what an honor! I'm so sorry that everything is in such a mess here - and there's no place to sit down. Such an honored guest — and so unexpected! Blima, come here! This is Mr. Raulston, Willie's principal. Mr. Raulston, perhaps you'd be good enough to come upstairs? Perhaps a glass of wine, a cup of coffee? 

Such an honor! Such an honor!" "Thank you, Mr. Newman, thank you very much. I'm glad 324

to see that Mrs. Newman is feeling better. I'm sorry that you couldn't come to your son's graduation yesterday. I just dropped in to congratulate you personally. Willie is a fine lad and I can see why now. He comes from a fine mother and father. Well, goodbye Mr. Newman; goodbye 

Mrs. Newman."

After Mr. Raulston's departure, Moses remained standing in his store engrossed in thought. He kept repeating to himself: "Willie's ashamed of us. He didn't want us at the graduation. He even lied. . . Why? Why is he running away from us?"

Blima gazed at her husband with great compassion and said quietly: "The strange pastures seem fresher and greener to him. The smell of strange orchards seems more fragrant to him." 

Willie entered High School. He grew tall like his mother, who was much taller than her husband. From his father he inherited what he considered a Semitic appearance black hair, brown eyes and an elongated nose. Each time he glanced in the mirror he saw the reflection of his father - and something in him rebelled.

He often feared that along with this Semitic appearance he had also inherited his father's Jewish accent. He was therefore very conscious of his speech, and careful about the precise enunciation of each word. His Jewish classmates felt that he was making a deliberate attempt to hide his Jewishness and wasted no love on him. The Gentile students, on the other hand, discerned an unnatural affectation about his manner of speaking and laughed at him either openly or behind his back. But he could not be ignored - he was the best student in his class. Year in, year out, he won the highest distinction in most of the subjects. 

He steered clear of Jewish students and fraternized mainly with Gentiles. He hardly ever invited friends to his home. "Willie, why don't you ever invite your friends here?" his mother finally asked. "You're not, God forbid, an orphan or homeless. We're not rich, but we have a comfortable home, thank God." 325

"Mother, don't start lecturing me. My friends have nothing to do here." "Tell me, my son, when you visit them what do you do there that they couldn't do here?" "When I visit them I'm one of them! It's different with them. With them everything is solid and normal. They all speak one language and have one culture. They even pray in the understandable daily language of America."

His father, infuriated by this statement, burst out: "Do you mean to tell me that when you visit a Christian home you're one of them? My son, you're making a great mistake! The Gentiles who invite you to their homes know very well that you're a Jew and it doesn't even enter their minds to include you in their intimate midst. They treat you politely and show you proper hospitality but you're not 'one of them'! I can assure you that if you would invite some of your Gentile friends here and behave with the same politeness and hospitality as they show you, your friends would feel just as much at home here as you do when you're their guest. They would not expect to be treated as if they were part of the family." "Invite a Christian here? That's a joke! Mother has already lived in New York for eighteen years and would rather talk Yiddish than English. And you, Father, speak

English as if you were chanting Psalms or passages from the Talmud. Do you want my friends to laugh at me and to mimic us wherever I am?”

Moses fell silent. His lips twisted, and it was hard to tell whether he was about to cry or laugh. 

"Willie my son," he said finally, shaking his head ruefully, "if you don't want them to laugh at you just be what you are and be it with dignity and pride! If someone makes fun of my Yiddish accent, at least he does it openly.
