But if you're ashamed of being a Jew and try to be something that you're not that they'll sneer at behind your back, and this, my son, is worse, much worse. 

"1 Wilfrid Newman was an outstanding student in the University as well. A short time prior to his graduation, the 

Dean of the Medical School called him in and said to him: 326

"Newman, what are your plans for the future? Judging by your academic record during the past five years here in the University, you could easily choose any branch of medicine to train in. I think that research would be a suitable choice for you." "No, sir; I'd rather be a surgeon. I've always dreamed of being a surgeon." "Do you have any special hospital in mind where you'd like to obtain your surgical training?" "Yes, sir. I would like to be with Professor Eugene Taylor. 

He's my favorite teacher in surgery." "A good choice Newman - a very good choice. Professor

Taylor is still a young man but his surgical skill has already earned him a fine reputation, not only in New York but all over the country and even abroad." 

Wilfrid Newman graduated from medical school magna cum laude, and started to train under the supervision of Dr. Eugene Taylor. He was one of a group of four internes who had undertaken their training program with this young surgeon.

Newman happened to be the only Jew in the group and was delighted about it. "It will spare me the unfriendly scowls of co-religionists when I refuse to hobnob with them," he reflected happily. 

The new group of internes was invited to dinner at Dr. 

Eugene Taylor's home. Newman was in seventh heaven. He was fascinated by this exclusive section of New York facing the most beautiful part of Central Park, where Dr. Taylor's imposing home was located; by the luxuriousness of the interior furnishings; by the festive table; by the whole calm, correct and aristocratic atmosphere. How he envied those who could enjoy this kind of life in an environment which he had never had.

That was the evening when the young, attractive Mrs. 

Taylor first thought of her novel idea for the entertainment of the guests: "Gentlemen," she began, "let's liven up the evening a bit.

Let each one of you give in no more than five minutes, a thumbnail biographical sketch of himself including the epi- 327

sode which he thinks contributed most to his decision to study medicine. We'll start with the boss himself. Eugene dear, you'll be the first. You're their teacher; you have to set a good example. Come on! And remember, no more than five minutes!" 

Silence ensued. They all looked eagerly at Dr. Taylor.

After a momentary pause, the latter, with a nostalgic look proceeded to tell them in short sentences about how his father, John Taylor, had been unable to persuade him to become a real estate magnate; and how his ailing feeble mother, Clarissa Taylor, with her warm tenderness had enrooted in him the will to heal the sick. 

When Wilfrid Newman's turn came he was in a dither. 

Like the others he began by giving his name, age and birthplace. Then he paused as if his tongue were suddenly paralyzed. The room became filled with an uncomfortable quiet and the guests exchanged meaningful glances. Mrs. Taylor endeavored to overcome this embarrassment. "Dr. Newman," she addressed him tactfully, "Which one of your parents, your mother or your father, had a greater influence on your decision to study medicine?" "Mother? Father?" Newman still embarrassed, reiterated. "The truth is that neither of them had any influence on me. 

It seems that I quickly grew out of the 'father-mother influence.' "Dr. Newman," Dr. Taylor interposed, "the influence of parents over their children – whether positive or negative - leaves a permanent indelible imprint on their offsprings' future. One doesn't grow out of it. . . I know that from my personal experience. My mother died when I was only six years old but I feel her influence to this day and probably always will." "It is said," Mrs. Taylor observed, "that this is especially true in connection with Jewish parents. Don't you think so 

Dr. Newman?” "I don't know, Mrs. Taylor. I don't consider myself an authority on Jewish matters." "I beg your pardon, doctor, but I was under the impression that you were Jewish." 328

Newman blushed. He began to squirm and was most anxious to put an end to this unpleasant situation, but didn't know how. Every passing moment of silence seemed to him like an eternity. At long last he forced a smile and said: 

"I am first of all, an American an American whose ambition it has always been to be a surgeon. You'll have to excuse me if I don't have any special dramatic episode to explain the primary cause of my ambition. . .' 

The previous intimate good-fellowship of the evening was now replaced by an uneasy tension. The lively conversation had come to a standstill and it seemed as though everyone was on his guard not to say the wrong thing. Newman sensed this most. Although everyone was as courteous and affable to him as they had been before, he still felt that they were sneering at him. He thought he could discern a concealed, mocking grin mirrored in their eyes, and suddenly he recalled his father's words, 'Willie, my son, if you don't want people to laugh at you, then be what you are. . "No I'll not flaunt my Jewishness!" he angrily reflected. “I held my own in Public School, High School and the University, not because of my Jewishness but because I beat them all in studies. 

I'll beat them in surgery too. I'll hold my own also in the future – not because of my Jewishness, but because of my surgery!..." Dr. Wilfrid Newman plunged into his hospital training with the full fervor of his ambition. He felt that technical skill alone did not necessarily make a good surgeon. That could be taught even to an illiterate orderly. Good surgery entailed absolute perfection in the basic sciences of medicine, such as anatomy, physiology, bacteriology and pathology. 

He delved deeper and deeper into his textbooks. He studied the published reports of all the leading surgical centers of the world. He attended all the clinical-pathological conferences, at which intricate and rare problems in surgery were discussed. He kept on absorbing the world's medical literature leaving no stone unturned in his avid search for knowledge.

None of the other internes studied as diligently as he did, and they all looked upon him as a walking medical encyclo- 329

pedia. If someone was in urgent need of information on any abstruse topic and had no time to look it up he had merely to ask: "Newman, from which segments of the spine do the nerve-branches which control the functions of the urinary bladder issue?" - "Newman, in what proportion does the blood serum change the content of sodium, potassium, calcium and phosphorus, in the case of a degenerative process in the bones?" - "Newman what's the statistical data on surgical versus medical treatment of peptic ulcers?” — “Newman, what is the accepted classification of ovarian tumors?” 

And Newman would answer all with assurance and exactness.

Dr. Newman also felt that in surgery there was no room for sentimentality. Surgery meant cutting and only one who possessed an indomitable, unswerving character, could be a good surgeon. 

At one of the weekly conferences that Dr. Taylor conducted with his medical staff in order to discuss various complicated problems that arose in the surgical department, Newman reported the following case: "A man aged fifty was admitted to hospital on account of loss of weight, loss of appetite, weakness and anemia. A thorough examination, including laboratory tests and X-rays, indicated that there was a growth in the stomach — most likely carcinoma, and an operation was decided upon. "No sooner had the patient learned what his illness was, than a radical change in his mental state was noticed. He became very depressed and showed no interest in anything. 

He made up his mind that he was doomed to die. "The operation proved successful. Most of the stomach was removed, along with the growth, and the subsequent pathological examination corroborated the diagnosis of cancer. 

The patient failed to improve however. He wouldn't eat or drink anything, became completely resigned and apathetic, and died fourteen days after the operation." "Gentlemen," Dr. Taylor said, "here you have a good example of the power of man's will! It was this patient's will to die, and die he did. Be on your guard against a patient who warns you that he'll die. Most often such a warning turns out to be true. I'm less apprehensive about operating upon a 330

patient whose condition is critical but whose will to live is strong, than a patient who is not seriously ill but whose will to live is weak... Dr. Newman, you've been with us for three years now. You're about to enter into private practice of surgery. Would you please tell me why this patient was told the nature of his ailment? What did you expect to achieve that way? Don't you think that his fear that cancer was an incurable disease contributed largely to his despair and mental depression?" "That is sentimentality Dr. Taylor, and I don't believe that a surgeon has to reckon with it. The surgeon's profession calls for endurance and callousness. Let the patient be aware of the seriousness of his illness! Then he'll realize what a great responsibility the surgeon is undertaking when he operates on him, and he'll appreciate more what's being done for him."

Dr. Taylor pondered over this for a few moments and then repeated as though to himself: "Endurance and callousness. . . It's quite possible that you're right, Newman - it's quite possible... But I maintain that the profession of surgery also calls for humaneness and sympathy for the sick. Like everything else in life it's a matter of interpretation and ideology. There are surgeons who perceive only the afflicted organ and believe that their duty rests in the skillful removal of diseased tissue. But there are other surgeons who perceive, first of all, the suffering individual, and believe that their duty rests in helping and curing the whole individual. Yes, Dr. Newman, the individual is the essential thing." 

"I beg your pardon Dr. Taylor, but I fail to grasp the distinction. The essential thing is skillful surgery! Sentimental attitudes toward the patient are, in my opinion, harmful. They interfere with the surgeon being callous and objective."

Dr. Newman's interneship was drawing to a close. He stood at the threshold of the turning-point in his life — the beginning of his career as a full-fledged, practicing surgeon. 

The cold stone wall that in the course of years seemed to have arisen between Dr. Newman's world and the world 331

of his parents had not, after all, congealed the fatherly heart of Moses Newman. He broke through this wall and gazing wistfully at his pride and joy - his doctor, Moses remarked tremulously: "Willie, I thank the Lord that my eyes have lived to see it... Nineteen years have gone by since the day when I brought you to Public School and said to you: "This is the beginning of your road to becoming a doctor'. . . It's a pity that Mother didn't live to see it." "Mother wasn't as eager as you about having me become a doctor. She wouldn't have cared if I'd been a shopkeeper on 

Essex Street." "Yes, Willie - perhaps it's silly to say it but she was a little jealous of your medical career. She was sure that that was what separated you from us.” "Did she tell you that?" "Yes, especially when you didn't insist on her being taken to the hospital where you were working when she got sick." 

Willie reddened and replied: "I didn't insist on it for her sake. I thought that she'd feel better in a Jewish hospital among fellow-Jews." 

"As a mother she reacted altogether different, Willie. The night she was dying, she told me: 'It's a strange world, Moses

A sick mother, dying – has an only son, a doctor, in the same city, and yet so far away. thousands of miles away. The nearer he came to being a doctor the further he drifted from me.' After she said that, I telephoned you but you came too late." .. right away

I'll be on the surgical staff there, working with Professor Eugene Taylor." "It's a pity... Ach, what am I saying? It's wonderful! 

Working with Professor Taylor - what an accomplishment! 

What about an office? Anyway, you could still have your office on East Broadway, eh? You'll have great success with

Jewish patients, Willie. I can imagine how your sign will look on East Broadway. There'll be friends of mine around there wherever you turn. . . A shiny copper sign, ‘Dr. Wilfrid 

Newman, Surgeon' — not bad, eh?” "No, father, I've got different plans. I want to build up a clientele not from the Jewish East Side, but from the richer more aristocratic population in uptown New York. 

I'll open my office where most of the doctors at our hospital have theirs on Park Avenue.” 

"Park Avenue! Is that so! That's wonderful, Willie! Too far from East Broadway. 

Moses Newman fell silent. He was seized by a strange nostalgia. Two lonely tears glistened in his eyes and through their mist the face of his son seemed remote and blurred. He took an envelope from his breast-pocket, and swallowing the lump that rose in his throat, he handed it to Willie and whispered: "Good luck to you, my son. . . Park Avenue! Not bad, eh?”

After his father had left, Willie opened the envelope. It contained twenty-five one-hundred-dollar bills along with a piece of paper on which was inscribed in a flourishing hand, Dr. Wilfrid Newman, Surgeon.

With his father's money Dr. Newman furnished one of the finest offices on Park Avenue, and waited to receive his first patients. His excellent training under Professor Taylor, his proficiency in surgery, and his erudition in medical literature were the determining factors in his acceptance on the hos pital staff. At first, however, he did not have much of a private practice. His first patients were minor cases, such as an 333

ingrown toenail, an abscess, thrombosed hemorrhoids, varicose veins and similar minor conditions. Important surgical problems were still dealt with by the older staff surgeons, especially 

Dr. Taylor. 

But Dr. Newman was neither disillusioned nor discouraged by this. He attended to all of his private practice with the utmost seriousness regardless of how minor the case might be. 

Word of this reached Dr. Taylor, and he considered it a good quality on the part of a beginner in surgery. On one occasion, when they were engaged in a friendly chat, Dr. 

Taylor asked: "How is it going, Newman?" "Not bad, Dr. Taylor - not bad for someone who's just starting out in practice." "I'm glad to hear that, Newman. To tell the truth, I was a bit apprehensive before about your theory of 'callousness in surgery.' I've just heard that you've been visiting one of your patients who has varicose veins twice a day, and attending to him as if it were a case of a major abdominal operation. 

That's very complimentary, especially for a surgeon who believes in 'callousness. "Dr. Taylor, I appreciate your interest but permit me to be candid. My attention and devotion to my patient with varicose veins has no relation to sentimental feelings. I simply have no alternative. My patient happens to be a big shot on Wall Street and don't forget that I'm in the process of building up a practice. . . As businessmen say, "The customer must always be satisfied.' Believe me, Dr. Taylor, there's no sentimentality involved here." 

Dr. Taylor shook his head deploringly: 

"You're going to be a lonely man, Newman. You'll build up your practice – not because you're applying business psychology but because you're a highly skilled surgeon. However, you're in danger of sealing your life in loneliness." "What do you mean by 'loneliness?' 

"That, my friend, you'll find out for yourself."

Dr. Taylor was right. Newman became well aware of the meaning of loneliness. He had actually experienced it all 334

along. He felt it as a youngster in Public School and High 

School; he felt it later at the University, and as an interne in the hospital; and he felt it now too as a practicing surgeon. 

He had made every effort to conceal his loneliness, but little could be hidden from the keen penetrating eye of Dr. 

Taylor, and the fact that he had sized him up so accurately preyed on his mind. 

Yes indeed, he was a lonely man - lonely even as far as his romantic life was concerned. 

At first there was Mary Jefferson. It happened during his third year in High School. He and Mary had submitted the two best compositions on the topic "The Effects of the French Revolution upon World Literature." They had both been singled out to read their papers before the entire student body and this was the beginning of a close friendship between them. 

Mary Jefferson was sixteen years old. She had thick red hair, lively greenish eyes and a chiseled nose lightly powdered with freckles, that enhanced her charm. She was not too tall, and had a slender, conspicuously attractive figure. Interested in athletics, she participated in most of the school sports activities and was fully conscious of the flurry of admiring male glances she provoked whenever she appeared in her gym suit. To these she responded in her customary gay and flirtatious manner. "Mary, your composition was wonderful and you recited it magnificently," Willie complimented her, as they left the school together on the day they had read their papers. "Thanks, Willie. I liked your composition better. You quoted from more novels. It's obvious that you've read more than I have."

That same afternoon they went to the movies together. In the dark theatre he held her hand and she nestled close to him, leaning her head on his shoulder. A torrid wave of excitement welled in him as he drew her closer and gently pressed his lips to her forehead and her half-closed eyes. "Willie, my father goes to his club on Saturday afternoon," 335

she said to him on their way home. "My mother and my two younger brothers go to visit my grandmother in the Bronx - and I stay home all alone..

This was Wednesday, and the interim until Saturday seemed like an eternity to Willie. For the first time in his life he could not concentrate on his studies. His eyes would rove over the printed lines but his thoughts would wander elsewhere. He visualized Mary, and recalled how her thick hair had felt when it brushed his cheek and shoulder. His heart pounded as he recalled her appealing way of nestling against him, and he would close his eyes and relive each kiss he gave her.

At night he lay awake tossing and turning, listening to the clock tick off each hour, which seemed to drag on endlessly. In the morning he dashed off to school much earlier than usual. He watched her from a distance and felt consumed by a burning desire to be with her. 

At last Saturday came. With palpitating heart he mounted the few steps outside her home and rang the bell. The door opened; she took his hand and led him into her room. He trembled feverishly. "Don't be afraid, Willie," she reassured him. "We're alone. No one will disturb us." "Mary, I'm so happy I'm with you. I couldn't stand having to wait any longer." 

They fell into each other's arms, sealing their mouths together until they gasped for breath. "Willie - tell me, do you really love me?” "Yes, Mary, I love you more than anything in the world."

She stretched out on her bed, and clutching his head with both hands, hugged it to her breasts. Willie's youthful blood seethed in all the cells of his body. She let him fondle her. 

His black sparkling eyes blazed. His passion-parched lips mumbled incoherently in bewitched ecstasy: "Mary, I love you. You're mine. . . mine now and mine forever. 

Mary did not lose herself. Her well-ingrained Christian upbringing stood by her in this moment of temptation. "Willie dear, I love you but we mustn't. . . Anything - but 336

not that. • · I could become pregnant and that would ruin me. And what's worse, it would ruin my parents. Even you wouldn't love me any more then."

Willie satiated his desire by merely hugging and kissing her, and quenched his passion in her sweet boundless tenderness.

After a few Saturdays like this, Mary once asked him: "Willie, why don't you ever speak of your parents? Why don't you invite me to meet them? You've met my father, my mother, my brothers, and I've not only never seen your folks, but you hardly ever even mention their names. Why? What's the matter? Are you afraid that they wouldn't approve of me?" "I don't get along with my parents. We belong to different worlds." 

Mary stared at him in amazement. "What world do your parents belong to?" "They're Jews and their world is Jewishness and Jewish tradition. I'm an American! I can't and don't want to live in their world." "Do you want to convert?" "Convert? Nonsense! I don't want to exchange the synagogue for the church. America, my dear, is the greatest democracy in the world. To be an American is in itself a faith. I don't have to add a religious lining to it." "Well when you get married what will your children be -Jews or Christians?" "Americans." 

"If you're feeling sad, or are suddenly hit by some misfortune and you want to pray — what God will you turn to?”

Willie started to laugh and tried to embrace Mary, but she slipped from his grasp and said sternly: "Willie, you're a swell fellow, you're a good student, and you'll probably be a great doctor, but life is lonesome without 

God. Loneliness is a curse. No one should be afflicted with it." • • 

That was the end of Mary Jefferson. 

Then came Jean Foster.

He met Jean in the spring of 1923. It was during his 337

final examinations in his graduating year in medicine. 

Willie did most of his studying in the school library, sitting all day long in a corner near a window which was shadowed by the boughs of two tall, massive trees. He was barely visible behind the stack of books piled on his small table. He would be the first to show up the moment the library opened and the last to leave when it closed at ten o'clock in the evening. He would leave for only a few minutes during the day, to drop in at the cafeteria for a sandwich and a glass of milk. He was completely absorbed in his studies, oblivious to the outside world. The hundreds of students who wandered in and out of the library either to study or simply to borrow or return books, were so used to seeing that little table in the corner occupied by a certain tall, black-haired, serious student, that had he ever been away from this spot for long they would have wondered why. 

One day Newman was preparing himself for an examination in pathology. Of all his subjects, pathology was the one he paid most attention to. He did not plan to become a pathologist, but this was, nevertheless, a science that he was particularly anxious to master. "Gentlemen, pathology is the science which often makes geniuses in medicine feel very humble by showing them their mistakes." That was the way Professor Silver put it and he would proceed to tell about different cases in which surgeons had operated on organs that later turned out to be perfectly healthy when examined under the pathologist's microscope. "Yes, gentlemen," he would say humorously, “many surgeons would feel happier if the tissues they removed never got into the hands of the pathologist. Their mistakes would never be discovered." "Sir," Newman once asked him, "if a pathologist would also perform the surgery, do you think the mistakes would then be less?" "Of course they would be less! It's not necessary though, that pathologists should be surgeons! It would be better if surgeons would just understand pathology a little more; but unfortunately this is rare." "Why?" 338

"Because pathology requires a great deal of study, and a surgeon who spends too much time studying is threatened by one of two dangers: if he has acquired any private practice he stands to lose it, and if he doesn't have any patients he'll never get them!”

All the students, with the exception of Newman, burst out laughing. The latter, at that moment, made up his mind to study and excel in pathology. “And it won't interfere with my acquiring a private practice either," he decided.

This subject became a challenge to Newman, a sort of undeclared war which he was waging against the cynical remarks of Professor Silver. He pored over the various textbooks on pathology, not skipping a single word. "Excuse me, please," he suddenly heard someone murmur to him in a barely audible voice. "I'm sorry to disturb you, but I must speak with you." Newman raised his head, and found himself looking into the large, bright blue eyes of a girl of about twenty standing in front of him. Her hair was combed high in an upsweep, accentuating her broad high forehead. She had a fresh attractive appearance and her eyes were friendly and kind. A light-blue coat hung casually from her straight, slim shoulders. A bow of fluffy, light-blue chiffon rested in the deep neckline of her snow-white blouse. She exuded a youthful charm, as well as a pleasantly light fragrance of perfume. 

Newman was taken aback by the sudden appearance of this beautiful girl. Caught off guard, he stood up awkwardly not knowing what to do or say. "Please sit down," she said to him. "I'm sorry to interrupt you. I'm Miss Foster - Jean Foster. I need information on some item in pathology. The librarian told me that all the textbooks on this subject are on your table." 

Newman gradually regained his composure, and asked: "Are you a medical student, Miss Foster?” "No, I'm working for an attorney." "An attorney? What does that have to do with pathology?" 

Students began craning their necks to stare at Newman, surprised and annoyed. They were surprised to see this serious student usually so completely engrossed in his studies, ac- 339

tually taking time out to chat with a pretty girl, and they were also annoyed because his voice was disrupting the traditional quiet of the library. Sensing their scowls, Newman said apologetically: "Not here Miss Foster, we're disturbing the other readers. 

If you'd like, let's go into the cafeteria. We can talk there.' 

In the cafeteria, Newman ordered his usual - a cheese sandwich and a glass of milk. Jean Foster ordered a cup of coffee and insisted on paying for it herself. "I'm an independent woman," she joked, "I won't be supported by a man whose name I don't even know." "How stupid of me - it didn't occur to me. My name is 

Newman - Wilfrid Newman - friends call me Willie."

The two sat at a side table. Newman, who ordinarily devoured his sandwich quickly, could hardly open his mouth now. He was enchanted by Jean's candid charm, and couldn't take his eyes off her. "Why are you staring at me like that?" she asked. "It looks as if you don't know whether to bite into the sandwich first 

or me. 

Her outspoken insinuation excited Newman. Licking his lips with the tip of his tongue, he said without attempting to restrain his excitement: "Miss Foster, in this cloister-like atmosphere of study and bookshelves you're wonderfully refreshing, like the beautiful spring after a bleak winter." 

Jean looked him straight in the eyes. She caught the flashing sparks of his burning desire for her. The young handsome student appealed to her. Her female instinct told her that this casual meeting was not the end... and she was delighted. "I mustn't forget the purpose of my visit here,” she said. "It concerns a young boy of twenty who fell while he was at work and bruised his right knee. He has been undergoing medical treatment for a long time, and now the affected area seems to have developed tuberculosis. He's suing for a considerable amount of compensation, and we represent his employer. It's important for us to obtain precise medical information as to whether this accident at work could have caused tuberculosis. We think it quite likely that tuberculosis 340

was dormant in that area prior to the accident. 

Newman continued to stare longingly at her expressive face. "Willie, you hardly paid any attention to what I just said." She shook a reproving finger with feigned anger. "Thanks for calling me 'Willie.' Where do you live, Jean?" "Why do you ask?" "I'll be through with my exams in a week. I'll not only bring you the volume you need - I'll help you find the proper information."

Jean paused for a moment. She took hold of his right hand, raised his coat sleeve a bit, and wrote with a pencil on his shirt cuff: 318 Central Park West, Apartment 17. Then she got up and walked out without saying another word. 

Newman eyed the departing girl in astonishment. “What a strange girl!" he mused. "Strange indeed, and very enticing!"

Willie was not mistaken in his impression of Jean. During his first visit to her small, immaculate two-room apartment, her behavior was as unpredictable as it had been at their initial meeting. 

Before he had a chance to look about him, Jean snatched the volume of "Bones and Joints Pathology" from under his arm and flinging it unceremoniously into an armchair, declared: "I don't need that any more. The case has been settled out of court." Then she took hold of both Willie's hands, and gazing mischievously into his eyes, softly said: "Hello Willie- or is it Doctor Willie? I imagine that by now you've passed your exams with flying colors."

Newman did not reply because he was at a loss for words. 

The familiarity with which she welcomed him, and her appearance in a negligee aroused in him a storm of passion. 

She wore a flowered kimono of fine Chinese silk. A tightlydrawn sash at her waistline accentuated her small round hips. 

The gown's half-open lapels exposed the round contours of her breasts. The litheness of her slender legs was clearly visible through the thin transparent silk. 

Newman's head was in a whirl. Through the partially 341

opened curtain across the doorway leading to the bedroom, her bed seemed to invite him. He pulled Jean toward him and pressed his lips against hers. He lifted her like a child, carried her into the bedroom and slid down onto the bed with her. Jean wound her bare arms around his neck and with a blissful sigh yielded to him. . .

During those days, Newman was extremely happy. He had been so lucky: he had passed his examinations with high honors and had become a doctor; he was among the few new internes chosen to work under the supervision of the eminent surgeon, Eugene Taylor; and, in addition to all this, he had captured the love of a most wonderful girl! How beautiful she was! Beautiful and flaming with passion! True, a little eccentric but what a girl! . . . ... "Willie," she said to him after she had given herself to him for the first time, "now that both of us are calm and rational, we ought to get to know each other." "Jean dear, I can't understand you! We're here in bed together, and now that we belong to each other you talk about our getting to know each other!" 

"A hungry man can enjoy a good meal - does that necessarily mean that he knows the cook? You wanted me and I had a yen for you too, so we're two human beings following healthy animal instincts. I can assure you that your M.D. degree and my finishing-school training didn't have much to do with it."

Newman turned around, and leaning on an elbow, gazed at her in great surprise: "Who are you, Jean?" "That's exactly what I mean! You've had a satisfying experience with me but now your super-ego is clamoring to know: 'Who the devil is she? What am I doing here? Where will this lead me?' That's why I said that it's time we got to know each other.”

She ran her thin fingers caressingly over his thick black hair. He bent his face close to hers and showered kisses on her forehead, brow, eyes, the tip of her nose and her lips. "Jean dear, who are you?" 342

"I'm Jean Foster, twenty years and eleven months old the only daughter of Winston Foster, prominent attorney.

My parents were divorced when I was a child. Sometimes I lived with my father, at times with my mother, but mostly with strangers. I attended the best private schools, but I always felt terribly alone. . . My mother remarried, and she's very happy with her second husband. My father, on the other hand, remained unattached- I doubt if he'll ever remarry.

He's a true lady-killer. They're always buzzing around him, and he gets whatever he wants from them whenever he feels like it. What a man! Eleven months ago I graduated from finishing school. Then I was stuck with the problem of what to do next. I didn't care to stay with my mother — her second husband is too attractive and I'd probably fall in love with him. Staying with my father was out of the question. He brings girls younger than myself to his bedroom. 

As you've gathered, I'm no saint but even for me a home like my father's is a little disgusting, so I decided to be independent. Yes sir — independent in every sense of the word. 

First I got a job. Abiding by the principle of not mixing business with pleasure, I chose an old impotent harmless attorney as my boss and became his private secretary. That, my dear, is the story of Jean Foster!" 

"What about love affairs? You haven't said a word about that. With your temperament my darling, you must have been devouring men while still in your cradle!" "Willie!" she laughed rapturously, flinging her arms around him and planting a kiss on his lips. "For a compliment like that you deserve my kingdom! Yes, love affairs - my dear, sweet love affairs!" "You speak as though you've had them by the hundreds. 

Tell me sweetheart, how do I rate on the list?" "Don't be so conceited! Whoever told you that you're included in my love affairs? It so happens that the men who had me most were not the ones I loved the most.” "Do you mean to tell me that you can do what you did without loving?" "Don't make me laugh! Do you really believe that only loving couples go in for sex orgies? How silly you are, my 343

handsome doctor! Anyway I do love you a little. You look so much like my mother's second husband." 

Newman felt particularly delighted when, after several weeks of their "friendship," Jean took him along on a visit to her father. Winston Foster was in his middle forties but looked much younger. He was tall, blonde, with fine features and youthful sprightliness. Judging by his scrupulous fastidiousness about both his person and his luxurious apartment it was obvious that he valued physical pleasures highly. 

Newman was greatly impressed by the relationship between Jean and Winston Foster. Theirs did not seem to be a relationship between father and daughter but rather between two close friends. "My baby!" Winston exclaimed at the sight of Jean, and clasped her in his arms. "You are so breathtakingly beautiful, that if I weren't your father I would be kneeling at your feet like a lovelorn suitor." "Winston dear," she remarked goodhumoredly, "you're the biggest liar in the world - you say that to all the girls. But 

I love you all the same. My God! Your brazen remarks have made me forget elementary politeness. I want you to meet 

Dr. Wilfrid Newman, a future genius in surgery!" "I'm very happy to know you, Doctor," Winston Foster said amiably shaking Newman's hand. “Where are you practicing? Where is your office?" "I'm not practicing yet; I've just graduated from Med

School. It will take a while until I'll practice. I'm just starting my interneship under Dr. Taylor." "Eugene Taylor? Wonderful! We're old friends. Eugene is an idealist. He regards medicine as a labor of love and self-sacrifice." "Yes I know; he can afford to. He doesn't have to earn a livelihood from it. When a man has inherited millions it's not hard to be an idealist." "Jean baby," Winston Foster said enthusiastically, "your friend is a very clever young man; I like him. Dr. Newman, I congratulate you! You're a practical man and you'll be al- 344

right. I can vouch for that!" 

On their way home, Jean said to Newman: "Willie, you made a great impression on my father." "Do you think so? I'm very pleased. I liked him too.

No wonder he's such a successful lawyer. He's got such a magnetic personality that he could easily bewitch a jury.”

Newman felt elated. Neither Jean nor her father had made any issue of his religion. In fact, Jean had never even asked him who his parents were. Winston Foster had received and treated him like an equal. "Yes sir," Newman assured himself, "it's good to be a native American!"

Shortly after that Jean took Newman to visit her mother. 

The latter likewise appeared much younger than her forty years. Newman was struck by the great resemblance between mother and daughter; they could have been mistaken for twin sisters. The relationship between these two, however, was more restrained and formal than it had seemed to be between father and daughter. By way of embracing one another, they offered each other a rather frigid cheek, and 

Jean remarked: "Mother dear, this is a good friend of mine - Dr. Wilfrid 

Newman. Willie, this is my mother, Mrs. Philip Graham. 

Mother, don't you think that he looks like Philip? Speaking of Philip — where is he? I haven't seen him in quite a while." 

"He's at a church meeting. Philip is chairman of the church building committee. They've undertaken a project to build a big recreation hall for church social functions." Turning to Newman, she continued: "My husband is an architect but he loves to devote a little time to welfare activities. The building committee simply insisted that he be their chairman." "Mother," Jean interrupted, "I doubt whether Philip's church activities are of any interest to Willie." "I beg your pardon, Dr. Newman - I didn't mean to bore you." "Not at all Mrs. Graham, you're not boring me. On the contrary, it's quite interesting and I admire your husband for being able to devote some time to the church." 345

"Willie!" Jean exclaimed, amazed, "I've just discovered a virtue in you that I never noticed before. You're a diplomat! 

Mother dear - Dr. Newman is Jewish." "Really?" Mrs. Graham said, unable to hide her embarrassment. "Well, what's the difference? Jews are human beings too... We belong to the church and the Jews belong to the synagogue - they're not too different are they, Dr. Newman?" 

Newman blushed. Resentment raged in him, and he felt a choking sensation in his throat. "Pardon me Mrs. Graham, but I don't consider myself an expert on religious matters."

On their way home Newman did not utter a word. Jean could not understand his changed mood. "What's the matter with you, Willie - why so silent?" she asked finally. "Jean, I love you, and if my love means anything to you 

I want you to promise me that you'll never think of me as a Jew." "Willie dear, you speak in riddles. I don't understand you. 

Aren't you a Jew?" "Not by choice. my parents are Jews." "This is incredible! What's wrong with a person being what he is? I'm a Christian, not by choice either, but just because my parents are Christians, yet I don't see why I should feel hurt by that. I am what I am!” "And I don't want to identify myself with Jewishness!" 

Newman retorted angrily. "I'm an American! The United 

States of America is my religion!"

Both fell silent again. At the entrance to her apartment, Jean stretched out her hand to Newman and said: "Good night, Willie. It was nice knowing you." 

Newman, puzzled, regarded her and asked: "What are you up to, Jean?" "It's very simple, dear. I got to love you more and more every day because I admired your strength. I'm repelled by weakness in a man; I can't love a weakling - I can only pity him... And tonight I suddenly began to feel a great pity for you." 

That was the end of Jean Foster. 346

And now, Ruth MacDonald. 

He had loved Mary Jefferson with the first glow of his sexual awakening; he had loved Jean Foster with all the fire of his youth; but he loved Ruth as he had never loved before. He loved her with all the vigor of his mature thirty-two years. He loved her with the practical view of an adult. In her, he saw the realization of his dream about an ideal wife. She was beautiful; she was an excellent nurse, and one who could properly appreciate the professional and social life of a surgeon. She would also be an ideal mother for his children. She was intelligent, accurate, authoritative, and had a high sense of responsibility. 

In addition she possessed a quality that he considered most important - like himself, she avoided talking about her home and her past, and like himself, led a life apparently devoid of any attachments to parental traditions.

Right now however, Newman's love was one-sided. Ruth had evinced no signs whatever that she reciprocated his feelings. He felt as though he were pushing against an impregnable wall each time he tried to become intimate with her. 

She was not entirely indifferent to him - he sensed that with certainty. But why did she remain so cool and aloof? 

Did she have another love? Was it Dr. Taylor by any chance? 

That was really hardly possible. Dr. Taylor was too fond of his wife, and idealized the integrity of his home and family too much. Besides which an illicit love affair would not fit in with Dr. Taylor's ethical standards. He even practiced surgery with a priestly approach. 

And yet, how she idolized him! Her eyes would light up at the mere sight of him. Whenever he entered the operating room she served him with almost religious awe. Why? The question preyed on Newman's mind incessantly. 

He recalled the evening when he had told Ruth about his love for her. They had dined at "Alfonso's," a cozy Italian restaurant in a basement-room illuminated by dim purple lights, where the guests, mostly couples, were seated around small tables with white cloths, nibbling in a leisurely way on highly-seasoned foods and sipping red wine which suffused bodies and minds with a tired sweet warmth.

It was a pleasant evening early in May. The New York 347

streets looked cleaner, brighter and livelier than usual to

Newman. The throngs of people walking seemed especially near and friendly. He was in high spirits and sensed a spring freshness in the turbulent scene about him. 

Elated, he walked arm in arm with Ruth, glancing at her face every moment. In his eyes she looked like a queen! He was proud of her majestic beauty and wanted the entire world to notice and envy him. 

Ruth lived in a small modest apartment on Sixty-Fifth Street. Newman had visited her several times before. She would always prepare coffee, put a classical record on the phonograph and they would spend a nice, quiet evening together. Around ten o'clock Ruth would usually say, “Dr. 

Newman, it's really been a very pleasant evening. Thank you very much. I hope we'll do it again, but we both have a heavy schedule in the operating room tomorrow. Thanks again. Good night.”

On several occasions, when he was taking leave of her at the door Newman had leaned towards her to kiss her. But she would gently push him aside and blushingly say, “Please, Dr. Newman, let's keep our friendship as it is. I appreciate these evenings with you very much. Let's not spoil them." 

On that evening, however, Newman changed the customary routine of their friendly, tranquil evenings. No sooner had they entered her apartment than he clasped her in his arms and sealed her lips in a long passionate kiss. She neither resisted nor responded. "Ruth darling," he said, with his arms still around her, “I love you! I'm sure you've known it all along. I love you deeply and want you to become my wife.” 

Ruth did not answer. Her big blue eyes suddenly filled, and the tears began to trickle down over the fine features of her beautiful face.

Newman was flabbergasted. He had never imagined that this stern nurse could break down and give way to tears. He could hardly believe it and his fingers touched the wetness of her cheeks. "Ruth dear," he exclaimed, "those are real tears! You're crying! Are these tears of love and happiness? Say that you 348

love me. Say that you'll be my wife.”

He wanted to kiss her again but she pushed him away, and wiping her tears, said with an unnatural forced smile: 

"I'm sorry I didn't want to cry but I simply couldn't help it... I know I should be very happy; after all, it's the dream of every girl to be loved, to marry, to be a wife - a mother... But much to my regret my heart just isn't in it yet." 

Newman was dejected. Ruth appeared to him even more fascinating now than before and he longed for her with all his heart and soul. "Why, Ruth, why?" he pleaded. "I love you. We could be so happy together... Tell me Ruth, is there someone else?"

Ruth took hold of his head with both hands, gazed into his eyes, kissed his forehead. "Thank you for wanting me. . . If you really love me, bear with me and wait." 

Newman loved her - and waited.

A jolly voice with its typical Yiddish intonation resounded behind the door of Dr. Newman's office. 

"I'm back again, Miss Laura! Is the doctor already in his office? Yes? Fine. I think I'll go in right now - before the patients start coming." "Surely, by all means, Mr. Newman. Your son is expecting you; told him that you had been here." "Thank you 

Miss Laura - many thanks. You're a dear girl, and may God bless you with a fine husband some day. 

Meanwhile, before God's blessing comes to pass I've brought you a chocolate bar. Eat it in good health." "How nice of you Mr. Newman. Thank you. Thank you very much. You're spoiling me — you're always bringing me candy. Why do you do it, Mr. Newman?" 

"Because you're a very kind-hearted nurse and because you always laugh at my jokes.” "I laugh because they're really funny - so funny that even when I'm riding home on the subway, I start laughing so loud that people think I've gone off my rocker." "Good, good. Do you know, Miss Laura - the greatest 349

Yiddish humorist, Sholem Aleichem, used to say. 'Laughing is healthy; doctors prescribe laughter.' By the way - I'll tell you a joke you'll like; it's about a doctor." "Please do." "A man came home from a visit to a doctor. 'Well what did the doctor say?' his wife asked him. 'He said that I should visit him once a week for a long time.' 'Is that so?' his wife asked wondering. 'What does he think you've got?' 'I don't know; he probably thinks I've got money.'" 

Laura burst out laughing: "Mr. Newman, you're a riot! The way you tell a joke - a person can't help laughing." 

The sharp sound of the buzzer interrupted her laughter.

This was Dr. Newman's signal that he was ready to receive. 

Laura could tell by the unusually loud prolonged buzz that today her employer felt irritable and impatient. Her voice quickly changed, and she said in a serious tone: “All right 

Mr. Newman, the doctor will see you now." And Moses entered his son's office. "Hello, Willie. Miss Laura is a fine, lovely girl. How are you doing, son? You look tired. Hard operations?" "Father," Dr. Newman began, looking down, “I've asked you many times not to turn the office into a place of social and family entertainment. I'd much rather you telephoned me at my apartment whenever you want to talk to me. This is my office where I receive only patients. I'm against your familiarity with the nurse, too, and I don't want you to tell her your Jewish jokes. It undermines the decorum that I have established here." "The what?" "Decorum!" "What's that?" "Good manners, quiet behavior, cleanliness and disciplined restraint - that's decorum! It's something that the East Side doesn't have!" "Tell me, Willie, an appendix in someone with — what do you call it? — decorum — is it really different from one in somebody who has no decorum?" 

350

"You're joking and I'm serious! I ran away from the East

Side and I don't intend to return there, or encourage its invasion here."

Moses remained silent, staring at his son who still looked downward. Then he replied: "Willie, my son, it's not from the East Side that you ran away but from your parents and your background. I don't know why... It could be that it was my fault. . . Maybe I didn't give you enough of the will to be a Jew. Yes, it's probably my fault. In my pride, I was too set on making a doctor out of you come what may. 'East Side' is just an excuse you use. It's not the East Side that you despise, but me, your Jewish father with his singsong. 

Willie, I can forgive you for that because you're my son; but the world is stern and won't forgive you." 

"Of what interest is my Jewishness or my background to the world? The world has more important problems to attend to." "That's where your mistake is! Each person creates around himself his own world in which he spends his years. What sort of a world can you have without a sense of belonging with no roots and no tradition? Why, you're neither here nor there-like an outcast. . .' 

99 “I'm a surgeon, and in the professional world I'm not an outcast. There my roots are deep and strong." "I doubt it!" "You don't need to doubt it; the proof is in the type of patients I have. My best patients are non-Jews. It may interest you to know that just this morning I operated on 

Milton Gleason of Gleason Construction, Inc."

Moses Newman stood up and walked slowly toward the door. "Father," his son called out, "you haven't told me what you came here for." "It's not important," Moses answered, standing by the door. "Tomorrow is the anniversary of your mother's death. And

I also wanted to know how the operation went on Mr. Gleason." 351

-- "You and Mr. Milton Gleason? How do you come to know 

Mr. Gleason? Why do you ask?" 

Moses smiled significantly. "His father, Mr. Lazarus Glickman, is my best friend. We pray in the same synagogue on the East Side. . .” 352

The Choice 

David Shanan was off duty that evening. He changed into his street clothes and strolled along the hospital's long subterranean corridor, leading to a side exit. At the door he was stopped by the door-man, who said: "Pardon me, Dr. Shanan. I've just received a phone call that Dr. Taylor wants to see you before you leave." "Thank you. Where is Dr. Taylor now? Did he mention where he wants to see me?" "Yes, Dr. Shanan. He's waiting for you in his office in the surgical pavilion." 

David's heart began to beat faster. "What could have happened?" he wondered. "Have I done something wrong? Is it about my work at the hospital?” 

He tapped on Dr. Taylor's door. "Come in. Ah, Shanan, I'm very glad that you came. I'm sorry I know it's your free evening and after being cooped up behind hospital walls for a whole week one is anxious to visit his family." "It's all right, Sir. My wife realizes by now what it means to be married to a doctor. She's really wonderful! She often says "I'm that much happier when I see you because I know that all the hours I've been waiting for you, you've been relieving the sick.'" "You're a very lucky man, Shanan. Well, sit down, It's you that I want to talk about." "Have I done something wrong?" "No no, on the contrary; your work is excellent. Tell me 

Shanan, what do you want to do in the future?” 353

"I want to heal the sick." "Well yes of course; we all want to do that. But that isn't what I had in mind. The progress of medicine is gigantic today. There is no lack of practicing physicians and surgeons. 

Research men that's what we lack! Those with the most capable minds among the new generation of doctors are being encouraged to go in for research." 

"I? Research? In what field?" "In what field? All fields are open! Practically untouched!

There isn't a branch in medicine where research is not essential! Take a subject as popular as high blood pressure is today. Why, everyone talks about it and yet medicine knows nothing about it. Right now there isn't a physician in the world who understands the causes of high blood pressure. 

No one knows what diabetes is. It's known that the diabetic has sugar in his urine and that he has an abnormally high concentration of sugar in his blood - but why? Where does diabetes originate? You won't find anyone who can answer that. And as for cancer - there we're completely in the dark. 

Yes, my friend, with the exception of a few infectious diseases we know nothing! We don't know the causes of diseases of the heart, kidneys, liver, spleen, stomach, intestines, or glands, and we most certainly know nothing about diseases of the mind! All this ignorance depresses me... We can't go on like this much longer. Medicine needs more scientists and research men. It is through scientific research that we'll uncover the hidden causes of diseases. For me it's somewhat late; but for you, Shanan, these horizons are wide-open and bright. You're young, willing and you love medicine."

David sat listening attentively to his teacher's revealing words. "Dr. Taylor," he replied quietly. "You've just convinced me that without a doubt research is not for me. My place is with the sick." "Not for you? Why?" "Because I don't want to be a discoverer. I want to be the kind of physician who properly applies the discoveries of others for the well-being of the patient." 354

"Through research, however, you could help not only the individual patient but suffering humanity at large." "Sir, I'm not denying that research is of the utmost importance. It's certainly the only means for extending medical knowledge. All the same, I can't leave the sick! It's just because we do know so little that I feel the patient needs us so much." "Shanan, you know that the trend in medicine today particularly of your generation, is research, and all forms of modernization. With your obsolete attitude you are running the risk of being brutally cast aside by your own contemporaries." "Dr. Taylor, according to Jewish tradition the doctor is 

God's emissary. When I was a child my mother used to tell me: 'A doctor is always accompanied by Angel Raphael'. . .

The name is significant: Rapha-el, the term in Hebrew stands for 'Heal me, O Lord.' I feel and even dare to believe, that as long as a child finds repose and happiness in the warmth of its mother's bosom, as long as love and sympathy command respect and merit existence in this world, and as long as man has God in his heart then anyone who brings relief and comfort to the suffering will never be cast aside as 'obsolete'."

Dr. Taylor and David looked at one another in silence. 

Their eyes reflected profound mutual understanding between teacher and pupil. On his way home David felt light-hearted and happy. He strode along proudly, head erect, shoulders thrown back as though the whole world belonged to him. Descending the soiled stairs leading to the subway, he had the feeling that he was not going down underground but was ascending to open brightness. The crowds of tired perspiring people thronging the speeding subway trains appeared to him like a huge, beloved family of kinsmen. An elderly Italian woman lugging a heavy basket of bargains stood swaying over him unsteadily, clutching one of the car straps. David arose and helped her sit down. She looked at him first with astonish- 355

ment and suspicion, then with gratitude. He acknowledged her look with a friendly smile and felt very happy. 

His joy stemmed from the outcome of his conversation with Dr. Taylor. It was the first time since he had become an interne under the supervision of that great man and surgeon that he had not followed in a line of action proposed by his teacher. And remarkably, Taylor had not resented it.

On the contrary, it seemed to David that the surgeon had actually been pleased with his reply, even though he had not said so. He saw it in his eyes. Yes, Dr. Taylor was pleased because he, too, had made the same choice. After all, research was not a special fad of 1930; scientists had been engaged in it right along and during Dr. Taylor's interneship too. Why hadn't Taylor devoted his life to research? Certainly not because he had wanted to earn more money as a practicing physician - he had been the only son of a New York multi-millionaire. He hadn't gone into research for the same reasons. For him, too, it was the patient who was most important and he could not leave the bedside of the sick.

David suddenly remembered the recent case of the homeless Negro who had had a strangulated hernia with acute intestinal obstruction. If Dr. Newman had performed his excellent operation earlier - before doing his non-emergency surgery on the millionaire Gleason - then the patient might have been saved.

David remembered also how on the night after the operation he had not left the patient's bedside for a moment. But the man's intestines had remained twisted too long in the strangulated hernia. Gangrene had set in and the surrounding tissue had quickly become necrotized. There isn't a deadlier poison than the one emanating from necrotic tissues in one's body. With his fine surgical technique Dr. Newman had managed to remove the gangrenous part of the intestines but the patient could not survive. The deep poisoning had thrown him into shock, then into a coma from which he never came out.

It had been around 4 o'clock in the morning when he had come out of Ward C where he had just drawn the sheet over 356

the face of the deceased patient. He had stumbled into Dr. Taylor. "Shanan, are you on duty now? It's good that I met you," 

Dr. Taylor had exclaimed in high spirits. "Come along with me to Ward A-I want to show you something."

In Ward A a boy of about seven lay on a bed, under an oxygen tent. He was a pale, delicate-looking youngster, and his closed eyelids were blue and swollen. He was breathing heavily - not through his mouth or nostrils but through a rubber tube projecting from a bandaged slit in his throat. 

"A very interesting case, Shanan. This child was in good health yesterday and went to school as usual. In the evening, his mother noticed that he didn't eat his supper and that he wasn't as jolly and playful as he ordinarily was. She felt his forehead, thought he had a little fever was getting a cold perhaps - so she gave him an aspirin and put him to bed. At midnight she was awakened by the sound of the boy gasping for air, with a wheezing noise in his chest. This kid's mother is a poor widow with four young children. She couldn't afford to call in a doctor at that late hour so she grabbed the child and brought him to our emergency clinic. 

The nurse in charge summoned the interne who was on duty then. During the fifteen or twenty minutes before the interne arrived the boy's condition deteriorated so much that he couldn't breathe and was turning blue from suffocation. The interne became alarmed thank God for thatand phoned me right away. When I heard his report I ordered the patient removed to the operating room and preparations rushed for a tracheotomy. Every minute was vitally important. It was obvious that if the trachea weren't slit open very quickly and a tube inserted into the bronchi through which air could come into the lungs, the patient would suffocate." 

"What's the diagnosis sir?" David inquired. "The diagnosis is angio-neurotic edema. It's a sudden massive swelling that results from the bodily reaction of some individuals to certain substances that they're sensitive to. As you are aware, it is commonly called allergy. One person can be allergic to certain foods or liquids, another to certain 357

odors, and a third, to contact with certain materials. The reactions to these sensitivities manifest themselves in various forms: one gets an itchy rash all over his body, another might develop a catarrh in the nose, as it is in the case of hay fever, and a third one, an asthmatic attack. There are still others whose reaction manifests itself in the form of a rapid massive swelling of various bodily tissues, and which we call angioneurotic edema. When the swelling occurs in the throat, the flow of air into the lungs is cut off and the sufferer simply becomes asphyxiated. In this case, the boy apparently was allergic to aspirin. He was saved by the tracheotomy; he'll be all right now." "That's wonderful, sir. I envy you. I wish I could feel as proud and pleased as you are now. I've just lost a patient." "That's a pity! I'm sorry. I know how a physician feels when a patient of his passes away but that can't be helped. 

A doctor mustn't let himself become discouraged. What sort of case was it?" "A strangulated hernia with acute intestinal obstruction." "Really? That's another example of how surgery can save a human life if it is only performed in time. Unfortunately we're often called when it's too late - then we're helpless. 

This must have been the case with your patient." 

David lowered his eyes in silence. "Shanan," Dr. Taylor went on, "as long as the patient receives from the doctor whatever treatment medical ethics requires a physician to render, there's no reason to feel disheartened," "Dr. Taylor, what does medical ethics consist of?" David asked hesitantly. "It consists of the absolute conviction that the quintessence of medicine is the patient!"

David suddenly thought of Reb Itzikel. How similar these two teachers of his were! His close contact with patients in the hospital convinced him more and more of the profound truth of Reb Itzikel's words: "Medicine may be a science, but the doctor must be more than a scientist - he must possess soulfulness. 358

David did not forget those simple words for a single minute and always gave the sick not only his time, knowledge and effort, but also his soulfulness. He was quite convinced now that healing the sick was not an ordinary, commonplace task - it was the fulfillment of a divine mission. 

Often at night as he sat near his patient, in the mystic hospital hush there would flit through his mind his mother's words: "God summons the Angel Raphael and commands him 'Descend on the earth, locate the sick person, and make him well.'"

The deafening scraping of wheels abruptly coming to a stop on steel rails broke David's trend of thought. The subway train halted, its doors opened with a squeak and throngs of people poured onto the platform, and headed, pushing and jostling, towards the street exits or towards other trains that would speed them home. 

David too, got out of the car. In this human mass, no one looked at him and no one cared about him — he was a mere drop in the ocean. This mattered little to David. His heart was filled with joy because he now found himself where he so often longed to be. He felt as if the huge Canal Street signs on the station walls welcomed him with friendly intimacy. David loved Manhattan's lower East Side, but above all he loved Canal Street. 

Despite his progress in the hospital and his acceptance by socially elite members of the American professional circle, he had not renounced his identity as a Jew. Neither did he abandon the study of the Torah. At every opportunity he would betake himself joyfully to the House of Study on Canal Street, and with his father-in-law Reb Zechariah, whom he always addressed as "Teacher," he would zealously pore over a Talmudic tract. Often when he was off duty he would go there from the hospital. Esther would be waiting there for him, and just as during his childhood years in Struga his mother would listen attentively to his father's chanting, his wife now listened lovingly to his voice of Torah. . .

This evening too, Esther was waiting for David on Canal 359

Street. It was already late and he had not yet shown up. 

Although she was used to his tardiness and surmised that he must have been detained because of some hospital emergency, this time however, she felt tense and jittery and was especially anxious for his arrival.

She had not been feeling well lately - was having frequent dizzy spells, had lost her appetite, often felt nauseated and vomited a lot. She had not complained to David about these symptoms, thinking: "He has more than his share of sick people in the hospital, let him at least be free of sighs and groans at home.”

She also noticed that her usually regular menstrual period was two weeks overdue. She sensed a strange tightening around her heart, and her breasts felt strangely heavy. She became worried and decided to see a doctor on her own. Yes, she would do it today before going to her parents' home to meet David.

She had left work a couple of hours earlier and had gone to a doctor in the Bronx, away from David's own medical sphere. Using an assumed name, she underwent a medical examination. "Are you married?" the doctor had asked her with some concern. "Yes." "That's good," he said smiling broadly. "Go home then and tell your husband the good news that you're going to have a baby."

Esther put her hands instinctively on her stomach, as though anxious to protect the little creature that was in her womb. 

"A child! David's child our child!" she mused in her rapture, feeling at the same time as though all the wellsprings of love and compassion coursing through her were now moving towards that part of her body where her child was mysteriously present.

The moment she entered her parents' home they noticed the change in her. "What's happened, Esther?" her father asked in alarm. "Has something happened to David? Have you two had a 360

quarrel? Why do you look so thin? I've never seen you like this.. 99 Esther did not reply, as though she had not heard her father's solicitous questions. She was still absorbed in the wonderful revelation that the Bronx doctor had made to her. Her dream-haunted eyes gazed into the distance.

Esther's appearance made a totally different impression upon her mother. She did not need a medical report to recognize her daughter's pregnancy. She sensed it with the natural perceptiveness of woman and mother and reacted as always with tears. 

"Esther darling - my little girl," she said, weeping. “Maybe you should move in with us until you give birth, God willing. There, among those uptown people, you're so lonely. 

David is in the hospital and you're left alone day and night. 

Why there's no one to even give you a glass of water." "No, mother dear, my place is in my own home with David," 

Esther answered placidly, as she embraced her mother. “I've always wanted it that way and now even more than before. 

Mother, father - please don't breathe a word about this to 

David. I'll tell him myself as soon as we get home." 

David's familiar footsteps sounded on the stairs. Esther joyfully ran to welcome him. "I'm rather late, my darling," David said, kissing her forehead. "You must have been worried. Have you been waiting long?" "It doesn't matter. Your work is more important than my waiting. Did you have an emergency?” "No, I'm not late because of a patient today." "Not because of a patient?" Esther asked, looking at him quizzically. “Did something else happen?" "Dr. Taylor wanted to talk to me." "About what?" "He suggested that I take up research. You know the field of research in medicine is practically untouched. A lot of serious problems in medicine have not been solved yet. 

Doctors are groping in the dark in their treatment of the sick. Our worst enemies - cancer, tuberculosis, heart disease, diabetes, polio, all kinds of infections - are still unknown 361

to medicine. Through research we could find out the causes of these diseases and uproot them. Yes, my dear, the progress of medicine lies in research!" "Do you intend to accept his suggestion?" Esther asked doubtfully.

David took hold of her hands, and gazed into her eyes: "No I rejected Dr. Taylor's suggestion."

Drawing a deep breath of relief, Esther said: "That's good medical research is not for you." "Why not?" David teased. "Because your ambition was always to be with the sick. 

To squat in a laboratory and experiment with dogs, monkeys, cats and mice, is more important maybe for medical science, but you weren't born for that."

David raised the palms of Esther's hands to his lips and kissed them tenderly, grateful for her deep understanding of his true feelings. 362

With God In His Heart 

Thomas, the old Negro servant in the Taylor household, had lived to experience the great joy of his life: his sister's son, Jerome Peterson, had graduated from Medical School. 

Thomas had hoped that when the happy time came Jerome would be accepted as an interne in the hospital where his master, the eminent Dr. Eugene Taylor, was surgeon-in-chief. 

He had voiced his hopes to his employer on several occasions and Dr. Taylor had promised him that if Jerome was a good student and distinguished himself in his studies he would help him to obtain this interneship. 

Jerome Peterson was a very good student and Dr. Taylor kept his promise and accepted him as an interne in his surgical department. This resulted in a stormy outburst of protest and resistance on the part of the other internes a resistance which nearly erupted into a public demonstration of racial discrimination. Fortunately, however, the incident was confined to the group behind the hospital walls. 

On the afternoon when Jerome Peterson's hospital appointment became known, the internes' quarters were filled with the tumult of loud, challenging voices. "Quiet! Quiet!" Alfred Mueller banging his fist on the table tried to outshout the others. "This ruckus will get us nowhere! We've got to solve an important problem and we have to do something drastic about it! The main thing is - we've got to stand united if we want to have our way!”

He spoke with bombastic fervor, tossing his head, with its striking silver-blonde hair. His greenish-blue eyes were aglow 

363

with excitement, and his thin lips closed tightly after each staccato-like phrase. "Speaking for myself," he raved. "I protest vehemently against our being forced to live together and work together with a nigger! This must be the attitude of each one of us! 

If Dr. Taylor has sentimental obligations towards his Negro servant and wants to favor him and help his nephew, let him fulfill these obligations some other way, not at our expense! He has no moral right to foist this nigger on us as a roommate and co-worker. I propose, therefore, that we all sign a petition to Dr. Taylor to the effect that for vital personal reasons we, the internes of the surgical department, categorically refuse to accept in our midst the newly-appointed interne, Dr. Jerome Peterson!" "What if Dr. Taylor rejects our petition?" Robert Young

III asked. "We all know how fanatically idealistic he is and how deeply he's absorbed in the hospital activities. What does he care about race or color? I can bet that he doesn't know who's white or colored around him when he's working.” "That's right! That's right!" the others exclaimed in unison. "What if he rejects our petition? How will we look if he just doesn't pay any attention to us?" "We'll fight him!" Mueller fulminated. "We'll turn to the Board of Directors! No one can force us to give up the social traditions in which we were brought up! As long as we stick together and make our demands in unison, we'll have our way! Let each one of us give his candid opinion about this right here and now! Robert, do you mean to tell me that you'd be intimidated by the possibility that Dr. Taylor might reject our petition? Where is the blood of the Robert Young dynasty and tradition? Do you think that your father, Robert

Young II, would submit to being forced to live and work with a Negro without challenging such social degradation?" "Hell no," Robert Young parried. "As far as that goes, I don't want this colored fellow any more than you do. 

Who needs him? The whole atmosphere will turn dark around here. I'm just trying to suggest that Dr. Taylor isn't the kind of guy who'd get scared at the sight of a petition. 

We've got to be prepared for any eventuality. . . As to whether 364

I'm against this darky being one of us- of course I am! 

And that goes for my roommate Julius, too."

Emboldened by this victory Mueller's eyes blazed even brighter. "Everyone's got to be against him," Julius Klein called out. "Most of the patients wouldn't want to have any contact with him and we'd have to do his share of the work. 

Aside from Dr. Taylor - who I think is a bit cracked there isn't another surgeon on the staff who'd want to be assisted by a Negro. We don't need any petitions. Let's go in to see Dr. Taylor and put our cards on the table - either the Negro or us! If he should side with the Negro, let him. 

We'll strike!" "Bill," Mueller exclaimed, addressing his roommate, William Ross, “I know how you feel about this because we've discussed it before, but I'd like you to declare your stand openly." "There's nothing much to say," William Ross stated calmly. "At home we never mingle with the colored. I come from generations of doctors and we've always lived in Washington, but we've never had any social contact with Negroes. My father has special office hours and a special waiting room for colored patients. It's a free country and no one can compel us to mingle with colored people. I join the protest and am ready to fight for my rights!"

Mueller felt triumphant. In his excitement his distended nostrils twitched a little and he shouted: "Sparks, it's your turn. What's your opinion?”

Peter Sparks, tall, wistful, a pastor's son who had almost become a pastor himself, shrugged his shoulders and replied in his Southern drawl: "I was born and raised in Atlanta, Georgia - this fact in itself is a sufficient answer... We've got nothing against the 

Negroes. They live their lives and we live ours. They don't expect us to associate much with them; they're quite happy among their own kind. How can a Negro be here with us? 

Who'd be willing to share a room with him? Why I couldn't undress in front of a Negro! Dr. Taylor is a clever man he'll understand. I don't think we'll have much difficulty 365

with him. His Negro servant asked him to do something for his nephew so he was obliging about it, not realizing what a predicament that would put us in. He'll understand. I'm sure he himself will send this Negro away; he'll find a spot for him somewhere else. · · "You sure have a lot of confidence in Dr. Taylor's common sense," Mueller chided, "but we don't! What would your attitude be in case Taylor is stubborn and forces this nigger on us? We've got to come to an agreement as to what to do then." 

Peter Sparks found himself on the horns of a dilemma. Clearing his throat, he answered hesitantly: “Well, I'm afraid we won't have unity on any decision we make. We've forgotten about my roommate, Shanan. He had to attend to an emergency appendectomy, so he couldn't come to this meeting, but I can assure you that what he thinks about this isn't at all in keeping with our attitude. Believe me, it's no cinch to answer his arguments." 

Mueller flushed with anger. Unthinkingly, he raved in 

German gritting his teeth: "Donnerwetter! Der verfluchte 

Jude! Who does he think he is? Since when does a foreigner, an immigrant, dare to dictate how full-blooded Americans should act on a problem that's not his? What would he want us to do act according to the sages of his Talmud?" "It's not fair to criticise a colleague in his absence," Peter 

Sparks remonstrated. "I think the right thing to do is to wait until Shanan can speak for himself." "There's no need to wait!" Julius Klein exclaimed. “Five against one is a solid majority. We'll proceed with our plan regardless of whether Shanan concurs or doesn't. Let's go to Taylor at once!" "Who's willing to be spokesman for the group?" Mueller asked. "I am." Julius jumped up.

A tense silence ensued in the room and the other internes seemed embarrassed. Without looking up, Robert Young 

III spoke apologetically: "Julius, I hope you won't misunderstand. . . I don't mean any wrong... You know how I feel about you... We're the 366

-- best of friends I even share my room with you. But I don't think it would look right for you to be our spokesman. Please don't take that as an insult. We, the Robert Youngs, have nothing against Jews. Some of my father's best friends are Jews..."

Julius reddened, and a sardonic grin appeared on Mueller's face. "Who is willing to speak in the name of the group?" 

Mueller reiterated. "Why not you yourself?" Robert Young asked. "I'm sure that none of us would object to having you represent us." “Thanks, Robert," Mueller replied complacently. “I accept. Are we ready? Let's go!"

Just then the door opened and David entered. Noticing that the group of internes was about to leave, he asked in surprise: "Where are you all heading? What's happened?” 

"The meeting has unanimously decided not to accept the newly-appointed interne, Peterson, as part of our group,' Mueller replied brusquely. "We're on our way to Dr. Taylor to tell him of our decision." "Why?" David asked sharply, his jet-black eyes boring into Mueller's. "You know very well why," Mueller snapped back, infuriated. "Yes, I know - but I don't believe it. I've got to hear with my own ears and I've got to see with my own eyes how you could all do this. I couldn't attend this meeting, and if a resolution has been adopted that includes my name I'm entitled to know why. Why do you refuse to accept the newly-appointed interne?" "Because he's a Negro," Julius blurted out. 

Astonished, David turned to Julius: "You - against Negroes? Since when is a Jew better than a Negro?" "I'm white!" Julius countered, a bit flustered. "I know you're white but you're also a Jew- and brother, whether you like it or not, there's mighty little difference between you and the Negro... Anyone who's against the 367

Negro because of his black skin is also against you, because you're a Jew. It's only a matter of degree: he's ready to expel the Negro without much fuss; he's magnanimous enough to tolerate you to a certain extent." 

David's words cut Julius sharply. Out of the corner of his eye he glanced at his roommate, Robert Young III.

Their eyes met and they both lowered their heads. in "Gentlemen," David addressed the others, "I can't concur your decision for three reasons: First, I'm convinced that all men are equal because they were all created in the image of God, without regard to the color of their skins. 

Second, this Negro, Peterson, is a doctor just as I am and his mission is as holy as mine is. I don't have the least moral right to interfere with his performance of his duty to heal the sick. Third, I've been brought up in the spirit of our 

Torah that teaches us that the basis of human ethics is 'Love thy neighbor as thyself.' There is nothing anywhere in the Bible to indicate that this applies only to the whites." "I knew it! I knew it all along!" Mueller flared up hysterically. "I warned you before that he'd introduce his 

Talmud! We Americans don't have to live according to the ethics of his sages! We have our own ethics! We don't need any moral teachings from foreigners!"

The other internes stood by silent and grim. It was apparent that the controversy between David and Mueller was symbolic of the inner conflict in each of them at that moment. 

On whose side was justice? What was to be done? With whom were they to side?

David sensed their feelings of doubt and indecision. Suddenly he recalled that scene of his childhood in Struga, when in the presence of a throng of peasants his father had had to cope with Vassilii Kravlenko's bigotry. "How strange," he thought. "Men's weaknesses are the same all over the world. 

In remote Struga it was Vassilii Kravlenko who poisoned the minds of kindly peasants against innocent, defenseless Jews; here in the United States, in the world's biggest metropolis, the same Vassilii in the guise of Alfred Mueller corrupts the minds of good-hearted American youth against 368

innocent, defenseless Negroes."
