David felt he had to challenge the darkness in which the Kravlenkos and the Muellers would endeavor to enshroud him, and ignoring Mueller he turned to the other internes: "My friends, America is the most wonderful country in the world. Do you know why? Because it was founded and developed along the principles of freedom. The United States is the only country in the world where human values are esteemed and every man is judged according to his worth and personal achievements. This is the only country where every individual, without exception, has an equal chance to reach the top in economic, political and social life. It's a great honor to be an American because Americans uphold the finest qualities of human liberty." "Thanks for the compliment!" Mueller broke in; “we are aware of our qualities and we didn't have to wait for you to come to America to tell us about our country." "Alfred, that's not fair," Sparks interposed. “Let him continue. When you had the floor no one interrupted you.” "That's right! That's right!" other voices shouted. 

David's heart filled with hope. He sensed that his words were not falling on deaf ears, and continued with greater courage: "Peterson is a human being. The fact that his skin is colored does not minimize his worth in the eyes of the Lord.

Those of us who have God in our hearts will not trample upon and destroy this man. Peterson is an American. His skin does not affect his status under the American Constitution. He was born and brought up in this land of liberty. 

Those of us who comprehend the true value of freedom, and for whom freedom is as important as breathing, will not smash Peterson with the bigotry of racial hatred. . ." 

"Wait a minute!" Mueller screamed. "I can't stand this any more! What are we a bunch of school kids? How can you all listen to this preaching? According to this foreigner we should even let our families intermarry with colored people. Why are we losing valuable time? Let's go and carry out our decision!" 369

"Goddamit, Alfred!" Robert Young III burst out angrily; "Why can't you be democratic and let your opponent have his say? What are you afraid of?" "That's true! That's true! Let Shanan go on!" the others chimed in. "My friends," David went on, "aside from being Americans, we are doctors. Peterson, too, is a doctor, and his skin doesn't change a thing as far as the Oath of Hippocrates goes. 

With our malicious and unjustified discrimination we'll be undermining his professional position. Those of us who dedicate our lives to helping the sick and the weak know that the weaker a person, the greater our responsibility toward him. We must not let Peterson be ruined because of us." 

David uttered these last words in a choked voice. He paused, cleared his throat and concluded: "Forgive my intrusion. . . . Now you can do as you please. 

"Thank you for your permission," Mueller retorted sarcastically. "Did you hear him, boys? The 'preacher of Zion' says that we can do as we please. You can bet your life that we'll do as we please. Let's go, boys!"

Mueller flung open the door but suddenly recoiled as he caught sight of the tall, lean figure of the Negro interne who had just arrived. "Hello - I'm Jerome Peterson. . . May I come in?” he asked hesitantly.

The other internes stood as though glued to the floor. 

Mueller regarded his colleagues challengingly, his eyes ablaze with hatred and anger.

David paled. He scanned the faces of the group imploringly, his eyes reflecting pity and compassion. 

Sparks made the first move. He advanced toward Peterson and stretching out his hand, said: "My name is Peter Sparks. Welcome to our midst. Come in and meet the gang.”

The door banged shut with a rumble that shook the windows in the room. No one was surprised; nor any issue made of the explosive sound, and Mueller's demonstrative exit passed almost unnoticed. 370

William Ross followed Sparks' example, and shaking the new interne's hand, said: "I'm William Ross. My friends call me Bill. If it's OK with you, we'll call you 'Jerry." " "Of course. Thank you, Bill." 

Robert Young III followed: 

"My name is Robert Young - Bob to you. Pleased to have you with us, Jerry. This is my roommate, Julius Klein." 

Julius extended his hand: "Hello Jerry. I hope we'll be friends." 

"Thanks, Julius. I'm sure we'll be friends - I'm sure." 

Peterson replied in a voice stirred with emotion.

David also shook the hand of the Negro interne, and said quietly: "I'm David Shanan." He was unable to utter another word and went into his room. He stretched out on his bed, closed his eyes, and his lips mumbled the verse of Psalms: "Depart from me, all ye workers of iniquity; for the Lord hath heard the voice of my weeping."

The door of his room opened slowly; Julius stuck his head in and asked: "David, may I come in?" "Sure, Julius, come right in."

Julius sat down on the edge of David's cot, and after a few moments of silence, said: "I want to thank you for what you've done for me. I didn't realize until now how wrong I was." "I didn't do it," David answered calmly. "You did it yourself and so did all the others." "How can you say that? You know very well that if it weren't for you, Mueller would have had his way. We were all on his side." "He would not have misled you too far." "How do you know?" "Because all of you have God in your hearts." David thought for a moment, then added: "During my childhood, a simple peasant by the name of Samborik engraved in my heart and mind some plain words of divine truth. If I placed all the words of the Torah that 

I've learned on one side of a scale and this simple peasant's 371

words on the other side — the two sides would balance.

" 

"What were this peasant's words?" Julius asked curiously, "What divine truth did he tell you?"

David closed his eyes again and reverently quoted Samborik's words: "The world of the wicked will vanish; the world of God in one's heart will never vanish." 372

Motherhood

Since the day when Esther first learned that she was pregnant she was overwhelmed by an enchanted feeling of motherhood, which dwarfed all other emotions she had ever experienced. It was even stronger than her boundless love for David.

Her heart was filled with such warmth for the child in her womb that now even David became a secondary interest to her. She did not know how to tell him her good news. 

She was afraid that he might recognize in her voice the thrill she felt over this unknown being within her, who long before emerging into this world had already contrived to usurp David's primary place in her heart. "Is anything the matter with you Esther, dear?" David asked her after they had returned from their visit to her parents that day. "You seem sort of worried and moody and you're so unusually quiet. You hardly said a word on the way home.” 

Clasping her delicate face in his hands tenderly and kissing her closed eyes, he went on affectionately: "You seem so pale, too; there are rings under your eyes. 

Darling, don't you feel well? Are you beginning to find the life of an interne's wife trying?" 

Embracing David and showering him with kisses she answered: "No my dear, it's not what you think; it's something else.. I didn't know it myself until this afternoon. 

David dear-I'm pregnant.. I'm going to have a baby 

— your baby. . . our baby. • • .. 

373

David was numb with astonishment. Then a wave of joy and happiness swept over him and he gazed at Esther in delight. He sank slowly to his knees, and embracing her hips with both hands, pressed his head against her and whispered: "My child - our child. . . I'll relive my life in my child.

I'll pour into my child all the joy I knew in my youth before sadness took its place." 

Both Esther and David were unable to sleep that night. 

They lay side by side sharing their dreams and hopes for the future of their child, who was just beginning to warm its nest in the mother's womb. "David, are you sleeping?" "No, darling. How can one sleep when one's heart is filled with so much bliss?" "David, how does our child look now?" "It looks like a tiny, soft egg, no bigger perhaps than a fresh drop of morning dew." "A drop? How can that have the power to arouse so much love in our hearts? How can it possess the magic to change our lives so completely? Only a drop? And yet I'd rather die than part from it." "Yes my dear, that's one of the heavenly secrets of life." "David, how do you know it's a drop?" "We learn it in embryology." "David, when you're a practicing physician, don't ever tell a pregnant woman that her child is a 'drop.' The doctor in the Bronx was afraid at first that I wasn't married. You should have seen how concerned he looked! I almost burst out laughing. Then he told me: 'Go home and tell your husband the good news, that you're going to have a baby.' 

A 'drop' indeed!" 

David kissed Esther on the forehead and said amiably: "Dearest, our values can't be measured by their physical dimensions. From the moment the half-cell of the woman, known as an ovum, unites with the man's half-cell, known as spermatoza, forming a whole cell, known as an embryo, a new human life begins." "A new human life," Esther reiterated. "Yes, I like that much better than 'a drop.'" 374

"Yes my dear, the embryo starts off as just one cell and yet it represents a human life. Just as a newborn infant develops through the years into an adult with all of a human being's attributes and faults, just so the microscopic one-cell embryo develops into an infant in the course of months." "David, when does the child's life begin?" "At the moment of conception. If it weren't alive it couldn't grow." 

Caressing Esther's body, David continued: "It nestles in the cozy warmth of the mother's womb, thriving on the mother's blood. That's how it stays alive and matures." "David, what will it be a boy, or a girl?" 

"I don't know, darling, but that doesn't matter." "Deep down in your heart you want it to be a boy; all men prefer sons, especially for their firstborn." "That could be. I don't know how other men feel about it. I only know what's going on in my heart right now." "What?" "An outburst of deep gratitude to God for perpetuating our love in the image of our child." “David, if it's a girl will she be beautiful?" "Yes, my dearest - very beautiful." "David, if it's a boy will you raise him to be a doctor, too?" "We'll just raise him. . . Raise him in the spirit of humaneness and justice."

They were quiet for a while. Then staring into the darkness of the night they began again to weave the dreams of their happiness to come..

David had a heavy day. He assisted Dr. Taylor on four abdominal operations from eight in the morning to two in the afternoon without any let-up. Shortly after that, he made his rounds in the wards, visiting all of the forty-eight patients who were under his care.

Finally the day's routine was over. It was 7 o'clock. David went to his room to get ready to go home. "Esther is all alone," he reflected, as he hurriedly changed his clothes. 375

"Poor girl 

she's due soon. I don't know how she can stand it. So many days and nights all alone. . . Just now when she needs me so much, I can't be with her." 

At that moment his roommate, Peter Sparks, dashed in and gripping David's shoulders, said breathlessly: "David, I'm so glad that I found you. A few more seconds and I would have missed you." "What's happened? Why are you so glad that I haven't gone yet?" "You must do me a favor, David! Relieve me tonight." "I'm sorry Peter, but Esther is all alone. You know her condition now. I have to be with her. Ask someone else.” "I've tried but I can't get anyone — they're all busy. Please,

David, it's very important - it's my mother, she's ill. She's arriving on the 9:30 train. I'm anxious to meet her and spend the evening with her. Tomorrow she has to see Taylor 

An unexplained progressive anemia. I'm worried about it." "I'm very sorry to hear that, Peter. I hope it's not serious.

Don't worry about your work in the hospital; I'll relieve you." "David, you're wonderful! I don't know how to thank you." "You don't have to thank me; I'm glad to do it. Do you have any unusual cases? Any special orders?" "No, everything is routine except for one case of Dr. 

Newman's that just arrived at the hospital. I haven't had time to look at her yet. Most likely one of his 'fancy patients.' 

She's in the North Wing, Room No. 406. Examine her, and then telephone Newman." "All right, Peter; leave it to me. Go ahead and spend some time with your mother. I'll take care of everything.” “Thanks, David - thanks a million!" Sparks repeated and left.

David changed into his white uniform again, and phoned Esther. He told her that he was not coming home. She did not become angry, and he detected no resentment in her voice. All she said was: "What can I do? That's the price 

I pay for being married to a doctor!"

Esther's kindness made David feel even more guilty about the fact that whenever he had to choose between her and the well-being of his patients, he always chose the latter. "I'd 376

feel better if she got hysterical and made a scene over it," he thought, as he made his way to the hospital's North Wing. 

The North Wing was the private pavilion for wealthy patients, who even during a siege of illness were reluctant to part with the opulent comforts they were accustomed to. 

Room 406 was one of the four most luxurious suites, each of which was situated in a corner of the building. Every suite consisted of a spacious bright room for the patient, a waiting-room for the visitors, a side room with separate bathroom for the nurse, and a large private bathroom for the patient. They were richly furnished, and seemed more like suites in a first-class hotel than hospital accommodations. 

When David entered Room 406, the patient was lying on the bed half-undressed, her head buried in the pillow, sobbing. David touched her shoulder gently and she looked up in alarm, hastily drawing the covers over her half-nude body.

She was young and exquisitely beautiful. Her features were perfect, as though carved by a sculptor. Her dark eyes, still wet, mirrored the fear of a scared child. Sitting up she quickly pushed back the curls which fell limply over her forehead. "Mrs. Marshall, how do you do? My name is Dr. Shanan. 

I'm a resident physician here. I hope I didn't scare you by coming in so suddenly." "Hello doctor," the patient replied, dabbing at her eyes with a corner of the bedsheet. “Don't mind me. I'm just a baby, a spoiled baby- the least little thing and I burst out crying. I'm sorry doctor, I don't want to cry but I can't help it." "It doesn't matter, Mrs. Marshall; crying is often very good - it eases one's heart. Can you keep a secret? I cry once in a while myself."

David's simple, kind words calmed the patient. She looked at him with gratitude and said: "My heart can never be eased. I have a feeling that when 

I leave the hospital I won't live long anyway."

David glanced at the patient's admission card and saw the letters D. and C. on the line marked "diagnosis." 377

"Mrs. Marshall," he smiled, "you really have no reason to worry. It's nothing serious. You'll only go through a D. and C." "What does that mean?" "It simply means a dilatation and curetage. We call it 

D. and C. for short."

A sad expression settled over the patient's lovely features. 

She sighed deeply and said: "You don't know my case, doctor." "That's true, Mrs. Marshall. I haven't taken your case history or examined you yet."

A nurse entered and said to David: "Doctor, you are wanted in the consulting room immediately. Dr. Newman is waiting for you." "Thank you, nurse. Excuse me, Mrs. Marshall. I'll be right back." 

Glancing at David, Dr. Newman, obviously annoyed, asked sternly: "You? Where is Sparks? Why isn't he on duty tonight? How did he dare to leave such an important patient without even phoning me about it?" "I was supposed to phone you as soon as I had finished examining the patient, sir." "When it's a patient like the one in Room 406, I expect the interne to phone me immediately after her admission to the hospital, and to consult me about what kind of examination should be made." "I beg your pardon, sir. Is there a different physical examination for patients in the private pavilion suites?" "Shanan, I resent your sarcastic remarks and I don't have to tolerate them. You're not my interne now; Sparks is my interne. I refuse to accept you in his place." 

Grabbing the telephone, he shouted angrily into it: "Information please! Hello, Dr. Newman speaking. Who is on night duty in surgery? I know of Dr. Shanan. Who else? There are always two. Who? Oh - never mind - thank you." 

Dr. Newman turned pale, his lips twitching in anger. He hung up the receiver and remained quiet and perturbed. 378

"Dr. Newman, I pity you," David remarked calmly. "You're between the devil and the deep 'black' sea. 

Well, you needn't worry. Jerome Peterson is an excellent interne; he knows his medicine. I hope your prestige won't suffer in the eyes of the patient in Room 406 because of your interne being colored. . . Good night, Dr. Newman." "Wait, Shanan - you don't seem to understand," Dr. Newman began in a conciliatory tone. “This is not just a patient - she's Mrs. Theodore William Marshall, wife of the only son of the multi-millionaire head of the Marshall Investment 

Trust Co." "I'm sorry to say, Dr. Newman, that her financial status doesn't impress me much. For me she's simply a sick young woman who is now lying on her bed and crying because she's afraid of being sick, and doesn't want to die. Her millions are worthless now. Dr. Newman, a sick person in 406 doesn't differ one iota from a poor patient in public ward C, just as a dead millionaire in a gold coffin is as dead as a dead beggar in a plain wooden box. There's no difference, Sir no difference. . ." "That's religious philosophy! Shanan, you won't make any progress here with this kind of philosophy. You can take my word for it! This is America - Society is important and money talks." "Yes, Dr. Newman, it talks - but not the language of true medicine." "Well, enough philosophizing, Shanan! I'm sorry to have lost my temper. Let's not waste time. Call the operating-room and ask them to be ready for a D. and C. I've decided to operate tonight. What time is it — eight-thirty? Well let's say ten o'clock.” 

"Ten o'clock? Is it that urgent? The patient is not prepared. I haven't had time to take her case-history, haven't even made a superficial physical examination yet." "That's not important in this case. Dr. Glen Chester, the

Marshall's family physician, has given me his written consent for the operation. Here it is. You can attach it to the patient's medical record. It contains the necessary information concerning her general condition, which, for the time being, 379

is more or less satisfactory. Further details can be added later or tomorrow."

David scanned the written lines on the single sheet and mumbled sympathetically: "She's pregnant? She's in her third month? A therapeutic abortion? No wonder she was crying so much. Poor Mrs.

Marshall... She doesn't want her baby to be destroyed." "She has a rheumatic heart disease," Dr. Newman declared emphatically, "full term pregnancy could be fatal for her.

Dr. Chester thinks that a therapeutic abortion is imperative and I agree with him." "Is her heart condition really that bad? She's only twentytwo years old." "Her heart condition is not my responsibility. I am a surgeon! Dr. Chester is an excellent and conscientious physician, and if he says that an abortion must be performed to save the young woman's life, his opinion is good enough for me. Please order all the preparations for surgery. We'll meet in the operating pavilion in an hour.”

Newman left the consulting room and David remained alone. He felt sad. He recalled the night when Esther had told him of her pregnancy. He had told her then that the embryo in her womb was merely like a drop of morning dew, and she had said: "A drop? It seems to me that I'd rather die than part from it." He could hear her words again now and was seized with a feeling of great pity for the twentytwo-year-old Mrs. Marshall. He picked up the telephone and said in a tired voice: 

"Hello, OR please! Hello, OR? Who's speaking? Miss 

MacDonald? Sorry – I didn't recognize your voice — I didn't expect you to be in the Operating Pavilion this late... Really?

Oddly enough the same thing happened to me. I was supposed to have my evening free but I had to relieve Dr. 

Sparks. Miss MacDonald, we have a D. and C. for ten o'clock. 

The surgeon? Dr. Newman. The patient? Mrs. Theodore 

William Marshall, North Wing, 406. Thank you, Miss Mac- 

Donald. I'll see you later."

When David returned to 406, he stumbled into two nurses at the door, one of them a special nurse for Mrs. Marshall, 380

the other a staff nurse who had come in to make the preoperative preparations for the patient. "Please come back in ten minutes," David told them. "I just want to take a short history of the case. It won't take me long."

Mrs. Marshall was no longer crying. She lay pensive and quiet. Her eyes were wide-open, staring blankly ahead, and she seemed totally indifferent to her surroundings. 

David took hold of her hand and pressed his fingers gently on her pulse. She turned her head toward him and said in a faraway voice: "They want to destroy my child in order to save my life. . . 

What sort of life? I don't want such a life-I don't want it. . . I'll die anyway. Doctor, I don't want the operation. 

I want to die together with my child.”

David bent over the desperate young woman and gazing straight at her, said ardently: "Why die together with the child? Why not live together with it?"

Her face lit up for an instant, then turned somber again as she mumbled: "My rheumatic heart. . . Dr. Chester. . . Dr. Newman. . ." "Dr. Chester and Dr. Newman are excellent physicians and mean well. But your will is stronger than their meaning! A person who wishes to live does so with a force that doctors are unable to comprehend. If your will to live is strong enough, you'll live together with your child! It depends on you more than on the doctors!"

Her eyes began to glow with a ray of hope and she whispered: "Thank you doctor, and may God bless you."

Dr. Newman did not accept Mrs. Marshall's determined refusal to submit to a therapeutic abortion lightly. He was greatly disturbed by her decision for a number of reasons:

First, he did not like missing a chance to operate upon a member of the Marshall family, as this case would have had immense importance for his prestige and the progress of his career as a surgeon of the elite. 381

Second, he had long yearned to be referred to as a surgical consultant by Dr. Chester, one of the most eminent physicians among New York's aristocracy. Now he, Dr. Newman, had finally been asked to take care of this Marshall case. He was not sure that Dr. Chester might not misconstrue Mrs. Marshall's present behavior and interpret it as a sign that she did not trust Dr. Newman's skill as a surgeon. This possibility disturbed him very much. 

Third, he felt terribly uncomfortable with Ruth now. As ill-luck would have it, she had happened to be on duty that evening. He had purposely come into the Operating Pavilion a half hour earlier. "Dr. Newman," Ruth had said to him in an impersonal official tone, "aren't you a bit early? Dr. Shanan just phoned to say that you would be operating at ten o'clock. It's only 9:30."

Looking about cautiously, and seeing none of the other nurses or staff-members present, Newman said quietly: "Ruth dear, come into your office. I want to tell you something." "I would much rather that we keep our private friendship outside of the Operating Pavilion," Ruth had answered, displeased. "Only for a minute - it's very important." 

She walked into her private Superintendent's Office and he followed her. With his back propped against the door he drew Ruth toward him, clasped her tightly, and began to kiss her. "Wilfrid, please, not here," she protested. "It's not right.

Things like this can undermine the discipline and decorum of the Operating Pavilion. Please, Wilfrid - I'll stay with you for a few minutes but only if you keep the door open." "All right, all right – you and your Operating Pavilion!

Sooner or later you'll have to make up your mind whether you want to marry me — or the Pavilion." 

"Wilfrid, don't talk nonsense. tell me?" 

What did you want to "Guess who my patient is and who recommended her to me.” 382

"I don't know who she is but I imagine that if she's in 

North Wing suite 406, she's quite rich." "Rich! She's wallowing in millions! She's Mrs. Marshall - Mrs. Theodore William Marshall! Glen Chester referred her to me." "Oh yes, she got married just a year ago." Ruth said. "The papers were full of her pictures; she's very beautiful and rather young. Why a D. and C.? What's wrong with her?” "Therapeutic abortion, rheumatic heart, three-month pregnancy - Chester finds that she must get rid of it. It would be too dangerous for her to go on with her pregnancy." "Poor woman," Ruth shook her head pityingly, “With all her millions she's very unfortunate." "Nonsense! She can adopt a child. With her money she could adopt a whole orphanage!" "How little you understand a woman's maternal feelings,”

Ruth murmured, regarding him with a distant, pensive look... "I can't become sentimental - I'm a surgeon. It's all in the game. Anyway, what matters is the fact that it's I who 

Chester referred this case to. I think that's wonderful and 

I wanted my future wife to feel proud of it too."

The phone rang: it was an urgent call for Dr. Newman. 

The head nurse of the North Wing informed him that the patient in 406 was refusing the operation and demanded to be discharged from the hospital immediately. 

Dr. Newman was flabbergasted. He hung up the receiver slowly, and asked himself in bewilderment: "She doesn't want the operation? She wants to leave the hospital at once? Why? What could have happened?"

Ruth remained silent, without offering him a single word of solace. He glanced at her and could have sworn that her face betrayed a faint smile of satisfaction. 

Above all, Dr. Newman was irked by the fact that Shanan, "that Goddamned immigrant," as he used to call him in his heart that "medical rabbi" or "rabbinical doctor," the devil knew which, was the culprit in the entire matter. He decided to have him dismissed from the hospital staff. In 383

order to make his accusation against David strong and effective Newman demanded that he be permitted to present his complaint personally to the Medical Board. "Don't you think that a letter to the Medical Board containing the details of the indictment would be enough?” Dr. 

Taylor asked, when Newman approached him as the chief of the surgical department with his grievances against David. "No, Dr. Taylor, I'm sure that's not enough. A letter could be interpreted in many ways. My accusation is a very serious one. This is not just a matter of the damage that he's caused me personally by influencing this patient to reject the operation which I recommended. It concerns a more fundamental principle of professional ethics. It amounts to a subversive activity on the part of an interne who's undermining the authority of the hospital physicians, not merely in the eyes of the other internes and nurses, but which is even worse - also in the eyes of the patients." 

"Well, if you're so anxious to present your complaint personally I don't see why you shouldn't be permitted to attend the Medical Board meeting. But I would advise you to make a thorough study of the issue first and be as objective as possible." "Dr. Taylor, the facts that I'll present will speak for themselves." "That's fine. I just want to warn you not to underestimate 

Shanan. It wouldn't hurt to find out what motivated his actions." "What do I care about his motivations? Every crime has some motivation. Does that justify the criminal's behavior?" 

Taylor thought for a moment and then asked: "Dr. Newman, how well do you know Shanan?” "I know him as well as I know any of the other internes. 

I never saw anything in him to make me want to know him better. To me he's only an immigrant with a philosophy of life that's alien to mine." "Really? That's surprising. I would have expected just the opposite. After all, you're both of the same faith." 

Newman blushed, losing his bearings for a moment, but he 384

quickly regained his composure and replied sharply: "Religion has nothing to do with this. Shanan's philosophy is foreign to me because I'm an American." "That's strange - strange indeed. . ." "What's strange?" "The fact that I, too, am an American and I don't find his way of looking at things foreign. In fact, I even find it to my liking very much to my liking."

During its next meeting, the Medical Board heard Dr. Newman's complaint against the interne, David Shanan. It was unanimously decided to refer the matter to a special committee of three members of the Board. This committee was authorized to investigate the situation and to act in accordance with their findings. Since both the plaintiff and the defendant were in the surgical department, the Board unanimously agreed that Dr. Taylor should act as chairman of the committee. The other two members were Dr. A. H. 

Corby, the chief of obstetrics, and Dr. Martin B. Preston, the chief of the medical department. 

A few days later David was summoned to appear before the committee in order to answer the charges brought against him in connection with the patient, Mrs. Theodore William Marshall. "Dr. Shanan," Dr. Taylor began. "I think you know the other two members of this committee - Dr. Corby and Dr. Preston." "Yes sir." "Dr. Shanan," Dr. Taylor continued, "The charge against you is of a very serious nature. The Medical Board has appointed us to investigate the issue. If you feel that you are guilty and acknowledge that you've made a mistake, we would advise you to give us an honest explanation about this matter. The Committee will then recommend that you apologize to Dr. Newman and to Dr. Chester, and ask them to forgive you. In view of the fact that your record in the hospital has been better than average there is a good chance that you won't be dismissed from your post as an interne before your training is completed." 385

David answered calmly: "I know very well, sir, that my dismissal from the hospital could be catastrophic to my career and perhaps to my whole life, but I can't apologize for an act which I would repeat without the slightest hesitation under similar circumstances." 

The jury of three exchanged meaningful glances. "Let's get this straight, Dr. Shanan," Dr. Preston spoke up. "Do you mean to say that if I were to send a pregnant woman into the hospital who had a rheumatic heart, and Dr. Taylor were to agree with me that a therapeutic abortion was indicated - that you would go against our decision and advise the patient to reject the surgery I recommended?" "I don't know, sir." "You don't know? Are you implying that in such a case you would also act the way you did in the case that was being handled by Dr. Chester and Dr. Newman?” "Quite likely, sir - if the circumstances were the same." "What sort of circumstances?" Dr. Corby interposed. "Was the diagnosis not the right one, perhaps? Was this woman pregnant, or wasn't she? Did she, or didn't she have a rheumatic heart?” "I have no reason to doubt the diagnosis of so distinguished a physician as Dr. Chester." "You admit then that this patient was pregnant and that she had a rheumatic heart. As a doctor, you know very well that continuation of her pregnancy for a full term exposes her to grave risk. Yet you persuaded her to reject the recommended therapeutic abortion. Why? What are your medical arguments to justify this?” "I don't know if my arguments could be termed 'medical.' 

I don't know if they have any specific name at all. In medicine it is known as a 'three-month-pregnancy,' but the twenty-two year-old Mrs. Marshall refers to it otherwise - she says 'my child.' In medicine, this is only a conglomeration of multiplying cells known as an embryo but for the twenty-two-year old Mrs. Marshall it's something entirely different. For her it's her son, who'll be like his father and follow in his father's footsteps; or else, her daughter, who'll be beautiful, and have blue eyes and blonde hair. In medicine, the recommended 386

surgery was labeled a 'therapeutic abortion'; for Mrs. Marshall it meant the killing of her child and of the most important dreams of her life. Medicine decided that Mrs. 

Marshall's heart wasn't strong enough to endure the hardships of pregnancy and ordered an abortion. But Mrs. Marshall thought that the destruction of her child would be far more unbearable for her heart than going through with her pregnancy, so she refused to submit to an abortion. She felt that that would not save her life; she did not want an empty, childless life. 'Doctor,' she told me, 'I'm not going to have this operation - I want to die together with my child.'" "Did she say that to you?" Dr. Preston asked in amazement. "Yes sir - she said that and I believed her." "As a doctor, don't you think that it was your duty to support the decision of the other doctors and try to influence the patient that way?”

David deliberated for a moment, and then answered quietly: "I suddenly felt that Mrs. Marshall's decision embodied a greater power than the decision of the doctors." "What sort of power?" "The power that brings man nearer to God." "What do you mean?" 

"To destroy a life - even in order to save another lifeis surely not a divine mission. In Mrs. Marshall's determination to die rather than to sacrifice the life of her child, I sensed the will of God. At that moment I believed with all my heart that if she should apply her God-given will towards life instead of death, she would survive the strain of childbirth together with her child. I wanted to help her, and with all the sincerity of my heart, without any compunction, I told her: 'A person who wants to live, lives on with a power that doctors cannot comprehend! If your will to live is strong enough, you'll live, together with your child. It depends on you more than on the doctors!' This, gentlemen, was my crime."

When David had left the room, the three Committee members sat silently for a while. "A strange fellow but a sincere one," Dr. Preston observed. "The accusation against him is without foundation." 387

"Without foundation," Dr. Corby concurred. "Thank you, gentlemen," Dr. Taylor said gripping the hands of his colleagues, and closed the meeting. 

Dr. Newman's accusation against David and the Medical 

Board Committee's subsequent decision was the main topic of conversation in the hospital for several days. David was admired as a hero. "Two giants like Chester and Newman pounced on him and he overcame them both!" Newman was regarded as a person who had failed miserably in some important undertaking.

Oddly enough, David was irked by this new situation. Although he did not regret his previous behavior, he was anxious now to have Newman learn the truth of the matter. 

Ideological differences of opinion did not worry him but misinterpretation of the facts did disturb him. 

He could not believe that Dr. Newman would have raised that accusation against him before the Medical Board unless Newman had been under the impression that he, David, was really responsible for the patient's rejection of the therapeutic abortion. Before the Medical Board Committee meeting, David had tried to talk to Dr. Newman on several occasions. He wanted to tell him exactly what had happened, but Newman had persisted in completely ignoring him. 

After he had been acquitted, David's first desire was to see Newman and to express his pleasure at having the misunderstanding cleared up at last. He approached him and said: "Dr. Newman, may I speak to you for a minute?” "We have nothing to talk about," he blurted out resentfully, turning sharply away. 

That same afternoon, David received a check for one hundred dollars, along with a letter: "Dear Dr. Shanan, "Please accept this small check as a token of gratitude for the wonderful way in which you treated my wife when she was a patient at the hospital. She took it upon herself to make a decision contrary to her doctors' advice, and was very de- 388

pressed about it. Your kind, encouraging words gave her the strength which she lacked. Thanks to you, she feels very much better now. "Sincerely, Theodore William Marshall

David felt a great sense of relief. He must show this letter to Newman - but how? It occurred to him that Ruth Mac- 

Donald might be of some help. She had a strong influence over Dr. Newman. It was no longer a secret that they were practically engaged. "No, Dr. Shanan," Ruth said to David after listening to his plea, "it's not necessary for me to show him your letter, you'll do it yourself." "That's impossible. I've tried to speak to him several times but he not only turns a deaf ear he shows an extraordinary hatred for me." 

"Well, this time he'll simply have to listen to you. I'm inviting you and Mrs. Shanan to my apartment tomorrow at eight for a cup of coffee. Dr. Newman will also be there, and I'm sure that peace will be restored between you two."

Newman was very surprised when he arrived at Ruth's apartment the next evening and found the Shanans there. 

His first impulse was to wheel around and leave at once through the half-opened door, but Ruth was behaving graciously and welcoming him with a warmth that was different from the official manner she had always assumed with him in the presence of others. "Wilfrid," she said, clasping both his hands, "you've stopped cold at my doorstep as though you'd stumbled on a corpse. 

Come in there are no strangers here." Escorting him into the center of the room, she continued: "Dr. Shanan needs no introduction; and I think you know Mrs. Shanan, too." 

Dr. Newman nodded briefly in Esther's direction, and ignoring David sat down in a lounge chair without uttering a word. A painful silence ensued. "How do you feel, Mrs. Shanan?" Ruth asked, to start the conversation. "It must be wonderful to be expecting a baby." 389

"Oh yes, but I must admit that the closer I come to the big day, the more cowardly I become. . . At times I'm scared that I'll die in childbirth.” "Nonsense!" David cut in, gripping Esther's hand. “Having a baby is no sickness. It's a natural, normal physiological process. One doesn't die from it." "David doesn't like me to talk about dying during childbirth. He always tells me that women don't die from that. 

But I know differently. I remember a woman in my native town in the Ukraine dying in childbirth. She'd been in labor for three days and couldn't give birth. According to Jewish law, a woman must not be buried while she's still with child; the offspring must be freed from its mother and then buried alongside her. The corpse of this mother, though, would not release the child, and so the local townsmen were faced with a serious dilemma: a corpse on a hot summer day that couldn't as yet be buried! The baffled townsmen ran to the rabbi and he resorted to the most "drastic" procedure he could think of - he ordered his fellow-Jews to assemble in the synagogue and to recite Psalms. An ordinary drab week-day was suddenly turned into a solemn Day of Atonement. The synagogues were crowded with worshippers. Then suddenly an outcry was heard: 'The corpse has consented, and yielded the child!' 'Fellow-Jews,' the rabbi said, 'let's proceed with the funeral'. . . What a funeral that woman had! The entire town turned out to it!" "How interesting!" Ruth said, frankly fascinated with 

Esther's story. Then turning to Newman: "Don't you think so, Wilfrid?" "I'm sorry," Newman answered, "but I'm not impressed by these things. Jews have a habit of disguising the realities of life with a religious mask and they stir up the emotions of the ignorant masses. For my part, that sort of thing sounds like the Middle Ages!" "Dr. Newman," Esther asked, "you've never experienced the urge for prayer?" "No, never! America was not built on divine miracles;

America is a land of wholesome reality. In America there's 390

no time to be wasted on prayer. Prayer is only good for those who want to escape from reality." "Wilfrid, you amaze me!" Ruth exclaimed. "You speak as though America were a godless land and the Americans an atheistic people! You'll admit, won't you, that Dr. Taylor is a good American? Yet on several occasions I've found him sitting in his office in meditation before a serious operation. 

I remember asking him the first time, 'Is there anything wrong, sir?' And he answered, 'No, Miss MacDonald, nothing;

I am merely asking in my heart for God's help. A human life is in my hands. . ." "You and your Dr. Taylor!" Newman blurted out, rising from his chair. "Let me tell you, if a doctor isn't up to par in his medical skill God will be of very little help to him. 

Making God responsible is just a convenient way of hiding one's ignorance."

A painful hush pervaded the room again. Ruth sat with downcast eyes unable to utter another word. Newman's contemptuous reference to Dr. Taylor had cut her to the quick, and she found herself fighting back tears. . . David slowly took the letter that he had received from Theodore William Marshall out of his pocket, and extending it to Newman, said: "This will undoubtedly interest you very much." 

Newman scanned the letter quickly and returned it: "I'm still positive that if you hadn't relieved Sparks that evening, Mrs. Marshall would have been operated upon. You just confused her by your religious nonsense." “'Your kind, encouraging words gave her the strength which she lacked,'" he quoted in a cynical tone. "These are words that you certainly can't deny! I'll never understand why you studied medicine in the first place; your vocation is preaching. Why didn't you become a rabbi?" "Every doctor is a preacher to a certain extent," David answered tranquilly. "Dr. Newman, you too, are a preacher." "I? I beg your pardon! Preaching is connected with sentimentality and I don't think sentimentality and surgery make a good team." 391

"That, Dr. Newman, doesn't prevent you from being a preacher in medicine. Some doctors preach comfort and compassion. They are governed by their hearts, and their deeds are full of warm sentiment. Others preach austerity and uncompromising determination. They are governed by their minds, and their actions are based only on cold logic. 

But one type doesn't cancel out the other both have a place in life and both are important." 

"Do you mean to tell me that in your religious philosophy you can also find a justification for my views?" Newman asked astonished. "I'm no authority on religious philosophy. I can only tell you about the two most eminent personalities in our history, Hillel and Shamai, whose teachings are the foundations of the entire Talmud. They each had their own academies, known in the Talmud as Beth Hillel and Beth Shamai (The House of Hillel; The House of Shamai). These two academies were forever engaged in polemics with each other. Beth Hillel preached warm sentiment; Beth Shamai, rigid, cold logic. But one school did not try to wipe out the other. Beth Hillel and Beth Shamai have their respective places in the world's philosophies and both are important and immortal. . . Yes, Dr. Newman, whether you want it that way or not, you too are a teacher and preacher in medicine, just as much as Dr. Taylor. The difference is that he belongs to Beth Hillel, and you, to Beth Shamai." "Beth Shamai," Newman repeated, "the academy of fact and logic. That appeals to me very much. Do you know,

Shanan, if the committee of the Medical Board would have acted according to Beth Shamai, you would have gotten your deserved punishment!" "It's more human to act in accordance with the principles of Beth Hillel, though. Most of the Talmudic laws are based on the spirit of Beth Hillel, and not Beth Shamai." "Why?" "Because loving-kindness brings God and man closer together. Compassion stems from one's heart. One feels, pities, comforts, cheers with one's heart - and above all - one loves with one's heart." 392

"Surgery is an exception," Newman's voice rose again. “Surgery calls for only cold logic and steady hands." "One needs to have God in his heart then, too," David insisted, "God in one's heart? What sort of God? Whose God shall 

I take into my heart? There are innumerable gods." "No, Dr. Newman, you are mistaken. There are not innumerable gods - there's only one God. There are innumerable people who approach Him differently and worship Him in their respective ways. The diversity is not in God, it's in men's conceptions of God." "In that case I can conceive God in my own way, too. My god is America – I don't have to serve any other god. America is good enough for me!" 

After David and Esther had left, Ruth sat silently engrossed in thought. Newman sat down beside her and wanted to kiss her but she turned her lips away. "What's the matter with you, Ruth?" Newman asked, offended. "Why this sudden coldness? Don't tell me that you too, have been influenced by that immigrant's phi losophy?" "Perhaps. . .” she answered absently, not looking at him.

After a momentary pause, she added, "Wilfrid, why do you show so much hatred toward Shanan? The case of Mrs.

Marshall is just an alibi you're using for your dislike for him. Deep down in your heart you know very well that the patient decided herself to reject the operation. What's the real reason for your hatred towards Shanan?” "I don't know. . . he grates on my nerves." "Is it because he's the shadow of your conscience?" "I don't know what you're talking about. What's he got to do with my conscience?" "He's a Jew. "So what if he's a Jew? There are three million Jews in 

New York." "That's exactly what I mean. You've broken away from these three million Jews and figured you'd found protection behind the shield of being an ‘absolute American.' So then 

Shanan comes along and brings back what you've been 393

escaping from. That's the real reason for your hatred towards him, isn't it? You hate him because he's a Jew. Why? 

You're Jewish yourself." "That's absurd! Julius Klein is a Jew and I don't hate him." "That's something else. Julius Klein is very much like yourself. He probably regards you as his ideal and looks up to you. You're not afraid of Julius Klein." "And I'm afraid of Shanan?" "Yes, you're afraid of him and that's why you hate him." "You're out of your mind! Why should I with my status as a native American, and a recognized surgeon, fear that 

Russian immigrant?" "Because that Russian immigrant has every prospect for achieving what you've achieved - and also something that you've failed to achieve." "What?" "Integrity! Individual and social integrity. He is what he is and is proud of his identity. He knows the values of his faith, and his gentile acquaintances have the greatest respect for him because of that. Do you know, Wilfrid, I've learned a lot about God this evening from Shanan's words. 

Tonight I realized that a good Christian and a good Jew have a lot in common and are very close to each other.. 

I can't forget Shanan's words, "The diversity is not in God, but in men's conceptions of God.' What a wonderful idea."

Newman's lips twitched nervously, and gritting his teeth he cried out vehemently: "That damned immigrant has bewitched you too! I hate him." "Why?" Ruth asked again.

Newman did not answer at once. He sat down again, wiping the perspiration off his forehead. He regretted his angry outburst and was anxious to make amends for his rude behavior. He began with forced restraint: "I'm sorry, Ruth. I didn't mean to shout at you or flare up like that, but ever since Shanan came to the hospital he's made it quite unpleasant for me. . . Maybe you're right. . .

Maybe I can't tolerate him because he represents something 394

from which I ran away. 99 Ruth gazed at him searchingly, and suddenly asked: "Wilfrid, what are Mrs. Marshall's chances for surviving together with her child?"

This unexpected question caught Newman off-guard, but he was grateful for it since this change of subject might break the tension between him and Ruth. "That's hard to say," he spoke calmly now. "But be that as it may, it's stupid of her to risk her life by continuing her pregnancy." "Is it so stupid to risk one's life for a child?” "What are you talking about? What child? She was only in her third month of pregnancy."

Ruth fell silent. Her thoughts took her back to the remote past and she went on quietly: 

"Three months or nine months there's not so much difference. . . To a mother, a child is a child, and she'd sacrifice her life rather than part from it." "This sentiment of motherhood is grossly exaggerated. Do you have any idea of how many thousands of women pay large sums of money in order to quietly terminate their pregnancies prematurely? They risk their lives to get rid of the child, not to give birth to it! Abortions are one of the leading illegal industries in this country! Maternal sentiment! Don't make me laugh! I would like to see an unmarried pregnant woman who would be so overcome by maternal sentiment that she would reject a chance for an abortion!"

Newman was astonished when he saw Ruth quickly lower her head, and covering her face with both hands, burst into tears. "What's happened, dear?" he asked, dismayed. "Why are you crying?" Stroking her hair he pleaded: "Ruth darling, I love you. Please tell me why you're crying."

Slowly raising her head and gazing at him through brimming eyes, she replied: "Thanks for loving me, but I can't marry you Wilfrid. . ."

Newman went down on his knees before her, and bringing both of her hands to his lips, he begged: "Why? Why?" 395

Ruth stood up, and wiping away her tears said calmly: "It's rather late Dr. Newman. There's a hard day's work ahead for both of us in the hospital tomorrow. Good night, Doctor." 396

Mercy Killing 

Peter Sparks' anxiety over his mother's condition was justified. Dr. Taylor examined her on the morning after her arrival in New York. With hidden fear, her eyes watched every move and facial expression the doctor made, and waited for his verdict. "Mrs. Sparks, it would be highly advisable for you to remain with us in the hospital for a few days," Dr. Taylor said to her with an amiable smile after his examination. "What did you find out, doctor?" Mrs. Sparks asked, trying to appear placid while her heart pounded furiously. "To tell you the truth, Mrs. Sparks, I haven't found out anything yet. You'll have to have various laboratory tests and analyses; also some X-ray pictures will have to be taken. 

I would therefore urge you to remain in the hospital and have all these things attended to." "Is it serious?" she asked apprehensively. "No of course not Mrs. Sparks - absolutely nothing to worry about. Only a few days and you'll return home hale and hearty." "Thank you, doctor. I'm feeling better already. It will actually be a pleasure for me to stay in the hospital. Just imagine - in the hospital where my Peter is an interne! 

I'll be so proud when I see him dressed in white, taking care of me. Not every patient can boast of her own son being her doctor in the hospital." "Well, Mrs. Sparks, I'm sure that Peter will see you often and will spend every free moment of his by your side but not as a doctor." "Why not? Isn't he as competent as any other interne?" 397

"Yes of course, he's quite competent and very conscientious. 

I think that he'll be an excellent doctor and that his future is most promising. But it's against accepted medical ethics for a doctor to treat his own relatives. That's a sort of unwritten law except in cases of some special emergency where there is no other alternative." “I understand, doctor, but I must admit that I'm disappointed." 

As soon as Mrs. Sparks was settled in her hospital room, Peter entered accompanied by David. He kissed his mother on the forehead and remarked with feigned cheerfulness: "Mother dear, you look beautiful! You know, I love you so much I purposely bribed Dr. Taylor to detain you here for a few days just so I could see you day and night. It's good to be with you, mother dear!" He hugged her fondly. "Peter dear, I'm very happy to be with you, but it's not the way I would like it. I'm afraid of being sick. . . I hope it's nothing serious." She uttered the last words in a trembling voice. "And who is this good-looking doctor with you?" she asked, staring at David. "Mother, this is David Shanan, my roommate and best friend. He'll also be your resident-physician. David, this is my mother- the best and prettiest mother in the world. 

Don't you think so?" "Yes sir, she sure is!" David agreed, extending his hand to the patient. "Peter has spoken of you so many times that 

I actually visualized you in my mind. Now I'm delighted to really meet you and I hope you'll get well very soon." “Thank you, Dr. Shanan. I feel as if I've known you for some time, too, because Peter has written about you in all of his letters." "Mrs. Sparks," David went on, still clasping her hand, "may I ask a favor of you? Don't call me Dr. Shanan please just call me David. All right?" 

Mrs. Sparks regarded the two young men with warmth and love. She smiled and said: "All right, David."

The moment Peter had left his mother, his cheerful air vanished completely and was replaced by tension and anxiety. 398

He trudged wearily beside David through the long, winding corridors, toward the internes' quarters. David cast a sidelong glance at him from time to time but neither of them said anything. "Do you know, David," Sparks finally said, “until now, in my heart I always agreed with Newman that a doctor must be objective and not permit sentimental emotions to interfere with the practice of medicine. Today, though, I became convinced that there's a humane streak in us that's more powerful than all the conventional following of one's duties and responsibilities. How can I be objective in my mother's case? Standing beside her there and looking at her thin, pale face, I was suddenly seized with a secret wish that my entire knowledge of medicine would vanish from my mind. I would feel better about my mother, it seemed to me, if I were like the average man who can look at the doctor with naive expectations and prayers hoping that everything will be all right. . But being a doctor, I know what to expect - I know the score. It's so hard to be objective when the patient is your own mother. . .. 99 "Yes, Peter - you're right, but it seems to me that in your mother's case you're too pessimistic. There has been no diagnosis as yet and it's quite likely that it's not as serious as you imagine." "Thanks for the reassurance, but I've got a gloomy premonition. A woman of forty-eight who loses twenty-five pounds in three months, who grows weaker and more anemic from day to day, and who has a constant, unexplained temperature above normal - these symptoms, you have to admit, are an indication of a destructive neoplastic disease." "Not always. There are some non-malignant ailments that have similar symptoms, as for instance, the menopausal syndrome, or a hidden nest of infection, or some benign, bleeding internal growth. Do you remember that patient in Ward 

F? Everyone thought she had a malignant growth, and the operation showed that she had a benign bleeding polyp in the large bowel. After the polyp had been removed she recovered fully." "I hope you're right, David. I hope so but it doesn't look good." 399

"Don't despair, Peter. She'll be all right. She's in good hands; you can depend on Taylor." "I know Taylor is a brilliant diagnostician and surgeon, but you and I have seen him time and again, helpless against the necrotic wounds of cancer. I'll never forget that patient, young Jackson. That was the first case I'd been assigned to. 

I hardly knew Taylor then. He called me to his office and asked: "Sparks, what do you think of young Jackson?' ' "He has leukemia sir', I said." 66 "Yes, that's right, leukemia — cancer of the blood. His body is being destroyed by the unlimited multiplication of leucocytes. An eighteen-year-old boy is dying before our eyes and we are powerless to save him.' "How long will he live, sir?' I asked Taylor." ""That I don't know, Sparks,' he told me. 'But we'll make every effort to prolong his life. If we can't prolong his years we'll prolong his months, his weeks, his days, or even his hours. ""How, sir?" " "'We'll remove the spleen. The majority of the leucocytes in this case are lymphocytes, which means that they are formed mostly in the spleen. The removal of the spleen will therefore inhibit an important source of the wild multiplication of the leucocytes." " "Taylor operated that same afternoon. Newman and I assisted. Young Jackson bled profusely during the operation. 

The prepared bottle of blood for transfusion was used up. 

The anesthetist told us that the patient was in shock.” "More blood!' Dr. Taylor growled, without looking up." ""There's no more blood available, sir,' Miss MacDonald said, looking scared." "Newman, take over!' Dr. Taylor stepped away from the operating-table, took off his gloves and gown, and stretching out his arm commanded: "Transfuse the patient with my blood!'" 66 "Without a blood test? What about the blood group?' the anesthetist asked, eyeing Taylor with surprise." “‘I am a universal donor,' Taylor answered. 'Don't waste 400

any time, please; just do as you are told!'” "Young Jackson recovered from the operation and improved enough to leave the hospital very cheerfully some time afterwards. Six months later, though, he was brought back this time in the terminal phase of his illness. I used to find 

Dr. Taylor at that dying boy's bedside late at night, wrapped in thought, staring at the patient. I could have sworn that 

Taylor's lips were moving as though in prayer.” 'Sir,' I asked him after Jackson died, 'may I ask you something?" " "'Of course you may. You're here to ask and to learn.' ""What good was that operation - with transfusions from your own blood too – when you knew all along that it was wasted effort?" " 

“Our duty, Sparks, is to defy death.' 66 66 999 "Even when you're sure it's a lost cause?' I asked him." "Yes, Sparks, even when you're sure it's a lost cause.' 

Then, looking straight into my eyes, he added: 'And don't you ever forget that!'"

Sparks had evidently finished his story. The monotonous echo of the microphone calling out the name of one doctor, now of another, pierced the prevailing quiet of the room from time to time. Sparks stretched out on his cot, clasped his hands behind the nape of his neck and staring at the ceiling, began talking again: 

"You know, David – my father says that both life and death are given to us by God. When death comes it should be accepted as the will of God and we should resign ourselves to His will."

David recalled his own first hospital case. He had left a dying patient and Taylor had shouted to him: "Go back! 

How dare you leave your post?" David shuddered now at the memory. "Peter, good and evil both come from God. The will to make a choice, however, God has left to our individual discretion. Both life and death are surely acts of God. Nevertheless, He has made us physicians - the custodians who shield life against death. The conscientious doctor, the one who is the true emissary of the Healer Of All Flesh must 401

not desert his post even for a second. Every minute of a person's life must be as dear to him as eternity."

The laboratory and X-ray findings on Mrs. Sparks' case were completed three days later. Peter and David both stood near Dr. Taylor, without taking their eyes off him as he remained engrossed in the medical record, scrutinizing each separate report. Frowning, Dr. Taylor finally said in a tone of anxiety: "There's no way out; we'll have to operate." 

Peter turned pale and asked in a strained voice: "What do you suspect, sir?" "Loss of weight, weakness, anemia, and persistent elevation of temperature, are the four cardinal symptoms of malignancy, especially when it occurs in middle-aged patients." "Where do you think her tumor is located?" "Somewhere around the first portion of the intestine; I would say in the neighborhood of the duodenum. The X-rays show a defect in that region. Aside from that, there is a certain degree of jaundice which suggests that something is pressing on the bile ducts as they enter the intestines at this point. The blood studies reveal a very high sugar content - 280 milligrams percent. That's pretty high. The normal maximum is 120 milligrams percent. This could be interpreted to mean that if it is a tumor, it is situated in the pancreas.' 

"If it is a tumor?" Peter repeated. "What else could it be?" "That can be determined only when the abdomen is explored," Taylor answered shortly. "When sir?” "Tomorrow." "Do you think that I should go with you when you break the news to her about her operation?" "As you wish Sparks, or shall we say, as you feel. I believe that your presence will have a soothing effect on her, especially if you're able to control yourself and act cheerful instead of depressed. I know that won't be easy for you but it's important for your mother."

Mrs. Sparks was pleased to see Dr. Taylor enter her room accompanied by Peter and David. In the course of the past 402

few days, she had become very attached to Dr. Taylor and to David.

She lay in bed with her head raised so high that she appeared to be half sitting. There was a smile on her emaciated, sallow face and her sunken eyes shone with kindness and tenderness. "My three beloved doctors!" she murmured in a faint yet happy voice. "I just look at all of you and feel better already. 

Well, what will you do with me today?" "Mrs. Sparks, today we won't do anything with you," Dr. 

Taylor smiled. “This is, let us say, a social visit." "Really? That's very nice of you. Had I known this before

I would have had some nice refreshments ready worthy of such great visitors," Mrs. Sparks jested. The next moment she became sad again. “The three of you are poor liars. The truth is written on your faces. . . All right, let me have it 

Dr. Taylor; I can take it. What's the verdict?" "Nothing serious, Mrs. Sparks; there's something in your gall-bladder — it requires a small operation, and in a few days you'll be as good as new." 

"This is no surprise to me," Mrs. Sparks said with a deep sigh. "I expected it. I had a feeling that I wouldn't escape the knife. . . I hope to God that you'll make me well. Life is so wonderful - I don't want to part with it. I don't want to die." "Mrs. Sparks, what sort of talk is this?" Peter said to her with feigned anger, as he hugged her. "Isn't that silly? And 

I bragged to everyone that my mother is clever. Sure you'll be well, and you'll live for many many years. I want to warn you: Dr. Taylor doesn't like patients who mention the word 'dying.'" 

Mrs. Sparks stretched out one hand to Taylor and the other to David, and said calmly: "I'm in your hands. May God be with you." 

Dr. Taylor's tentative diagnosis of Mrs. Sparks' condition proved correct during the operation. The pancreas had been almost entirely invaded by a tumor which had spread to the stomach, intestines and liver. Taylor thrust his right hand up to his elbow into the deep wound of the opened abdomen, 403

and feeling the anatomical regions which the tumor had gnawed into, he spoke as though to himself: "What a horror. . . Poor woman inoperable carcinoma of the pancreas spread over all adjacent organs. . . Hopeless close!" 

Peter Sparks had not slept a wink on the night preceding the operation. Although he felt certain that his mother's condition was cancerous, he had nevertheless hoped for some unexpected miracle — that in the course of the operation Taylor would find some diseased section that could be removed surgically. He racked his brain going over the list of all possible non-fatal ailments that might cause such symptoms. But all he could think of as likely was the dreaded cancer. He struggled through sleepless hours, and at six o'clock was already in his mother's room. "Hello beautiful," he greeted her, kissing her forehead. "You're looking fine. Did you sleep well?" "Not bad, son. I was given a sleeping pill. I dreamed the whole night - such good, sweet dreams. Every time I woke up

I felt disappointed and wanted to doze off again so I could dream some more. I don't remember exactly what I dreamed but it was all about my youth — and Spring. I dreamed all night long about the wonderful inexhaustible strength and happiness I had when I was young and healthy. . .' 

At seven o'clock the nurse came in carrying a syringe and a needle. Mrs. Sparks was apprehensive and instinctively gripped Peter's hand. 

"Don't be afraid mother dear, this is only an injection given to patients before an operation. It quiets the nerves and relaxes all the stiffness in the body." 

A few moments after the morphine injection, Mrs. Sparks dozed off, a smile of childish submission on her face. She was still murmuring about her dream of the previous night but her words were becoming increasingly incoherent; her eyelids lowered, and she was transported to a world of peace and forgetfulness. . . She was placed on a stretcher and wheeled out to the Operating Room.

Peter could not sit still. He paced up and down his mother's room like a restless lion. Every now and then he would 404

glance at his wristwatch; seconds seemed so interminably long. "My God," he thought to himself, "there are sixty seconds to a minute, sixty minutes to an hour. Where does one get the patience and strength to endure all this?”

Through his mind there suddenly flitted a happy thought that filled him with a sense of well-being and relief: Dr. 

Taylor would probably find an ordinary chronic inflammation of the bile-ducts and perhaps also a small stone or two. 

These would be removed and his mother would recover completely.

He grabbed at this comforting thought and felt soothed by it, just as his mother had been soothed by the morphine before she had been wheeled to the operating room. He slumped into the soft armchair near his mother's empty bed and forced his mind to concentrate only on this pleasant idea of her eventual recovery.

Peter soon felt the effects of his sleepless night and dozed off. He dreamed that he was at his graduation ball again, only he was with his mother. She was young and beautiful; everyone's eyes were on them. Everyone thought that she was his sweetheart, but he knew that she was his mother and was happy and proud of it. He danced with her continuously. She was so light-footed that he was soaring with her in the air. Suddenly she slipped out of his hands and fell down. Her face turned pale and wrinkled, aging in an instant. She was lying on the floor sighing and crying... 

Peter awakened with a start. He jumped to his feet. The door of the room was wide open, and the stretcher bearing his mother was being wheeled in. Her appearance was so altered that she was scarcely recognizable. Her face was swollen and cyanotic; her pointed nose and foaming mouth were encircled by a deeply furrowed ring left by the pressure of the anesthetic mask. Tears rolled down the corners of her closed eyes; a monotonous rasping moan issued from her congested throat.

Peter stood for a moment, bewildered, as though he couldn't decide whether this was reality or the continuation of his dream. He shook his head vigorously and awakened completely. He caught sight of David near the orderlies 405

and nurses, wheeling in the stretcher. David was at the right side of the patient where the intravenous apparatus was installed, carefully watching to see that the needle remained in place. He did not take his fingers off her pulse and kept his eyes riveted on the hanging flask from which drops of nourishing fluid were steadily trickling into the vein of the patient.

Peter was stunned. Judging by David's serious expression and by the fact that upon entering the room the latter had not even looked at him, he could guess the tragic outcome of the operation.

Cancer is often like a wild, devouring animal. When injured and provoked it becomes more ferocious and its assault may then be more devastating. The exploratory operation performed on Mrs. Sparks had this type of irritating effect upon the cancerous growths in her abdomen. The malignant cells now raged on without any restraint spreading with the rapidity of wildfire. 

During her first few post-operative days Mrs. Sparks' nausea, vomiting, and sharp abdominal pains were interpreted as a direct result of the operation. The anesthesia, the incision, the sutures these are all among the factors which cause a patient to look and feel so sick during the first days following surgery. It soon became evident, however, that in the case of Mrs. Sparks these symptoms were not simply temporary, post-operative effects, but rather signs that her actual basic illness had suddenly flared up at an alarming rate. 

Her pains were excruciating and persisted without any let-up. At first, injections of morphine eased her shooting pains and distress and afforded her a respite of several hours of continuous sleep. Soon, however, her body became accustomed to this narcotic so that the dosage had to be increased and administered at more frequent intervals. She could digest no food whatsoever, and her tongue, palate and throat became as dry as parchment because of her continuous high temperature. She yearned for a drink of water yet threw up each mouthful swallowed. The ever-spreading cancerous mass was consuming her entrails. Her body became 406

more emaciated every day; she began to look like a skeleton covered with a wizened skin, yellow as wax.

David spent his days and nights at Mrs. Sparks' bedside. 

Only the nourishment supplied by intravenous infusions of physiological serum and by blood transfusions was keeping her alive now. All her veins were swollen and painful; there was almost no place left into which one could thrust a needle. 

David kept looking for vessels which were still untouched, healing the others with warm compresses so that they could be used again. Each time he stuck a needle into her gaunt arm or leg he would comfort her, saying: "I'm sorry if I'm hurting you Mrs. Sparks, but this will make you well." "Thank you David dear," she would answer in a voice which was barely audible because of her steadily-weakening state. "God knows how much I've suffered already." "It won't be long now, Mrs. Sparks, and you'll get well. 

Another day or two and you'll be much better," David kept telling her, watching constantly to see that the fluids providing her sole sustenance continued to flow into her veins. 

Dr. Taylor visited the patient mostly at night. He would sit down at her bedside, take her pulse and gaze at her for a long time. Her face would light up when she opened her eyes and caught sight of him. "Dr. Taylor, I'm so glad to see you," she would murmur, "How do you find me? Do you think I'll ever get better?" "Of course - of course. You're getting along fine - very fine."

Dr. Taylor's visits and his few simple words always had a wonderful effect on the sick woman. After he had been there she would calm down and her pains seemed to subside. She would ask to have her head raised and for something to drink. At such times, miraculously enough, she was able to retain the liquid she took...

Seeing this, David recalled Reb Itzikel. He, too, had been able to lift drooping spirits with a few simple words and to give renewed hope and happiness to unfortunate fellow-men. "Reb Itzikel and Dr. Taylor-how they resemble one another," David mused. Just as he had as a student in the 407

Polipovka Yeshiva worshipped his rabbi, Reb Itzikel, so now, as a young surgeon in training in New York, he worshipped his teacher, Dr. Eugene Taylor. 

However, both Taylor and his devoted pupil, David Shanan, seemed to be affecting Peter Sparks adversely of late. The sympathetic humaneness which they showed towards his mother was interpreted by Peter as a kind of “ritual cruelty." 

The first time that David told Peter how well his mother had reacted to Dr. Taylor's visit Peter had shouted: "Taylor is a fanatical sentimentalist, even though you worship him like an idol! Do you know what I'll tell you? 

You're both sadists! You derive pleasure from a patient's torture. If you really had some compassion you'd cut short my mother's suffering instead of prolonging her wretched life." "I'm sorry Peter," David answered. "I know what you've been through lately, and I'm sure you're talking this way because of your grief. You wouldn't say things like that in your right mind," David replied. Then he again turned his attention to the patient.

It was, indeed, because of his overwhelming grief that Peter had spoken as he did. He could no longer witness his mother's struggles in the throes of death, or her being consumed by the horrible cancer. Nor could he disguise his anguish and heartache in front of her, but would sit on the edge of her bed looking desperate and helpless. She noticed this and instead of indulging in self-pity or complaining she would try to comfort him, saying: "Peter my dear, you look worried. I'm sorry to cause you so much aggravation." "Nonsense! Who's worried? What aggravation? I'm just tired that's all. There's so much work here at the hospital." "Yes, that's true; you doctors work hard. You can see that only when you're lying in the hospital. Poor David, he has such trouble with me. He's so devoted a true angel. How glad I am that you've got him as a friend, and that you've got Dr. Taylor as a teacher." 

Her words suddenly broke off. She writhed in pain. A wave of nausea enveloped her and she broke out in a sweat; 408

her eyes became glazed. She pleaded: "Nurse, please give me something; I can't stand it." "Mrs. Sparks, it isn't even two hours since your last injection," the nurse replied, glancing at her watch. "The orders are - every four hours." 

"It doesn't matter nurse," Peter snapped, hardly able to control himself. "Give her a hypo. I'll be responsible and 

I'll talk to Dr. Shanan about it."

A few minutes after the injection his mother's pinched, sallow face became tranquil. "What a wonder drug," Peter thought to himself. “And only a quarter-of-a-grain. . . Several grains at once and. . .” 

Peter got scared and jumped to his feet. He moved away from his mother's bed and reproached himself: "David is right; I'm not in my right mind."

Mrs. Sparks' pains became more and more unbearable. She had to be given injections at two-hour and then one-hour intervals. It had been several days since she had been able to swallow and retain a single drop of fluid. Feeding her intravenously became increasingly difficult. The veins on her arms and legs had become swollen and thrombosed as a result of the incessant puncturing with needles. "Calling Dr. Shanan, calling Dr. Shanan - Room Sixteen. 

Urgent. Room Sixteen. Urgent," echoed through the hospital. "Excuse me sir," said David, turning to Dr. Taylor with whom he was making ward rounds; "it's Room Sixteen- 

Mrs. Sparks. I've been having a hard time with the intravenous lately; her veins are all thrombosed. They're calling me. The infusion must have stopped again." "Sure, sure, go right over! Poor woman - it takes her so long... If you're having so much difficulty with the intravenous, resort to a cut-down. Isolate a deep untouched vein, introduce a needle of a good caliber into its lumen, and keep it in place with a silk suture. In that way you can assure a steady flow, for as long as it continues to be necessary." "Thank you, sir. I'll do that, sir."

At that moment Peter was also making ward rounds with 409

Dr. Newman. When he heard "Room Sixteen-urgent," he shuddered and became restless. "Excuse me sir; Room Sixteen. over to take a look.. 99 my mother. . . I'll run "But of course," Newman answered. "I'm sorry about your mother, but these are the cruelties of life and there's nothing you can do about it." "Yes sir, I imagine there's nothing you can do about it."

Both Peter and David arrived at Room Sixteen on the run. 

The nurse in attendance stood there distraught. “It's no use no matter what I do I can't get the intravenous to work. 

Look, it's clogged again." 

"Nurse, kindly fetch the necessary instruments for a cutdown from the Operating Room," David ordered. "The intravenous must not stop!" "It will take a while, Dr. Shanan. Who will watch the patient in the meantime?” "It's all right, nurse. Dr. Sparks and I will be here."

The nurse left the room. The patient had just been given an injection of morphine. She lay with closed eyes in a deep semi-coma. David deliberately fixed his gaze on the hanging flask of the intravenous serum in order to avoid looking into his friend's eyes haunted by fear and pain. 

Suddenly Peter gripped David's shoulders and pleaded in a trembling voice: "David, you can't be serious - you won't do a cut-down! 

It's inhuman! What do you want to accomplish with it? 

Please David, have mercy! Let her die peacefully!" He snatched the bottle of morphine from the nurse's table, and extending it to David, he whispered, drenched in perspiration, "A large dose of this will end her horrible torture. Do it for the love of God! Do it for the sake of mercy! Don't prolong her misery!" 

David struck Peter's outstretched hand; the flask of morphine dropped on the cement floor, breaking into a shower of glass. "Are you out of your mind? Do you realize what you're asking me to do? To commit murder!" "Murder? It's an act of mercy not murder! She's doomed 410

anyway; her death is unavoidable. . . It's a question of hours, days or weeks. Why should she suffer so much? My heart is breaking with pity for her." "It's not her you're pitying but yourself! You can't stand to see her suffer. You'd feel easier if it were all over with - and the sooner the better." "David, how can you talk like that? Can't you see how terribly she's suffering?" "No one knows how much or how little another's suffering is. You're a healthy young man and you're gauging your sick mother's reactions in terms of your own healthy conception of life. It seems to you that for her death is preferable to what she's going through now. But how do you know that she'd be willing to pay that price to end her suffering? Has it ever occurred to you that in order to live another couple of weeks, or couple of days, or even couple of hours, a person is often willing to accept the greatest suffering?" "Is it worth suffering that much in order to live for another couple of weeks or days?" "A couple of days? A couple of weeks? Do you consider that trivial? A new-born infant has prospects for a seventyyear span of life. Your mother has prospects to survive for a couple of weeks. Who has authorized you or me to decide that it's worth while to live a span of seventy years but not a couple of weeks? For me, two weeks are as much a segment of life worth preserving as the prospective seventy years of an infant. . .Besides, we are doctors. We have been entrusted with one of the holiest missions in the world to guard and protect the life of the sick against death and not to hand the victims over to death on a silver platter!"
