"Don't talk to me about your holy mission! The mission of the A.S.P.C.A. is more humane than yours. They are more considerate of animals than we doctors are of human beings. 

They don't allow a dying animal to suffer; they stop its agony while we doctors prolong it. . . Sacred mission indeed!

In whose name do we carry out such missions without a spark of mercy?" "In the name of the One, who instills in us love and compassion – in the name of the Healer Of All Flesh. How can 411

you compare the ethical values of a human life with those of an animal? How can you compare the compassion towards animals with the compassion towards human beings? Why, we kill animals in the prime of their lives and enjoy the delicious meals we prepare from the meat of their bodies. 

And you offer me the example of compassion towards animals as a guide toward compassion to sick humans?” "It's easy for you to talk. It's easy for you to philosophize. 

You'd talk and think differently if this poor, shrivelled-up sick woman were your mother."

A long dormant, apparently healed wound seemed to rip open inside of David as he answered: "Please, Peter, believe me that my heart is full of pity for your mother. I pray to God for her just as I did when my own mother lay dying in the hospital of the same disease." 

Just then Mrs. Sparks woke up. Both internes fell silent and stood staring at her in surprise. Her eyes were crystalclear, and a smile of happiness transformed her wizened face... A gentle breeze wafted through the open window, raising and parting the long thin curtains through which rays of pleasant Spring sunlight streamed into the small hospital room. 

"Peter, David - I'm so happy to see you both," Mrs. Sparks said in a weak voice, as she stretched out her skeletal hands. "How wonderfully beautiful the sun is... It's a georgeous 

Spring how I love Spring. . ." She inhaled with visible effort and continued: "It's good to live and to smell the sweet fragrance of Spring. It makes up for the suffering a person has to go through sometimes to stay alive. I do so want to live."

Peter went down on his knees by her bedside and kissing his mother's forehead, spoke tenderly to her: "My dear mother, my good, wonderful mother - sure you'll live! You're getting along fine - very fine. . .” 

Mrs. Sparks held her son's head and pressed it against her bony chest as she mumbled: "I hope so, my son I hope so. 

It's good to live and to be with you.. 

She felt another stabbing pain. Her eyelids closed and her lips twisted pitifully. The nurse returned with a tray loaded 412

with all the instruments for the cut-down. "Nurse," David instructed, "give the patient another hypo and expose her right leg, please. I'll proceed with the cutdown immediately.”

Peter stood up. He placed his hand on David's shoulder, gripped it gratefully, and left the room, his eyes downcast. 413

Healer of all Flesh

For the past thirty-six hours Esther had been in the hospital suffering excruciating labor pains, but still she had not given birth. Severe pains recurring every five minutes stiffened her abdominal muscles, but the short distance from the mother's womb to the great world outside still remained too narrow a passage for the baby to push through. These persistent pains which had already lasted for thirty-six hours had weakened Esther so, that she now lay completely exhausted and resigned. 

David stood near Esther and each time she cried out fearfully during a seizure of pain, David lost himself and did not know what to do. He would caress and kiss her. She gripped his hands spasmodically, digging her nails in so hard that his palms bled.

Her membranes had ruptured long ago but the baby was still far from able to force its head through the neck of the womb. This endless labor, along with her loss of blood, brought Esther to a state that threatened both her life and that of the baby. "David help me," she begged him. "You're a doctor - such a good, kind doctor - why don't you help me?”

All his medical knowledge seemed to have flown from David. He stood by watching Esther hovering between life and death, trying to give birth to his first child and was unable to assist her in any way. He could not even think medically; he only knew and felt that he would gladly give his life to relieve her of her misery. He was filled with pity and despair. 414

The obstetrician, Dr. Corby, called David out of Esther's room and said to him: “Shanan, your wife's condition is rather serious. I want to consult a surgeon." "A surgeon?" David repeated apprehensively. "Why a surgeon?" "Because the muscles of her uterus have been weakened so much now that they've lost the power of contracting and expanding with enough force to push the baby out. We can't wait any longer. I think that a Caeserian section should be performed immediately and a surgical opinion is imperative. 

You're an interne here and you've worked under many different surgeons. The patient is your wife; it's your privilege to choose whichever member of the surgical staff you want." "Dr. Taylor," David answered automatically.

Dr. Corby lost no time in telephoning the Operating Pavilion. He was informed that Dr. Taylor had just begun a gastrectomy and would not be available for at least another three hours. "Shanan, three hours is too long to wait now. "Who's your second choice?" "Dr. Newman," David replied, after a moment's hesitation.

While Dr. Corby and Dr. Newman were examining Esther, David stood outside pressing on his chest with both hands to slow down the tempo of his pounding heart. It suddenly occurred to him that Esther's life and the life of their child depended on the decision of these two men huddled in consultation. Closing his eyes, he fervently mumbled: "I beseech Thee, O Lord, Healer Of All Flesh, assuage my pain and renew my youth as the eagle's. Bestow wisdom upon the physician that he may cure my wound."

What the two physicians had to tell David was far from encouraging. David knew Newman well enough to realize that he would give his opinion coldly and matter-of-factly without any sentimentality, and he therefore stared steadily at him as if to say that he was prepared for any verdict. "Shanan," Newman began, "we're faced with a serious problem, and a decision must be reached immediately. These are the facts: The patient's labor pains for the past thirtysix hours along with her steady loss of blood have weakened 415

her so much that she is now in a state of shock. Her life is in danger, especially if any infection were to develop now, and in a case like this that's almost unavoidable. The process of delivery stopped half-way. Her whole pelvic area is edematous and covered with blood and mucus which makes it a fertile breeding-ground for all sorts of micro-organisms. The delivery must be terminated now without any further delay. An emergency Caesarian section is the only way to save your wife's life. I must warn you, though, that the child's condition is not favorable; it shows very weak signs of life. It may be necessary to sacrifice the life of the child in order to save that of the mother - and we need your consent to it." 

Without uttering a word David transferred his gaze from 

Dr. Newman to Dr. Corby, pleading mutely for guidance.

Dr. Corby looked at David sympathetically, and nodding his head he repeated several times: "The mother must be saved; it may become necessary to give up the child. The mother must be saved at all costs." 

David remained as silent as if he had been struck dumb.

Deep in his heart he felt certain that one life should not be sacrificed for another. He felt also that a good doctor does not enter into compromising peace-negotiations with death, as if saying: "You have two human lives in your hands; let's one for you and one for me." No, this was not the mission of the emissary of the Healer Of All Flesh!

His mission was to save every human life and to have faith that he was doing the right thing. come to terms 

· • This last thought frightened him. He suddenly realized that in his own crisis he lacked that faith. He recalled the case of Mrs. Marshall. There he had had no misgivings; he had had faith that the will of the woman would overpower the judgment of the doctors. Now, however, when his Esther's life was at stake, he lacked the same sureness of conviction. “Shanan, we are losing precious moments," Newman remarked. “I wouldn't want to be sorry later for having wasted so much valuable time. We are only waiting for your consent." "You have my consent," David said at last, "Esther's life must be saved." 416

Newman promptly contacted the Operating Pavilion: "Hello! Miss MacDonald? Dr. Newman speaking. I have an emergency Caesarian section. Yes, it's very urgent - every minute counts. The patient? Esther Shanan."

Ruth shivered when she heard the name of Esther Shanan. 

She understood immediately that the only reason why Newman had been called for this case was because Dr. Taylor was unavailable. Although she knew that according to medical ethics a physician will treat even his worst enemies, she was nevertheless a little uneasy. She had no doubt that Newman would carry out his duties as a surgeon conscientiously and with all the skill at his disposal. Yet she could not help remembering that evening at her apartment when Newman had expressed his hatred for David so openly and viciously and that memory increased her apprehension even more. 

Esther was wheeled into the preparatory side room. Her small, pale face appeared like that of a child. Her huge, round belly protruded grotesquely under the white cover, as if some gigantic mass had been deposited upon the tender body of a youngster. She breathed heavily, emitting a weak sigh from time to time. Ruth bent over her, and placing her hand on Esther's forehead said gently: "Hello Esther. How do you feel, dear?"

Esther opened her eyes, glazed from fatigue and strain and managing a smile, she whispered: "Ruth, I'm so glad to see you. Where am I? What are they going to do with me? Where is David? Why isn't David near me?" "Don't get excited, dear. You're in the Operating Pavilion. 

You will have your child here." "When?" "Soon 

very soon." "Ruth, stay with me please. Please Ruth, don't leave me alone. I'm afraid I don't want to die." "Don't be afraid, my dear; there is nothing to be scared of. 

I will stay with you."

A nurse came into the room and asked: "Miss MacDonald, am I the scrub-nurse in this case? Everything is ready; can 

I start washing up?" 417

"No, Miss Stanley. I myself will be the scrub-nurse in this case," Ruth answered.

Dr. Newman and Dr. Corby stood over the deep washbasins in the Operating Room scrubbing their arms up to their elbows. From time to time they glanced up at the clock which hung on the wall facing them, to see how many of the ten required "scrub-minutes" had passed. As usual they discussed the case they were about to operate upon. "It's lucky that she's naturally so slender," Dr. Corby commented. "That makes the operation a lot easier. Don't you think so?" "That's usually true in surgical cases. Here, though, the risk is pretty great anyway. She has lost too much blood and she is terribly weak. This operation must be performed in a minimum of time with a minimum of bleeding. A hemorrhage during the operation could be fatal." "Poor woman," Dr. Corby remarked sorrowfully. "I'm afraid she will lose the child." "There can't even be any talk now of the child. Let's save the mother and I'll be satisfied." "That Shanan is a strange fellow," Dr. Corby changed the subject. "I was afraid there for a couple of minutes that he wasn't going to give us his consent." "He's like all preachers. They preach for the benefit of others, but when it concerns themselves they act like ordinary human beings," Newman commented sarcastically. 

The ten "scrub-minutes" ended. Newman put on the long white smock, pulled on the rubber gloves and walked over to the operating table. Esther lay swathed in surgical draperies with only a long narrow segment, in the middle of her protruding belly, exposed. "Condition of patient, please," Newman asked the anesthetist. "Pulse, ninety; blood pressure 110 over 80; breathing, regular; the patient is asleep; you can proceed." 

Newman stretched out his hand to the scrub-nurse: "Scalpel please."

As he touched the handle of the scalpel, Newman instinctively recognized a pleasant intimacy. He turned his 418

head, and to his great surprise, saw Ruth as scrub-nurse. 

Their masks hid the rest of their features but their eyes met and spoke to each other wordlessly. Newman at once guessed Ruth's motive for assisting at this operation on David's wife and it hurt him deeply. Ruth, in turn, was aware that Newman was hurt but had no regrets about her deed. "I want the patient's blood-pressure maintained at no less than the present level. Use intravenous without let-up and also adrenalin if necessary," Newman ordered the anesthetist.

He made a quick incision across the entire length of the patient's abdomen.

The thinned-out skin and muscles were retracted easily like layers peeled from an overripe fruit. The uterus, with its protruding fullness, came into view. Newman incised, and a mass of dark clotted blood oozed forth. In the next instant, Newman thrust his hands into the opened void and delivered the baby. "Not a sign of life," he said, and cutting the umbilical cord between two clamps he placed the baby on a table nearby. "Hot towels, please!" he ordered. "She mustn't lose too much blood - that's our aim!"

Ruth's extensive experience as an operating-room nurse came in handy. All the required instruments, clamps, needles, and catgut, were at Newman's disposal even before he had a chance to call for them. Everyone handled the necessary equipment efficiently, working together like a precision machine - rhythmically, harmoniously, and on split-second timing.

The baby was violet-blue; it did not greet the world with an outcry; its lungs made no effort at breathing. It lay lifeless and motionless, its hands and feet stretched out, its head dangling like that of a bird which has been shot, and its tiny body soft as dough, without the least stiffness in a single muscle.

Everybody regarded the baby as dead. Only Dr. Corby, the experienced obstetrician, noticed the skin on the left side of the baby's chest flutter lightly a couple of times. "I think the heart is still beating. Excuse me, Dr. Newman - you finish the operation without me and I'll work on the 

419

baby. Two basins of water, quick - one warm, the other cold!"

Dr. Corby dipped the little body in the warm water for a few seconds, lifted it out and then dipped it in the cold water for a few seconds. He repeated this procedure several times. 

"Warmth expands, cold shrinks — this sets the muscles in motion. The baby is asphyxiated. A single breath! If it would only take one breath! . . .”

Dr. Corby suddenly opened the child's tiny mouth and sealing it with his own, breathed into it with all his might. 

The baby began to choke, its little chest expanded, and an infant's cry pierced the room.

Dr. Corby's perspiring face beamed with happiness as he declared: "It's a boy - I didn't notice before. Yes sir, a boy and a fine lad at that." 

"It's alive, it's alive, it will be all right!" the nurses and orderlies called out happily. "Condition of patient good," the usually monotonous voice of the anesthetist now rang out loudly and joyfully, "pulse, excellent; blood-pressure unchanged!"

Dr. Corby tied the umbilical cord, removed the clamps, wrapped the child in a warm cover and handing it to one of the nurses, remarked: "Take good care of it; this child is a gift from God Himself."

Newman remained aloof from the surrounding commotion. 

Without looking up from the surgical wound, he sutured one layer after another with his nimble fingers. Ruth stood at her post, serving the surgeon; tears of joy trickled from her eyes soaking through the mask over her face.

As soon as Esther opened her eyes after the anesthetic, her first words were: "My baby - I want my baby."

David bent over her, and as though confiding a great secret he whispered to her: "Esther dear, it's a boy - we have a son." "You're not telling me the truth," she wailed loudly. "I lost my baby - I want my baby."

The nurse gave her an injection and she calmed down and 420

fell into a deep sleep. On the following morning she again clamored for the baby. The nurse brought in the infant and placed him at Esther's side, saying: 

"Here he is 'the gift from God.'" Eyeing David coquettishly she added, “A fine lad – I wonder who he looks like?"

The child was asleep, its tiny lips puckering up as though it were dreaming of suckling at its mother's breast. Esther cautiously put one arm around the infant, and extending her other arm to David, she said in awe: “A son! A true, living son! I bore you a son! We will name him Avrum after your father. Yes, Avrum Shanan has 

been brought back to life!"

David's heart was filled with gratitude to God, as he looked down at Esther and the infant. Words were inadequate to express his happiness. He stood fascinated and silent...

It was the last month of David's interneship. The time when he would become an independent practicing surgeon was drawing near. Like every interne completing his training and about to launch into private practice, David was apprehensive about the various problems, hardships, and responsibilities inevitably associated with starting out in this profession. He was chiefly disturbed, however, by his own lack of confidence in his ability to undertake this task, especially when he compared himself to Dr. Taylor whom he idolized. During his three-year training period at the hospital he had become fully convinced that Dr. Taylor was the embodiment of the perfect emissary of the Healer 

Of All Flesh. Dr. Taylor was the only surgeon who cherished a patient's life above his own. David regarded him with great awe and deep in his heart hoped that he would one day achieve those qualities of his ideal. . . "Why have you been so moody and worried lately?" Esther asked him once, when he had been lying beside her for a long time unable to fall asleep. "I'm thinking that in thirty days I won't be an interne anymore." "Is that a cause for worry? I should think that it's one of 421

the happiest moments in a doctor's life. On his own, at last! 

Facing the world on his own at last! On his own responsibility!" "Yes, that's how it should be, but strangely enough it often feels differently. . . I remember how, when I entered the 

Polipovka State Academy, my aim was to graduate as soon as possible so I could be admitted to the University. But when graduation time came I felt sad. . . I had become very fond of the State Academy and the thought of leaving it made me nostalgic. The life of an interne is wonderful and in a way sort of carefree. You're sad at the thought of leaving it..." "At the University you found new interests that kept you from longing for the State Academy. I am sure that as an independent surgeon, in your private practice you will also find new interests to help you overcome the nostalgia for your life as an interne.” "Private practice - it scares me so." "Why?" "Probably because I'm not sure I'm completely prepared for it." "You're talking nonsense. You're a good doctor, you've had excellent training, you have a very pleasant personality and you are devoted to your patients. Completely prepared? 

Of course you are prepared!" "I often think: Why should a patient come to me when he could go to such a genius like Taylor?" "Taylor wasn't born a genius; he became one and you can become one too. Look at Dr. Newman. He got his training under Taylor but that didn't give him an inferiority complex about being as good as Taylor, or keep him from going into private practice on his own when the time came for it. Patients know they could go to Dr. Taylor, but they also go to Dr. Newman, and will also go to Dr. Shanan and to many other surgeons." "Maybe you're right," David answered pensively. "I guess if my ambition was to become a Newman it would be easier for me now. It seems, however, that to become a Taylor is much more difficult." 422

"Newman isn't a bad surgeon. You must think so yourself or you wouldn't have entrusted the lives of your wife and 

Ichild to him." 

David turned his head toward Esther, kissed her eyelids, and said amiably: "Good night dear; I must get some rest - there is a heavy operating schedule tomorrow." 

Something unusual occurred on the following day - Dr. 

Taylor failed to show up in the Operating Pavilion. Someone had telephoned from his home to say that he was not feeling well, and that Dr. Newman was to take charge of his surgical cases for the day.

Ruth MacDonald became restless. During the past fifteen years that she had worked with Dr. Taylor he had never missed a day's work in the Operating Room. Sometimes he would come in coughing because of a cold, or suffering with indigestion, and she would say to him: "You don't seem to be up to par today, sir. Why don't you take a day or two off?" "It's nothing, Miss MacDonald," he would answer, “just a slight cold. I assure you that the patients who have to be operated on are worse off than I am.” "You're working too hard, sir," Ruth would remonstrate. "You ought to rest more you are not taking care of your- 

self. I'll complain to Mrs. Taylor." "Miss MacDonald," he replied, "I want you to know that 

Mrs. Taylor is happy when I am happy, and I am happy when

I operate. It's morally wrong for a surgeon to waste his time at home and neglect sick people who need surgery just because he has a cold or a little belly-ache. Besides, what right do you have to encourage me to take time off? You are no better than I am. At least I take a month's vacation in the mountains every summer, whereas you always cut your vacation short and rush back to the hospital. I notice things like that... Miss MacDonald, we practice medicine. It's a life of sacrifice - and that's how it should be." 

Her heart would fill with joy. At moments like this she would be grateful for the privilege of working side by side with Dr. Taylor. 423

Ruth's present restfulness increased because of a bad premonition she had. She became sad. She was unable to free herself of a vague fear that some misfortune was pending. 

The only one who thoroughly understood the cause of Ruth MacDonald's current frame of mind was Newman. "She made an idol out of him," Newman reflected, feeling vengefully gleeful over the present situation; "and now the idol is indisposed and has left his beloved lamb feeling alone and forsaken." 

Newman knew Taylor well enough to realize that no slight indisposition would deter him from the performance of his duties at the operating table. Whatever was keeping him at home was probably quite serious. Inwardly Newman felt pleased. This might be the beginning of Taylor's end. 

Who knows? The post of surgeon-in-chief might suddenly become vacant. In Dr. Newman's mind there now gleamed a bright hope for a new milestone in his career. • · "Good morning Miss MacDonald," Newman greeted Ruth in her office, using a tone in which authority overshadowed any friendliness, "what's today's schedule for Dr. Taylor's operations?"

Ruth handed him the record book as well as the timeschedule, names of the patients, diagnoses, surgeon, assistants, and anesthetist. Newman looked at the record then said with restrained irony: "I can't understand it! Why does he do this? What guilt is he anxious to redeem?" "I beg your pardon, but I don't know what you are talking about," Ruth answered with astonishment. "Look at the schedule yourself," Newman said, pointing to the record. "First case - possible abscess of the appendix, public patient; second case - breast biopsy, public patient; third case - cyst on the left ovary, public patient; fourth and last case - hemorrhoidectomy, private, and the patient is none other than Ronald B. Cooper, the oil magnate! Why keep him for last? Why isn't he the first on the list? The others are public cases they can wait. Public cases are usually operated on by senior internes anyway."

Newman's tone and particularly his disparaging attitude 424

toward Dr. Taylor's arrangement of the operating schedule upset Ruth very much. "Dr. Newman," she replied politely but sternly, "Dr.

Taylor makes up the schedule according to the degree of seriousness and urgency of the cases and not according to the patient's status on Wall Street." "Miss MacDonald, it's mighty nice to be generous to the poor, but you should have a sense of proportion. After all, it's the rich who with their millions make it possible to build and maintain hospitals. Don't you think that the least we doctors could do to thank them would be to extend to them consideration and special preference when they come to us as patients?"

Silently, Ruth fixed her deep-blue eyes on Newman. He met her gaze with an air of dogged resolution and challenge: "The hemorrhoidectomy first!" he ordered. "Please send for Mr. Ronald B. Cooper in ten minutes. That will give me enough time to go into his room and make his acquaintance before I operate on him. As for those three other cases they will be operated on by the senior interne in attendance." 

Every interne in surgery waits impatiently for the moment when he is permitted to operate by himself, at first on minor cases and then on the more serious ones. David had already operated on several occasions, but such a rare opportunity as the one that presented itself today to operate on three major cases in succession — he had never experienced before. 

Under normal circumstances he would naturally have been overjoyed by this. Today, however, he could not enjoy it and felt depressed and worried. 

He was depressed because of Taylor's sudden indisposition. 

He remembered making ward rounds with him on the previous night. As usual they had discussed the cases scheduled for operations on the following day. He now recalled that 

Taylor had appeared very tired and had paused to inhale deeply from time to time, squeezing his left shoulder with his right hand and lightly massaging the fingers of his left hand. "Anything wrong, sir?" David had asked. 425

"No, just a little stiffness," Taylor had answered. "A hot shower will take care of it." "At what time do you wish to start on the first case tomorrow sir?" "Why do you ask? Eight, as usual." Taylor had answered sternly, adding "I am very anxious to see what we will find in the first case. It's a complicated clinical picture." "The history and the physical examination, sir, seem to indicate an appendiceal abscess. What else could it be?" 

"It's not that simple, Shanan. The patient has had pains in the right lower quadrant for the past eight days — also, a low-grade fever, nausea, and some vomiting. The examination reveals a round tender mass in the region of the appendix. The diagnosis of appendicitis with the probable formation of an abscess is therefore quite likely and logical. 

But other questions arise. Why is the leucocyte blood count only 9,000? Appendicitis with an abscess ought to raise the count to 15 or 20,000. Also, the temperature isn't high enough for an appendiceal abscess. The patient's general condition doesn't seem to indicate the toxicity which is so characteristic of this kind of infection. Rectal examination showed a certain amount of fullness in the right side, but again, not enough for an appendiceal abcess. As you see, Shanan, it's not that simple." "What do you think it is, sir?” "The best guess is still your diagnosis and that's the way we'll post it in the operating room. One must however be prepared for other possibilities too." "What are the other possibilities, sir?” 

Dr. Taylor did not answer; he again drew a deep breath, opening and closing the fingers of his left hand. .

Now David was mentally re-assessing Dr. Taylor's behavior on the preceding evening. Taylor's unusual fatigue, his frequent pauses for deep inhalations, the way he had kept squeezing at his left shoulder, and the numbness he had apparently felt in the fingers of his left hand - all of these things now seemed to David to be symptomatic of much more than "a little stiffness" that "a hot shower will take care of," and this depressed him. 426

He was worried because the diagnosis on the first case he was about to operate on was not certain. Although Taylor had agreed yesterday that the diagnosis of appendiceal abscess was a reasonable one, his remarks pointing out the other possibilities had been too important to be ignored. 

The problem had so intrigued David that he had stayed up past midnight on the previous night poring over his textbooks, and delving into accounts of differential diagnosis of appendicitis. He had done this purely from an academic standpoint because the practical aspect did not worry him. 

He knew he could rely on Taylor - he would clear up everything at the operation. The one thing he had not expected was that he and not Taylor would be operating. 

After Newman had completed the hemorrhoidectomy on the oil tycoon, Ronald B. Cooper, he turned to David who had assisted him, and asked: "How long did it take us, Shanan?" "Twelve minutes, sir; a very fine and smooth job, sir." "Thank you, Shanan. I hope that the three public patients have survived this delay. . . You can now begin on your own cases. Who is going to assist you?" "Dr. Sparks and one of the junior assistants." "Fine. Good luck!" "Sir," David spoke hesitantly, "I would like to have a few words with you about Mrs. Carelli." "Who is Mrs. Carelli?" "She's the first case 

of three small children.” a young woman, 34 years old, mother "Without drama please," Newman cut him short. "Her marital status doesn't interest me. What's the diagnosis?” "A possible appendiceal abscess." "Good; the answer is operate!" 

"Yes sir, but there are certain clinical aspects that are not entirely in accordance with this diagnosis.' "What's the difference? You've got to open her up anyway. In surgery, you don't hesitate; you open and you see!

That's the great advantage surgery has over all other branches of medicine. No speculations and no long-winded discussions! 

You open up, you look, and you know! 'Veni, vidi, vici'! A 427

surgeon must not be in doubt and must not let himself be influenced at all by the vagaries of human emotions. The surgeon's only motto must be: a sober mind, a calloused heart, and a swift, sure hand! If you have to cut, you cut!"

Having had his say, Newman turned on his heel and left. 

David felt increasingly uneasy. "Who knows," he thought, "perhaps Newman's motto is right. Why do I feel so uncertain? Maybe I do lack the necessary qualifications for a good surgeon."

He was so absorbed in his own thoughts that he failed to notice that Mrs. Carelli had already been wheeled into the Operating Room. 

Ruth MacDonald came over to him, and said: "Good luck, Dr. Shanan. I am sure that Dr. Taylor will telephone later and inquire about everything. I can't believe that his illness, whatever it is, would keep him from thinking about what is going on in the hospital." 

David was still wrapped in thought as though he had not heard what Ruth had told him. At any other time she would have been surprised, because it was not like David to be inattentive when spoken to. Today, however, his unusual behavior did not surprise her. She attributed it to a depression similar to her own, probably also due to Dr. Taylor's sudden absence. "It's strange not to see Dr. Taylor in the Operating Pavilion," she remarked quietly. "He's being missed. . .” "Yes, Miss MacDonald he certainly is missed. If you only knew how much he's missed," David answered, and began to prepare himself for the operation.

Mrs. Carelli took the anesthetic without any difficulty and dozed off quickly, breathing regularly and rhythmically. The anesthetist declared that the patient's blood-pressure, pulse, and general condition were excellent. David made an incision on the right side of her abdomen. As he began to retract the muscles and got close to the peritoneum, he was amazed to find the surrounding tissue normal in appearance. An acute inflammation of the appendix with abscess formation would have produced a certain degree of redness and edema in this area. 428

He opened the peritoneum. There was no abscess. With every breath of the sleeping patient, loops of intestine that appeared completely normal protruded outward. Manipulating with the patient's caecum, David brought forth the appendix which revealed no signs of inflammation. 

David was in a cold sweat. The thought exploded in his mind – “My diagnosis was wrong! It's not appendicitis! It's not an abscess! What is it?" 

He put his hand into the hollow of the abdominal wound and felt what he had so erroneously diagnosed as an appendiceal abscess. It was a round mass situated in the bony pelvis outside of the peritoneum. The mass was not hard and did not adhere to the neighboring structures. "What do you think it is?" Sparks, who was assisting him, asked. 

"I don't know," David answered, his hand still lodged in the abdomen. "I can feel the ovaries and the uterus they are normal. Besides, this mass is retroperitoneal anyhow. 

It must be a tumor, a retroperitoneal tumor. You put your hand in there and see what you think of it."

Sparks did so and fumbling with the mass, stated: "Retroperitoneal all right- low in the bony pelvis. There are a lot of blood-vessels around the mass; I can feel their pulsation. I don't know what it is. The best guess is a retroperitoneal tumor, maybe a sarcoma. Why don't you call 

Newman in and let him have a look at it?" "No, not Newman; I know his answer in advance. We'll remove the mass!"

As soon as David had made this decision his misgivings disappeared. He proceeded to remove the tumor quickly and fearlessly from the thick fibrous tissues in which it was situated. He clamped, tied, and cut all the blood-vessels and cordlike tissues which had kept the tumor fixed in its place. 

He cut the last vessel and the dislodged mass lay in his hands. 

The fact that David did not know exactly what it was that he had just removed, plunged him once again into a fever of anxiety. He had no patience to complete the operation and asked Sparks to close up. He grabbed the specimen and hastened to pathology with it. 429

The chief pathologist, Dr. Walter Clark, happened to be in his laboratory. "Hello, Shanan. I hear that Dr. Taylor is not feeling well. 

What's the matter with him?" Clark inquired. "I don't know, sir, it happened only this morning. We haven't heard anything further from him yet." "Well, I hope it's nothing serious. Now, let's have a look at what you've got there. What's the diagnosis?" "I don't know, sir." 

"Oh, it's one of those! You want to catch the pathologist!" 

Dr. Clark jested, as he scrutinized the specimen. “Hm, it's interesting - very interesting. Let's cut it open and see whether it's what I think it is."

Dr. Clark bisected the mass lengthwise into symmetrical halves, and extending these to David, remarked: "Yes, exactly as I thought - a kidney."

David paled, and felt his knees buckling under him. "A kidney?" He barely articulated, "so low in the bony pelvis?" "That's not unusual - it's an ectopic kidney. If it happens to be on the right side it frequently brings about symptoms very much like those of appendicitis." "A kidney - an ectopic kidney!" David murmured. "That possibility never even occurred to me. I was sure I had removed a retroperitoneal tumor." "That, my friend, is a mistake that others have made before you and I can assure you that you won't be the last and others will make it after you. It's not such a great calamity — as long as the patient has a kidney on the other side. If it ever happens that the ectopic kidney is the only one with which someone has been born, then. 

99 "Excuse me, sir," David suddenly interrupted him. “Thank you sir. I must hurry back, sir," and he hastened out. "Poor fellow," Dr. Clark said to himself shaking his head. "He is still young and green. A mistake and he can't take it. He won't forget it for a long time." 

David ran back to the Operating Pavilion, breathless. Sparks had just finished suturing the patient's skin and was about to bandage the closed wound. The orderlies stood by ready 430

to transfer the patient from the operating table to a stretcher in order to wheel her back to the ward. "Don't take the patient back to the ward!" David ordered. "Take her directly to the X-ray department!"

All those present were astonished. Sparks pulled the mask off his face and advancing toward David, asked: "X-ray? Now? The patient is still under the anesthetic. 

What's happened? Why are you so excited? What did the specimen show?" “A kidney! An ectopic kidney! I cut out a kidney and I didn't know what I was cutting! I don't even know whether the patient has a kidney on the other side! Unforgivable! I must take X-rays at once to see if there is a kidney on the other side. I'm going out of my mind!” 

"Relax, David; it's nobody's fault this was a very unusual case. If I were you I'd phone Taylor immediately and tell him about this, or better still, tell Newman about it; he's in charge now. In a complicated situation it's best to be covered by a staff surgeon. Come, come, let's talk to 

Newman before he leaves."

When he had heard the details of the operation, Newman said calmly without batting an eye: "There is no sense in taking X-rays now. You will know the result within a few hours anyhow. The presence or absence of urine will determine whether there is or is not another kidney. Well, in surgery everyone does his best. Every surgeon loses some cases and you have to be prepared for it. You don't have to get hysterical about it. 'Where timber is cut, chips fly!' . . . Proceed with the other two cases." "Dr. Newman," David pleaded, "the other two are not urgent; I would like to postpone them until tomorrow." "As you wish," Newman replied coldly. "And another thing, sir," David continued unsteadily, "may I go on with the X-rays? Waiting for the natural outcome of the case will drive me insane." 

An ironic smile played about the corners of Newman's mouth. Eyeing David disdainfully he declared: "Why Taylor ever encouraged you to become a surgeon 

I'll never understand. . . All right, take X-rays if you want to!” 431

Mrs. Carelli was still under the influence of the anesthetic when she was wheeled into the X-ray Department. She had already awakened from her deep sleep and was crying in a monotonous whine.

To bring a patient in for X-rays immediately following an operation and before she had completely recovered from the anesthetic was so unusual that the entire X-ray Department staff - chief radiologist, assistants, internes, and technicians were all astounded. Their surprise became even greater when they learned the reason for this haste to X-ray the patient.

All the Department personnel burned with curiosity. Seldom had so much depended on what would be disclosed on an X-ray film. In a few minutes an ordinary X-ray would determine whether this young woman was to live or die of uremia within forty-eight hours. The staff, so accustomed to the usual routine of taking X-ray pictures, now stood tingling with excitement around the table on which Mrs. 

Carelli lay. They all had their eyes fixed upon the small red bulb above the locked door of the darkroom where the film was now being developed. 

At last the bell rang, sounding like an alarm clock - the sign that the picture was ready. Then faintly from behind the door of the darkroom, the technician's outcry, "It's all right! 

It's there!"

The door opened and a dripping film was raised up toward the light of the lamp. It revealed a good-functioning kidney on the left side. They all breathed a sigh of relief. Their faces beamed happily as they returned to their posts. 

There was no happy smile of relief on David's face, nor could he return contentedly to his usual hospital duties. 

He felt beaten and crushed. He was thankful that Mrs.

Carelli would survive but in his mind this did not minimize by one iota the enormity of his mistake. For the first time on his long and arduous road toward his life's goal, he had stumbled into a deep, treacherous abyss, about which he knew nothing until now the abyss of mistakes! Mistakes that 

432

kill! The thought kept hammering in his mind: "I made a mistake which only by a miracle didn't kill a 34-year-old mother of three small children. Where is the assurance that I won't do it tomorrow - and this time without a miracle to save the patient. By what right can I continue to endanger human lives?"

Steeped in these thoughts, David returned to his room in the internes' quarters, threw himself on his cot and murmured in his despair: “Oh, Lord, Healer Of All Flesh, help me! Relieve me of the fear of the abyss, that has sprung open before my eyes!"

His belief in the human ethics which had been instilled in him by his mother, his father, Reb Itzikel, and Samborik had always helped to lead him out of darkness and show him the road of justice. Now again, he implored their guidance with all his heart and soul - but he sensed that this time he could expect no help from them. It seemed to him as if each of them was answering: "Here, my child, you must find your own way." "Every surgeon loses some cases," Newman had said. "Where timber is cut, chips fly." As he recalled Newman's words

David could feel his temples pounding. He squeezed his head with both hands. "No - men are not timber, and human lives are not chips!" his entire being clamored in protest. "That cannot be the road - it must not be that way!" 

David suddenly sat up. "Taylor!" flashed through his mind. "Why haven't I thought of Taylor? I must see him — I must talk with him. It's my only hope." 

Still wearing his interne's uniform David left the hospital, made his way through Central Park, and rang the bell at 

Dr. Taylor's residence. 

"Good afternoon, Dr. Shanan sir," Thomas, the old Negro servant greeted David with a mixture of cordiality and surprise. "It's good to see you, Dr. Shanan. Anything happened?

It's a bit unusual for you to call in the middle of the day and in hospital clothes at that. Everything is unusual today. 

Dr. Taylor didn't go to the hospital — and that's never happened before. He has never been sick before, either, but today 

433

Dr. Chester came to examine him. And now here you are too in the middle of the day, and in white. Yes sir, Dr.

Shanan, everything has been unusual today." "Who is it, Thomas? Who are you talking to?" Mrs. Taylor's mellow voice could be heard as she came down the winding stairway which led from the bedrooms on the second floor. 

Catching sight of David, she too greeted him with surprise. "Dr. Shanan, how nice of you to come. This is a strange coincidence - Dr. Taylor just mentioned your name and told me that he would like to see you. A real case of mental telepathy!" "Yes ma'am - very strange things happen in life," David replied. "How does Dr. Taylor feel? I hope it's not serious." "I don't know what to say. My husband is not a good patient; he does just the opposite of what he advises his own patients to do. Dr. Chester says that he should stay in bed and rest for several weeks but my husband laughs at that. I have barely managed to keep him at home today.” "What does Dr. Chester think it is?" "He suspects angina pectoris," Mrs. Taylor answered, lowering her voice. "Angina pectoris?" David repeated with concern. "That could be very serious. Rest is important for him. I think I had better not bother him now - I will come and see him again some other time." "No, no, Dr. Shanan - please don't go away. He's very anxious to talk to you. If he should find out that you were here and didn't go up to see him he'd be very upset over it. 

Please come with me."

Dr. Taylor was sitting up in his bed, his back propped against a raised pillow, browsing through a medical journal. 

David's sudden appearance caught him off guard. Taking off his reading glasses, he regarded his guest with astonishment. "You see, Eugene dear," Mrs. Taylor said, "your wishes are instantly fulfilled. Imagine, just after saying you wanted to see Dr. Shanan the door opened and in he came. I have just told him that it must be a real case of telepathy." "Call it what you like, my dear, but I'm very glad he 434

came. Well, Shanan, what's new at the hospital? Sit down, tell me all about it." "If you want to talk shop," Mrs. Taylor interjected, “I'll leave you two alone. But please, not for long. Eugene dear, remember you've got to rest," she pleaded, and left.

It was quiet in the room, as Taylor and David looked silently at each other. Taylor knew his beloved pupil well, and could perceive the grief and despair reflected in David's eyes. "Well, Shanan," Taylor was the first to speak, "what happened? It's spread all over your face."

After listening to David's account of the Carelli case, Taylor thought for a moment and then began: "Yes, the responsibility of a surgeon, my friend, is always very great. Human lives are in your hands the slightest mistake may cost a human life. A surgeon must always bear that danger in mind." "But always to be mindful of danger means to be afraid," David protested. "How can one have the courage to operate if one is always afraid?” 66 "How can one have the courage to operate if one is always afraid?'" Taylor repeated, raising his voice, and with a glint of anger in his eyes. “On the contrary! The more you fear, the greater your courage. You don't need courage to do things that you are not afraid to do! A surgeon must have fear. If he has no fear then he shouldn't be a surgeon! I've got fears to this day! There isn't a single time I pick up a scalpel when my heart doesn't pound with fear! Fear is the highest value of human responsibility. Of all people it was you, Shanan, who I had expected would grasp the meaning of this. Don't you know that the entire foundation of religion rests on the fear of God? What did you think surgery was a trade? Yes, in surgery one can kill through a mistake, and killing a human being is like destroying the whole world. And you expect to do surgery without fear? 

Who do you think God?" you are 

Taylor's unusual excitement had drained his face of color; beads of perspiration glistened on his forehead and he pressed his hand on his chest several times. 435

"I'm sorry, Shanan," he went on quietly and apologetically, "I didn't mean to raise my voice. Go back to the hospital and proceed with your work. I hope from the bottom of my heart that you will never stop being afraid when a human life is in your hands. I'm really very tired now. My wife is right I have to rest a bit.. 

David stood up and said hesitantly: "Sir, may I ask you another question?" "Sure, Shanan, sure." "Did it ever happen that a patient died because of a mistake of yours?" "Yes, Shanan - it happened." "How did it affect you?" "That my friend cannot be described. I can only express it in one word underlined a hundred times - horrible!" "How did you overcome it?” "With the strength I had to make myself return to the operating room and keep on working. There is no other way to find solace, or the courage to go on."

David stood silent for a while. Taylor had made it all clear - "the strength to return to the operating room!" He was seized by a deep sadness. Gazing at Taylor with his dark, wistful eyes, he said: "It seems that I haven't been endowed by God with such strength. . . I can't return to the operating room." 

Taylor was about to answer David but a sharp pain in his chest, as though a nail were boring through him, cut his breath short. He closed his eyes and clenched his fists. David interpreted this as a sign of his complete exhaustion and tiptoed out of the room. The moment that Taylor was left alone he snatched the vial of nitroglycerin from his night table and placed one of the pills under his tongue. His pain gradually subsided; he drew a deep breath and felt relieved.

He lay thinking of David. The door opened slowly and 

Mrs. Taylor peeked in. She glanced at him, assumed that he had fallen asleep, and reluctant to awaken him was about to withdraw again. “Come in, dear; I'm not sleeping," he called out to her in a feeble voice. 436

She walked in, sat on the edge of his bed, placed a hand on his forehead and said: "Are you perspiring? How come? It's cool in the room." "It's nothing; I'm a little tired and. . . also depressed." "Depressed? Why?" "It's Shanan.” "Shanan? What's happened?" "He's going through a serious crisis. A false move can ruin his whole future." "But you can advise him how to act, so why be so upset?" "Advising isn't enough in this case; I must be with him." "So that's it! Don't you think, dear, that you are taking this too seriously? After all, what's Shanan to you?" "He is my pupil. I have led him and he has followed in my footsteps. He has permitted himself to be molded like clay in my hands. You don't know what it means to see yourself reborn in one of your students." "You have guided dozens of pupils, and some of them today are among the most outstanding surgeons in the country. 

I can't understand. How is Shanan so different from all the others?" "Because in him all my weaknesses are reflected. . . In him, as in me, the pain of every sick person finds an echo. For him as for me, a human life is most sacred of all. Like myself, he refused to do research because the research man is bound to experiment from time to time not only with animals, but with human beings. For him, as for me, medicine is not a trade but a divine mission. He calls it 'the mission of the Healer Of All Flesh.' I once asked him what the meaning was of 'Healer Of All Flesh,' and he answered, 'it's the medical title of the Lord.' He quoted a wonderful prayer for me, recited by Jews when they cleanse their bodies. It goes something like this: 'Blessed be Thou, Oh Lord, our God, King of the universe, Who hast formed man and wisdom, and created in him many passages and vessels. It is well known before Thy glorious throne, that if but one of these be opened, or one of these be closed, it would be impossible to exist and stand before Thee. 437

Blessed art Thou, Oh Lord, Who art the wondrous

Healer Of All Flesh.' 

Yes my dear, Shanan needs my help now and it is my duty not to desert him." "What do you intend to do?" "To go to the hospital tomorrow." "Eugene dear, you can't be serious! Dr. Chester ordered you to stay in bed for several weeks. Your heart has been strained." "Nonsense! Chester is an alarmist; several weeks would be an eternity for me. Staying in bed like this could kill me more quickly than the worst heart attack. Shanan is now very much like a swimmer who has nearly drowned, and he dreads the water as a terrifying enemy. The only way to overcome this dread is to force the nearly-drowned swimmer to plunge immediately into the water again and swim. . that's what I must do now with Shanan. There's no other way." 

The next morning at twenty minutes to eight, Dr. Taylor entered his private office in the Operating Pavilion and rang for Ruth MacDonald. Ruth's heart pounded with joy as she came running to him breathlessly. Her happiness vanished however, when she caught sight of him. Taylor looked sick - his face was ash-grey, his lips bloodless, and his lustreless eyes, sunken in their orbits, were hemmed by dark rings. "Good morning Miss MacDonald," he greeted her, in a voice which lacked its usual lively ring. "Good morning sir; it's good to see you back." "Thank you Miss MacDonald — it's good to be back. Only one day and it seems I've been away for God knows how long. What's today's schedule?" "It's a mix-up, sir." "How come? What's happened?” "Dr. Shanan postponed two of yesterday's cases for today.

They were re-scheduled to begin at eight o'clock this morning, but then he suddenly informed us that he wouldn't do any surgery today and that further instructions were to be awaited 438

from Dr. Newman." "These two cases must be the breast biopsy and the ovarian cyst if I'm not mistaken. We'll begin at the specified time.

Please call for the first patient and inform Dr. Shanan that he is to be ready to assist." 

Ruth looked at Taylor without budging. "Why are you staring at me that way, Miss MacDonald? 

Anything wrong?" "Sir," she asked hesitantly, "are you sure that you are feeling all right?" "Yes, surely; why do you ask?" "Forgive me Dr. Taylor, but you look so tired and worn out. Must you operate today?" "Yes, Miss MacDonald, I think so."

When David learned that Taylor had returned and had taken charge of the two cases re-scheduled for operation that morning, he was very happy. His recent misgivings suddenly assumed much smaller proportions. 

Hurrying through the long hospital corridors that led from the internes' quarters to the Operating Pavilion, David kept repeating exultantly: "Taylor is back! He's not sick! He's here with me again! At his side everything will become clear and sure again. I'll tell him I'm not mature enough yet to launch into the world on my own. . . I'll ask him to let me stay with him for another year or two, to work with him. 

He'll understand me - he'll agree."

Exhilarated and full of new hope, David stood beside 

Taylor at the wash-basin in the Operating Pavilion energetically scrubbing his hands, casting continuous glances at him to convince himself that Taylor was really there next to him. "What do you think of this case?" Taylor asked; "What do you expect to find?" "The little growth in the breast is hard but the surrounding tissues don't adhere to it. There is a good chance that it is benign. Breast tumors, however, are so unpredictable that you have to be ready for anything. All the necessary preparations have been made for a frozen section. If it turns out to 439

be malignant, the entire breast will immediately be removed together with the lymphatic glands of the axilla." "What do you think of the second case?" "There are several possibilities there. According to the physical examination there appears to be a cyst in the left ovary. That's probably the best guess. But it could also be another kind of tumor that originated in the uterus, or. 

Before David could complete his sentence the scrub-brush dropped out of Taylor's hands; his knees buckled and he slumped to the ground, his head and arms hanging over the edge of the wash-basin.

David was petrified and remained rooted to the spot. The entire staff was seized by panic - "Dr. Taylor fainted! Dr. 

Taylor fainted!" voices resounded on all sides. A few orderlies and doctors lifted the unconscious surgeon and carried him to his private office. 

Ruth MacDonald came running, her eyes wide with fear. 

Clutching David's shoulders and shaking him she screamed hysterically: "What's happened to him? What's happened to him?”

David stared ahead blankly and said in a monotone: "I don't know. . . He was just talking to me just this minute.” Then he blurted out, "Angina pectoris! Oh God, don't do it - don't take him away from us." no 

After Taylor had been placed on the divan in his private office he regained consciousness. His face was deathly pale; a cold, clammy sweat covered his body; his lips were cyanotic; his breathing short and shallow; his half-open eyes, glazed. "Shanan - where's Shanan?" he murmured. "Call in Shanan."

David walked over to Taylor and bent over him to catch the faint words: "Plunge back. . . and swim. You're a fine surgeon, Shanan. 

The Lord gave you a good heart. A human life. . . is holy to you... Yes, holy. . . And that's the way. . . of the Healer 

Of All Flesh.. 

With these words Taylor breathed his last. David kneeled down, and placing his forehead on the motionless chest of his teacher, he whispered: 440

"Yes, yours is the sure way. There is no abyss in your way.

I shall always follow it. I shall never deviate from it." 

Suddenly David felt a light tap on his shoulder. He raised his head and saw Dr. Chester standing near him. David rose, wiped his eyes and calmed down at once. He opened the door and stepped into the corridor. Her face pressed against the wall, Ruth stood weeping bitterly. In a voice ringing with assurance and determination David said: "Miss Mac- 

Donald, I am ready to operate. Please send for the first patient!" 441
